# Temple Architecture and Chandella Khajuraho - Live Learning Session

> **Learner-first lock:** This session teaches you to read a Hindu temple as three things at once - a sacred object, a ritual machine and a building - and then applies that reading across the whole structural tradition, from the first Gupta experiments to the last great Nayaka enclosures, with the Chandella temples at Khajuraho as its worked case. The dynastic politics of the Chandellas, Pallavas, Cholas, Chalukyas, Hoysalas, Kakatiyas, Vijayanagara and the Nayakas belongs to Ancient and Medieval History. Rock-cut excavation and the Ellora Kailasa belong to Topic 02. Freestanding sculpture and iconographic doctrine belong to Topic 06. Sultanate, Mughal and regional Indo-Islamic building belongs to Topic 04. Mural painting belongs to Topic 07, and heritage institutions, counts and policy belong to Topic 14. What is owned here is the temple as built form: plan, elevation, fabric, superstructure, the placement of images on it, the patronage that raised it, the ritual movement it was designed for, and the limits of every style label used to classify it.

## How to read this session

Work down the lessons in order. Each opens with the question a learner actually has at that point, draws the building before naming its parts, and only then supplies the Sanskrit term. Three habits are built deliberately.

1. **Describe before you classify.** "Nagara", "Dravida" and "Vesara" are modern organising labels laid over buildings that were raised by patrons who never used them. An answer that names the label first and describes afterwards is asserting; an answer that describes plan, superstructure and fabric and *then* names the family is arguing.
2. **Keep the three chronologies apart.** When a temple was built, when it was added to or repaired, and when it was rediscovered, protected or inscribed are three different dates. Most factual accidents in this topic come from collapsing them.
3. **Attach every claim to a named monument.** "Temples became more elaborate" earns nothing. "At Pattadakal the Virupaksha carries a Dravidian shikhara and the Papanatha a north Indian Nagara shikhara on a similar plan" earns the mark, because it is checkable.

Everything is drawn in text. No plan or elevation here needs an image file.

**Boundary map:** Ancient and Medieval History own the dynastic, military and revenue narrative of every dynasty named here; these lessons use politics only where it explains the timing and confidence of building. Topic 02 owns excavated architecture - chaitya, vihara, and the Ellora Kailasa in detail. Topic 04 owns Indo-Islamic and later regional building, and receives one correction recorded here. Topic 05 owns the Parliament-building resemblance. Topic 06 owns freestanding sculpture, iconographic doctrine and the reading method for images. Topic 07 owns mural painting at Kanchipuram, Panamalai, Thanjavur, Lepakshi and in Kerala. Topic 09 owns dance posture. Topic 11 owns inscriptions read as literature and script. Topic 13 owns bhakti and Vedanta doctrine. Topic 14 owns heritage institutions, protected-monument counts, danger-listing and conservation policy. These lessons touch each only where an architectural claim would otherwise be unsafe.

## Lesson 1 - Reading a temple: sacred object, ritual machine, and building

Progress: 1/21 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: what a temple is along three simultaneous axes, the four reading questions, and the exact scope of this topic

### Visual: one building, three simultaneous descriptions

```text
                       ONE STRUCTURE AT KHAJURAHO
                                  |
     +----------------------------+----------------------------+
     |                            |                            |
 SACRED OBJECT               RITUAL MACHINE                 BUILDING
     |                            |                            |

 deity: the sanctum          worshipper walks:            mass of dressed
 is a "womb house"           approach -> hall ->          stone on a
 holding the                 vestibule -> darshan         platform, with a
 principal image             -> circumambulation          spire above the
     |                            |                       sanctum
     |                            |                            |
 evidence: the               evidence: the                evidence: fabric,
 dedication, the             sequence of spaces           module, joint,
 image, the                  and the ambulatory           span, offset,
 subsidiary shrines          passage                      superstructure
     |                            |                            |

 temple it is                was used                     could be built
     |                            |                            |
     +----------------------------+----------------------------+
                                  |
                       and a fourth layer sits ON the building:
                       THE IMAGE PROGRAMME - who is carved, where,
                       and in what company
```

*Read the three columns as three questions asked of the same stone. A weak answer uses only the first. A strong answer moves between all three, and then uses the fourth to argue.*

### 1. The learner's real difficulty

Temple architecture is usually met as a vocabulary list followed by a two-column table: Nagara on the left, Dravida on the right, Vesara somewhere in the middle. The list is memorised and reproduced, and it scores badly, for a reason worth naming at the start. A table of contrasts answers the question "how do these two differ?" - but the examiner almost never asks that. The examiner asks what a dynasty contributed, what a style amalgamates, what a sculptor achieved, or what a carving evidences. Each of those demands a *reason*, and the table contains none.

The repair is to stop treating the temple as a shape to be recognised and start treating it as a set of decisions. Every part of a temple exists because someone had to solve a problem: where to put the image, how to bring worshippers to it, how to get them round it, how to mark the sanctum from outside, how to keep a heavy superstructure standing, and what to show on the walls that the public could actually see. Once the parts are attached to the problems, the vocabulary stops being a list.

### 2. The three axes, stated precisely

✅ **Fact - the sacred axis.** The basic form of a Hindu temple comprises a **garbhagriha** or sanctum sanctorum, "literally womb house", a small and generally cubicle room housing the principal deity; a **mandapa**, the entrance, which may be a portico or a hall and is generally designed to house a large number of worshippers; a **shikhara**, a mountain-like spire whose shapes vary from pyramidal to curvilinear; and a **vahana**, the mount or vehicle of the main deity, placed just before the sanctum sanctorum.

Notice how much is already implied. The sanctum is small and dark because it is a container, not an auditorium. The hall is large because people gather in it. The spire is above the sanctum, not above the hall, because it marks from outside what is most sacred inside. The vahana faces the deity because it belongs to the deity, not to the visitor.

✅ **Fact - the ritual axis.** An **ambulatory passageway around the sanctum sanctorum, used as a pradakshina path**, appears in the second stage of Gupta temple development, and in the Nagara school this covered passage runs around the garbhagriha. Circumambulation is therefore not decoration; it is a designed circulation, and it constrains the plan. A sanctum that is to be walked around must be free-standing within its enclosing wall.

⚠️ **Analytical inference - the built axis.** Sacred intention and ritual requirement still have to be executed in stone by people who can only build what the material and the technique allow. This is why the same religious idea produces a soapstone star-plan in Karnataka, a granite mass in the Kaveri delta, a fine sandstone cliff of sculpture in Bundelkhand and a moulded brick façade in Bengal. Holding the built axis separate from the other two is what allows you to explain variation instead of merely listing it.

### 3. The four reading questions

Every temple in this topic - and every temple you will be shown in an examination hall - can be described by answering four questions in this order.

| Question | What it establishes | Khajuraho example | Chola example |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Plan** - what is the shape on the ground and how do the parts connect? | Ritual sequence and the presence or absence of an ambulatory | High platform, connected halls running axially to the sanctum, no enclosure wall | Axial shrine inside a huge rectangular walled enclosure with gateways |
| **Elevation** - what is stacked above the sanctum, and what silhouette results? | Family membership and regional signature | Curvilinear shikhara massed with smaller replica spires | Stepped pyramidal vimana rising linearly |
| **Fabric** - what is it made of, and what does that permit? | Carving depth, span, durability and cost | Fine-grained sandstone in buff, pink and pale-yellow shades | Granite throughout, the first fully granite temple in the world |
| **Image programme** - who is carved, where, and with whom? | Patron priorities and the social world made visible | Deities, worshippers, dancers, musicians, teachers and disciples, courtly life, Vyalas and maithuna reliefs | Deity images in wall niches, a lion frieze at the base, bronzes housed within |

⚠️ **Analytical point:** plan, elevation and fabric are observational - you can settle them by looking. The image programme is observational in its content and interpretive in its meaning. That asymmetry is the reason a question can ask what temple sculpture *represents* and expect a qualified answer rather than a description.

### 4. What this topic owns, and where it stops

This is worth fixing now, because the single commonest way to lose marks here is to write an excellent paragraph that belongs to another topic.

| Owned here | Not owned here |
|---|---|
| Temple plan, elevation, fabric, superstructure and the architectural placement of sculpture | Freestanding sculpture, iconographic doctrine and the method of reading an image (Topic 06) |
| Structural temple building from the Gupta experiments onward | Excavated architecture: chaitya, vihara, and the Ellora Kailasa in detail (Topic 02) |
| Named patrons where patronage explains the building | Dynastic chronology, warfare, administration and revenue (Ancient and Medieval History) |
| The architectural record of a dynasty's temple programme | Mosque, tomb and palace vocabulary (Topic 04) |
| Inscriptions used as evidence of building, endowment or administration | Inscriptions read as literature, script history or epigraphic method (Topic 11) |
| Rediscovery and the fact of protection or inscription, with its year | Protected-monument counts, danger-listing, policy and institutional structure (Topic 14) |
| Murals named only as part of a temple's surviving programme | Painting technique, pigment, school and style (Topic 07) |

Two boundaries deserve an explicit word because they are genuinely close.

**With Topic 02.** The Rashtrakuta Kailasa at Ellora is a temple in every architectural sense - it has a sanctum, a hall, a superstructure and a full sculptural programme - but it was *excavated* from living rock rather than built up from the ground. Its detail belongs to the rock-cut topic. This session names it once, in its Deccan place, to show the limiting case of Deccan experiment, and does not duplicate its treatment.

**With Topic 06.** The Chola bronze Nataraja is the most famous object associated with Chola temples, and it is not architecture. This session may say that Chola temples placed exceptional importance on sculptural decoration, that many South Indian Shiva temples have a separate *natana-sabha* where the Nataraja image is placed, and that bronzes are one of the achievements that survive. The iconography of the dance - what the drum, the flame, the dwarf and the ring of fire mean - is Topic 06's.

### Comparative note: why this topic reads differently from Topic 01

For Harappan architecture, no building is inscriptionally named, no patron is recorded and the script is undeciphered, so function has to be reconstructed from form alone. Temple architecture inverts every one of those conditions. Buildings carry dedications; patrons are named, sometimes with the occasion of the commission; inscriptions record endowment, ritual and administration; and in several cases the craftsmen sign their work. The consequence is that vagueness has no excuse here. Where Topic 01 rewards a disciplined hedge, Topic 03 rewards a named monument with a named patron and an approximate date - and punishes the generic sentence that could have been written about any temple anywhere.

### Examination use

Every demand family in this topic rests on the four reading questions. A description demand on a school's salient features wants plan, elevation and fabric in that order. A contribution demand on a dynasty wants a *sequence* of decisions, which means plan and technique over time. An amalgamation demand wants you to identify which inherited element came from where, which is impossible without the vocabulary. And an evidence demand on what temple sculpture shows of social life wants the image programme read with its limits stated. Fix the four questions now and every later lesson slots into them.

### Revision notes

1. A temple is three things at once: a house for a deity, a route for a worshipper, and a load-bearing structure.
2. Basic form: garbhagriha (sanctum, "womb house", small, generally cubicle, houses the principal deity); mandapa (entrance portico or hall for a large number of worshippers); shikhara (mountain-like spire, pyramidal to curvilinear); vahana (the deity's mount, placed just before the sanctum).
3. The spire sits over the sanctum, not over the hall - it marks the sacred point from outside.
4. The ambulatory passage around the sanctum, used as a pradakshina path, is a designed circulation and constrains the plan.
5. Four reading questions, always in order: plan, elevation, fabric, image programme.
6. Plan, elevation and fabric are observational; the *meaning* of the image programme is interpretive.
7. Style labels are modern organising categories, not builders' self-descriptions - describe first, classify second.
8. Three chronologies stay separate: construction, later addition or repair, and modern rediscovery or inscription.
9. Owned here: built temple form, its fabric, its patronage and the architectural placement of images.
10. Not owned here: rock-cut detail (Topic 02), Indo-Islamic form (Topic 04), freestanding sculpture and iconographic doctrine (Topic 06), painting (Topic 07), heritage policy and counts (Topic 14), dynastic politics (Ancient and Medieval History).
11. Unlike Topic 01, this topic has named patrons, dated commissions and signed craftsmen - so generic sentences are not defensible.
12. The examiner's real question is almost never "how do Nagara and Dravida differ?" but "what was contributed, amalgamated, achieved or evidenced?"

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate opens an answer on Khajuraho with: "Khajuraho temples are built in the Nagara style, which is the north Indian style characterised by a curvilinear shikhara and the absence of a boundary wall." Identify what is analytically wrong with this opening even though every clause in it is factually defensible, and rewrite it so that the same facts do more work.

**Model answer:** The opening classifies before it describes, so the facts arrive as the consequences of a label rather than as evidence for one. "Nagara" is a modern organising category; saying a temple *is* Nagara and then listing the category's attributes tells the examiner that the candidate recognised a name, not that the candidate looked at a building. It also wastes the strongest Khajuraho fact by demoting it to a category trait: the absence of an enclosure wall is not a generic Nagara habit but the specific condition that turns the exterior into the display surface. A stronger rewrite: "The Khajuraho temples stand on high platform terraces without any enclosure wall, their halls running axially to a small sanctum crowned by a curvilinear spire massed with smaller replica spires - the fully developed form of the northern, Nagara, family, and a plan that makes the outer wall itself the principal field of sculpture." The facts are identical; the sentence now argues.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that naming the correct style is the achievement. Style identification is the entry ticket, not the answer. Two candidates can both write "Nagara" correctly and be separated entirely by whether they explain what the form *does* - here, that an unwalled, raised, axial temple is designed to be seen and circled from outside in full daylight, which is precisely why its sculptural programme is so ambitious.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Explain why a description of Indian temple architecture that proceeds from style labels to features is weaker than one that proceeds from features to style labels. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

Style labels such as Nagara, Dravida and Vesara are modern classificatory conveniences applied to buildings whose patrons and craftsmen never used them. An answer that begins from the label can only deduce features from the category, which reverses the historical direction of evidence and yields attributes that are true of a family rather than of a monument.

Proceeding from features restores that direction. Describing the Khajuraho temples as raised on high platform terraces without any enclosure wall, with axially linked halls and a curvilinear spire massed with subsidiary spirelets, establishes the morphology first; the family name then summarises a description already earned. The same discipline prevents category error, as at Pattadakal, where the Virupaksha carries a Dravidian shikhara and the Papanatha a north Indian Nagara shikhara on a similar plan.

Labels remain useful for organisation and comparison, provided they close an argument rather than open one.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the historical-direction-of-evidence point 3 + one fully described monument used as demonstration 3 + the Pattadakal counter-case that a label-first method would misfile 2 + the concession that labels retain organising value 1 + control of length and structure 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 2:** The four reading questions assume a temple already has a sanctum, a hall and a spire. None of that existed at the start. Before the vocabulary can be used, you need to see how a small flat-roofed square shrine acquired a platform, an ambulatory, a spire and four subsidiary shrines - because that sequence is itself an examinable chain.

## Lesson 2 - From shrine to temple: the Gupta five-stage sequence

                |

                v
STAGE 3        /\           SHIKHARA replaces the flat roof
              /  \          (still low, almost square, curvilinear)
          +--------+        PANCHAYATANA plan introduced
          |  GBH   |        Dashavatara temple, Deogarh (UP)
          +--------+        Durga temple, Aihole (Karnataka)
           s [main] s       s = four subsidiary shrines at the corners
             [mandap]       main shrine square + elongated mandap in front
           s         s      => ground plan reads as a CRUCIFIED shape
                |
                |  problem: the hall keeps growing against a square shrine
                v
STAGE 4   main shrine becomes MORE RECTANGULAR
          Ter temple (Maharashtra)
                |
                |  experiment: does the shrine have to be rectilinear at all?
                v
STAGE 5   CIRCULAR temple with shallow rectangular projections
          Maniyar Math, Rajgir (Bihar)

*Each arrow is a problem, not merely a date. Learn the five stages by the problem each solves and the example becomes easy to attach.*

### 1. Start from the problem: what does a shrine have to become?

A cult image needs a room. A room with a door and a roof is the whole of stage one, and that is genuinely where structural temple building begins. Everything that follows answers a practical or a ritual demand. Worshippers want to circle the deity, so an ambulatory is needed. A pilgrim approaching across open ground should be able to see where the sacred point is, so the roof over the sanctum rises. A great temple should not present a single deity in isolation, so subsidiary shrines appear. A crowd has to be sheltered, so the hall in front lengthens. And once builders are confident, they test whether the square is necessary at all.

This is why the five-stage sequence is worth memorising properly rather than as a list. It supplies the causal spine of any "development of temple architecture" question, and the examples are short enough to carry.

### 2. The five stages, exactly as they are recorded

✅ **Fact - the frame.** Temple architecture, with the development of a square sanctum and a pillared portico, emerged during the Gupta period, and there was a gradual progression from the flat-roofed, monolithic temples of the initial stages to the sculptured shikhara of the later years. The progression is divided into five stages.

✅ **Stage one.** The temples had a flat roof; they were square in shape; the portico was developed on shallow pillars; the entire structure was built on a low platform. Example: **Temple 17 at Sanchi, Madhya Pradesh**.

✅ **Stage two.** Most features of the earlier phase continued, but the platforms were higher or upraised, and some instances of two-storeyed temples have been found. The important addition of this phase was **a covered ambulatory passageway around the sanctum sanctorum or garbhagriha, used as a pradakshina path**. Example: **Parvati temple at Nachna Kuthara, Madhya Pradesh**.

✅ **Stage three.** This stage saw the emergence of **shikharas in place of a flat roof**, though they were still quite low and almost square, that is, curvilinear. The **Panchayatana style** of temple making was introduced: four subsidiary shrines along with the temple of the principal deity, the main temple square with an elongated mandap in front of it giving it a rectangular shape, and the subsidiary shrines placed opposite each other on either side of the mandap, giving the ground plan a **crucified shape**. Examples: **Dashavatara temple at Deogarh, Uttar Pradesh**, and **Durga temple at Aihole, Karnataka**.

✅ **Stage four.** The temples of this stage were almost similar except that the **main shrine became more rectangular**. Example: **Ter temple in Maharashtra**.

✅ **Stage five.** **Circular temples with shallow rectangular projections** were introduced, the rest of the features of the previous phase continuing. Example: **Maniyar Math at Rajgir, Bihar**.

### 3. Why stage three is the hinge

Stages one, two, four and five are refinements. Stage three is a change of kind, and it is the stage examiners return to, because two permanent features of Indian temple architecture enter together there.

The first is the **shikhara**. Once a superstructure rises over the sanctum, the temple acquires an exterior identity. The building can now be recognised at a distance, and the vertical mass becomes the thing regional schools compete over for the next eight centuries - curving inward at Khajuraho and Bhubaneswar, stepping up in linear tiers in the Tamil country, and being selected between, monument by monument, in the Deccan.

The second is the **panchayatana plan**, and it is worth pausing on because it is repeatedly half-remembered. It is not simply "five shrines". It is a principal shrine with four subsidiary shrines placed at the corners of the platform, opposite each other on either side of the mandapa, so that the composite ground plan reads as a cross. That plan does three things at once: it subordinates four deities to one without excluding them, it gives the main shrine a visual base of smaller masses, and it fixes the temple's orientation by making the axis unmistakable.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the panchayatana idea also travels. The Nagara family later takes it as a general habit, and the Dravida family adopts the same principal-plus-four arrangement inside a walled enclosure - which is exactly why a plan-level comparison between the two families is possible at all.

### 4. One example that will return, and one caution

**Dashavatara temple, Deogarh (Uttar Pradesh)** is worth holding firmly. It is the stage-three exemplar - an early temple with an emergent shikhara and a panchayatana arrangement - and it reappears in Lesson 5 and again in the verified 2026 Prelims item, where it is one of four monuments a candidate is asked to sort by shikhara style. Fix its location, **Uttar Pradesh**, because the state a temple stands in is exactly the sort of detail an objective question isolates.

❌ **Caution worth stating once.** The initial stages are described as "flat-roofed, monolithic temples". Do not convert the word *monolithic* here into a claim that Gupta structural temples were carved from single rocks in the Mahabalipuram sense. The five-stage sequence is a sequence of *built* structures; the genuinely monolithic temples of the tradition - the Pancha Rathas and the Ellora Kailasa - belong to the rock-cut and Deccan discussions in Lessons 15 and 17.

### Comparative note: a decline and a beginning in the same period

✅ The Gupta age saw a **decline in the development of stupas**; the Dhamek Stupa at Sarnath near Varanasi is a fine example of *renovation* of an earlier stupa during this period, marked as the spot where the Buddha gave his first sermon. Set that beside the five-stage temple sequence and the period acquires its architectural meaning: the dominant religious building type changes. One repertoire slows while another begins, and the same masons, patronage networks and quarries serve the new type. Stupa detail belongs to Topic 02; the *fact of the transition* belongs here because it explains why structural temple building accelerates when it does.

### Examination use

Any question beginning "trace the development", "discuss the evolution" or "the origins of temple architecture in India" is answered from this lesson plus Lesson 4. The marks are in the causal links, not in the five labels: platform raised *so that* an ambulatory can be enclosed; roof raised *so that* the sanctum is legible from outside; subsidiary shrines added *so that* a single dedication becomes a composite programme. Name Sanchi Temple 17, Deogarh and Aihole's Durga temple, and the answer is anchored. The 2026 Prelims item on Nagara-style shikharas is also partly answerable from here, because Deogarh is a stage-three temple of the northern tradition.

### Revision notes

1. Structural temple architecture, with a square sanctum and a pillared portico, emerged in the Gupta period.
2. The progression runs from flat-roofed shrines to the sculptured shikhara, in five stages.
3. Stage 1: flat roof, square plan, portico on shallow pillars, low platform - Temple 17, Sanchi (MP).
4. Stage 2: higher or upraised platform, some two-storeyed instances, and a covered ambulatory around the garbhagriha used as a pradakshina path - Parvati temple, Nachna Kuthara (MP).
5. Stage 3: shikhara replaces the flat roof, still low and almost square, that is, curvilinear; panchayatana introduced - Dashavatara, Deogarh (UP) and Durga temple, Aihole (Karnataka).
6. Panchayatana: principal shrine plus four subsidiary shrines, main temple square with an elongated mandap in front, subsidiary shrines opposite each other beside the mandap, producing a crucified ground plan.
7. Stage 4: the main shrine becomes more rectangular - Ter temple, Maharashtra.
8. Stage 5: circular temples with shallow rectangular projections - Maniyar Math, Rajgir (Bihar).
9. Stage 3 is the hinge: exterior legibility and composite dedication arrive together.
10. The Gupta age saw a decline in stupa development; Dhamek Stupa at Sarnath is a renovation of an earlier stupa, at the site of the first sermon.
11. "Flat-roofed, monolithic" in stage one must not be read as rock-cut monolithic temple building.
12. Deogarh recurs: stage-three exemplar, northern shikhara, and one of the four monuments in the verified 2026 Prelims shikhara item.

### Concept check

**Question:** The five-stage sequence is often reproduced as a list of roof shapes. State what is actually being solved at stages two and three, and explain why the panchayatana plan is a stronger indicator of temple maturity than the shikhara alone.

**Model answer:** Stage two solves circulation: raising the platform allows a covered ambulatory to be enclosed around the sanctum so that circumambulation becomes part of the building rather than an activity performed outside it. Stage three solves external legibility and composite dedication together: the shikhara replaces the flat roof so the sanctum is identifiable from a distance, and the panchayatana plan places four subsidiary shrines around the principal one, producing a crucified ground plan. The panchayatana is the stronger maturity indicator because a spire is a single vertical decision that can be executed on an otherwise simple shrine, whereas a five-shrine arrangement requires a governed platform, a fixed axis, a hierarchy among deities and a hall long enough to sit between the flanking shrines. It therefore evidences planning at the level of the whole site rather than one improvement to one roof.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating panchayatana as a count of five buildings. The examinable content is the *arrangement* - principal shrine, four subsidiaries placed opposite one another beside an elongated mandapa, the resulting cruciform plan - and the fact that not every example preserves every subsidiary shrine today, so a surviving four-shrine ruin may still be a panchayatana temple.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Trace the evolution of the structural Hindu temple in the Gupta period, and assess what the sequence tells us about changing ritual and patronage requirements. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model answer

Structural temple building in India begins with a square sanctum and a pillared portico in the Gupta period, and develops through five identifiable stages from a flat-roofed shrine to a spired temple.

The earliest temples were square, flat-roofed, carried a portico on shallow pillars and stood on a low platform, as at Temple 17, Sanchi. The second stage raised the platform, produced some two-storeyed instances, and added a covered ambulatory passage around the sanctum for circumambulation, as at the Parvati temple, Nachna Kuthara. The third stage replaced the flat roof with a low, almost square curvilinear shikhara and introduced the panchayatana plan of a principal shrine with four subsidiary shrines flanking an elongated mandapa, producing a cruciform ground plan, at Deogarh's Dashavatara temple and Aihole's Durga temple. The fourth made the main shrine more rectangular, as at Ter. The fifth introduced circular shrines with shallow rectangular projections, as at Maniyar Math, Rajgir.

Read as requirements rather than shapes, the sequence shows ritual pressure and patronage ambition together. The ambulatory materialises a rite in stone. The spire converts a private container into a public landmark. The panchayatana converts a single dedication into a programme, presupposing a patron able to endow five shrines.

Two qualifications matter. The stages describe a general progression, not a strict national chronology, and the same period saw stupa building decline, so the temple's rise is a transfer of architectural energy rather than a simple addition to it.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = all five stages with their correct defining feature 5 + the named example at each stage 4 + the ritual-and-patronage reading rather than a shape list 4 + the two qualifications, general-progression and stupa decline 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 3:** The sequence has now produced a temple with a platform, a hall, an ambulatory, a spire and subsidiary shrines. Before any regional school can be compared, each of those parts needs its exact name - and the names have to be learnt in the order a worshipper meets them, not in alphabetical order.

## Lesson 3 - The plan, walked: from the platform to the womb house

Progress: 3/21 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: complete plan vocabulary in ritual sequence - jagati, ardhamandapa, mandapa variants, antarala, garbhagriha, pradakshinapatha, panchayatana, parivaralaya, and the enclosure devices

CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the axial chain, drawn as a walk

```text
   YOU ARRIVE                                              YOU ARE DEEPEST
        |                                                         |
        v                                                         v

  [ JAGATI ]      [ ARDHAMANDAPA ]   [ MANDAPA ]  [ ANTARALA ]  [ GARBHAGRIHA ]
  raised           half-hall /        assembly     vestibule      sanctum
  platform         entrance porch     hall         connecting     "womb house",
  terrace          before the main    for many     hall to        small, generally
  you climb        hall               worshippers  sanctum        cubicle, dark
        |                |                |            |               |
   changes the      slows the        gathers      compresses      holds the
   ground level     approach         the crowd    the approach    principal deity
   and marks        and shelters                  so the last     with the VAHANA
   sacred ground    arrival                       step is         set just before it
                                                  narrow
        |----------------------------------------------------------|
                                  |
                        + PRADAKSHINAPATHA
                          ambulatory running AROUND the garbhagriha,
                          covered in the Nagara school; at Hoysala
                          temples the extended terrace on the star-shaped
                          platform performs the same duty in the open

  AROUND THE WHOLE COMPOSITION:
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
  |  NAGARA HABIT: generally NO elaborate boundary wall or        |
  |  gateway. Khajuraho: NO enclosure wall at all, high jagati.   |
  |                                                              |
  |  DRAVIDA HABIT: HIGH BOUNDARY WALLS, entered through a        |
  |  GOPURAM on the front wall; a WATER TANK inside the enclosure;|
  |  later, CONCENTRIC enclosures with graded gopurams and        |
  |  PRAKARAM corridors                                          |
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+

  PANCHAYATANA (plan, both families):     PARIVARALAYA (early Chola):
        s       s                          subsidiary shrines placed
          [MAIN]                           around the main shrine -
        [mandapa]                          six of them at Narttamalai
        s       s
   => cruciform ground plan
```

*Learn the chain in walking order. Every regional school in this topic is a variation on this one sequence, so the names only have to be learnt once.*

### 1. Why walking order beats alphabetical order

Plan vocabulary is usually taught as a glossary, and glossaries are forgotten because the terms have no relation to each other in the list. In a temple they have a strict relation: each space exists because of the space before it. The ardhamandapa exists because you cannot walk straight from open ground into an assembly hall. The antarala exists because you cannot walk straight from a crowded hall into a sanctum barely wider than the image. The sequence is a deliberate compression - wide and public at the front, narrow and private at the back - and the vocabulary is simply the list of stations along it.

Once you have that, the exam use becomes obvious. Describing a temple "from jagati to garbhagriha" gives you an ordered paragraph without any planning effort, and it demonstrates control of terminology in a way that a scattered glossary never does.

### 2. The stations, defined exactly

| Term | Precise meaning | Source status |
|---|---|---|
| **Jagati** | The high platform terrace on which a temple stands. At Khajuraho the temples are lofty and erected on a high platform terrace **without any enclosure wall**; at Hoysala temples the jagati follows a stellate design and its extended terrace acts as the pradakshinapatha | ✅ Fact |
| **Ardhamandapa** | The intermediate porch or half-hall before the main mandapa; a *mukhamandapa* or porch is recorded as added to the Chola sequence, and an *ardha-mandapa* as joined to the vimana in the earliest Chola phase | ✅ Fact |
| **Mandapa** | The entrance to the temple; a portico or a hall, generally designed to house a large number of worshippers | ✅ Fact |
| **Antarala** | The vestibule connecting the assembly hall to the garbhagriha; in the Dravida description it is explicitly the "vestibular tunnel" joining assembly hall and sanctum | ✅ Fact |
| **Garbhagriha** | The sanctum sanctorum, literally "womb house", a small, generally cubicle room housing the principal deity | ✅ Fact |
| **Vahana** | The mount or vehicle of the main deity, placed just before the sanctum sanctorum | ✅ Fact |
| **Pradakshinapatha** | The ambulatory passage around the sanctum, used for circumambulation; covered in the Nagara school | ✅ Fact |
| **Panchayatana** | Principal shrine plus four subsidiary shrines flanking an elongated mandapa, giving a cruciform ground plan; recorded as a Nagara habit and as the Dravida enclosure layout of "a principal temple and four subsidiary shrines" | ✅ Fact |
| **Parivaralaya** | A subsidiary shrine surrounding a Chola main shrine - six of them around the Narttamalai Shiva temple in the earliest phase | ✅ Fact |
| **Jagamohan** | The frontal mandapa of an Odisha temple, a hall for devotees to gather and participate in rituals | ✅ Fact |
| **Gopuram** | The high entrance gateway on the front wall of a Dravida temple's boundary wall | ✅ Fact |
| **Prakaram** | The wide corridor of a late Dravida temple, an outer part around the sanctum, enclosed or open, facilitating processions and circumambulation | ✅ Fact |
| **Toran** | The decorative arched gateway at the portico of a Solanki temple | ✅ Fact |
| **Kalyana mandapa** | A wedding hall within a temple complex, used for celebrations and events - recorded at the Vitthala complex, Hampi, and in incomplete form at Lepakshi | ✅ Fact |
| **Natana-sabha** | The separate hall in many South Indian Shiva temples where the image of Nataraja is placed | ✅ Fact |
| **Devakoshtha** | Niches formed by pilasters dividing the outer walls of a Chola temple, containing images of deities | ✅ Fact |

⚠️ **Analytical inference on the compression:** the sanctum is small in every school, however enormous the temple. Brihadishvara's vimana rises roughly sixty-six metres over a sanctum that a handful of people can occupy. That disproportion is the point: scale is expended on the approach, the enclosure and the superstructure, because those are the parts the public sees, while the sanctum stays a container.

### 3. What happens *around* the axis - the decisive plan difference

The axial chain is shared across the subcontinent. What differs is what encloses it, and this is where the two great families genuinely part.

✅ In the **Nagara** description, the temple premises generally did *not* have elaborate boundary walls or gateways; assembly halls stood in front of the principal shrine; temples were generally built on upraised platforms; porticos had a pillared approach; images of the river goddesses **Ganga and Yamuna** were placed outside the garbhagriha; and there were generally **no water tanks or reservoirs** in the temple premises.

✅ In the **Dravida** description, the temples were surrounded by **high boundary walls**; the front wall had a high entrance gateway, the **gopuram**; the premises were laid out in panchayatana style with a principal temple and four subsidiary shrines; the assembly hall was connected to the garbhagriha by the **antarala**; the entrance of the garbhagriha carried sculptures of **dvarapala, mithuna and yaksha**; and the presence of a **water tank inside the temple enclosure** was another feature.

⚠️ **Analytical point worth carrying into every comparison:** this is not a difference of decoration. An unwalled temple on a terrace is designed to be approached and seen from every side, so its outer wall must be worth looking at. A walled temple is designed to be entered through a controlled gate, so the gate becomes the thing worth looking at and the enclosure can grow by addition. The entire later history of the Dravida tradition - concentric enclosures, graded gopurams, corridors, tanks - is latent in the decision to build a wall.

### 4. Two plan devices that are repeatedly confused

**Panchayatana against parivaralaya.** Both mean "subsidiary shrines around a main shrine", and they are not interchangeable. Panchayatana is a specific arrangement of *four* subsidiary shrines producing a cruciform plan, named as such in the Gupta third stage and carried into both families. Parivaralaya is the Chola term for the subsidiary shrines encircling an early main shrine, and at Narttamalai there are **six** of them. If a question supplies a number, the number decides the term.

**Mandapa against gopuram.** A mandapa is a hall inside the composition; a gopuram is a gateway tower in the boundary wall. The confusion matters because the late Dravida story is precisely that the *gateway* outgrows the *sanctum tower*, and a candidate who treats a gopuram as a kind of shikhara cannot narrate that shift at all.

### Criticism and reply: is the axial chain a real plan or a textbook abstraction?

**The objection.** Actual temples are messier than the diagram. Many have more than one mandapa, some have none, the Odisha tradition names its hall differently, Hoysala temples put several shrines around a single hall so that no one axis dominates, and Lad Khan at Aihole has a rooftop shrine instead of a spire and a plan likened in the standard description to a village panchayat hall. Reducing all this to jagati-to-garbhagriha is a simplification.

**The reply.** The chain is a description of *relations*, not of a fixed number of rooms. Whatever a school adds, the ritual logic holds: something raises the temple, something shelters the arrival, something gathers the congregation, something narrows the approach, and something contains the image. The Odisha jagamohan is a mandapa by function; the Hoysala star-plan multiplies the sanctum end of the chain rather than abolishing it. Where the chain genuinely fails - Lad Khan's rooftop shrine, the Chausath Yogini enclosures with their open sky and ring of cells - that failure is itself examinable, because it identifies the monument as exceptional. The abstraction is useful precisely because its exceptions are informative.

### Examination use

Plan vocabulary is tested in two ways. Objectively, it is tested by pairing - a term against a school, or a term against a monument - which is why the panchayatana/parivaralaya and mandapa/gopuram distinctions above are worth hardening. In Mains it is tested implicitly: an answer that says "sanctum" throughout is competent, and an answer that says "garbhagriha entered through an antarala from a pillared mandapa, the whole raised on a jagati" is demonstrably in command. Use the terms, but define each on first use inside the answer - one clause is enough, and it protects you if the examiner is not a specialist.

### Revision notes

1. Walking order: jagati, ardhamandapa, mandapa, antarala, garbhagriha - wide and public in front, narrow and private behind.
2. Garbhagriha: sanctum, "womb house", small, generally cubicle, holds the principal deity; the vahana stands just before it.
3. Mandapa: the entrance, a portico or hall, generally for a large number of worshippers; Odisha calls its frontal hall the jagamohan.
4. Antarala: the vestibule or vestibular tunnel joining hall to sanctum.
5. Jagati: the raised platform terrace - high and unwalled at Khajuraho, stellate with an ambulatory terrace at Hoysala temples.
6. Pradakshinapatha: the ambulatory around the sanctum, covered in the Nagara school.
7. Panchayatana: principal shrine plus four subsidiaries flanking an elongated mandapa, cruciform plan; recorded for both Nagara and Dravida layouts.
8. Parivaralaya: Chola subsidiary shrines around the main shrine - six at Narttamalai.
9. Nagara habits: generally no elaborate boundary wall or gateway; upraised platform; pillared portico; Ganga and Yamuna outside the garbhagriha; generally no water tank in the premises.
10. Dravida habits: high boundary walls; gopuram on the front wall; panchayatana layout; antarala; dvarapala, mithuna and yaksha at the sanctum entrance; a water tank inside the enclosure.
11. Devakoshtha: pilaster-formed niches on Chola outer walls holding deity images.
12. Prakaram: wide late-Dravida corridor for processions and pradakshina; toran: Solanki arched portico gateway; kalyana mandapa: wedding hall; natana-sabha: separate Nataraja hall.
13. A gopuram is a gateway tower, never a sanctum tower - the late Dravida story depends on keeping them apart.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two temples have identical axial chains - platform, porch, hall, vestibule, sanctum - but one has no boundary wall and the other has a high wall entered through a gateway tower. Explain why this single difference changes where each temple invests its sculpture, and name one monument on each side.

**Model answer:** An unwalled temple is approached and seen from all sides, so its outer wall is the display surface and sculpture is concentrated there, across the whole elevation, to be read in daylight while circling the building; Khajuraho, whose lofty temples stand on a high platform terrace without any enclosure wall, is the clearest case, and its dense integrated figural programme follows from that exposure. A walled temple is seen first as a boundary and entered through one controlled point, so the gateway becomes the principal public surface and the sanctum tower is reserved for the interior view; the Chola enclosure at Thanjavur, with its huge rectangular enclosure and large gopurams, and its successor tradition in which the gopura becomes more dominant than the vimana, is the counter-case. The enclosure decision therefore determines the sculptural economy, not merely the security of the site.

**Misconception to avoid:** Thinking the wall is a defensive or administrative detail with no artistic consequence. Enclosure is a design decision about visibility, and it explains why two traditions with the same ritual chain produce completely different sculptural distributions - a dense continuous wall at Khajuraho, a towered gateway in the Tamil country.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Describe the plan of a developed Hindu temple, and explain how the presence or absence of an enclosure wall shapes the character of a regional school. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

A developed Hindu temple is organised as a compressed axial sequence. A raised platform terrace, the jagati, lifts the structure; an entrance porch, the ardhamandapa, precedes a pillared mandapa built to hold a large number of worshippers; a vestibule, the antarala, narrows the approach to the garbhagriha, the small cubicle "womb house" containing the principal deity, with the deity's vahana placed just before it. An ambulatory passage around the sanctum serves circumambulation, and four subsidiary shrines may flank the hall in the panchayatana arrangement.

Enclosure then decides character. Nagara temples generally lack elaborate boundary walls or gateways, and Khajuraho, standing on a high terrace with no enclosure wall at all, therefore makes its outer wall the principal field of sculpture. Dravida temples are ringed by high walls entered through a gopuram, so artistic emphasis migrates outward to the gateway, and enclosures can be added concentrically.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the axial chain named in correct ritual order with each term glossed 4 + ambulatory and panchayatana 2 + the enclosure contrast stated as a design decision rather than a difference of taste 3 + one named monument on each side 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 4:** The plan is now complete at ground level. What has not been described is everything above it - and since regional schools are distinguished above all by what they stack over the sanctum, the elevation vocabulary is where the classification argument actually happens.

## Lesson 4 - The elevation, built up: spires, offsets and finials

Progress: 4/21 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: shikhara types, ratha offsets, urushringa clustering, amalaka and kalasha, vimana against shikhara, the Dravida crowning octagon, and the gopuram distinction

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the elevation, from wall plane to finial

```text
                              O   <- KALASHA  (spherical, the finial)
                            =====  <- AMALAKA (horizontal FLUTED DISC at
                             | |                the vertical end of the spire)
              NAGARA        /   \
              SHIKHARA     /     \      three Nagara types:
              (curves     /       \     (1) LATINA / REKHA-PRASAD
               inward    /         \        square base, walls CURVE INWARD
               to a     /           \       to a point at the top
               point)  /  u   u   u  \  (2) PHAMSANA
                      /  u  [MAIN] u  \     broader base, SHORTER than Latina,
                     /  u    spire  u  \    slopes upward on a STRAIGHT LINE
                    +-----------------+ (3) VALABHI
                    |   WALL PLANE    |     rectangular base, roof rises into
                    |  divided into   |     VAULTED chambers = wagon-vaulted
                    |  vertical RATHAS|
                    +-----------------+
         u = URUSHRINGA, subsidiary miniature spires clustered against
             the main shikhara -> the mountain-range silhouette

   RATHA OFFSETS, seen from ABOVE (the wall in plan):

     TRIRATHA        PANCHARATHA        SAPTARATHA       NAVARATHA
     _|‾|_           _|‾|_|‾|_          _|‾|_|‾|_|‾|_    ... and so on
     3 vertical      5 vertical         7 vertical       9 vertical
     planes          planes             planes           planes
     -> each plane is a PANEL for narrative sculpture

   ------------------------------------------------------------------

                        /\          DRAVIDA VIMANA
                       /--\         stepped PYRAMID, rising LINEARLY
                      /----\        (not curved)
                     /------\       crowning element shaped as an
                    /--------\      OCTAGON, called the shikhara here -
                   /----------\     similar in position to the Nagara
                  +------------+    kalash, but NOT spherical
                  | GARBHAGRIHA|
                  +------------+
          ONE vimana only, over the main temple.
          Subsidiary shrines do NOT carry vimanas - unlike Nagara.

   AND, IN THE BOUNDARY WALL, NOT OVER THE SANCTUM:

         /‾‾‾‾‾‾‾‾‾‾‾‾\      GOPURAM - the high entrance gateway
        /   G O P U R A \     tower on the front wall. A GATE, not a
       /------------------\   sanctum tower. Confusing the two makes
      +--------------------+  the later Dravida story unnarratable.
```

*Everything above the wall plane is where regional identity is declared. Learn this diagram and most style questions become describable rather than guessable.*

### 1. The problem: two traditions, one word

The single most persistent confusion in this topic is that *shikhara* means two different things depending on which tradition is speaking, and the standard vocabulary carries both meanings without merging them.

In the north, the **shikhara** is the whole superstructure over the sanctum - the mountain-like spire, curvilinear in the mature form. In the south, the whole superstructure over the sanctum is the **vimana**, a stepped pyramid that rises linearly rather than curving, and the word **shikhara** is reserved for the *crowning element* at its top, which is shaped as an octagon and is similar in position to the Nagara kalash but not spherical.

So: a northern temple's shikhara is a tower; a southern temple's shikhara is a finial. Once that is fixed, a whole family of careless sentences disappears.

### 2. The three Nagara spire types, and what distinguishes them

✅ **Fact.** Nagara shikharas were generally of three types.

| Type | Shape, exactly | How to recognise it in a description |
|---|---|---|
| **Latina** or **Rekha-prasad** | Square at the base; the walls **curve inward to a point** at the top | The classic single curvilinear tower - this is what most people picture as "the north Indian spire" |
| **Phamsana** | **Broader base**, **shorter** in height than the Latina, sloping upwards **on a straight line** | Squat and pyramid-like; straight slope, not a curve - frequently used over halls rather than sanctums |
| **Valabhi** | **Rectangular base**, the roof rising into **vaulted chambers**; also called **wagon-vaulted** roofs | A barrel or wagon profile - the odd one out, and the one that recurs in the Odisha khakhara deul |

⚠️ **Analytical inference worth stating in an answer:** these three are not three fashions. They correspond loosely to three jobs - a tall marker over the sanctum, a lower covering over an assembly space, and a long barrel over a rectangular cell. A developed northern temple can therefore carry more than one of them at once, which is why the silhouette of a mature Nagara temple reads as a ridge line rather than a single peak.

### 3. Rathas: the wall as a set of vertical panels

✅ **Fact.** Inside the temple, the wall was divided into **three vertical planes or rathas**, giving **triratha** temples; later **pancharatha**, **saptaratha** and even **navaratha** temples came into existence, and the vertical planes were used as different panels to make narrative sculptures.

This is a deceptively important fact, because it explains the connection between plan and sculpture. A ratha is a projection of the wall plane - a vertical offset that runs from the base to the top of the spire. Increasing the number of offsets does three things simultaneously: it complicates the plan into a star-like or serrated outline, it multiplies the vertical bands of the tower, and it creates more framed surfaces for figural carving. A saptaratha temple is not merely more elaborate than a triratha one; it has more places to put sculpture and a more animated shadow line.

⚠️ **This is the structural reason Khajuraho's sculpture can be as dense as it is.** A deeply offset wall plane produces recesses and projections, so figures can be set in bands that turn corners, catch raking light and appear to move as the viewer circles the building.

### 4. Amalaka, kalasha and urushringa: the top of the tower

✅ **Fact.** The vertical end of the shikhara ended in a **horizontal fluted disc known as the amalaka**, and on top of that a **spherical shape known as the kalash** was placed.

✅ **Fact.** The **urushringa** is a subsidiary miniature spire clustered against the main shikhara; clustering produces the mountain-range silhouette of mature Nagara temples.

⚠️ **Analytical inference.** The urushringa is the device that converts a single tower into a massif. Each small replica spire repeats the profile of the main one at reduced scale, so the eye reads a series of ascending peaks culminating in the largest. That is why the mature Khajuraho elevation looks like a mountain range rather than a spike, and why it is fair to call it the architectural expression of the shikhara-as-mountain idea, since the shikhara is itself defined as a "mountain like" spire.

### 5. The Dravida elevation, and its two decisive restrictions

✅ **Fact.** Under the Dravida style, the spire is in the form of a **stepped pyramid that rises up linearly rather than curved**, known as the **vimana**; the crowning element is shaped as an **octagon** and is known as the **shikhara**, similar to the kalash of the Nagara temple but not spherical; and there is **only one vimana** in Dravidian architecture, on top of the main temple - **the subsidiary shrines do not have vimanas, unlike Nagara architecture**.

Those last two clauses are the ones objective questions live on. The Dravida composition concentrates verticality on one point. The Nagara composition distributes it, with subsidiary shrines carrying their own superstructures and urushringas clustering against the main tower. That is a real morphological difference, and it is far more useful in an answer than "curved versus pyramidal".

❌ **Trap.** Nagara shikhara and Dravida vimana are not simply the same object, and it is not the case that one appears exactly once while the other appears over every shrine. Both are sanctum-superstructure terms with regional morphologies, and multi-shrine complexes require monument-specific description rather than a rule applied from memory.

### 6. The gopuram, kept in its place

✅ **Fact.** The front wall of a Dravida temple had a **high entrance gateway known as gopuram**.

A gopuram belongs to the boundary wall. It is not over the sanctum, it does not mark the sacred point, and it is not a variety of vimana. The reason this matters far beyond terminology is that the most important single narrative in southern temple architecture is the migration of emphasis from the vimana to the gopura - visible in the last Chola phase of the twelfth and thirteenth centuries, carried into Vijayanagara with the Raya Gopuram, and completed under the Nayakas with concentric enclosures whose gopurams grow larger with each outer wall. A candidate who has merged the two words cannot tell that story.

### Comparative note: what a silhouette argues

Stand far enough back from each tradition and the silhouettes make an argument about where authority is placed. The mature Nagara temple concentrates its mass over the sanctum and gathers subordinate peaks around it: the sacred centre is also the visual centre, and the composition is centripetal. The mature Dravida complex puts its tallest structures on its outermost walls, so the sacred centre is the *least* visible point from outside and the visitor is drawn through successive thresholds: the composition is centrifugal. Neither is superior. But an answer that notices this converts a shape comparison into an argument about religious space, which is what "compare" questions are actually asking for.

### Examination use

The verified 2026 Prelims question asks which of four named temples has or have a Nagara-style shikhara, listing Malegitti Shivalaya at Badami, the Huchimalligudi Temple at Aihole, the Dashavatara Temple at Deogarh and the Virupaksha Temple at Pattadakal. That item is unanswerable from a two-column table and entirely answerable from this lesson plus Lessons 2 and 7: it tests whether you know that Deccan sites carry *both* superstructure grammars, and that a monument's location tells you nothing about its spire. The Mains use is different: elevation vocabulary is what allows a 15-mark comparison to be specific, and it is the only way to narrate the vimana-to-gopura shift that carries a 20-mark southern trajectory answer.

### Revision notes

1. North: shikhara = the whole superstructure over the sanctum. South: vimana = the whole superstructure; shikhara = the octagonal crowning element.
2. Three Nagara shikhara types: Latina or Rekha-prasad (square base, walls curve inward to a point); Phamsana (broader base, shorter, straight upward slope); Valabhi (rectangular base, roof rising into vaulted chambers, wagon-vaulted).
3. The spire's vertical end carries the amalaka, a horizontal fluted disc; the kalash, a spherical form, sits on top of it.
4. Urushringas are subsidiary miniature spires clustered against the main shikhara, producing the mountain-range silhouette.
5. The wall is divided into vertical planes or rathas: triratha, then pancharatha, saptaratha, navaratha; each plane is a panel for narrative sculpture.
6. More rathas means a more serrated plan, a more banded tower and more framed surfaces for figures.
7. Dravida vimana: stepped pyramid rising linearly, not curved.
8. Only one vimana, over the main temple; Dravida subsidiary shrines do not carry vimanas, unlike Nagara.
9. The Dravida crowning shikhara is octagonal, positionally like the Nagara kalash but not spherical.
10. Gopuram: a high entrance gateway in the boundary wall - a gate tower, never a sanctum tower.
11. The vimana-to-gopura migration is the central southern narrative: late Chola, then Vijayanagara's Raya Gopuram, then Nayaka graded enclosures.
12. Silhouette argument: Nagara is centripetal, massing over the sanctum; mature Dravida is centrifugal, massing on the outer walls.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "The Dravida temple has a vimana over the main shrine and smaller vimanas over its four subsidiary shrines, while the Nagara temple has a single shikhara." Correct both halves of the sentence precisely, and state what the corrected version reveals about the two compositions.

**Model answer:** Both halves are inverted. In Dravidian architecture there is only one vimana, on top of the main temple, and the subsidiary shrines do not carry vimanas, a point stated explicitly and contrasted with Nagara practice. The Nagara composition is the one that distributes superstructures: subsidiary shrines in a panchayatana arrangement may carry their own spires, and the main shikhara is additionally massed with clustered urushringas, miniature replica spires that build the mountain-range silhouette. Corrected, the sentence reveals the real compositional difference: Dravida concentrates verticality at a single point and expends its scale on enclosure and gateway, while Nagara distributes verticality across the whole composition and expends its scale on the sanctum tower and the wall beneath it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that "bigger temple" means "more towers" in both traditions. The Dravida tradition grows by adding enclosures and gateways, not by adding sanctum towers; a vast Nayaka complex may still have one modest vimana at its centre surrounded by fourteen gopurams, as at Madurai.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The superstructure, not the plan, is where Indian regional temple schools declare their identity." Critically examine. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model answer

The claim is largely sound, and its limits are instructive.

Its strength is comparative. Plans across the subcontinent are convergent: a raised platform, a hall, a vestibule and a small cubicle sanctum with an ambulatory recur from Bundelkhand to the Kaveri delta, and panchayatana is recorded for both families. Superstructures diverge sharply. The north develops three distinct spire types - Latina curving inward to a point, Phamsana sloping straight from a broader base, Valabhi rising into wagon-vaulted chambers - and masses the sanctum tower with clustered urushringas beneath an amalaka and kalasha. The south builds a single stepped pyramidal vimana rising linearly, crowned by an octagonal element, and permits no vimana over subsidiary shrines. Odisha names its own tower types - rekha, pidha and khakhara deula - which is regional identity expressed in the superstructure vocabulary itself.

The claim nevertheless overstates. Enclosure is a plan decision with the largest consequences of all: an unwalled Khajuraho temple makes its outer wall the display surface, while a walled Dravida temple migrates emphasis to the gopuram, and that migration is a plan-level growth by concentric addition. Ratha offsets are likewise plan decisions that determine how much sculpture the elevation can hold.

The examined verdict is that the superstructure is where identity is most *legibly* declared, while the plan is where it is most consequentially decided - and Pattadakal, where one plan carries two different spire grammars, proves both halves at once.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = a two-sided verdict rather than agreement 3 + the convergent-plan evidence 3 + the divergent-superstructure evidence with exact type names 5 + the enclosure and ratha counter-argument 3 + the Pattadakal case used to close both halves 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 5:** The vocabulary is now complete enough to classify. The next question is whether "Nagara" names a style at all, or a family of quite different regional solutions that happen to share a curve - and the answer determines how every later comparison must be written.

## Lesson 5 - The Nagara family: one grammar, several solutions

Progress: 5/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the defining Nagara features, the three named sub-schools plus the eastern school, and why internal variation is the examinable content
