CA search: "Not required - classification lesson"
CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the family and its members

```text
                     NAGARA SCHOOL
          northern India, from the 5TH CENTURY AD onward
          sub-schools emerged in the WESTERN, CENTRAL and
          EASTERN parts of the country
                          |
     SHARED GRAMMAR (the recorded feature list)
     - generally PANCHAYATANA: subsidiary shrines on a crucified plan
     - assembly halls / mandaps IN FRONT of the principal shrine
     - GANGA and YAMUNA images placed OUTSIDE the garbhagriha
     - generally NO water tanks or reservoirs in the premises
     - built on UPRAISED PLATFORMS
     - porticos with a PILLARED approach
     - shikhara in three types: Latina / Phamsana / Valabhi
     - amalaka then kalash at the summit
     - wall divided into RATHAS: tri / pancha / sapta / nava
     - pradakshina path around the sanctum, COVERED
     - generally NO elaborate boundary walls or gateways
                          |
     +--------------+-----+------+-----------------+
     |              |            |                 |
  ODISHA        KHAJURAHO     SOLANKI          (PALA-SENA,
  (Kalinga)     (Chandella)   (Maru-Gurjara)    Bengal: tall
     |              |            |               curving shikhara
  deul types:   high jagati,  walls DEVOID       crowned by a large
  rekha /       NO enclosure  of carvings,       amalaka, "SIMILAR
  pidha /       wall at all,  TORAN gateways,    TO THE ODISHA
  khakhara;     dense figural SURYA KUND step    SCHOOL" - but in
  BOUNDARY      programme on  tank, equinox      TERRACOTTA BRICK)
  WALLS usual;  the outer     alignment
  exteriors     wall
  lavish,
  interiors
  bare
     |              |            |                 |
  THE POINT: four solutions to ONE grammar. "Nagara" is a FAMILY,
  and the examinable content is the VARIATION, not the label.
```

*If you can fill this diagram from memory you can answer any "regional schools of north Indian temple architecture" demand.*

### 1. The learner's problem: a label that flattens everything

"Nagara" is usually reduced to a single sentence - north Indian, curvilinear spire, no boundary wall - and that sentence is then applied indiscriminately to Bhubaneswar, Khajuraho and Modhera. It survives contact with a description question and collapses immediately on a comparison question, because the three places disagree on almost everything that matters.

Odisha temples usually have boundary walls; Khajuraho has none at all. Odisha exteriors are lavishly decorated while interiors are generally quite bare; Khajuraho integrates its figural programme into a fully articulated wall; Solanki temple walls are recorded as **devoid of any carvings**, with decoration concentrated in arched *toran* gateways at the porticos. One family, three incompatible surfaces. The examiner is aware of this, which is why "regional schools of Nagara architecture" is a recurring demand and why the generic sentence cannot answer it.

### 2. The shared grammar, stated exactly

✅ **Fact.** From the **5th century AD onwards**, a distinct style of temple architecture developed in the northern part of India, known as the Nagara style, and even within it different sub-schools emerged in the **western, central and eastern** parts of the country. Its recorded features are these:

- temples generally followed the **panchayatana** style, with subsidiary shrines laid out in a crucified ground plan with respect to the principal shrine;
- **assembly halls or mandaps** stood in front of the principal shrine;
- outside the garbhagriha, images of the **river goddesses Ganga and Yamuna** were placed;
- generally there were **no water tanks or reservoirs** in the temple premises;
- temples were generally built on **upraised platforms**;
- the porticos had a **pillared approach**;
- shikharas were generally of three types - **Latina or Rekha-prasad, Phamsana and Valabhi**;
- the vertical end of the shikhara ended in the **amalak**, with a **kalash** above it;
- the wall was divided into vertical planes or **rathas**, from triratha to navaratha, used as panels for narrative sculpture;
- the **pradakshina path** around the sanctum was **covered**;
- the premises generally did **not** have elaborate **boundary walls or gateways**.

Two of these are easy to skip and are worth marking. The **Ganga and Yamuna** placement is a genuinely useful identification cue and a rare instance of a fixed iconographic rule in a plan description - and it belongs here, as architectural placement, while the iconography of the goddesses belongs to Topic 06. The absence of water tanks is the cleanest single contrast with the Dravida enclosure, which is recorded as containing one.

### 3. The three named sub-schools, and the fourth that behaves like one

✅ **Fact.** Under the Nagara school, three sub-schools emerged: **Odisha**, **Khajuraho** and **Solanki**, also known as the **Maru-Gurjara** style.

✅ **Fact.** In the Bengal region, the style of architecture came to be known as the **Pala and Sena school**, developing between the **8th and 12th century AD**; its temples had a **tall, curving shikhara crowned by a large amalaka, similar to the Odisha School**.

⚠️ **Analytical point on the fourth.** Pala-Sena is conventionally treated separately rather than listed as a Nagara sub-school, yet its superstructure is described as an Odisha-like curving shikhara under a large amalaka. The honest formulation for an answer is that the eastern school shares the Nagara superstructure grammar while differing radically in material - burnt brick and terracotta rather than dressed stone - and that this makes it the best single case for arguing that material, not geography, produces regional character. Lesson 9 develops it. Do not silently promote it to a fourth sub-school.

### 4. Variation as the examinable content

Set the four side by side on the dimensions that actually differ, and the "family" claim becomes demonstrable rather than asserted.

| Dimension | Odisha / Kalinga | Khajuraho / Chandella | Solanki / Maru-Gurjara | Pala-Sena / Bengal |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Superstructure | Three deul types - rekha (curvilinear sanctum tower), pidha (pyramidal ritual hall), khakhara (barrel-roofed, often goddess shrines) | Curvilinear shikhara massed with clustered urushringas | Shikhara, with the school's signature complement being a step tank rather than a tower device | Tall curving shikhara crowned by a **large amalaka**, similar to Odisha |
| Enclosure | Boundary walls usual | **No enclosure wall**; high platform terrace | Temple with a stepped *surya kund* nearby | Not recorded for the school |
| Wall treatment | Exteriors lavishly decorated, interiors generally bare | Dense integrated figural programme over the whole outer wall | Walls **devoid of carvings**; decorative arched *torans* at the porticos | Moulded terracotta brick; sculpture in stone and metal with a highly lustrous finish |
| Material | Dressed stone, including the reddish-gold sandstone that names the Rajarani temple | Fine-grained sandstone in buff, pink and pale-yellow shades | Sandstone, black basalt and soft marble | **Burnt bricks and clay - terracotta bricks - as the principal building material** |
| Orientation and alignment | Not recorded as a school rule | All temples except the Chaturbhuja face the sunrise | Most temples east-facing; at the **equinoxes** sun rays fall directly into the central shrine | Not recorded for the school |
| Erotic register | **Prominent at Konark and Bhubaneswar** | Present, as one register among many | Not the school's emphasis | Not recorded for the school |
| Named exemplars | Parsurameswar (Shailodbhava, 7th c.), Vaitala (Khakhara, Queen Tribhuvana-Mahadevi, Bhauma Kara, 9th c.), Mukteswar (Somavamshi, 10th c.), Rajarani (Somavamshi, 11th c.), Lingaraja (Somavamshi, 11th c.), Jagannath Puri (Anantavarman Chodaganga, Eastern Ganga, 12th c.), Konark (Narasimhadeva I, Eastern Ganga, 13th c., UNESCO 1984) | Kandariya Mahadeva, Lakshmana, Vishvanath, Parsvanath, Chitragupta, Chausath-yogini, Matangeshvara | **Modhera Sun Temple, Gujarat, built 1026-27 AD by Bhima I** of the Chaulukya or Solanki dynasty | **Siddhesvara Mahadeva Temple, Barakar**, and temples around **Bishnupur** (West Bengal) |

⚠️ **The argument this table supports:** "Nagara" names a shared grammar of plan, spire profile and finial, within which regional builders made opposite decisions about enclosure, wall surface and material. Writing that sentence, and then proving it with two contrasting rows from this table, is a complete answer to any regional-schools demand.

### Criticism and reply: is "Nagara" a useful category at all?

**The objection.** If Odisha walls it and Khajuraho does not; if Khajuraho covers its walls with figures and Solanki leaves them bare; if Bengal builds in brick and the others in stone - then the category is doing very little work. It may even mislead, by implying a coherence that the buildings deny.

**The reply.** The category survives because the invariants are real and specific. Every member raises the temple on a platform, crowns the sanctum with a curvilinear spire whose vertical end carries a fluted amalaka and a kalash above it, and treats the wall as a set of vertical offsets. Those are not vague resemblances; they are a shared construction logic. What the objection correctly establishes is the *scope* of the label: Nagara is a family term at the level of superstructure and plan logic, and it has no authority at the level of enclosure, surface or material. An answer that uses it at the first level and describes at the second is using it correctly.

### Examination use

Two demand families sit directly on this lesson. "Discuss the regional schools of the Nagara style" is answered by the table above with one named exemplar per school and the variation argument stated explicitly. "Compare Nagara and Dravida" - Lesson 6 - is answered far better once you can say that Nagara is itself plural, because the comparison then becomes principled rather than a two-column list. The 2026 Prelims item on Nagara shikharas also tests exactly this: it lists Deccan monuments to see whether the candidate believes that "Nagara" is a geographical fact rather than a morphological one.

### Revision notes

1. Nagara: northern India, from the 5th century AD, with sub-schools emerging in western, central and eastern parts.
2. Shared grammar: panchayatana tendency; mandaps in front of the principal shrine; Ganga and Yamuna outside the garbhagriha; generally no water tank; upraised platforms; pillared porticos; Latina, Phamsana and Valabhi spire types; amalaka and kalash; ratha divisions; covered pradakshina; generally no elaborate boundary wall or gateway.
3. Three named sub-schools: Odisha (Kalinga), Khajuraho (Chandella), Solanki (Maru-Gurjara).
4. Pala-Sena, Bengal, 8th-12th century AD: tall curving shikhara crowned by a large amalaka, "similar to the Odisha School", but built in terracotta brick - treat as a distinct regional school sharing the Nagara superstructure grammar.
5. Odisha usually walls its temples; Khajuraho has no enclosure wall at all.
6. Odisha exteriors are lavishly decorated and interiors generally bare; Solanki walls are devoid of carvings, with decorative torans at the porticos.
7. Solanki signature: the stepped *surya kund*, and equinox alignment into the central shrine; Modhera Sun Temple, 1026-27 AD, Bhima I.
8. Khajuraho signature: high jagati, no enclosure, dense figural programme, all temples except the Chaturbhuja facing sunrise.
9. Erotic imagery is prominent at Konark and Bhubaneswar as well as Khajuraho - it is a regional parallel, not a Chandella monopoly.
10. Bengal signature: burnt bricks and clay, that is, terracotta bricks, as the principal building material; sculpture in stone and metal with a highly lustrous finish.
11. "Nagara" has authority over superstructure and plan logic and none over enclosure, wall surface or material.
12. The examinable content is the variation, supported by two contrasting rows and named exemplars.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the statement "Nagara temples are richly carved on the outside and have no boundary wall" cannot be used as a general description of the northern family, and state the most defensible general claim that can replace it.

**Model answer:** Both halves fail on members of the family. Solanki temple walls are recorded as devoid of carvings, with ornament concentrated in decorative arched torans at the porticos, so rich exterior carving is not general; and Odisha temples usually have boundary walls, so the absence of enclosure is not general either. The statement is in fact a description of Khajuraho generalised to the family. The defensible replacement operates only where the family is genuinely uniform: Nagara temples stand on upraised platforms, carry a curvilinear spire over the sanctum whose vertical end bears a fluted amalaka surmounted by a kalash, divide the wall into vertical ratha planes, and run a covered ambulatory around the sanctum - while enclosure, wall surface and material vary sharply by sub-school and must be stated monument by monument.

**Misconception to avoid:** Taking the most famous member of a family as the definition of the family. Khajuraho is the most photographed Nagara site and the least typical one, precisely because it abandons the enclosure entirely and pushes surface sculpture to an extreme that Solanki practice rejects.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Examine the claim that "Nagara" names a family of solutions rather than a style, and state precisely what the label does and does not authorise a candidate to assert. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model answer

The claim holds, and its value lies in fixing the label's scope rather than in disputing its usefulness.

What the label authorises is a set of real invariants. Every member raises the temple on an upraised platform, sets mandapas before the principal shrine, runs a covered ambulatory around the sanctum, divides the wall into vertical ratha planes used as narrative panels, and crowns the sanctum with a curvilinear spire whose vertical end carries a fluted amalaka surmounted by a spherical kalash. Two further rules are usable in identification: images of Ganga and Yamuna stand outside the garbhagriha, and the premises generally hold no water tank. These are construction and ritual logic, not resemblance.

What the label does not authorise is any inference about enclosure, surface or material, because members disagree fundamentally. Odisha temples usually stand within boundary walls, lavish on exteriors and bare within; Khajuraho has no enclosure wall at all and turns the whole outer wall into a field of sculpture; Solanki walls are recorded as devoid of carvings, with ornament concentrated in arched torans and a stepped surya kund outside the temple, as at Modhera.

Two qualifications. The family term is genuinely useful for comparison and organisation, so it should not be discarded. And the eastern Pala-Sena tradition keeps the invariant curving shikhara under a large amalaka while building in terracotta, which shows the spire logic travelling independently of fabric.

Authority at the level of spire and plan logic; silence at the level of enclosure and surface.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the scope-fixing thesis rather than a defence or a rejection 3 + the invariants listed exactly, including the two identification rules 5 + three members disagreeing on enclosure and surface with one dated exemplar 5 + the usefulness qualification 1 + the Pala-Sena case showing spire logic independent of fabric 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 6:** The northern family is plural, but it is still recognisably one family. The southern tradition is organised on a different principle altogether - and the difference begins not with the spire but with a wall.

## Lesson 6 - The Dravida family: enclosure, axis and the growing gateway

### Visual: the enclosure principle, and how it grows

```text
  STAGE A - the Dravida idea, complete in one diagram

  +==============================================================+
  ||  HIGH BOUNDARY WALL                                        ||
  ||                                                            ||
  ||   s                                             s          ||
  ||        +----------+   +-------+   +----------+             ||
  ||        | MANDAPA  |---|ANTARALA|--|GARBHAGRIHA|            ||
  ||        | assembly |   |vestib. |  |  + VIMANA  |           ||
  ||        +----------+   +-------+   +----------+             ||
  ||   s      dvarapala / mithuna / yaksha           s          ||
  ||          at the sanctum entrance                           ||
  ||                                                            ||
  ||        [ WATER TANK inside the enclosure ]                 ||
  ||                                                            ||
  ||   lion frieze along the base of the temple (Chola mark)    ||
  +==============================================================+
              ||  GOPURAM  ||   <- high entrance gateway
              ||  in the   ||      in the FRONT WALL
              ||  wall     ||
                   ^
              s = the four subsidiary shrines of the panchayatana layout;
                  they carry NO vimanas

  STAGE B - what the wall makes possible over time

   CHOLA CLIMAX            LATE CHOLA            VIJAYANAGARA / NAYAKA
   vimana dominant   ->    gopura becomes   ->   concentric enclosures,
   (Thanjavur)             MORE DOMINANT         each outer gopuram
                           than the vimana       LARGER than the last
                           (Chidambaram,         (Madurai: 14 gopurams)
                            Kulottunga I)
        |                        |                        |
        +------------------------+------------------------+
                                 |
        THE MECHANISM: an enclosure can be ADDED TO without touching
        the sanctum. A sanctum tower cannot be enlarged after the fact.
        So growth migrates outward - and so does artistic emphasis.
```

*The whole later history of southern temple building is contained in the decision to build a wall. Hold that and the trajectory questions answer themselves.*

### 1. Start from the difference that is not about spires

Comparisons of the two families almost always begin with the spire, and that is the least productive place to begin. The spire difference is real but static: one curves, one steps. The enclosure difference is dynamic, because it determines what can happen next.

✅ **Fact.** Just as the Nagara school with its sub-schools emerged in northern India, a distinct style emerged in Peninsular India known as the **Dravidian style**, and the recorded features are these: **unlike the Nagara temples, the Dravidian temples were surrounded by high boundary walls**; the front wall had a high entrance gateway known as the **gopuram**; the premises were laid out in **panchayatana** style with a principal temple and four subsidiary shrines; the spire is a **stepped pyramid rising linearly**, known as the **vimana**; the crowning element is **octagonal** and known as the shikhara, similar to the Nagara kalash but not spherical; there is **only one vimana**, on the main temple, and subsidiary shrines do not have vimanas, unlike Nagara architecture; the assembly hall is connected to the garbhagriha by a **vestibular tunnel known as antarala**; the entrance of the garbhagriha carried sculptures of **dvarapala, mithuna and yaksha**; a **water tank inside the temple enclosure** was another feature; and the **frieze of lions along the base** of the temple is a typical feature of Chola temples.

Read that list again as a set of consequences rather than a set of traits. A wall requires a gate. A gate on a ceremonial route must be monumental. A walled precinct can hold a tank, so water becomes internal rather than adjacent - the exact inverse of the Solanki *surya kund*, which sits outside near the temple. A walled precinct can also be extended by adding another wall outside the first, and that is the engine of everything that follows.

### 2. Why "only one vimana" is the sharpest single line in the list

It is the cleanest morphological discriminator available, and it is frequently mis-stated. In the Dravida composition the four subsidiary shrines of the panchayatana layout are *not* miniature temples with their own towers; the vertical accent is reserved for the main shrine. In the Nagara composition, by contrast, subsidiary shrines may carry superstructures and the main spire is additionally surrounded by clustered urushringas.

⚠️ **Analytical consequence.** This is why a Dravida complex photographs badly from inside and magnificently from outside, while a Nagara temple photographs magnificently from any angle at close range. The Dravida temple is composed for the approach; the Nagara temple is composed for the circuit.

❌ **The corresponding caution:** do not convert this into a rule that one type appears exactly once and the other over every shrine in all periods and all complexes. Both terms describe sanctum superstructures with regional morphologies, and multi-shrine complexes need monument-specific description.

### 3. The sanctum door, and a piece of vocabulary that pays

✅ **Fact.** The entrance of the garbhagriha had sculptures of **dvarapala, mithuna and yaksha**.

This is worth holding for three reasons. First, it is a rare instance of a *placement* rule - the sculpture is specified by position, not merely by presence, which is exactly the kind of statement an "iconographic placement" question wants. Second, it supplies the southern counterpart to the Nagara rule about Ganga and Yamuna outside the garbhagriha, so the two families can be compared on the same dimension instead of on unrelated ones. Third, it records that **mithuna** figures appear at the sanctum door in the Dravida tradition, which is a useful corrective the moment anyone claims that auspicious-couple imagery is a Khajuraho peculiarity.

✅ **Fact.** The **frieze of lions along the base** of the temple is a typical feature of Chola temples - a base-level band, which again is a placement fact, and one that anticipates the later Vijayanagara and Nayaka fondness for the composite lion-bodied **Yali** on pillars and gopurams.

### 4. The trajectory the wall creates

The Dravida tradition is the only one in this topic with a clearly documented, centuries-long shift of emphasis, and the enclosure is what permits it.

✅ In the earliest Chola phase, the vimana is joined by an ardha-mandapa and the main shrine is surrounded by six parivaralayas, with dvarapalas flanking the entrance. ✅ By the early eleventh century the vimana at Thanjavur rises roughly sixty-six metres inside a huge rectangular enclosure with large gopurams - the vimana still dominant. ✅ In the last Chola phase, of the twelfth and thirteenth centuries, **the gopura became more dominant than the vimana**, exemplified by the Chidambaram temple under Kulottunga I. ✅ Under Vijayanagara, the **Raya Gopuram**, a monumental entrance tower, was introduced in temple architecture for the first time by Krishnadevaraya, and the Hampi Virupaksha carries a fifty-metre gopuram. ✅ Under the Nayakas, concentric rectangles were added within higher enclosing walls, with four entrances and **each wall's gopuram larger than the previous one**, and Madurai's Meenakshi temple has **fourteen gopurams**.

⚠️ **The mechanism to state in an answer:** a sanctum tower is fixed at construction, but an enclosure can always be enlarged by building another wall outside the last. Temple growth in the south therefore had to be centrifugal, and artistic emphasis followed the growth. That single sentence carries a 20-mark "trace the development of temple architecture in South India" answer from the Pallavas to the Nayakas.

### 5. One southern entry that sits awkwardly with the rest

Immediately after the Chola material, the standard account carries a short **Chera architecture** entry. It is recorded here because leaving it out would be a silent omission, and because its chronology needs correcting before it can be used.

✅ **Fact - the architecture, which is what this topic owns.** Chera temples **"were built in Dravidian style, characterised by octagonal or rectangular temples built from granite or sandstone, featuring a prominent entrance tower (gopuram) and incorporating elements like intricately carved wooden pillars and ceilings, along with mural paintings."** Named examples are **Thirunelli**, the **Vadakkunnathan** temples, **Kodungallur Bhagavathy** and **Kandiyur Siva**. The dynasty's emblem was the **bow and arrow**, and its kings were also known as **Keralaputas**; **Uthiyan Cheralathan** is the earliest known Chera ruler.

⚠️ **A disclosed chronological correction, and the version to write.** The standard account prints a single continuous span - "the Sangam period, around the 4th century BCE to the 12th century CE" - which conflates two distinct and separated phases and should not be reproduced as one range. The defensible version keeps them apart:

| Phase | Conventional span | Seat | What may be said |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Early Cheras**, of the Sangam age | broadly **c. 3rd century BCE to c. 3rd century CE** | **Vanji**, in present Tamil Nadu, with the seaports **Tondi and Musiri** | A Sangam-age chiefdom known chiefly from literature, ports and trade; no developed temple architecture is securely attributable to it |
| **Chera Perumals of Mahodayapuram**, the later Cheras | broadly **c. 9th to 12th century CE** | **Mahodayapuram**, modern **Kodungalloor** | The phase in which the named temples and the Dravidian-style description belong |

⚠️ **The two phases are separated by a long interval** for which the Chera line is not continuously documented, so "the Cheras, 4th century BCE to 12th century CE" is a compression rather than a reign-span. ✅ Write "the Early Cheras of the Sangam age" or "the later Chera Perumals of Mahodayapuram", never an undifferentiated millennium and a half.

⚠️ **Why it is handled in one section and not expanded into a school.** Standard treatments of this topic bound Kerala to murals, performance and named palaces and record **no Kerala architectural style**, while the passage above describes one. The honest response is to record the disagreement rather than to resolve it by building a regional school on a single short entry. ❌ Do not extend it: no *srikovil* sanctum form, no *nalambalam* enclosure, no *koothambalam* theatre hall and no sloping tiled-roof timber vocabulary appears anywhere in the material used here, and inventing that vocabulary to look complete is precisely the failure this discipline exists to prevent. ✅ What may safely be written is the Dravidian-style sentence, the named temples, the corrected two-phase chronology and the Vanji-to-Mahodayapuram shift of seat.

⚠️ **One useful detail inside it.** The reference to **intricately carved wooden pillars and ceilings** is the only place in this topic where *timber* appears as a surviving temple element rather than as a prototype imitated in stone, as at Lad Khan. That makes it a genuine, if isolated, material data point in a topic otherwise dominated by stone and brick.

### Comparative note: the two families on the same dimensions

| Dimension | Nagara | Dravida |
|---|---|---|
| Enclosure | Generally no elaborate boundary walls or gateways | High boundary walls with a gopuram in the front wall |
| Water | Generally no water tanks or reservoirs in the premises | A water tank inside the enclosure |
| Sanctum superstructure | Curvilinear shikhara, three recorded types, massed with urushringas | Vimana: stepped pyramid rising linearly |
| Finial | Fluted amalaka, then a spherical kalash | Octagonal crowning element, positionally similar, not spherical |
| Subsidiary shrines | May carry their own superstructures | Carry no vimanas |
| Sanctum-door sculpture | Ganga and Yamuna placed outside the garbhagriha | Dvarapala, mithuna and yaksha at the entrance |
| Link to the sanctum | Covered pradakshina path around the garbhagriha | Antarala, the vestibular tunnel from hall to sanctum |
| Growth over time | By elaboration of the tower and wall offsets | By addition of enclosures and ever-larger gateways |

⚠️ Use this table as a comparison *of principles*. Each row should be explained by the row above it: the wall creates the gateway, the gateway attracts the emphasis, the emphasis migrates outward, and the sanctum tower stops growing.

### Examination use

The direct 2024 demand on Chola achievement and the direct 2026 demand on Vijayanagara amalgamation both presuppose this lesson; neither is answerable by a candidate who cannot say what a Dravida temple is structurally. A "compare Nagara and Dravida" demand should use the eight-row table but must explain at least three rows causally, or it becomes the dynastic list masquerading as a comparison this topic's discipline warns against. And in Prelims, the "only one vimana" clause and the "water tank inside the enclosure" clause are exactly the kind of crisp discriminators that statement-based items are built from.

### Revision notes

1. The Dravidian style emerged in Peninsular India as the southern counterpart to the northern Nagara family.
2. High boundary walls, explicitly unlike Nagara temples, with a high entrance gateway - the gopuram - in the front wall.
3. Premises laid out in panchayatana style: a principal temple and four subsidiary shrines.
4. Vimana: stepped pyramid rising linearly, not curved; crowning element octagonal, called the shikhara here, similar to the Nagara kalash but not spherical.
5. Only one vimana, on the main temple; subsidiary shrines do not carry vimanas, unlike Nagara.
6. Antarala: the vestibular tunnel connecting the assembly hall to the garbhagriha.
7. Dvarapala, mithuna and yaksha sculptures at the garbhagriha entrance - the southern placement rule, matching the northern Ganga-Yamuna rule.
8. A water tank inside the temple enclosure - the inverse of the Solanki surya kund outside the temple.
9. A frieze of lions along the base is a typical Chola feature.
10. Growth mechanism: enclosures can be added, sanctum towers cannot be enlarged - so emphasis migrates outward.
11. The documented shift: early Chola parivaralayas, then a dominant vimana at Thanjavur, then the gopura becoming more dominant than the vimana at Chidambaram under Kulottunga I, then the Raya Gopuram under Krishnadevaraya, then graded Nayaka gopurams culminating in fourteen at Madurai.
12. Mithuna imagery at Dravida sanctum doors refutes any claim that auspicious-couple sculpture is peculiar to Khajuraho.
13. Chera entry, recorded with its corrected chronology: **Early Cheras** of the Sangam age, broadly c. 3rd century BCE to c. 3rd century CE, seated at **Vanji** with the seaports **Tondi and Musiri**; and the later **Chera Perumals of Mahodayapuram**, modern Kodungalloor, broadly c. 9th to 12th century CE, to which the named temples belong. Emblem the bow and arrow; kings also called Keralaputas; Uthiyan Cheralathan the earliest known ruler; temples Thirunelli, Vadakkunnathan, Kodungallur Bhagavathy and Kandiyur Siva, built in Dravidian style, octagonal or rectangular, in granite or sandstone, with a prominent gopuram and intricately carved wooden pillars and ceilings along with mural paintings.
14. The single printed span "around the 4th century BCE to the 12th century CE" compresses those two separated phases and must not be reproduced as one range.
15. Standard treatments of this topic record no Kerala architectural style while this passage describes one; the disagreement is disclosed and the entry is not expanded into a regional school.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate compares the two families by writing that "Nagara temples are curvilinear and Dravida temples are pyramidal". Explain why this comparison, though accurate, cannot support a 15-mark answer, and identify the one structural difference that can.

**Model answer:** The spire contrast is accurate but static: it distinguishes two shapes and generates no further consequence, so an answer built on it can only repeat itself in different words. The generative difference is the enclosure. Dravida temples are surrounded by high boundary walls entered through a gopuram and contain a water tank within the precinct, while Nagara premises generally lack elaborate boundary walls or gateways and generally lack tanks. From the wall everything else follows: the gateway becomes the principal public surface; the precinct can be extended by adding further walls outside the first; artistic emphasis therefore migrates outward over time, producing the documented shift in which the gopura becomes more dominant than the vimana in the last Chola phase and culminates in graded Nayaka gopurams. A spire contrast describes two buildings; an enclosure contrast explains eight centuries of them.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming the walls were primarily defensive. Their architectural consequence is about visibility and extensibility - controlling the approach and permitting concentric growth - and it is that consequence, not a security function, which the comparison should be built on.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Compare the Nagara and Dravida styles of temple architecture, explaining the reason for each difference rather than merely listing them. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

The two families differ most consequentially in enclosure, and the rest follows.

Dravida temples stand within high boundary walls entered through a gopuram, and hold a water tank inside the precinct; Nagara temples generally have no elaborate boundary wall or gateway and generally no tank. Enclosure makes the gateway the principal public surface and allows growth by adding further walls, which is why the gopura becomes more dominant than the vimana in the last Chola phase and why Nayaka enclosures grade outward. An unwalled Nagara temple is seen from every side, so its outer wall carries the sculpture.

Superstructure follows the same logic. Dravida concentrates one linearly rising stepped vimana over the main shrine, its subsidiary shrines carrying none; Nagara distributes a curvilinear shikhara with clustered urushringas and spired subsidiary shrines.

The contrast is therefore centripetal against centrifugal composition, not merely curved against pyramidal.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = enclosure identified as the generative difference 3 + the tank and gateway consequences 2 + the documented vimana-to-gopura migration as proof 2 + the superstructure contrast stated with the one-vimana rule 2 + the centripetal-centrifugal formulation as close 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 7:** Two families have now been described on their own terms. Between them sits a third label that appears in every syllabus and satisfies almost nobody - and testing it honestly, at one site where both grammars stand side by side, is the best training this topic offers in describing before classifying.

## Lesson 7 - Vesara: testing a third box at the one site that settles it

Progress: 7/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Vesara definition and its three named dynasties, the Pattadakal evidence, the Hoysala formulation, and the discipline of describing before labelling

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Vesara definition, the Pattadakal Virupaksha and Papanatha pair, and the Hoysala style statement
CA search: "Not required - classification and historiography lesson"
CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the evidence that decides the question

```text
               PATTADAKAL - ONE SITE, ONE PATRON FAMILY, ONE PERIOD
                  UNESCO World Heritage Site since 1987
              9 Hindu temples + 1 Jain temple, largely 7th-8th c. AD
                                   |
        +--------------------------+--------------------------+
        |                                                     |
   VIRUPAKSHA TEMPLE                                    PAPANATHA TEMPLE
   commissioned by Queen LOKMAHADEVI, 8th c. AD,        dedicated to Shiva
   to commemorate her husband VIKRAMADITYA II's
   conquest of the Pallava capital Kanchipuram
        |                                                     |
   PLAN: pillared hall + antechamber + sanctum          PLAN: "SIMILAR TO
         with an enclosed circumambulation passage            VIRUPAKSHA
        |                                                     IN ITS PLAN"
   SHIKHARA: built in DRAVIDIAN style                   SHIKHARA: built in
        |                                                     NORTH INDIAN
   WALLS: deep carvings, mostly of Shiva,                     NAGARA style
          in outer-wall niches                          WALLS: Ramayana panels
        |                                                     |
        +--------------------------+--------------------------+
                                   |
        SAME PLAN. SAME SITE. SAME PERIOD. DIFFERENT SUPERSTRUCTURE GRAMMAR.
                                   |
        => "Vesara" cannot mean a fused hybrid object, because here the
           two grammars are NOT fused; they are SELECTED BETWEEN, temple
           by temple, by the same builders.

   THE BOOK'S OWN THREE-STAGE SPINE:
   AIHOLE  ----------->  BADAMI  ----------->  PATTADAKAL
   "incubator for        concepts "further     "the culmination of this
    the concepts"        refined ... during     into a matured phase"
                         the 6th-7th centuries"
```

*This is the single most valuable diagram in the classification half of the topic. It converts a vague label into a testable claim and then tests it.*

### 1. The learner's problem: a category that behaves like an answer

Vesara is usually learnt as a formula - "a mixture of Nagara and Dravida" - and the formula feels like knowledge. It is not, for a simple reason: it names no monument, no patron, no plan and no material, so it cannot be used to describe anything. A candidate who writes "Vesara is a hybrid of Nagara and Dravida styles found in Karnataka" has written a dictionary entry, and dictionary entries do not score.

Worse, the formula implies something the buildings do not support - that Deccan builders averaged two traditions into a third object. What the evidence shows is more interesting and far more defensible: Deccan patrons *selected* from both northern and southern grammars, sometimes on the same site, and the category was invented afterwards to describe what they had already done.

### 2. What the record actually says

✅ **Fact - the definition.** The **Vesara School**, also known as the **Karnataka school of architecture**, combined features of both the Nagara school and the Dravidian school and resulted in a hybridised style.

✅ **Fact - the three dynasties named as its makers.** The **Early Chalukyan dynasty of Badami** - the Badami rock-cut cave temple complex and the temples at Aihole and Pattadakal; the **Rashtrakuta dynasty (750-983 AD)** - the Kailashnatha temple at the Ellora cave complex; and the **Hoysala dynasty (1050-1300 AD)** - the temples at Belur, Halebidu and Somanathapura.

✅ **Fact - the Hoysala formulation, which is the safest sentence on the label in the whole source.** Hoysala temples "are constructed in **Vesara** style of architecture. The temple has a **Dravidian morphology** along with substantial influences from the **Bhumija style** prevalent in Central India, the **Nagara traditions** of northern and western India."

Notice what that last sentence does. It does not say fifty-fifty. It states a **base morphology** - Dravidian - and then names **two separate influences** on it, one of them a central Indian style, Bhumija, that the two-box formula does not even contain. A three-source description cannot be a two-way average.

### 3. The Pattadakal test, in full

✅ **Fact.** The Group of Monuments at **Pattadakal** is a UNESCO World Heritage Site **since 1987**, in Karnataka, renowned for Chalukyan architecture that **blends the north Indian Naagar style and the South Indian Dravidian style**. It lies along the Malprabha in Bagalkot district, historically the site of Chalukyan royal coronations and ceremonies - **Pattabhisheka**. Its monuments are associated mainly with the Early Chalukyan dynasty and were largely built during the **7th-8th century AD**. **Nine Hindu temples and one Jain temple** are prominent there, the Hindu temples generally dedicated to Shiva though with elements of Vaishnavism and Shaktism.

✅ **Fact - the Virupaksha.** It is the largest and most profusely sculpted shrine at the site. This Shiva temple was commissioned by **Queen Lokmahadevi in the 8th century AD to commemorate the conquest of the Pallava capital Kanchipuram by her husband, the Chalukyan king Vikramaditya II**. It consists of a pillared hall, an antechamber and the sanctum with an enclosed passage for circumambulation. **Its shikhara is built in Dravidian style**, and the niches on the outer walls carry fine, deep carvings, mostly of Shiva.

✅ **Fact - the Mallikarjuna.** A similar though smaller temple was commissioned by **Queen Trilok Mahadevi**, with carvings of **Samudra Manthan** and **Mahisasuramardini** on the walls.

✅ **Fact - the Papanatha, which is the decisive row.** Dedicated to Shiva, it is **similar to the Virupaksha temple in its plan, but its shikhara is built in the north Indian Nagara style**, and its outer walls are carved with many panels depicting scenes from the Ramayana.

✅ **Fact - the site's later layer.** **Unlike the Hindu temples, which were constructed by the Chalukyas, the Jain Narayana Temple of Pattadakal was constructed by the Rashtrakuta dynasty during the 9th century AD.**

⚠️ **The inference this licenses, stated carefully.** If the same builders, on the same site, within the same generation, could raise a Dravidian shikhara over one plan and a Nagara shikhara over an almost identical plan, then the superstructure was a *choice* rather than an inherited necessity. "Vesara" is therefore best defended as a label for a practice of selection, not as the name of a third object produced by mixing two others.

### 4. The three-stage spine, quotable as a structure

✅ **Fact.** The standard three-stage conclusion is that **"Aihole served as the incubator for the concepts that would lead to this integration of styles. These concepts were further refined at Badami during the 6th-7th centuries. And finally, the culmination of this into a matured phase came out in the monuments of Pattadakal."**

That gives a ready-made three-part structure for any Vesara or Early Chalukya demand: incubator, refinement, culmination, each with its own site. It is also honest about direction - the tradition was experimenting toward integration, not applying a settled formula.

### Criticism and reply: should "Vesara" be used at all?

**The objection.** The label is used inconsistently. It is applied to a rock-cut cave complex at Badami, a monolithic excavated temple at Ellora and a soapstone star-plan at Belur - three utterly different things. It is defined as a two-way hybrid but described, in its clearest instance, as a three-source morphology. And it is a modern classificatory convenience whose extension no two writers agree on. If a term cannot fix its own referents, it may be better abandoned.

**The reply, and the balanced verdict.** The label does name something real. Across the Deccan, from the sixth to the fourteenth century, patrons and builders demonstrably drew on both northern and southern grammars and on central Indian ones, in ways that neither family's internal history predicts. That practice needs a name. What must be abandoned is not the word but the *formula*. The disciplined use is to describe **plan, tower and material at a named monument first**, and then to say that the result is conventionally placed in the Vesara category - adding, where it is supported, the exact three-source formulation. Used that way the label closes a description instead of substituting for one.

### Examination use

Three things follow directly. First, the 2026 Prelims item on Nagara-style shikharas is, in substance, a Vesara question: it asks whether the candidate knows that a Deccan location does not determine a spire grammar. Second, any "Vesara" or "Deccan hybrid" Mains demand should run the Pattadakal pair as its central evidence, because it is the only case where the claim can be *demonstrated* rather than asserted. Third, in an amalgamation demand - the exact 2026 Vijayanagara wording - the Pattadakal method transfers directly: identify which inherited element came from where, monument by monument, rather than saying "eclectic".

⚠️ **One attribution distinction worth carrying to the hall.** There are two famous Virupaksha temples and they belong to different dynasties, centuries and styles. The **Pattadakal** Virupaksha is Early Chalukyan, commissioned by Queen Lokmahadevi in the 8th century, with a Dravidian shikhara. The **Hampi** Virupaksha is Vijayanagara, begun in the Sangama period and expanded by Krishnadevaraya, with a fifty-metre gopuram. Naming the wrong dynasty is a recorded objective trap.

### Revision notes

1. Vesara, also called the Karnataka school, is defined as combining features of the Nagara and Dravidian schools into a hybridised style.
2. Three dynasties are named as its makers: Early Chalukya of Badami (Badami caves, Aihole, Pattadakal); Rashtrakuta, 750-983 AD (Kailashnatha, Ellora); Hoysala, 1050-1300 AD (Belur, Halebidu, Somanathapura).
3. The safest source sentence: Hoysala temples have a Dravidian morphology with substantial influences from the Bhumija style of Central India and the Nagara traditions of northern and western India - three sources, not two.
4. Pattadakal: UNESCO 1987; Malprabha river, Bagalkot district; site of Chalukyan coronations, Pattabhisheka; largely 7th-8th century AD; nine Hindu and one Jain temple.
5. Virupaksha, Pattadakal: largest and most profusely sculpted; commissioned by Queen Lokmahadevi in the 8th century to commemorate Vikramaditya II's conquest of Kanchipuram; pillared hall, antechamber, sanctum with enclosed circumambulation passage; **Dravidian shikhara**; deep outer-niche carvings mostly of Shiva.
6. Mallikarjuna, Pattadakal: similar but smaller, commissioned by Queen Trilok Mahadevi; Samudra Manthan and Mahisasuramardini carvings.
7. Papanatha, Pattadakal: plan similar to the Virupaksha but **north Indian Nagara shikhara**; outer walls carved with Ramayana panels.
8. Jain Narayana Temple, Pattadakal: built by the **Rashtrakutas** in the 9th century, not by the Chalukyas.
9. Same plan, same site, same period, different spire grammar - so Vesara names a practice of selection, not a fused object.
10. The three-stage spine: Aihole the incubator, Badami the refinement in the 6th-7th centuries, Pattadakal the matured culmination.
11. Method rule: describe plan, tower and material at a named monument, then apply the label; never the reverse.
12. Two Virupakshas: Pattadakal (Early Chalukya, Lokmahadevi, Dravidian shikhara) and Hampi (Vijayanagara, Sangama-founded, expanded by Krishnadevaraya, fifty-metre gopuram).

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain, using one site, why "Vesara is a mixture of Nagara and Dravida" is a weaker claim than "Deccan builders selected between northern and southern grammars", and state one piece of evidence that the mixture formula cannot accommodate.

**Model answer:** At Pattadakal the Virupaksha and the Papanatha stand on the same site, in the same Early Chalukyan period, and the Papanatha is described as similar to the Virupaksha in plan - yet the Virupaksha's shikhara is built in Dravidian style and the Papanatha's in the north Indian Nagara style. A mixture would have produced one intermediate superstructure on both; selection produced two distinct ones on the same plan, which is what the monuments show. The formula also cannot accommodate the recorded description of Hoysala temples as having a Dravidian morphology with substantial influences from the Bhumija style of Central India *and* the Nagara traditions - three named sources, which no two-way average can express. Selection explains both facts; mixture explains neither.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that because the word "hybridised" is used, the mixture formula is authorised. The same account supplies the Pattadakal pair and the three-source Hoysala sentence, which together show that "hybridised" describes an outcome at the level of a regional tradition, not a fusion inside any single monument.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Vesara is better understood as a label for a practice than as a third style of Indian temple architecture." Critically examine with reference to named monuments. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model answer

The proposition is largely correct, and the Deccan evidence tests it precisely.

The conventional definition treats Vesara, the Karnataka school, as combining Nagara and Dravidian features into a hybridised style, and names three makers: the Early Chalukyas of Badami, the Rashtrakutas at Ellora and the Hoysalas at Belur, Halebidu and Somanathapura.

The decisive evidence against reading it as a fused third object stands at Pattadakal, a UNESCO site since 1987 with nine Hindu and one Jain temple, largely of the seventh and eighth centuries. The Virupaksha, commissioned by Queen Lokmahadevi to commemorate Vikramaditya II's conquest of Kanchipuram, carries a Dravidian shikhara over a pillared hall, antechamber and sanctum with an enclosed circumambulatory. The Papanatha, similar in plan, carries a north Indian Nagara shikhara. One plan, one site, one period, two grammars - selection, not averaging.

The Hoysala description reinforces it: Hoysala temples are said to have a Dravidian morphology with substantial influences from the Central Indian Bhumija style and the Nagara traditions, a three-source account that no two-way mixture can render.

Two qualifications are owed. The label does name a real and sustained Deccan practice, and abandoning it would obscure the continuity from Aihole as incubator through Badami's refinement to Pattadakal's culmination. And the category's extension is genuinely inconsistent, covering rock-cut caves, a monolithic temple and a soapstone star-plan.

The examined verdict is that Vesara should close a description of plan, tower and material at a named monument, never replace one.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the practice-versus-object thesis stated and tested 3 + the Pattadakal pair with patron, occasion and both spire grammars 5 + the three-source Hoysala formulation as corroboration 3 + the two qualifications, real practice and inconsistent extension 2 + the method verdict on how the label may be used 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 8:** Classification has now been tested to its limit. The next three lessons put it to work on the Nagara sub-schools, beginning with the one that names its own towers and therefore forces the most precise vocabulary in the topic.

```text
              |
   13th c. KONARK (Narasimhadeva I, Eastern Ganga) - UNESCO 1984
           colossal CHARIOT of the Sun God: 12 carved wheels = 12 months,
           7 horses; erotic mithuna scenes; "Black Pagoda" to European
           sailors; Gajasinghas - lions crushing elephants - in front
```

*The dated ladder is the point. Odisha is the one sub-school with a near-continuous sequence of dated, dynasty-attached monuments, which makes it the natural material for any "evolution of a regional school" demand.*

### 1. Why Odisha is taught immediately before Khajuraho

Because it is the correct comparator, and for three reasons that are all examinable.

First, the two are contemporaneous across their peak centuries and both belong to the Nagara family, so a comparison between them is genuinely like-for-like rather than a north-south contrast in disguise. Second, they disagree on the one structural decision that matters most - Odisha walls its temples, Khajuraho does not - which makes the comparison productive. Third, and decisively for the topic's most famous controversy, ✅ **the erotic element is prominent at Konark and Bhubaneswar as well as at Khajuraho**, which means the single motif most often treated as a Chandella peculiarity is documented as a regional parallel within the very same family.

### 2. The vocabulary Odisha forces you to learn

✅ **Fact.** The style followed in Odisha is known as the **Kalinga style**, a sub-style developed under the Nagara style. Its primary features begin with a **vimana or sanctum** housing the deity, crowned with the spire; **the sanctum and the surmounting curvilinear spire together are called the Deul**, and structurally the deul is vertical almost until the top, when it suddenly **curves sharply inwards**. The frontal mandapa is called the **Jagamohan**, a hall for devotees to gather and participate in rituals. The exterior walls were generally **lavishly decorated** but the interiors are **generally quite bare**, and Odisha temples **usually have boundary walls**.

⚠️ **One term to handle carefully.** The source uses *vimana* here for the Odishan sanctum, in its general sense of "sanctum with its crowning spire", not in the specific Dravida sense of a stepped pyramidal superstructure. Say "the sanctum and its curvilinear spire, together called the deul" and the ambiguity disappears. This is a good example of why the describe-then-label rule exists: the same word does different work in different regional vocabularies.

✅ **Fact - the three deul types.** **Rekha Deula**: a tower-like sanctum sanctorum with a curvilinear spire. **Pidha Deula**: a square-shaped hall with a pyramid-like roof, used for rituals. **Khakhara Deula**: a barrel-shaped shrine, often associated with goddess worship.

⚠️ **Analytical point.** These are not three rival styles; they are three *jobs* within one complex. A developed Odishan temple can carry a rekha deula over the sanctum and a pidha deula over the ritual hall in the same composition - which is exactly the northern three-spire-type logic from Lesson 4 applied regionally, with the khakhara corresponding to the valabhi wagon-vault. Making that connection explicitly is worth a mark in any comparison answer.

### 3. The sequence, with what each monument contributes

✅ The **earliest temples**, such as **Parsurameswar**, built by the **Shailodbhava dynasty in the 7th century AD**, exhibit simpler designs with minimal ornamentation; Shree Parsu-rameswara at Bhubaneswar features a **modest Rekha Deula and a Jagamohana**, its walls adorned with intricate carvings of Hindu deities, floral motifs and mythological scenes. ✅ As time progressed the architecture became more intricate and elaborate, reaching its zenith **between the 8th and 13th centuries AD**, which witnessed the Lingaraja, Jagannath and Konark Sun temples.

✅ The **Vaitala Temple** at Bhubaneswar, constructed by **Queen Tribhuvana-Mahadevi of the Bhauma Kara dynasty in the 9th century AD**, is unique for its **Khakhara Deula** style with a distinctive barrel-shaped roof; dedicated to the goddess **Chamunda**, it has a dark and mystical atmosphere, its walls adorned with tantric imagery, carvings of deities and scenes of daily life.

✅ The **Mukteswar Temple**, under the **Somavamshi** kingdom in the **10th century**, is often called the **"Gem of Odishan Architecture"** - a small but elegant temple with a beautifully sculpted **Mangaltorana** or arched gateway at the entrance, intricate carvings on its spire and a well-proportioned Jagamohana.

✅ The **Rajarani Temple**, Somavamshi, **11th century**, is renowned for its aesthetic beauty but **lacks a presiding deity**; its name derives from the **reddish-gold sandstone, called Rajarani**, used in its construction.

✅ The **Lingaraja Temple**, the **largest in Bhubaneswar**, was constructed by **three generations of the Somavamshi dynasty in the 11th century**, dedicated to Shiva; it **exemplifies the fully developed Kalinga style** and was constructed in the **Rekha Deula order**.

✅ In the **12th century**, the worship of **Purushottama**, later known as **Jagannatha**, was raised to the status of an **imperial cult** with the reconstruction of the Purushottama or **Jagannath temple at Puri** by the **Eastern Ganga king Anantavarman Chodaganga**; it is a monumental example of **Pidha Deula** architecture and includes multiple shrines and halls. As per temple records, **King Indradyumna of Avanti** initially built the main temple of Jagannath at Puri.

✅ The **Konark Sun Temple** was constructed by **King Narasimhadeva I of the Eastern Ganga dynasty in the 13th century**, and was declared a **UNESCO World Heritage Site in 1984**. It stands around **35 km north-east of Puri** near the present Chandrabhaga beach on the Bay of Bengal. Designed as a **colossal chariot of the Sun God**, it features **twelve intricately carved wheels representing the twelve months of the year** and **seven horses**, with walls adorned with sculptures, **some of which depict erotic mithuna scenes**. It was called the **"Black Pagoda"** in European sailor accounts because it looked like a great tiered tower that appeared black. In front of the Sun temple are **two lion sculptures crushing elephants**, known as the **Gajasinghas**. The **ring-stirrup** is one of the sophisticated architectural elements in its horse sculptures, an element also found in the temples of Khajuraho. ✅ Rabindranath Tagore wrote of it: "Here the language of stone surpasses the language of human." ✅ Konark is depicted on the ₹10 Indian currency note.

⚠️ **Two disciplines on this material.** First, keep the imperial-cult point where it belongs: the *raising of Purushottama worship to an imperial cult* is a religious-history statement; and it is used here only to explain why a monumental Pidha Deula was rebuilt at that moment. Second, the attribution of an initial building to Indradyumna of Avanti rests on temple records rather than on an independently dated fact, and must be cited as such.

### Comparative note: Odisha against Khajuraho, on four dimensions

| Dimension | Odisha / Kalinga | Khajuraho / Chandella | What the contrast proves |
|---|---|---|---|
| Enclosure | Boundary walls usual | No enclosure wall; high platform terrace | Enclosure is a sub-school decision inside Nagara, not a family rule |
| Wall and interior | Exteriors lavishly decorated, interiors generally bare | Dense integrated figural programme on the outer wall | Both invest outward, but Odisha does so inside a precinct and Khajuraho in the open |
| Tower vocabulary | Named deul types for distinct jobs - rekha, pidha, khakhara | A single mature curvilinear shikhara massed with urushringas | Two ways of organising the same three-spire-type logic |
| Erotic register | Prominent at Konark and Bhubaneswar; erotic mithuna scenes on Konark's chariot walls | Present, one register among many | Kills the "unique to Khajuraho" claim from within the same family |

### Examination use

Odisha is the strongest single supply of *dated, dynasty-attached* examples in the whole topic, which makes it the best material for any "evolution of a regional school" or "trace the development" demand where the examiner expects a chronology. It is also the obligatory comparator in any Khajuraho answer: naming Konark and Bhubaneswar as parallels for the erotic register is what converts a defensive paragraph into an argued one. And Konark's 1984 inscription is the closing status line for any Odisha answer, with the caution that counts and policy belong to Topic 14.

### Revision notes

1. Odisha's style is the Kalinga style, a sub-style under Nagara.
2. Deul = sanctum plus its curvilinear spire; vertical almost to the top, then curving sharply inward. Jagamohan = the frontal mandapa where devotees gather for rituals.
3. Exteriors lavishly decorated, interiors generally bare; boundary walls usual.
4. Three deul types: Rekha (tower-like sanctum, curvilinear spire), Pidha (square hall, pyramid-like roof, for rituals), Khakhara (barrel-shaped, often goddess worship).
5. The three types are three jobs within one complex, matching the Latina-Phamsana-Valabhi logic.
6. Parsurameswar, Shailodbhava, 7th century: earliest, simpler, modest Rekha Deula with Jagamohana.
7. Vaitala, Queen Tribhuvana-Mahadevi, Bhauma Kara, 9th century: Khakhara Deula; Chamunda; tantric imagery.
8. Mukteswar, Somavamshi, 10th century: "Gem of Odishan Architecture"; sculpted Mangaltorana gateway.
9. Rajarani, Somavamshi, 11th century: renowned for beauty, lacks a presiding deity, named from its reddish-gold Rajarani sandstone.
10. Lingaraja, three generations of Somavamshi, 11th century: largest in Bhubaneswar; fully developed Kalinga style; Rekha Deula order.
11. Jagannath, Puri, Anantavarman Chodaganga, Eastern Ganga, 12th century: monumental Pidha Deula; temple records credit an initial building to Indradyumna of Avanti.
12. Konark, Narasimhadeva I, Eastern Ganga, 13th century, UNESCO 1984: chariot of the Sun God, twelve wheels for twelve months, seven horses, erotic mithuna scenes, "Black Pagoda", Gajasinghas, ring-stirrups; Tagore's line; on the ₹10 note.
13. Zenith of the school: 8th to 13th centuries AD.
14. The erotic register is prominent at Konark and Bhubaneswar, so it is a regional parallel to Khajuraho, not a Chandella invention.

### Concept check

**Question:** A question asks you to illustrate the evolution of a regional temple school with dated examples. Explain why the Odisha school answers this better than Khajuraho does, and name the four monuments you would use.

**Model answer:** The Odisha school is the only Nagara sub-school with a near-continuous, dynasty-attached chronology, so evolution can be shown rather than asserted. Khajuraho's temples fall into an earlier, less splendid set and a later sandstone set, and beyond the general statement that several were built during Dhanga's reign, individual temples cannot be tied to named rulers or precise dates, so a Khajuraho answer must argue from morphology rather than from chronology. The four monuments to use are Parsurameswar, Shailodbhava, seventh century, showing simple early design with a modest rekha deula and jagamohana; Vaitala, ninth century, Queen Tribhuvana-Mahadevi of the Bhauma Karas, showing the khakhara barrel-roof type and a goddess dedication; Lingaraja, eleventh century, three generations of Somavamshis, showing the fully developed Kalinga style in the rekha order; and Konark, Narasimhadeva I, Eastern Ganga, thirteenth century, inscribed by UNESCO in 1984, showing the culminating chariot conception.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that the most celebrated site is the best evidence for any demand. The right monument is the one whose documentation matches the question's verb: "trace the evolution" needs dates and dynasties, which Odisha supplies and Khajuraho does not.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Examine the distinctive features of the Kalinga school of temple architecture with reference to named monuments. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

The Kalinga school of Odisha is a Nagara sub-style organised around a named pair of structures: the deul, the sanctum with its curvilinear spire that rises almost vertically before curving sharply inward, and the jagamohan, the frontal hall where devotees gather for rituals. Its exteriors are lavishly decorated while interiors remain generally bare, and its temples usually stand within boundary walls.

Its distinctive contribution is a vocabulary of three tower types for three functions: the rekha deula over the sanctum, the pyramid-roofed pidha deula over the ritual hall, and the barrel-roofed khakhara deula, often for goddess worship, as at the ninth-century Vaitala temple built by Queen Tribhuvana-Mahadevi and dedicated to Chamunda.

Development is datable: Parsurameswar under the Shailodbhavas in the seventh century, the eleventh-century Lingaraja in fully developed rekha form, and Konark, built by Narasimhadeva I in the thirteenth century as a solar chariot, inscribed by UNESCO in 1984.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = deul and jagamohan defined precisely 2 + the three deul types tied to three functions 3 + exterior-interior and enclosure characteristics 1 + four named monuments with dynasty or patron 3 + the dated closing status line 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 9:** Odisha's distinctiveness comes from tower vocabulary. The next two schools are distinctive for a different reason entirely - one is shaped by the scarcity of water, the other by the scarcity of stone - which makes them the topic's cleanest demonstration that environment and material, not taste, produce regional style.

## Lesson 9 - Where material and water decide form: Solanki in the west, Pala-Sena in the east

Progress: 9/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Part A the Solanki or Maru-Gurjara school, its bare walls, torans, surya kund and equinox alignment; Part B the Pala-Sena school of Bengal, its terracotta fabric, Bangla roof and amalaka-crowned shikhara
