━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two environments, two solutions

```text
   WEST: GUJARAT / RAJASTHAN               EAST: BENGAL DELTA
   problem = WATER IS SCARCE               problem = THERE IS NO BUILDING STONE
            |                                        |
            v                                        v
   SOLANKI / MARU-GURJARA                  PALA AND SENA SCHOOL
   8th-13th c. patronage of Solanki        developed 8th-12th c. AD under
   rulers                                  the PALA and SENA dynasties
            |                                        |
   TEMPLE  +  SURYA KUND                    BUILDING MATERIAL:
   (step tank near the temple,              BURNT BRICKS AND CLAY, known as
    its steps FULL of small                 TERRACOTTA BRICKS - the principal
    temples with wooden carvings)           material used
            |                                        |
   WALLS: DEVOID of carvings                ROOF: CURVED or SLOPING,
   PORTICOS: decorative arched               "as in bamboo huts" -> the
             gateways = TORANS               "BANGLA ROOF", LATER ADOPTED
            |                                 BY THE MUGHAL ARCHITECTS
   MATERIALS: sandstone, black basalt,               |
              soft marble                   TOWER: a tall, CURVING SHIKHARA
            |                                crowned by a LARGE AMALAKA,
   ORIENTATION: most east-facing; at the     "SIMILAR TO THE ODISHA SCHOOL"
   EQUINOXES sun rays fall DIRECTLY                  |
   into the central shrine                   SCULPTURE: stone and metal,
            |                                stone the major component;
   EXAMPLE: MODHERA SUN TEMPLE, Gujarat      figures unique in their
   built 1026-27 AD by BHIMA I of the        HIGHLY LUSTROUS FINISH
   Chaulukya / Solanki dynasty                       |
                                             PALA monuments: universities of
                                             NALANDA, JAGADDALA, ODANTAPURI,
                                             VIKRAMSHILA; SOMAPURA MAHAVIHARA
                                             (Bangladesh)
                                             SENA examples: SIDDHESVARA
                                             MAHADEVA, BARAKAR; temples around
                                             BISHNUPUR (West Bengal)
            |                                        |
            +--------------------+-------------------+
                                 |
        ONE ARGUMENT: the environment sets the problem, the material
        sets the possible answers, and STYLE is the solution that results.
```

*Two schools, one lesson, because they make the same argument from opposite ends.*

### 1. The learner's problem: style as taste, and why that fails

Regional schools are often explained as regional preferences - as though builders in Gujarat simply liked bare walls and builders in Bengal simply liked curved roofs. That account cannot explain why the preferences are so stable over centuries, why they correlate so closely with geology and climate, or why a "preference" like the Bangla roof was later adopted by an entirely different imperial tradition.

The stronger account is that each school is a solution to a local problem. In the arid west, water storage becomes architectural: the temple acquires a monumental tank. In the alluvial delta, the absence of building stone forces brick, and brick forces moulded relief rather than deep undercutting, and a bamboo-hut roof profile becomes reproducible in masonry. Neither is taste. Both are engineering, and both then become identity.

### 2. Part A - Solanki, or Maru-Gurjara

✅ **Fact - setting and patronage.** In the **north-western parts of India including Gujarat and Rajasthan**, this school developed under the patronage of **Solanki rulers**.

✅ **Fact - the recorded features.**
- The **temple walls were devoid of any carvings**.
- The **garbhagriha was connected with the mandapa both internally and externally**.
- The porticos had **decorative arched gateways known as torans**.
- A unique feature is the presence of a **step tank, known as surya kund, in the proximity of the temple**.
- The **steps of the tank are full of small temples with wooden carvings** present in them.
- The Solankis used a variety of material: **sandstone, black basalt and soft marble**.
- **Most of the temples are east-facing and designed so that every year during the equinoxes the sun rays fall directly into the central shrine.**
- Example: **Modhera Sun Temple, Gujarat, built in 1026-27 AD by Bhima I of the Chaulukya or Solanki dynasty.**

⚠️ **Three analytical points worth making in an answer.**

First, the **bare wall is a genuine discriminator**, and the single most useful Solanki fact in a comparison. It refutes any claim that Nagara temples are characteristically covered in sculpture, and it forces the candidate to explain where ornament went instead - into the torans and into the tank.

Second, the **surya kund is not a pond beside a temple**; its steps are recorded as full of small temples. That makes it a second sacred structure, with its own architecture, which is why "step tank" undersells it. An answer should call it what it is - a monumentalised water-storage structure integrated into the temple's sacred programme.

Third, **equinox alignment is functional evidence, not decoration**. A temple designed so that sunlight reaches the central shrine at the equinoxes has been sited, oriented and dimensioned for an astronomical event, which is a claim about planning capability. It also pairs naturally with the Khajuraho orientation fact - all temples there except the Chaturbhuja face the sunrise - to make a short, high-value paragraph on solar orientation in Nagara practice.

❌ **Boundary caution.** Stepwells as a building type, their patrons, dates and protected status, belong elsewhere in this subject and to Topic 14. What is owned here is the *surya kund as a component of the Solanki temple complex*, and the Modhera example with its 1026-27 date and named patron.

### 3. Part B - Pala and Sena, the eastern school

✅ **Fact - identity and period.** In the **Bengal region**, the style came to be known as the **Pala and Sena school** of architecture, developing between the **8th and 12th century AD** under the patronage of the **Pala** and **Sena** dynasties.

✅ **Fact - the two-religion patronage structure.** The **Palas** were primarily **Buddhist** rulers following the **Mahayana** tradition, but were very tolerant and patronised both religions, and Pala kings built a lot of **viharas, chaityas and stupas**. The **Senas** were **Hindus** and built temples of Hindu gods, and **also sustained Buddhist architecture**. The architecture therefore reflected the influence of **both** religions.

✅ **Fact - Pala monuments.** The universities of **Nalanda, Jagaddala, Odantapuri and Vikramshila**, and **Somapura Mahavihara**, a magnificent monastery in **Bangladesh**.

✅ **Fact - Sena form and fabric.** The buildings had a **curved or sloping roof, as in bamboo huts**; this became popularly known as the **"Bangla roof"** and was **later adopted by the Mughal architects**. **Burnt bricks and clay, known as terracotta bricks, was the principal building material used.** The temples of this region had a **tall, curving shikhara crowned by a large amalaka, similar to the Odisha School**. For sculpture, **both stone and metal were used, stone the major component**, and the figures were **unique in their highly lustrous finish**. Examples: **Siddhesvara Mahadeva Temple in Barakar**, and temples around **Bishnupur, West Bengal**.

⚠️ **The three arguments this school supplies, each worth a paragraph.**

**Material drives form.** The delta has no building stone. Burnt brick permits moulded and repeated relief but not the deep undercutting of Khajuraho sandstone, so surface richness is achieved by pattern and repetition rather than by figures released from the wall. That is a causal explanation of style from geology, and it is the cleanest one in the whole topic.

**Religion layers rather than replaces.** One school, two dynasties, two religions, and each sustaining the other's building type. This is a far more precise statement of plurality than a general remark about tolerance, because it is anchored in who built what.

**Influence can run upward.** The Bangla roof begins as the profile of a bamboo hut, becomes a masonry form in a regional temple tradition, and is then adopted by Mughal architects. A vernacular form entering imperial architecture is the exact counter-example to a top-down account of Indian style, and it hands off cleanly to Topic 04.

❌ **Boundaries to hold.** The Pala universities and the Somapura Mahavihara are monastic and educational institutions whose detailed treatment belongs with Buddhist architecture and with the learning-institutions topic; they appear here as evidence of the school's patronage character. Do not date individual Bengal temples, name their patrons, or describe Bishnupur's terracotta panel iconography - the record gives none of it - and do not upgrade Bishnupur's heritage status, which is a Topic 14 matter.

### Comparative note: four Nagara solutions, one dimension at a time

| Dimension | Odisha | Khajuraho | Solanki | Pala-Sena |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Where ornament goes | Exterior walls, interiors bare | The whole outer wall, densely figured | Torans and the tank; walls bare | Moulded terracotta surfaces; lustrous stone and metal sculpture |
| Relation to water | Not a school marker here | No tank recorded in the premises | The surya kund, a stepped tank with small temples on its steps | Not a school marker here |
| Material | Dressed stone, incl. reddish-gold Rajarani sandstone | Fine-grained buff, pink and pale-yellow sandstone | Sandstone, black basalt, soft marble | Burnt bricks and clay - terracotta |
| Superstructure | Rekha, pidha and khakhara deul types | Curvilinear shikhara with clustered urushringas | Shikhara, with the tank as the school's signature complement | Tall curving shikhara crowned by a large amalaka |
| Solar design | Not recorded here | All temples except the Chaturbhuja face sunrise | Most east-facing; equinox sunlight reaches the central shrine | Not recorded here |

### Examination use

This lesson is what makes a "regional schools" answer analytical instead of enumerative. The organising sentence - environment sets the problem, material sets the possible answers, style is the resulting solution - can open any such answer, and the Solanki and Pala-Sena rows prove it in two opposite directions. The Bangla roof is additionally one of the very few facts in this topic that reaches forward into Indo-Islamic architecture, which makes it valuable in synthesis and essay contexts.

### Revision notes

1. Solanki, also called Maru-Gurjara: north-western India including Gujarat and Rajasthan, under Solanki patronage.
2. Solanki walls are devoid of carvings; the garbhagriha connects with the mandapa both internally and externally; porticos carry decorative arched gateways called torans.
3. The school's unique feature is the step tank, the surya kund, near the temple, its steps full of small temples with wooden carvings.
4. Solanki materials: sandstone, black basalt and soft marble.
5. Most Solanki temples are east-facing and designed so that at the equinoxes sun rays fall directly into the central shrine.
6. Modhera Sun Temple, Gujarat, built 1026-27 AD by Bhima I of the Chaulukya or Solanki dynasty.
7. Pala and Sena school: Bengal, 8th-12th century AD, under Pala and Sena patronage.
8. Palas were primarily Buddhist, following the Mahayana tradition, tolerant, patronising both religions, and built many viharas, chaityas and stupas; Senas were Hindus who built Hindu temples and also sustained Buddhist architecture.
9. Pala monuments: universities of Nalanda, Jagaddala, Odantapuri and Vikramshila; Somapura Mahavihara in Bangladesh.
10. Sena buildings had a curved or sloping roof as in bamboo huts - the "Bangla roof", later adopted by Mughal architects.
11. Principal material: burnt bricks and clay, that is, terracotta bricks.
12. Tower: a tall curving shikhara crowned by a large amalaka, similar to the Odisha school.
13. Sculpture in stone and metal, stone the major component, the figures unique in their highly lustrous finish.
14. Named examples: Siddhesvara Mahadeva Temple, Barakar; temples around Bishnupur, West Bengal.
15. The organising argument: environment sets the problem, material sets the possible answers, style is the solution.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Pala-Sena school is the strongest available evidence that regional temple style is produced by material conditions rather than by regional preference, and identify the one fact in that school that additionally shows influence travelling upward rather than downward.

**Model answer:** The Bengal delta has no building stone, and the school's principal material is accordingly burnt bricks and clay, that is, terracotta. That single constraint explains the school's whole surface character: brick supports moulded and repeated relief but not the deep undercutting that fine-grained sandstone permits at Khajuraho, so richness is achieved by pattern rather than by figures released from the wall. Style here is therefore visibly downstream of geology, not of taste, and the school nonetheless retains the Nagara superstructure grammar, with a tall curving shikhara crowned by a large amalaka similar to the Odisha school, showing that material governs surface while the shared family logic governs form. The upward-influence fact is the Bangla roof: a curved or sloping profile taken from bamboo huts, rendered in masonry, and later adopted by Mughal architects - a vernacular form entering imperial architecture.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "tolerance" as the examinable content of Pala-Sena patronage. The precise and creditable statement is structural: Buddhist Pala rulers of the Mahayana tradition built viharas, chaityas and stupas while patronising both religions, and Hindu Sena rulers built Hindu temples while sustaining Buddhist architecture - one school carrying two religious building programmes across a dynastic change.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Regional schools of Indian temple architecture are best explained by material and environment rather than by aesthetic preference." Discuss with reference to at least three schools. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model answer

The proposition holds well, provided the shared family grammar is credited to convention rather than to geology.

Bengal is the clearest case. The alluvial delta yields no building stone, so the Pala and Sena school of the eighth to twelfth centuries used burnt bricks and clay - terracotta - as its principal material. Brick permits moulded, repeated relief rather than deep undercutting, which fixes the school's surface character, while its curved or sloping bamboo-hut roof, the Bangla roof, later adopted by Mughal architects, shows a vernacular building logic becoming architectural identity.

Arid western India makes the same point from the opposite direction. The Solanki or Maru-Gurjara school of Gujarat and Rajasthan leaves temple walls devoid of carvings, concentrates ornament in arched torans, and adds a stepped surya kund whose steps carry small temples - water storage monumentalised into sacred architecture - with most temples east-facing so that equinox sunlight enters the central shrine, as at the Modhera Sun Temple of 1026-27 AD built by Bhima I.

Bundelkhand completes the contrast. Fine-grained sandstone in buff, pink and pale-yellow shades takes deep undercutting, so the Chandella temples at Khajuraho could release figures from the wall plane across unwalled elevations.

The qualification is real: all three retain the Nagara superstructure grammar, Bengal's tall curving shikhara being crowned by a large amalaka similar to the Odisha school. Material and environment therefore explain surface, fabric and ancillary form, while the shared spire logic is inherited convention.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the thesis stated with its qualification built in 3 + Bengal's material-to-surface chain including the Bangla roof 4 + Solanki's bare walls, torans, surya kund and equinox alignment 4 + Khajuraho's sandstone-to-undercutting chain 2 + the closing distinction between what material explains and what convention explains 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 10:** The Nagara family has now been described from every direction except its most famous member. Khajuraho gets four lessons, and the first of them is not about buildings at all - because the scale of what was built there is unintelligible without the political moment that made it possible.

### Visual: the political sequence, and where the temples enter it

```text
  THE TRACT, RENAMED THREE TIMES
  ancient: VATS  ->  medieval: JEJABHUKTI  ->  since the 14th c.: BUNDELKHAND
                            ^
                            |  named after JAYASHAKTI, also known as
                            |  JEJJAKA or JEJA
                            |
  4th-6th c. AD  GUPTA-PERIOD RESURGENCE OF THE ARTS in this region:
                 leading centres at BHUMARA, KHOH, NACHNA and DEOGARH,
                 "all of which boast significant temples"
                            |
                            v
  9th-13th c. AD  THE CHANDELA DYNASTY rules CENTRAL INDIA
                  one of the most stable kingdoms of central India;
                  contemporaries of the RASHTRAKUTAS, PRATIHARAS and PALAS
                  capital: KHARJJU-RAVAHAKA (Khajuraho), per inscriptions
                            |
        +-------------------+-------------------+
        |                                       |
  EARLY RULERS                            EXPANSION
  Jayashakti (= Jejjaka / Jeja)           under HARSHA and YASHOVARMAN
  Vijayashakti                            - they "seem to have acknowledged
                                            the overlordship of the Pala
                                            ruler DEVAPALA"
        |                                       |
        +-------------------+-------------------+
                            |
                            v
  DECLINE OF THE PRATIHARAS AND PALAS
                            |
                            v
  DHANGA - THE FIRST INDEPENDENT CHANDELA KING
  takes the title MAHARAJADHIRAJA
  "Several of the Khajuraho temples were built during his reign."
                            |
                            v
  >>> THE TEMPLES ARE BUILT AFTER, NOT BEFORE, POLITICAL SELF-ASSERTION <<<
                            |
                            v
  13th c.  THE CHANDELLA DYNASTY EXPIRES
           the temple town "sank from public consciousness"
```

*The arrow that matters is the second-to-last one. Independence precedes monumental patronage; it does not follow it.*

### 1. Why a political lesson sits inside an architecture topic

Because the examiner has asked, in plain words, what the *sculptors* achieved, and a serious answer has to explain how a sculptural programme of that ambition became possible in a small central Indian principality. Scale of this kind requires three things at once: a surplus large enough to pay for it, a patron confident enough to commission it, and a reason to make a permanent public statement. The Chandella sequence supplies all three at a datable moment.

The boundary must still be observed. The dynastic and military narrative of the Chandellas, the Pratiharas and the Palas belongs to Medieval History. What is used here is only the part that explains the **timing and confidence** of the building programme.

### 2. The tract, and its three names

✅ **Fact.** The tract around Khajuraho was known **during ancient times as Vats, in medieval times as Jejabhukti, and since the 14th century as Bundelkhand**.

✅ **Fact.** **Between the 4th and 6th century AD, during the Gupta period, there was a resurgence of the arts, with leading centres at Bhumara, Khoh, Nachna and Deogarh, all of which boast significant temples.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference, and a genuinely useful one.** That second fact prevents the commonest framing error about Khajuraho - that it appears from nowhere as a sudden efflorescence. Central India already had a temple-building tradition four to five centuries earlier, and two of those named centres have already appeared in these lessons: **Nachna**, whose Parvati temple is the stage-two exemplar with the first covered ambulatory, and **Deogarh**, whose Dashavatara temple is the stage-three exemplar with the emergent shikhara and the panchayatana plan. Khajuraho is therefore the culmination of a regional tradition, not its beginning, and saying so in an answer immediately raises its level.

### 3. The dynasty, stated exactly

✅ **Fact.** The **Chandela dynasty ruled in central India between the 9th and 13th century AD**. They were **one of the most stable kingdoms of central India** and were **contemporaries of the Rashtrakutas, Pratiharas and Palas**.

✅ **Fact.** **Inscriptions connect the early kings of this dynasty with Kharjju-ravahaka (Khajuraho), which was their capital.**

✅ **Fact.** **Prominent initial rulers were Jayashakti and Vijayashakti. Jayashakti was also known as Jejjaka, or Jeja, and the Chandela principality came to be known as Jejabhukti after him.**

⚠️ **A disclosed attribution discrepancy.** Chandella expansion continued under **Harsha and Yashovarman**, and the mainstream account places that expansion **within the Gurjara-Pratihara political sphere**, the Chandellas rising as Pratihara feudatories in Jejabhukti before asserting independence. The textbook account consulted here instead records, in hedged form, that Harsha and Yashovarman "**seem to have acknowledged the overlordship of the Pala ruler Devapala**". ✅ Write the Gurjara-Pratihara association, which is the mainstream position and which the sequence in the next paragraph presupposes; ❌ do not adopt the Devapala claim as settled, and note the disagreement if the point is pressed.

✅ **Fact.** **With the decline of the Pratiharas and Palas, the Chandelas took advantage to assert their independence. Dhanga, the first independent Chandela king, took the title of maharajadhiraja. Several of the Khajuraho temples were built during his reign.**

⚠️ **Two evidence notes that protect an answer.**

First, the overlordship claim above is hedged even where it is made - "**seem to have** acknowledged" - so it cannot carry a flat statement that the Chandellas were Pala vassals. Write the Gurjara-Pratihara association, and if the Devapala clause is raised, report it as a disputed reading rather than as settled chronology.

Second, the attribution to Dhanga is general: **several** of the Khajuraho temples were built during his reign. ❌ Do not assign an individual named temple to Dhanga or to any other named Chandella ruler - nothing supports a one-to-one ruler-to-temple attribution, and inventing one is the most likely factual accident in this part of the topic.

### 4. The patronage mechanism, stated as an argument

⚠️ **The inference to write.** Monumental temple building on the Khajuraho scale **follows** political self-assertion rather than preceding it. Independence creates both the surplus and the *need* for a visible, permanent statement of sovereignty; a newly independent dynasty has to be seen to be independent, and stone is the most durable medium available for saying so. The title *maharajadhiraja* and a programme of major temples are the same claim made in two registers.

This is not a Chandella peculiarity, which is what makes it a usable general mechanism.

| Case | The political moment | The building that follows |
|---|---|---|
| **Chandella** | Dhanga, the first independent Chandela king, takes the title *maharajadhiraja* on the decline of the Pratiharas and Palas | Several Khajuraho temples built during his reign |
| **Chola** | Rajaraja I's imperial consolidation in the early eleventh century | Brihadishvara, Thanjavur - a fully granite temple with a vimana of roughly sixty-six metres |
| **Early Chalukya** | Vikramaditya II's conquest of the Pallava capital Kanchipuram | The Virupaksha at Pattadakal, commissioned by Queen Lokmahadevi to commemorate it |
| **Hoysala** | Vishnuvardhana's victory over the Cholas, alongside his conversion from Jainism to Vaishnavism under Ramanujacharya's influence | The Chennakeshava temple at Belur, commissioned to commemorate that victory |
| **Vijayanagara** | Krishnadevaraya's victorious military campaign in Odisha in the early sixteenth century | The Mahanavami Dibba at Hampi, per the Portuguese traveller Domingo Paes - a royal ceremonial platform, not a temple |

❌ **The limit of the mechanism, and it must be stated.** Do not extend this into a claim that a *particular* Khajuraho temple was built to commemorate a *particular* victory. The commemorative commissions in the table are the ones explicitly recorded; the Chandella case supports the general link between independence and building, and nothing narrower. Note also that the last row is deliberately a non-temple, and rests on a traveller's account rather than an inscription - both facts must be carried with it.

### 5. External notice: the site as others saw it

✅ **Fact.** The **11th-century Persian traveller Al-Biruni mentioned Khajuraho as the capital of Jajahuti**. ✅ **Ibn Battuta, who visited India in the 14th century, referred to Khajuraho as "Kajarra".**

✅ **Fact.** **The temple town sank from public consciousness soon after the Chandella dynasty expired in the 13th century.**

⚠️ **What this pair is for.** Together they establish that the site had external, written, non-local notice while it still mattered politically, and that its disappearance from view is datable to the dynasty's end rather than to any single destructive event. That is precisely the evidence a conservation or rediscovery question needs, and Lesson 20 picks it up.

### Comparative note: two kinds of temple patron

The Chandella case is patronage by a newly sovereign *ruler*. It is worth setting against the very different case recorded at Aihole, where an inscription in the **Lad Khan temple** refers to the **Ayyavole merchant guild**, the "500 Lords of Ayyavole" who linked trading communities across Tamil Nadu, Karnataka and Andhra Pradesh, engaged in long-distance maritime trade, and whose inscriptions between the ninth and fourteenth centuries record **endowments made to temples**. Royal patronage builds statements of sovereignty; mercantile patronage builds statements of standing and secures religious merit for a corporate body. Any question about who built India's temples should name both, because an answer that names only kings has described half the record.

### Examination use

The 2025 demand is about sculptors, not kings, so the political material must be used with restraint: one opening sentence locating Khajuraho in Jejabhukti under a newly independent Chandella dynasty, and no more. Where this lesson pays fully is in patronage, state-formation and "temple as legitimacy" demands, where the five-row table above supplies five dated, named cases with their mechanisms. It also supplies the safest possible opening for any Khajuraho answer, because beginning with Dhanga's independence rather than with the erotic sculpture immediately signals analytical control.

### Revision notes

1. The tract: Vats in ancient times, Jejabhukti in medieval times, Bundelkhand since the 14th century.
2. Jejabhukti is named after Jayashakti, also known as Jejjaka or Jeja.
3. Between the 4th and 6th centuries AD the Gupta period saw a resurgence of the arts here, with leading centres at Bhumara, Khoh, Nachna and Deogarh, all with significant temples.
4. Nachna and Deogarh are the stage-two and stage-three Gupta temple exemplars - so Khajuraho is a culmination of a central Indian tradition.
5. The Chandela dynasty ruled central India between the 9th and 13th centuries AD, one of the most stable kingdoms of central India, contemporary with the Rashtrakutas, Pratiharas and Palas.
6. Inscriptions connect the early kings with Kharjju-ravahaka, that is, Khajuraho, their capital.
7. Prominent initial rulers: Jayashakti and Vijayashakti; expansion continued under Harsha and Yashovarman, mainstream accounts placing that expansion within the Gurjara-Pratihara political sphere. The textbook account consulted here instead records, hedged, an acknowledgement of Pala overlordship under Devapala - a disclosed discrepancy, not the position adopted.
8. With the decline of the Pratiharas and Palas the Chandelas asserted independence; Dhanga, the first independent Chandela king, took the title maharajadhiraja, and several Khajuraho temples were built during his reign.
9. Do not attribute an individual temple to a named Chandella ruler; the recorded attribution is general.
10. Mechanism: monumental temple building follows political self-assertion, which supplies both the surplus and the need for a permanent public statement.
11. Parallel commemorative commissions: Rajaraja I and Brihadishvara; Lokmahadevi and the Pattadakal Virupaksha after Vikramaditya II's conquest of Kanchipuram; Vishnuvardhana and the Belur Chennakeshava after his victory over the Cholas; Krishnadevaraya and the Mahanavami Dibba after his Odisha campaign, per Domingo Paes.
12. Non-royal patronage exists: the Ayyavole 500 merchant guild is named in a Lad Khan temple inscription at Aihole, and its inscriptions record endowments to temples.
13. External notice: Al-Biruni, 11th century, records Khajuraho as the capital of Jajahuti; Ibn Battuta, 14th century, calls it "Kajarra".
14. The town sank from public consciousness soon after the Chandella dynasty expired in the 13th century.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes that "the Chandellas built the Khajuraho temples to celebrate their military victories, and Dhanga built the Kandariya Mahadeva temple." Identify the two separate errors, explain what the record actually supports, and state the mechanism that can safely be claimed.

**Model answer:** The first error is transferring a commemorative motive recorded elsewhere - the Pattadakal Virupaksha commissioned by Queen Lokmahadevi to commemorate Vikramaditya II's conquest of Kanchipuram, the Belur Chennakeshava commissioned by Vishnuvardhana to commemorate his victory over the Cholas - onto the Chandella programme, for which no such link is recorded. The second is a one-to-one attribution: the evidence supports only the general statement that several of the Khajuraho temples were built during Dhanga's reign, and names no individual temple's royal builder. What can safely be claimed is the mechanism: with the decline of the Pratiharas and Palas, the Chandelas asserted independence and Dhanga took the title maharajadhiraja, and monumental temple building followed that self-assertion, since independence supplies both the surplus and the need for a durable public statement of sovereignty.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a plausible motive may be supplied where a source is silent. Patronage claims are attribution claims, and attribution is exactly what objective questions and hostile examiners test; a general mechanism stated confidently scores more than a specific motive stated without support.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Monumental temple building in early medieval India was a consequence of political self-assertion rather than of religious sentiment alone." Comment. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model answer

The proposition is well supported, provided religious sentiment is treated as the enabling condition rather than as a rival explanation.

The Chandella case is the clearest. The tract known as Jejabhukti was ruled from Kharjju-ravahaka by a dynasty that expanded under Harsha and Yashovarman within the Gurjara-Pratihara political sphere; only with the decline of the Pratiharas and Palas did the Chandelas assert independence, Dhanga taking the title maharajadhiraja, and several of the Khajuraho temples were built during his reign. The sequence is the argument: the building follows the sovereignty claim.

Comparable cases recur across regions. Rajaraja I's imperial consolidation preceded Brihadishvara at Thanjavur, the first fully granite temple, with a vimana of roughly sixty-six metres. Queen Lokmahadevi commissioned the Pattadakal Virupaksha explicitly to commemorate her husband Vikramaditya II's conquest of Kanchipuram. Vishnuvardhana commissioned the Chennakeshava temple at Belur to commemorate his victory over the Cholas. Krishnadevaraya's Mahanavami Dibba, per Domingo Paes, marked his Odisha campaign - and was a royal ceremonial platform, not a temple.

Three qualifications are owed. Not all patronage was royal: the Ayyavole merchant guild is recorded endowing temples. Not all commissions are commemorative, and extending the motive where sources are silent, as at Khajuraho, is unwarranted. And a temple is a functioning ritual institution, so devotion, endowment and daily worship are not reducible to display.

The comment therefore stands as a statement about timing and scale, not about motive in the singular.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the thesis reframed as timing-and-scale rather than motive 3 + the Chandella sequence stated in order with the hedged overlordship preserved 4 + three further named commemorative commissions with patrons 4 + the mercantile-patronage counterweight 2 + the silence-of-sources and ritual-function qualifications 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 11:** The political moment explains why so much was built. It explains nothing about what was built. The next lesson describes the Khajuraho temples as physical objects - platform, fabric, orientation and the one absence that governs everything else.

## Lesson 11 - Khajuraho as a built site: terrace, fabric, orientation and one decisive absence

### Visual: the Khajuraho temple, and what its absence of a wall does

```text
                        NO ENCLOSURE WALL
   <------------- open ground, seen from every side ------------->

                          /\
                         /  \        CURVILINEAR SHIKHARA
                    /\  /    \  /\   massed with CLUSTERED URUSHRINGAS
                   /  \/      \/  \  -> a mountain-range silhouette
              /\  /              \  /\
             +----------------------------+
             |  RATHA-OFFSET WALL PLANE   |  <- the DISPLAY SURFACE
             |  bands of figures turning  |     Because nothing encloses
             |  every projection and      |     the temple, the outer
             |  recess; raking daylight   |     wall IS the public face.
             |  models every limb         |
             +----------------------------+
             | ardhamandapa | mandapa |   |
             |   antarala   | GARBHAGRIHA |
             +----------------------------+
        =========== HIGH JAGATI (platform terrace) ===========
                 the temple is LIFTED off the ground

   FABRIC:  fine-grained SANDSTONE - buff, pink, pale yellow
            fine even grain  ->  DEEP UNDERCUTTING possible
            ->  limbs and ornament RELEASED from the wall plane
            EXCEPTIONS: the Chausath Yogini enclosure is GRANITE
                        (and the earlier group is not uniformly sandstone)

   ORIENTATION: ALL temples FACE THE SUNRISE
                ... EXCEPT ONE: the CHATURBHUJA temple

   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   | THE CHAIN OF CONSEQUENCE, WHICH IS THE WHOLE ANSWER:         |
   | no wall -> exterior is the display surface                    |
   | high terrace -> the wall is seen from below, at an angle      |
   | ratha offsets -> more framed panels, more shadow              |
   | fine sandstone -> deep undercutting, figures in the round     |
   | => a continuous, legible, MOVING programme over the whole     |
   |    elevation, which is what "resilient vigour" describes      |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
```

*Every later claim about Khajuraho's sculpture depends on this diagram. The sculpture is that ambitious because the architecture made it visible.*

### 1. The one fact that organises everything

✅ **Fact.** The Khajuraho temples are **lofty temples without any enclosure-wall**, erected on a **high platform terrace**.

Pause on how unusual that is. Odisha temples usually have boundary walls. Dravida temples are defined by them. The Solanki complex pairs a temple with a tank. Khajuraho does away with the enclosure entirely and raises the building instead. Three consequences follow immediately, and stating them is what turns a description into an argument.

**The exterior becomes the public face.** With no boundary wall, there is no inner and outer precinct, no controlled gate, and nothing to look at *before* the temple. The building is seen whole, from every angle, in open daylight.

**The viewing angle changes.** A temple on a high terrace is seen from below, so the wall bands run above eye level and the upper registers are legible instead of foreshortened. Sculpture can therefore be distributed up the elevation rather than concentrated where a standing visitor can reach.

**Circulation is external.** Worshippers approach, climb, and circle the temple in the open. The circuit is the viewing sequence, which is why a continuous programme that rewards walking round the building makes architectural sense here in a way it would not inside a walled precinct.

### 2. Style, fabric and the material argument

✅ **Fact.** **All the temples in Khajuraho were constructed in the Nagara style of temple architecture, which reached its climax during this period.**

✅ **Fact.** **The Khajuraho temples were built of fine-grained sandstone, in varying shades of buff, pink and pale yellow.**

⚠️ **The material argument, which is worth a full sentence in any 2025-type answer.** A fine, even grain will take deep undercutting without shearing. That is a technical property with an artistic consequence: limbs, jewellery, drapery and the space between interlocking figures can be cut back from the wall plane, so figures stand nearly free while remaining structurally part of the wall. Coarse or brittle stone would force shallower relief and a flatter, more linear treatment. The vigour of Khajuraho sculpture is therefore not only a matter of the sculptors' intention; it is a matter of what the quarry allowed.

⚠️ **Material is not uniform, and the qualification must be carried.** The earlier group is recorded as Chausath-yogini, Brahma, Varaha and Lalgu, and the second group - Lakshmana, Duladeo and the Mahadeva temples - is described as composed entirely of sandstone structures, while the very next clause says "these are made partly of granite and partly of sandstone." That is an internal inconsistency, and the disciplined response is to record it rather than to choose silently. Heritage documentation identifies **Chausath Yogini, Brahma and Mahadeva as granite exceptions** at the site. Write "predominantly fine-grained sandstone, with granite used in the earliest and some smaller structures" and you are safe on every version of the evidence.

### 3. The axial chain at Khajuraho, and the clustered spire

⚠️ **The sequence to describe.** Khajuraho presents the fully developed northern axial chain in its most elaborate form: **jagati** (the platform terrace) to **ardhamandapa** (entrance porch) to **mandapa** (hall) to **antarala** (vestibule) to **garbhagriha** (sanctum), the sanctum crowned by the **shikhara**. The halls are connected rather than detached, so the temple reads as one continuous mass rising from front to back, and its silhouette ascends toward the sanctum tower.

⚠️ **The spire treatment.** The main shikhara is surrounded by **clustered urushringas** - subsidiary miniature spires repeating the profile of the main tower at reduced scale. The effect is a mountain-range mass rather than a single peak, which visually amplifies the height of the central tower and expresses in built form the definition of the shikhara as a "mountain like" spire. This is the specific device that distinguishes mature Khajuraho elevations from earlier, simpler northern spires.

### 4. Orientation, and the exception that must not be dropped

✅ **Fact.** **All temples, except the one - the Chaturbhuja temple - face the sunrise.**

❌ **The trap.** Writing "all Khajuraho temples face east" is factually wrong by one monument, and it is wrong in exactly the way examiners test. The correct formulation is "all except the Chaturbhuja temple face the sunrise", and carrying the exception costs four words while demonstrating that the candidate has read the description rather than a summary of it.

⚠️ **Why the exception is worth more than accuracy alone.** It shows that orientation at Khajuraho was a rule with a recognised departure, which means orientation was a deliberate design decision rather than a default. Paired with the Solanki equinox alignment from Lesson 9, it gives a short, evidenced paragraph on solar design in Nagara practice - and solar design is a recurring examiner interest because it links architecture to astronomy and to ritual time.

### 5. Plural dedication as an architectural fact

✅ **Fact.** The temples there **variously belong to the Shaiva, Vaishnava and Jain sects**, and **of the surviving temples, six are dedicated to Lord Shiva, eight to Lord Vishnu, one to Lord Ganesha, one to Lord Sun, and four to Jain Tirthankaras**. ✅ **Of all the temples, the Matangeshvara temple remains an active site of worship.**

⚠️ **Why this belongs in a *built-form* lesson rather than a religion lesson.** Because the plurality is expressed architecturally: three sects, one site, one architectural idiom. The Jain temples at Khajuraho are not built in a separate Jain manner; they use the same Nagara morphology and the same sculptural vocabulary as the Hindu temples. That is a statement about shared craft traditions and a shared patronage environment, and it is far stronger evidence than any general remark about coexistence.

❌ **Numerical discipline.** The breakdown above describes the surviving temples as recorded. ❌ Do not present a count of standing Khajuraho temples as a current figure; current site numbers are a matter for the protecting authority's own dated record, and belong to Topic 14.

### Comparative note: why Khajuraho is a hard comparator

Most temple comparisons work because the two sides differ on one axis. Khajuraho against Odisha works on enclosure. Khajuraho against Solanki works on wall surface. Khajuraho against Chola works on almost everything - family, fabric, enclosure, spire type, growth mechanism - which makes it a *contrast* rather than a comparison, and contrasts are only useful if the candidate names the dimension being contrasted. The safest structure is to pick one dimension, state both positions, and explain the reason for each: for example, "enclosure - Khajuraho has none and therefore invests everything in the outer wall; the Chola complex is walled and therefore invests in the gateway - because an unwalled temple is seen from all sides while a walled one is entered at one point."

### Examination use

This is the architectural half of the 2025 Chandella demand, and it must be present even though that question is about sculptors: the carving method described in Lesson 13 is only intelligible once the fabric and the viewing conditions are established. It is also the core of any "salient features of Khajuraho architecture" or "Khajuraho as mature Nagara" demand. The three facts most likely to be tested objectively are the absence of an enclosure wall, the sunrise orientation with its single exception, and the sandstone fabric with its granite exceptions.

### Revision notes

1. All Khajuraho temples were constructed in the Nagara style, which reached its climax in this period.
2. They are lofty temples without any enclosure wall, erected on a high platform terrace.
3. Three consequences of having no wall: the exterior becomes the public face; the high terrace makes upper wall registers legible from below; circulation and viewing happen in the open, on the circuit.
4. Fabric: fine-grained sandstone in varying shades of buff, pink and pale yellow.
5. Fine even grain permits deep undercutting, so figures can be released from the wall plane - the technical basis of the sculpture's vigour.
6. Material is not uniform: the recorded account of the groups is internally inconsistent, and Chausath Yogini, Brahma and Mahadeva are identified as granite exceptions - write "predominantly sandstone, with granite in the earliest and some smaller structures".
7. Axial chain: jagati, ardhamandapa, mandapa, antarala, garbhagriha, crowned by the shikhara, with connected rather than detached halls.
8. The main shikhara is massed with clustered urushringas, producing the mountain-range silhouette.
9. All temples except the Chaturbhuja temple face the sunrise - always carry the exception.
10. Orientation with a recognised exception shows deliberate solar design; pair it with the Solanki equinox alignment.
11. The temples variously belong to the Shaiva, Vaishnava and Jain sects: six to Shiva, eight to Vishnu, one to Ganesha, one to the Sun and four to Jain Tirthankaras among the surviving temples.
12. Matangeshvara remains an active site of worship - the site's one living temple.
13. Plurality is architectural: three sects, one site, one idiom, one craft vocabulary.
14. Do not state a current count of standing temples; that belongs to the protecting authority's dated record and to Topic 14.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how the absence of an enclosure wall at Khajuraho, combined with the high platform terrace and the fine-grained sandstone, together account for the character of its sculptural programme - and state one qualification about the material that a careful answer must include.

**Model answer:** The three conditions compound. Without an enclosure wall there is no precinct and no gate, so the temple is seen whole from every side in open daylight and the outer wall becomes the only public surface available for display. The high platform terrace means the wall is viewed from below at an angle, so bands of sculpture placed well above eye level remain legible and the programme can run up the whole elevation rather than clustering at reachable height. The fine, even grain of the buff, pink and pale-yellow sandstone permits deep undercutting without shearing, so figures can be cut back from the wall plane until limbs and ornament stand nearly free, which is what produces the impression of movement in raking light. Architecture, siting and material therefore jointly make a continuous, dense, moving programme both possible and worth commissioning. The qualification is that the fabric is not uniform: the recorded account of the temple groups is internally inconsistent, and Chausath Yogini, Brahma and Mahadeva are identified as granite, so the claim must be stated as predominantly sandstone with granite exceptions.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the sculpture as an independent artistic achievement laid onto a neutral building. The programme is a response to specific architectural conditions; a walled temple with a controlled gate, or a coarse-grained stone, would have produced a different and shallower result from sculptors of identical skill.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Discuss the salient architectural features of the Khajuraho group of temples, and explain why they are described as representing the climax of the Nagara style. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

All the Khajuraho temples were built in the Nagara style, described as reaching its climax here.

Their defining decision is negative: they are lofty temples on a high platform terrace with no enclosure wall at all. The developed northern axial chain - jagati, entrance porch, hall, vestibule and sanctum - is executed as one connected mass, and the curvilinear shikhara is surrounded by clustered miniature spires building a mountain-range silhouette. The fabric is fine-grained sandstone in buff, pink and pale-yellow shades, with granite used in the earliest and some smaller structures, and the fine grain permits the deep undercutting that releases figures from the ratha-offset wall plane. All temples except the Chaturbhuja face the sunrise.

They mark a climax because plan, elevation, fabric and sculpture are resolved into a single integrated composition rather than assembled as separate achievements.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the unwalled high-terrace plan named as the organising decision 3 + the complete axial chain and clustered urushringas 2 + fabric with the granite qualification 2 + the orientation rule with its exception 1 + the climax claim argued as integration rather than asserted 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 12:** A description of the site as a whole is still too general to answer an objective question or to give a Mains answer its named climax. The temples have to be separated - and each one has to be attached to the single point it is best evidence for.

## Lesson 12 - The Khajuraho monuments, named and distinguished

Progress: 12/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the two groups, the individual temples and what each proves, the Chausath Yogini problem and its Morena counterpart, and the "Little Khajuraho" afterlife

CA search: "Not required - monument-identification lesson"
CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one temple, one job

```text
   EARLIER GROUP ("the less splendid temples")
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | CHAUSATH-YOGINI  -> the EARLIEST BUILDING at Khajuraho;        |

   |                     TANTRIC and SHAIVA traditions              |
   |                     [PLAN DISPUTE: see the box below]          |
   | BRAHMA           -> granite; part of the earliest group        |

   | CHITRAGUPTA        -> the SURYA (Sun) temple at Khajuraho      |
   | PARSVANATH         -> the principal JAIN temple                |
```
