```text
   | CHATURBHUJA        -> THE ONE TEMPLE THAT DOES NOT FACE SUNRISE|
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+

   +===============================================================+
   | THE CHAUSATH YOGINI PLAN DISPUTE, RECORDED NOT RESOLVED       |
   |                                                               |
   | Core art source (PDF p. 81): "Its CIRCULAR and open-air       |
   |   structures reflect Tantric and Shaiva traditions."          |
   | The same account's sidebar: Chausath Yogini temples are       |
   |   "typically with CIRCULAR OR SOMETIMES RECTANGULAR           |
   |   enclosures", about 13 surviving in India, mostly 9th-11th   |
   |   centuries, most in Madhya Pradesh, then UP, Odisha, TN.     |
   | Heritage documentation for THIS shrine: RECTANGULAR courtyard |
   |   ringed with small cells, built of coarse GRANITE blocks -   |
   |   the site's one major granite structure.                     |
   |                                                               |
   | THIS SESSION FOLLOWS THE HERITAGE DOCUMENTATION (rectangular, |
   | granite) AND FLAGS THE CONFLICT RATHER THAN CHOOSING SILENTLY.|
   +===============================================================+

   DO NOT MERGE:  KHAJURAHO's Chausath-yogini  vs  MORENA / MITAOLI's
   Chandella, granite, rectangular             Kachchhapaghata, circular
   (per heritage documentation)                (per the reproduced item's key)
```

*A named monument attached to a single analytical job is worth more than five names in a list.*

### 1. The learner's problem: a site that dissolves into a list

Khajuraho is usually revised as a string of temple names, which is the least useful form the information can take. Names are only valuable when each is attached to a job - the earliest, the largest, the Jain one, the solar one, the living one, the exception to the orientation rule. Attached that way, six names give a Mains answer six distinct pieces of evidence and give an objective question six distinct discriminators. Unattached, they give nothing.

### 2. The two groups, exactly as recorded

✅ **Fact.** **The earlier group of the less splendid temples comprises the Chausath-yogini, Brahma, Varaha and Lalgu.** ✅ **The second group, composed entirely of sandstone structures, comprises Lakshmana and Duladeo Temples, and the Mahadeva Temples.**

✅ **Fact.** **There are many other temples such as Parsvanath, Visvanath, Jagadambi and Chitragupta temples.** ✅ **The peak of the architectural and sculptural efflorescence can be found in Kandariya-Mahadeva Temple.** ✅ **There is also one Surya temple in Khajuraho, known as Chitragupta Temple.** ✅ **Kandariya-Mahadev Temple, dedicated to Lord Shiva, is the largest and loftiest monument of Khajuraho.** ✅ **Of all temples, the Matangeshvara temple remains an active site of worship.**

⚠️ **A note on the group description.** As recorded in Lesson 11, the second group is called "composed entirely of sandstone structures" and the next clause says "these are made partly of granite and partly of sandstone". The honest treatment is to use the groups as a *relative chronology* - an earlier, plainer set and a later, more developed set - and to state the fabric separately with its qualification, rather than to reproduce a self-contradicting sentence.

### 3. Each monument, with the job it does

| Temple | What is recorded | The one thing it is best evidence for |
|---|---|---|
| **Chausath-yogini** | The **earliest building at Khajuraho**; open-air Yogini shrine reflecting **Tantric and Shaiva traditions**; granite; plan disputed between the accounts | That the site begins with an *atypical* structure, and that Shakta and Tantric traditions are present at Khajuraho from the outset |
| **Brahma, Varaha, Lalgu** | The earlier, less splendid group with Chausath-yogini | That Khajuraho has a developmental sequence and is not a single uniform campaign |
| **Lakshmana** | Vaishnava; the second, sandstone group | The mature group's Vaishnava dedication, useful for the plural-patronage argument |
| **Duladeo and the Mahadeva temples** | The second group with Lakshmana | Group membership, and the scale shift between the two groups |
| **Kandariya-Mahadeva** | Dedicated to **Shiva**; the **largest and loftiest** monument; the site of "the peak of the architectural and sculptural efflorescence" | The single named climax that every Khajuraho answer should close on |
| **Visvanath / Vishvanath** | A major temple of the site | A second named Shaiva monument when breadth is required |
| **Jagadambi** | A major temple of the site | Breadth of the named corpus |
| **Chitragupta** | The **Surya**, or Sun, temple of Khajuraho | That the site's dedications extend beyond the three main sects, alongside the one Ganesha temple |
| **Parsvanath** | The principal **Jain** temple | That Jain temples at Khajuraho use the same Nagara idiom and the same sculptural vocabulary - the strongest plurality evidence |
| **Matangeshvara** | The one temple that **remains an active site of worship** | That Khajuraho is partly a *living* monument, which changes what conservation must accommodate |
| **Chaturbhuja** | The **one temple that does not face the sunrise** | The exception that disciplines the orientation claim |

⚠️ **How to use this table under time pressure.** A 150-word answer needs three names, not eleven: Chausath-yogini for the beginning, Parsvanath or the sect breakdown for plurality, and Kandariya Mahadeva for the climax. A 250-word answer can afford five or six. Choosing by job rather than by fame is what keeps the selection defensible.

### 4. The Chausath Yogini problem, handled properly

This is the one place in the Khajuraho material where the record contradicts itself, and it is worth working through because it models a skill the whole topic needs.

✅ **What the standard textbook account says.** **"Chausath-yogini Temple is the earliest building at Khajuraho. Its circular and open-air structures reflect Tantric and Shaiva traditions."**

✅ **What the same account says two lines later, in its own sidebar.** Chausath Yogini temples are "a fascinating group of open-to-sky shrines in India dedicated to the worship of numerous Yogini which are female divine figures associated with the Shakti and Tantric traditions. These are unusual in Indian temple-architecture **typically with circular or sometimes rectangular enclosures**, and date **mostly from around 9th-11th centuries AD**, under various regional dynasties. **India has around 13 surviving Chausath Yogini temples**, with their locations in **Madhya Pradesh (highest number), Uttar Pradesh, Odisha and Tamil Nadu**."

⚠️ **What heritage documentation records for this specific shrine**, and the description followed here: a **rectangular** or quadrangular courtyard ringed with small shrine cells, built of coarse **granite** blocks rather than the sandstone favoured for the later and more ornate Khajuraho temples, which makes it the site's one major granite structure.

⚠️ **The disciplined position, and the one to write.** The Khajuraho Chausath Yogini is the site's earliest structure, open to the sky, built in granite, and associated with Yogini, Shakta and Tantric worship. Its plan is described as circular by the textbook account and as rectangular by the heritage record, and the textbook's own sidebar concedes that Chausath Yogini temples may be circular *or sometimes rectangular*. This session follows the heritage record and states the conflict. ❌ What must not happen is a silent choice: asserting "circular" or "rectangular" as settled, with no indication that the evidence differs, is exactly the kind of confident error that an examiner with the other source in mind will mark down.

### 5. Khajuraho's Chausath Yogini against Morena's - a distinction that has been examined

A **Civil Services 2021** objective item tested four statements about the Chausath Yogini temple **situated near Morena**, and its published key accepted two of them. Its exact stem, statements and key are reproduced in the final linkage section; what matters here is what that key establishes.

⚠️ **What follows, stated with its provenance.** On that key, the **Morena or Mitaoli** Chausath Yogini is **circular** and of the **Kachchhapaghata** period, and the Parliament-inspiration story is a **popular belief** rather than an established fact - a reading corroborated by the separate record that the old Parliament building, completed in 1927 to designs by Herbert Baker and Edwin Lutyens, "was influenced by Chausath Yogini Temple at Mitaoli in Madhya Pradesh". The statements that it is the only circular temple in India and that it was meant to promote the Vaishnava sect are not supported.

❌ **The distinction to hold.** Khajuraho's Chausath-yogini is **Chandella**, granite, at Khajuraho, and described by the heritage record as rectangular. Mitaoli's is at **Morena**, of the **Kachchhapaghata** period, and circular. They are different monuments, and an objective question naming Morena is not answered from the Khajuraho row. The Parliament-design resemblance itself is owned by Topic 05, which treats it as a debated resemblance - which is exactly what "popular belief" means.

### 6. The afterlife of the idiom

✅ **Fact.** **The 10th-century Bhand Deva Temple in Rajasthan was built in the style of the Khajuraho monuments and is often referred to as "Little Khajuraho".**

⚠️ **Why this small fact earns its place.** It shows the Khajuraho idiom travelling beyond Chandella territory within the same century, which means the style was recognised and reproducible rather than being a single site's peculiarity. In a question about regional influence or the diffusion of architectural style, it is a short, precise, checkable example.

### Examination use

Named-monument control is what separates grades here. For Mains, close every Khajuraho answer on Kandariya Mahadeva as the named climax and open it with Chausath-yogini as the named beginning - the answer then has a shape before a single adjective is spent. For objective questions, the highest-value discriminators are: earliest building; largest and loftiest; the Surya dedication belonging to Chitragupta; the Jain dedication of Parsvanath; Matangeshvara as the living temple; Chaturbhuja as the orientation exception; and the Khajuraho-versus-Morena Chausath Yogini distinction.

### Revision notes

1. Earlier, less splendid group: Chausath-yogini, Brahma, Varaha and Lalgu.
2. Second group: Lakshmana and Duladeo temples and the Mahadeva temples - described as entirely sandstone in one clause and as partly granite in the next, so state the fabric separately with its qualification.
3. Other named temples: Parsvanath, Visvanath, Jagadambi and Chitragupta.
4. Kandariya-Mahadeva: dedicated to Shiva; the largest and loftiest monument; the peak of the architectural and sculptural efflorescence.
5. Chitragupta is the Surya, or Sun, temple of Khajuraho.
6. Parsvanath is the principal Jain temple, built in the same Nagara idiom as the Hindu temples.
7. Matangeshvara is the one temple that remains an active site of worship.
8. Chaturbhuja is the one temple that does not face the sunrise.
9. Chausath-yogini is the earliest building at Khajuraho, open to the sky, granite, reflecting Yogini, Shakta, Tantric and Shaiva traditions.
10. Its plan is disputed: the textbook calls it circular, the heritage record rectangular, and the textbook's own sidebar allows both for the type.
11. Chausath Yogini temples as a type: open-to-sky shrines to Yogini figures of the Shakti and Tantric traditions; typically circular or sometimes rectangular enclosures; mostly 9th-11th centuries AD; about 13 surviving in India, most in Madhya Pradesh, then Uttar Pradesh, Odisha and Tamil Nadu.
12. Morena or Mitaoli Chausath Yogini: circular, Kachchhapaghata, and the inspiration for the old Parliament building only as a popular belief; it is not the only circular temple in India and was not meant to promote the Vaishnava sect.
13. The old Parliament building was built in 1927 to designs by Herbert Baker and Edwin Lutyens; the Mitaoli influence is a debated resemblance owned by Topic 05.
14. Bhand Deva Temple, Rajasthan, 10th century: built in the style of the Khajuraho monuments, often called "Little Khajuraho" - evidence that the idiom travelled.

### Concept check

**Question:** An objective question asks about "the Chausath Yogini temple near Morena". A candidate answers from what they remember about Khajuraho's Chausath-yogini. Explain every way in which that substitution fails, and state what may and may not be asserted about each monument.

**Model answer:** The substitution fails on site, dynasty, plan and associated claim. The Khajuraho Chausath-yogini is at Khajuraho, is Chandella-period, is the earliest building at that site, is built of granite, and is described by the heritage record as rectangular while the textbook account calls it circular - so its plan may only be asserted with the conflict stated. The Morena or Mitaoli monument is a different building, of the Kachchhapaghata period, and is circular on the reading supported by the reproduced question's own key; that key also supports treating the Parliament-building inspiration as a popular belief, while rejecting the claims that it is the only circular temple in India and that it was meant to promote the Vaishnava sect. What may safely be said of both is that they belong to a type of open-to-sky Yogini shrine associated with Shakti and Tantric worship, of which about thirteen survive in India, mostly from the ninth to eleventh centuries, with the highest number in Madhya Pradesh.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a shared temple type as a shared identity. "Chausath Yogini" names a category of shrine with multiple surviving examples under various regional dynasties, so the name alone identifies nothing; the site name in the question is the operative word.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The Khajuraho group is better understood as a plural religious landscape than as a single architectural campaign." Examine with reference to named temples. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model answer

The proposition holds on both halves of its claim, and each requires separate evidence.

Plurality is demonstrable by dedication. Among the surviving temples, six are dedicated to Shiva, eight to Vishnu, one to Ganesha, one to the Sun and four to Jain Tirthankaras, so the site carries Shaiva, Vaishnava and Jain worship together, with the Chitragupta temple supplying the solar dedication and Parsvanath the principal Jain one. The earliest structure, the Chausath-yogini, is a Yogini shrine reflecting Tantric and Shaiva traditions, adding a Shakta presence at the site's very beginning. Decisively, the Jain temples are built in the same Nagara idiom and carry the same sculptural vocabulary as the Hindu temples, which evidences shared craftsmen and a shared patronage environment rather than mere coexistence.

The site is equally not one campaign. The sources distinguish an earlier, less splendid group - Chausath-yogini, Brahma, Varaha and Lalgu - from a later group of Lakshmana, Duladeo and the Mahadeva temples, culminating in Kandariya-Mahadeva, the largest and loftiest monument and the peak of the architectural and sculptural efflorescence. Fabric varies with that sequence, the earliest structures including granite while the developed temples are predominantly fine-grained sandstone.

Two qualifications: individual temples cannot be attributed to named rulers beyond the general statement that several were built during Dhanga's reign, and the surviving dedication counts are a description of the recorded corpus rather than a current site inventory.

Khajuraho is therefore a landscape accumulated over generations across sects, not a single commission.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = both halves of the proposition separated and evidenced independently 3 + the dedication breakdown with named temples 4 + the shared-idiom argument for Jain temples as the strongest plurality evidence 3 + the two-group sequence with Kandariya Mahadeva as climax 3 + the attribution and counting qualifications 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 13:** The buildings are now named and distinguished. What remains is the thing the 2025 examiner actually asked about - not the temples, but the sculptors, and what they put on these walls.

## Lesson 13 - The sculptural programme: vigour, breadth, and the logic of placement

Progress: 13/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: how the Chandella programme is carved and what it contains, the two halves of the 2025 demand, iconographic placement rules across schools, and the Vyala motif

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Khajuraho artwork passage and the placement rules in the Nagara, Dravida and Chola descriptions
CA search: "Not required - sculptural-programme lesson"
CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two questions a Chandella-sculpture demand asks separately

```text
   A SCULPTURE CLAIM WITH TWO HALVES:
     RESILIENT VIGOUR  and   BREADTH OF LIFE
            |                      |
            |                      |
        HOW it is carved       WHAT is carved
            |                      |
   +--------+--------+    +--------+-----------------------------+
   |                 |    |                                      |
 MATERIAL        ARCHITECTURE   SUBJECT REGISTERS ON THE RECORD
 fine-grained    no enclosure   +--------------------------------+
 sandstone,      wall; high     | deities                        |
 buff/pink/      jagati; ratha- | worshippers                    |
 pale yellow     offset wall    | dancers                        |
     |           planes         | musicians                      |
 fine even           |          | teachers and disciples         |
 grain takes     exterior is    | courtly / secular life         |
 DEEP            the display    | VYALAS - hybrid imaginary      |
 UNDERCUTTING    surface, seen  |   animals with LIONS' BODIES,  |
     |           from below in  |   found in OTHER Indian temples|
 limbs and       raking light   |   too, on Vishnu-dedicated     |
 ornament            |          |   shrines at Khajuraho         |
 RELEASED from   figures read   | MAITHUNA reliefs - ONE ITEM    |
 the wall        as MOVING as   |   WITHIN a variety, in BOTH    |
 plane           you walk round |   Hindu AND Jain temples       |
     |               |          +--------------------------------+
     +-------+-------+                        |
             |                                |
      => "RESILIENT VIGOR"              => "BREADTH OF LIFE"
         movement generated by             a full social and sacred
         flexed, twisting,                 world placed on one wall
         interacting bodies
         rather than frontal
         static icons

   THE ONE-SENTENCE VERDICT THAT UNITES THEM:
   the carving method that gives the figures their movement is the
   same method that allows an entire world to be placed on one wall.
```

*Split the quotation before you answer it. Candidates who treat "vigour and breadth" as one idea write half an answer twice.*

### 1. The learner's problem: praise instead of analysis

Sculpture invites adjectives, and adjectives score nothing. "Exquisite", "lifelike", "sensuous" and "magnificent" could be written about any sculpture anywhere, and an examiner reading them learns only that the candidate has seen a photograph.

The repair is to convert every impression into a cause. If the figures look alive, say what makes them look alive - the depth of cut, the flexed posture, the interaction between figures, the angle of view, the raking daylight on an unwalled elevation. If the programme feels comprehensive, say what it comprises - and name the registers. That is the difference between describing an effect and explaining it, and it is precisely what the 2025 verb "elucidate" demands.

### 2. What is actually recorded as carved

✅ **Fact.** **Both the Hindu and Jain temples in Khajuraho feature a variety of artwork, including erotic element depicted through maithuna reliefs on the temple walls.**

✅ **Fact.** **Some sculptures at the Khajuraho monuments dedicated to Vishnu include the Vyalas, which are hybrid imaginary animals with lions' bodies, and are found in other Indian temples.**

✅ **Fact, from the same description.** The Khajuraho sculptural programme spans **deities, minor and clan deities, worshippers, secular and courtly life, dancers, musicians, teachers and disciples, Vyalas, and maithuna reliefs**.

⚠️ **Three disciplines this evidence imposes.**

**On proportion.** Maithuna is listed as *one item within* a variety, never as the whole. ❌ Do not state any percentage or fraction of Khajuraho sculpture as erotic; no record supplies one, so any figure would be invented.

**On the Vyala.** ❌ Do not present it as unique to Khajuraho. Vyalas are explicitly recorded in other Indian temples too. Its value is that it is *specific and shared* - a named, recognisable motif that can be used to connect Khajuraho to a wider practice, and which anticipates the composite lion-bodied **Yali** of the Vijayanagara and Nayaka south.

**On posture.** ⚠️ Describe posture in general terms - flexed, twisting, interacting, in motion rather than frontal and static. ❌ Do not invent named postures for specific panels; the record does not identify individual figures by pose, and dance-posture terminology belongs to Topic 09 in any case.

### 3. "Resilient vigour" - the four causes of movement

Each of these is a *cause*, which is what an elucidation needs.

| Cause | The evidence | Why it produces movement |
|---|---|---|
| **Material** | Fine-grained sandstone in buff, pink and pale-yellow shades | A fine, even grain takes deep undercutting without shearing, so limbs, ornament and the gaps between figures can be released from the wall plane |
| **The wall as a display field** | Lofty temples on a high platform terrace **without any enclosure wall** | With no boundary, the exterior *is* the public surface, seen from every side in raking daylight, so modelling reads as movement as the viewer circles |
| **The offset wall plane** | The northern wall is divided into vertical **rathas** - triratha, pancharatha, saptaratha, navaratha - used as panels for narrative sculpture | Projections and recesses create alternating light and shadow, so bands of figures appear to advance and retreat rather than sitting on a flat face |
| **The figural treatment itself** | A programme of deities, worshippers, dancers, musicians, teachers and disciples, courtly figures, Vyalas and maithuna couples | Movement is generated by flexed, twisting, interacting bodies rather than by frontal static icons; interaction between figures implies action, and action implies time |

⚠️ **The synthesis.** "Resilient" is worth a clause of its own. The figures are not fragile, attenuated or floating; they are substantial, weighted bodies that remain part of the wall even while standing almost free of it. That is a structural as well as an aesthetic fact: deep undercutting in a load-bearing wall is only possible if enough stone is left behind, so the sculptors were solving an engineering problem at the same time as a compositional one.

### 4. "Breadth of life" - the registers, and the strongest single fact

⚠️ **The strongest evidence for breadth arrives before any individual sculpture is discussed.** Among the surviving temples, six are dedicated to Shiva, eight to Vishnu, one to Ganesha, one to the Sun and four to Jain Tirthankaras. One site, one architectural idiom, three sects and five dedications: that is programme breadth stated in the plan of the site itself, and it is the single hardest fact to argue with.

⚠️ **Then the social registers.** The programme includes worshippers, dancers, musicians, teachers and disciples, and courtly life alongside deities. This - not the erotic register - is what the phrase "breadth of life" most directly names, and an answer that reaches for the maithuna reliefs to explain "breadth of life" has misread the question.

⚠️ **Then the imaginative register.** Vyalas, hybrid imaginary animals with lions' bodies, extend the programme beyond the observed world into the mythical, which is itself part of the claimed breadth.

⚠️ **And finally the intimate register**, treated fully in Lesson 14: maithuna reliefs, present in both the Hindu and the Jain temples, as one item within a variety.

### 5. Placement: where images go, and why it is examinable

Placement is the part of sculpture that genuinely belongs to an *architecture* topic, and more placement rules are recorded than candidates usually realise.

| Placement rule | Where it applies | What it shows |
|---|---|---|
| Images of the river goddesses **Ganga and Yamuna** placed **outside the garbhagriha** | Nagara school generally | A fixed iconographic position tied to the threshold of the sanctum |
| **Dvarapala, mithuna and yaksha** at the **entrance of the garbhagriha** | Dravida school generally | The southern threshold rule - and evidence that mithuna imagery at sanctum doors is a wide practice, not a Khajuraho peculiarity |
| The wall divided into vertical **ratha** planes, "used as different panels to make **narrative sculptures**" | Nagara school generally | Narrative is organised by architectural division, so the plan determines the storytelling grid |
| **Devakoshtha** - niches formed by pilasters dividing the outer walls, containing images of various deities | Chola temples | Deities are framed individually in architectural niches rather than massed in bands |
| A **frieze of lions along the base** of the temple | Typical of Chola temples | Register-by-height organisation: animals at the base, deities above |
| **Sculptural friezes** in bands of elephants, lions, scrolls, horses, makaras, swans and deities on the outer walls | Hoysala temples | Horizontal banding as a regional alternative to vertical panelling |
| The **vahana** placed **just before the sanctum sanctorum** | General | Position encodes relationship: the mount attends the deity |
| A separate **natana-sabha** where the image of Nataraja is placed | Many South Indian Shiva temples | A dedicated architectural space created for a single iconographic type |

⚠️ **The analytical point to extract.** Two different logics are visible. Khajuraho and the northern schools organise sculpture **vertically**, by wall plane, so the programme is read as you move around and your eye travels up. The Chola and Hoysala traditions organise it **horizontally**, by band and niche, so the programme is read as a series of registers at fixed heights. Neither is richer; they are different reading systems, and naming the difference is a genuinely high-level comparative move.

❌ **Boundary.** Everything above is *placement*. What each image *means* - the iconography of Ganga and Yamuna, the symbolism of the Nataraja, the reading of the saptamatrikas - belongs to Topic 06. Say where an image stands and what its position implies architecturally; do not drift into doctrine.

### Criticism and reply: is "vigour" a property of the stone or of the sculptor?

**The objection.** If deep undercutting depends on fine-grained sandstone, and legibility depends on an unwalled elevation and a raised terrace, then the celebrated vigour of Khajuraho sculpture is largely a function of quarry and site. Praising the sculptors is sentimental; the material did the work.

**The reply.** The objection correctly identifies the enabling conditions and wrongly treats them as sufficient. Fine sandstone was available across central India for centuries without producing this result, and the same site's earliest structures - built in granite - show that the Chandella programme's character is not simply the stone talking. What the sculptors contributed is compositional: the decision to interact figures rather than isolate them, to use flexed and twisting postures rather than frontal ones, to run the programme up the whole elevation, and to include a social and imaginative range far beyond the cult image. **The balanced verdict** is that material and architecture set the ceiling and the sculptors reached it - which is precisely why the examiner's sentence credits *the sculptors* while a complete answer still explains the conditions.

### Examination use

This lesson is the substantive core of the verified 2025 demand. The directive "elucidate" means make clear by explanation, so the marks lie in causes and registers, not in praise. Split the quotation into its two halves, give each its own evidence, name three or four registers rather than all of them, and close on Kandariya Mahadeva as the named climax. The same material answers any "sculptural programme of medieval Indian temples" demand, and it feeds directly into the social-evidence demand treated in Lesson 19.

### Revision notes

1. A Chandella-sculpture claim of this kind contains two separate parts: vigour is *how* it is carved, breadth of life is *what* is carved.
2. Four causes of vigour: fine-grained sandstone permitting deep undercutting; an unwalled elevation on a high terrace making the exterior the display surface; ratha offsets creating light and shadow; flexed, twisting, interacting figures rather than frontal icons.
3. "Resilient" carries a structural sense: substantial, weighted bodies that remain part of a load-bearing wall while standing almost free of it.
4. The strongest breadth evidence is the site's dedications - six Shiva, eight Vishnu, one Ganesha, one Sun, four Jain among the surviving temples.
5. Social registers: worshippers, dancers, musicians, teachers and disciples, courtly life - this is what "breadth of life" most directly names.
6. Imaginative register: Vyalas, hybrid imaginary animals with lions' bodies, on Vishnu-dedicated shrines - and explicitly found in other Indian temples too.
7. Intimate register: maithuna reliefs, in both Hindu and Jain temples, as one item within a variety.
8. Never state a percentage or fraction of Khajuraho sculpture as erotic; no record supplies one.
9. Never present Vyalas as unique to Khajuraho; never invent named postures for specific panels.
10. Placement rules: Ganga and Yamuna outside the garbhagriha in Nagara practice; dvarapala, mithuna and yaksha at the garbhagriha entrance in Dravida practice; vahana just before the sanctum.
11. Ratha planes are used as panels for narrative sculpture, so the plan supplies the storytelling grid.
12. Chola placement: devakoshtha niches formed by pilasters holding deity images, and a lion frieze along the base. Hoysala placement: horizontal friezes of elephants, lions, scrolls, horses, makaras, swans and deities.
13. Northern programmes organise sculpture vertically by wall plane; Chola and Hoysala programmes organise it horizontally by band and niche.
14. Placement is owned here; meaning and iconographic doctrine belong to Topic 06.

### Concept check

**Question:** "Elucidate" asks you to make something clear by explaining it. Take the claim that Chandella sculptors achieved "resilient vigour" and convert it into an explanation, giving at least three causes, and then state the sentence that connects vigour to breadth rather than treating them as two unrelated compliments.

**Model answer:** Three causes carry the explanation. The fabric is fine-grained sandstone in buff, pink and pale-yellow shades, and a fine even grain takes deep undercutting without shearing, so limbs, ornament and the spaces between interlocking figures can be cut back until the figures stand almost free of the wall. The architecture exposes that carving: the temples are lofty, raised on a high platform terrace and built without any enclosure wall, so the exterior is the display surface, seen from every side and from below in raking daylight, which models every projection. And the wall itself is divided into vertical ratha planes used as panels for narrative sculpture, so alternating projection and recess makes bands of figures appear to advance and retreat as the viewer circles the building; the figures are themselves flexed, twisting and interacting rather than frontal and static. The connecting sentence is that the carving method which gives the figures their movement is the same method that allows an entire social and sacred world - deities, worshippers, dancers, musicians, teachers and disciples, courtly life, Vyalas and maithuna couples - to be placed legibly on one wall. Vigour and breadth are one achievement, not two.

**Misconception to avoid:** Answering "breadth of life" with the erotic sculpture. The phrase most directly names the social range of the programme - worshippers, performers, teachers, courtiers - and the plural dedications of the site; the intimate register is one part of that range, and leading with it both misreads the question and narrows the answer.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Explain how the placement of sculpture on a temple, rather than its subject matter, distinguishes northern from southern practice. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

Both traditions carve deities, guardians and narrative; they differ in where images are put and therefore in how they are read.

Northern practice organises sculpture vertically. The wall is divided into vertical planes or rathas - triratha to navaratha - used as panels for narrative sculpture, so projection and recess carry bands of figures up the whole elevation; at the threshold, images of Ganga and Yamuna stand outside the garbhagriha. An unwalled Khajuraho temple on a high terrace is therefore read by circling and looking upward.

Southern practice organises sculpture horizontally, by register at fixed heights. Pilasters divide Chola outer walls into devakoshtha niches holding individual deities, a lion frieze runs along the base, and dvarapala, mithuna and yaksha occupy the sanctum entrance. Hoysala walls extend the logic into continuous bands of elephants, lions, scrolls, horses, makaras and swans.

Placement, not iconography, encodes the reading system.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the placement-not-subject thesis stated first 2 + the northern vertical system with ratha panels and the Ganga-Yamuna threshold rule 3 + the southern horizontal system with devakoshtha, base frieze and sanctum-door figures 3 + the Hoysala band extension 1 + the closing formulation that placement encodes a reading system 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 14:** One register of that programme has attracted more attention than everything else put together, and has produced this topic's most durable factual error. It needs a lesson of its own - not to dwell on it, but to size it correctly and to say honestly what can and cannot be concluded from it.

## Lesson 14 - The erotic register: proportion, parallel and interpretation

Progress: 14/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: what the evidence records about maithuna reliefs, the regional parallels that refute exclusivity, the three prohibitions, and the discipline of reporting competing interpretations

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Khajuraho artwork passage, the Odisha parallel and the list of temples noted for erotic sculpture
CA search: "Not required - interpretive-discipline lesson"
CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: three separate questions, three separate answers

```text
   QUESTION 1 - HOW MUCH OF IT IS THERE?  (proportion)
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | SOURCE SAYS: Hindu AND Jain temples "feature a VARIETY of      |
   | artwork, INCLUDING erotic element depicted through maithuna    |
   | reliefs".                                                      |
   | => maithuna is ONE ITEM WITHIN a variety.                      |
   | => NO percentage or fraction exists on the record at all.      |
   |    ANY figure you have seen quoted is unsourced. DO NOT USE IT.|
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+

   QUESTION 2 - IS IT PECULIAR TO KHAJURAHO?  (parallel)
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | SOURCE SAYS: "LIKE KHAJURAHO TEMPLES, the erotic element is    |
   | ALSO PROMINENT in the temples of KONARK and BHUBANESWAR."      |
   | And: Konark's chariot walls carry "erotic MITHUNA scenes".     |
   | And: MITHUNA figures stand at the GARBHAGRIHA ENTRANCE in the  |
   |      DRAVIDA description - a different family entirely.        |
   | And: a standard list of TEMPLES FAMOUS FOR EROTIC SCULPTURE    |
   |      across MP, Odisha, Karnataka, Gujarat, TN, Rajasthan,     |
   |      Maharashtra and Uttarakhand.                              |
   | => Khajuraho's erotic imagery is a REGIONAL PARALLEL,          |
   |    NOT a Chandella invention.                                  |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+

   QUESTION 3 - WHAT DOES IT MEAN?  (interpretation)
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | Competing readings exist: Tantric, ritual, symbolic, didactic, |
   | or simply part of a full depiction of worldly life.            |
   | THE EVIDENCE BASE ENDORSES NONE OF THEM.                       |
   | => STATE that interpretations differ. Do NOT assert one as     |
   |    proven. Do NOT invent a textual authority for any of them.  |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
```

*Three questions, three different kinds of answer: a quantity you must refuse to state, a comparison you must state, and a meaning you must leave open.*

### 1. Why this needs its own lesson

Not because it is large - the whole point is that it is not - but because it is the single most reliable source of error in the topic, and because a Chandella-sculpture demand makes handling it correctly worth marks. A candidate asked to elucidate breadth of life who spends a third of the answer on maithuna reliefs has both misread the question and, paradoxically, narrowed the very breadth they were asked to demonstrate.

There is also an intellectual reason. This register is the topic's best training in a general skill: separating *how much there is*, *whether it is distinctive* and *what it means* into three independent questions with three different evidentiary standards. Candidates who fuse them produce confident sentences that are wrong in three ways at once.

### 2. Question one - proportion

✅ **Fact.** **Both the Hindu and Jain temples in Khajuraho feature a variety of artwork, including erotic element depicted through maithuna reliefs on the temple walls.**

Read the grammar. "A variety of artwork, **including** erotic element" places maithuna as one member of a set. The same passage names the other members - deities, worshippers, dancers, musicians, teachers and disciples, courtly life, Vyalas - and the site's dedications supply five more categories of sacred subject.

❌ **The prohibition.** Do not state any percentage or fraction of Khajuraho sculpture that is erotic. No record supplies one, so any figure would be fabricated. This matters more than it may seem: quoted fractions circulate widely, sound authoritative, and are exactly the kind of unsourced number that an examiner or a hostile reader can neither verify nor ignore.

⚠️ **What to write instead.** "The erotic register is one element within a varied programme that also includes deities, worshippers, performers, teachers, courtly figures and mythical animals, and it appears in both the Hindu and the Jain temples of the site." That sentence is fully supported, does the analytical work, and commits to no number.

### 3. Question two - parallel

✅ **Fact.** **"Like Khajuraho temples, the erotic element is also prominent in the temples of Konark and Bhubaneswar."**

✅ **Fact.** Konark's walls are **adorned with marvellous sculptures, some of which depict erotic mithuna scenes**.

✅ **Fact.** In the Dravida description, the entrance of the garbhagriha carried sculptures of **dvarapala, mithuna and yaksha** - so auspicious-couple imagery occupies a *prescribed threshold position* in the southern tradition as well.

✅ **Fact.** A standard list of **temples in India famous for erotic sculptures** names Khajuraho in Madhya Pradesh; the Sun Temple at Konark; the Virupaksha Temple in Karnataka; the Sun Temple at Modhera, Gujarat; Sathyamurthi Perumal Temple in Tamil Nadu; Lingaraj Temple, Odisha; Rajarani Temple, Odisha; the Jain Temple at Ranakpur, Rajasthan; Markandeshwar in Maharashtra; Tirupantaka Temple in Karnataka; the Kailasa temple at Ellora; and Nanda Devi Temple, Almora.

⚠️ **The argument this licenses.** Khajuraho's erotic imagery is a **regional and pan-regional parallel**, not a Chandella invention. Twelve named sites across at least eight states, spanning Nagara and Dravida families, Hindu and Jain dedications, and structural and rock-cut construction, make the exclusivity claim untenable on the record itself.

⚠️ **A second-order point worth one line.** The Solanki school's temple walls are recorded as *devoid of carvings*, yet the Modhera Sun Temple appears on that list - which is a reminder that a school-level generalisation and a monument-level observation can both be true and must be reported separately rather than reconciled by guesswork.

❌ **The trap in its usual form.** "Khajuraho is famous for its erotic temples" treats the site as defined by one motif. The recorded breakdown - six Shaiva, eight Vaishnava, one Ganesha, one Surya and four Jain temples, with sculpture spanning deities, secular life, Vyalas and Tantric imagery - refutes any single-motif reduction.

### 4. Question three - interpretation

⚠️ **The honest position.** Competing readings of the erotic register exist: Tantric, ritual, symbolic, didactic, or simply part of a full depiction of worldly life alongside the sacred. These are *interpretations*. No account used here endorses any of them.

⚠️ **What an answer should therefore do.** State that interpretations differ; give one or two of them in a single clause; and decline to adjudicate. ❌ Do not assert any one reading as proven, and ❌ do not invent a textual authority - a named treatise, a scriptural sanction, a doctrinal school - for any of them. Inventing an authority is worse than leaving the question open, because it converts an honest gap into a false citation.

⚠️ **One contextual observation that is defensible.** The programme places the intimate register alongside worship, teaching, performance and courtly life on the same continuous wall. Whatever its intended meaning, its *placement* treats it as part of a depicted world rather than as a segregated category. That is an architectural observation about placement, which this topic owns, rather than a doctrinal claim, which it does not.

❌ **Boundary, stated plainly.** The deeper doctrinal reading of Tantric and Shakta traditions, and any gender-studies or normative argument about sexuality and sacred space, belongs to Society, Philosophy and Topic 13. This session presents the sculptural programme as art-historical evidence, not as a doctrinal or social-theory argument.

### Comparative note: what the Chausath-yogini adds, and what it does not

The site's earliest building is a Yogini shrine reflecting Tantric and Shaiva traditions, which establishes that Shakta and Tantric worship is present at Khajuraho from the beginning. It is tempting to run that straight into an explanation of the maithuna reliefs - Tantra is present, therefore the erotic sculpture is Tantric.

⚠️ **Resist it.** The Chausath-yogini is an early, granite, open-to-sky structure architecturally unlike anything else on the site, and the developed sandstone temples with the maithuna reliefs are a later group of Shaiva, Vaishnava and Jain dedication. Co-presence at one site over three centuries is not a causal link. The defensible statement is that Khajuraho's religious landscape includes Shakta and Tantric elements from its earliest structure onward, and that the interpretation of the erotic register remains contested.

### Examination use

Three uses, all direct. In the 2025 Chandella demand, the erotic register gets one clause inside the breadth paragraph and the *parallel* is what demonstrates comparative fluency - naming Konark and Bhubaneswar in a 150-word answer is a disproportionately high-value move. In any "temple sculpture as social evidence" demand, this register supplies the register that shows the *limit* of the inference, developed in Lesson 19. And in objective questions, the examinable facts are the parallel sites and the presence of the register in both Hindu and Jain temples at Khajuraho - never a proportion, which cannot be asked because it is not recorded.

### Revision notes

1. It is recorded that both the Hindu and Jain temples at Khajuraho feature a variety of artwork, *including* erotic element depicted through maithuna reliefs.
2. Maithuna is one item within a variety; never state a percentage or fraction, because none exists on the record.
3. "Like Khajuraho temples, the erotic element is also prominent in the temples of Konark and Bhubaneswar."
4. Konark's chariot walls carry erotic mithuna scenes.
5. Mithuna figures occupy a prescribed position at the garbhagriha entrance in the Dravida tradition, alongside dvarapala and yaksha.
6. A standard list names twelve Indian temples famous for erotic sculptures, including Khajuraho, Konark, Virupaksha in Karnataka, Modhera, Sathyamurthi Perumal in Tamil Nadu, Lingaraj and Rajarani in Odisha, the Jain temple at Ranakpur, Markandeshwar in Maharashtra, Tirupantaka in Karnataka, Kailasa at Ellora and Nanda Devi at Almora.
7. The register is therefore a regional and pan-regional parallel, not a Chandella invention.
8. Solanki walls are recorded as devoid of carvings while Modhera appears on the erotic-sculpture list - report school-level and monument-level statements separately.
9. Interpretations - Tantric, ritual, symbolic, didactic, or part of a full depiction of worldly life - differ; state that they differ and adjudicate none.
10. Never invent a textual authority for any interpretation.
11. Defensible observation: the register's *placement* alongside worship, teaching, performance and courtly life treats it as part of a depicted world - an architectural point, not a doctrinal one.
12. Doctrinal readings of Tantra and normative arguments about sexuality and sacred space belong to Topic 13 and to Society.
13. The Chausath-yogini establishes early Shakta and Tantric presence at the site but does not by itself explain the later maithuna reliefs; co-presence is not causation.
14. The single-motif reduction is refuted by the site's own dedication breakdown across three sects and five dedications.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "About ten per cent of Khajuraho's sculpture is erotic, and it is unique to the Chandellas, reflecting the influence of Tantric cults." Identify the three separate failures, and rewrite the sentence so that everything defensible survives.

**Model answer:** The first failure is a fabricated quantity: no record states any proportion of Khajuraho sculpture as erotic, so the figure is invented regardless of how often it is repeated. The second is a false exclusivity claim: it is recorded that, like the Khajuraho temples, the erotic element is also prominent in the temples of Konark and Bhubaneswar, records erotic mithuna scenes on Konark's chariot walls, places mithuna figures at the garbhagriha entrance in the Dravida tradition, and lists a dozen temples across many states as famous for erotic sculpture. The third is an unsupported interpretation asserted as fact: Tantric, ritual, symbolic, didactic and worldly-life readings all circulate, and none is established. A defensible rewrite: "Maithuna reliefs are one element within a varied programme at Khajuraho, present in both the Hindu and the Jain temples, and the register is a regional parallel - it is also prominent at Konark and Bhubaneswar - rather than a Chandella invention; its meaning remains interpretively contested."

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that a widely repeated figure or explanation is therefore sourced. Familiarity is not provenance; in this register in particular, the most confident-sounding sentences are usually the least supportable, and the disciplined answer gains marks precisely by declining to supply them.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Assess how the erotic sculpture of Khajuraho should be treated in an examination answer on Chandella art. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

It should be treated as one register within a varied programme, sized correctly, compared, and left interpretively open.

Proportion first. Both the Hindu and Jain temples are recorded as featuring a variety of artwork including erotic element depicted through maithuna reliefs, so it is one item within a set that also contains deities, worshippers, dancers, musicians, teachers and disciples, courtly life and Vyalas. No record supplies a proportion, so none may be stated.

Comparison second. The same evidence states that, like Khajuraho, the erotic element is prominent at Konark and Bhubaneswar, records erotic mithuna scenes at Konark, and places mithuna figures at the sanctum entrance in Dravida practice. The register is a regional parallel, not a Chandella invention.

Interpretation last. Tantric, ritual, symbolic and worldly-life readings compete and none is established, so an answer should report the disagreement rather than resolve it.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the three-question separation of proportion, parallel and interpretation 3 + the "variety including" formulation with named co-registers 3 + Konark and Bhubaneswar cited as the parallel 2 + explicit refusal of an unsourced proportion 1 + interpretive openness stated rather than adjudicated 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 15:** The Chandella case is complete. The topic now turns south, where an entirely different family solved the same problems - and where the examiner has asked, in two separate years, what two southern dynasties contributed. The first of them converted rock into architecture within three centuries.

## Lesson 15 - Pallava: converting rock into architecture

Progress: 15/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the four-stage Pallava sequence, the Mahabalipuram monuments, the shift from rock-cut to structural building, the capital at Kanchipuram, and the limits to state when the directive is "estimate"

### Visual: four styles, one technical revolution

```text

   with NO TIMBER,    |Arjuna: DVITALA |     (Shiva + Uma
   NO METAL,          |  vimana +      |      + Skanda)

        |             |Bhima: rectangl.|     on ANANTA
   Mamandur cave      |  WAGON-VAULTED |
   temples, near      |Dharmaraja:     |   Ganesh Ratha

   carry his titles   |Nakula-Sahadeva:|   built with a Shiva

                      "indicates the

                       architect"

  ALSO AT MAHABALIPURAM: Varaha mandapa; Mahisasurmardini mandapa;
  the gigantic OPEN-AIR RELIEF across two boulders, read EITHER as the
  DESCENT OF THE GANGA OR as ARJUNA'S PENANCE; and nearby the boulder
  locally called KRISHNA'S BUTTER BALL.

  STATUS: Marco Polo's "SEVEN PAGODAS", of which the Shore Temple
  survives;  UNESCO World Heritage Site 1984, "Group of Monuments
  at Mahabalipuram".
```

*Read the top row as one sentence: the same dynasty moved from cutting into rock, to carving rock free, to building with cut rock, in roughly three centuries.*

### 1. The problem the Pallavas solved

Rock-cut architecture is subtractive. You begin with a cliff and remove everything that is not the temple. It is durable and needs no foundation, but it is confined to where suitable rock exists, it cannot be extended, and its scale is limited by the outcrop.

Structural architecture is additive. You quarry, dress, transport and assemble blocks. It can be built anywhere, extended indefinitely, and scaled to ambition - but it requires an entirely different set of skills: jointing, load calculation, corbelling, and the organisation of transport and labour.

⚠️ **The Pallava contribution, framed correctly.** It is best estimated as a **transition** rather than as a style: within roughly three centuries the same dynasty moved from excavated rock-cut mandapas to monolithic rathas to structural stone temples. That framing is what the directive "estimate" rewards, because it identifies a *change* and its mechanism rather than listing monuments.

### 2. The four stages, exactly as recorded

✅ **Fact.** **Temple architecture in South India began under the Pallava ruler Mahendravarman I (610 AD-630 AD).** The temples developed during the Pallava dynasty reflected the stylistic taste of the individual rulers and can be classified into **four stages chronologically**. The prominent archaeologist **A. H. Longhurst** described Pallava architecture, including that found at Mahabalipuram, into four chronological styles: **Mahendravarman style (610-640)**, **Mamalla style (640-670, under Narasimhavarman I)**, **Rajasimha style (674-800)** and **Nandivarman style (800-900)**.

✅ **Fact - stage one.** Under the initial Mahendravarman style, rock-cut temples featured **pillared halls or mandapas with a garbhagriha or sanctum sanctorum at the end**.

✅ **Fact - stage two.** Major development was noticed during the reign of **Narasimhavarman I**: mandapas were now divided into **separate rathas or rock-cut monolithic temples**, such as the **Pancha Rathas**, a group of five temples in one premises. The Pancha Ratha premises has five temples **carved out of a single rock with a choice of all known forms of plan and elevation**, each named after the legendary **Pandavas and their wife Draupadi**. The **Draupadi ratha** is a simple **hut-like** structure; the **Arjuna ratha** is a **dvitala vimana with a mukhamandapa**; the **Bhima ratha** is **rectangular on plan with a wagon-vaulted roof**; the **Dharmaraja ratha** is a **tritala vimana**; and the **Nakula-Sahadeva ratha** has an **apsidal plan and elevation**, "which indicates the **experimental tendency of the architect**".

✅ **Fact - stage three.** The **Shore Temple complex** at Mamallapuram, overlooking the Bay of Bengal, is **assigned to the reign of the later Pallava king Rajasimha of the 8th century AD**. **"During his reign, the rock-cut technique of temple was replaced by structural temples."** The complex has three shrine areas containing a **stone Shiva linga**, a **Somaskanda** sculpture - Shiva with Uma and Skanda - and **Vishnu depicted resting on the serpent Ananta**.

⚠️ **The single most important sentence in the lesson** is the one quoted in stage three. It is the recorded moment at which southern temple building changes technique, and it is what makes a "contribution" answer arguable rather than descriptive.

### 3. The rest of the Mahabalipuram evidence

✅ **Fact - the town.** Mahabalipuram or Mamallapuram stands on the **Coromandel coast, about 60 km south of Chennai** in Tamil Nadu. ⚠️ The standard account prints "south-west"; that is a direction slip, since a Coromandel-coast town cannot lie inland of the city it is measured from. The town was **named after the 7th-century Pallava king Narasimhavarman I, who was given the title Mamalla, meaning a great hero**. A prominent feature of the majority of its monuments is that they are **monolithic rock-cut temples, carved from a single rock**.

✅ **Fact - further monuments.** The **Ganesh Ratha**, another monolithic rock-cut temple of the **late seventh century**, was initially constructed with a **Shiva linga** but is **now deified with Ganesha after the linga was removed**. Other notable rock-cut cave temples are the **Varaha mandapa** and the **Mahisasurmardini mandapa**.

✅ **Fact - the great relief, and a recorded interpretive dispute.** There is a famous **gigantic open-air relief carved across two boulders**, with many figures including people and elephants in life-size dimensions. **The scene is interpreted by historians in two different ways - one as the descent of the Ganga and the other as Arjuna's penance.** In its proximity lies a big rock boulder resting on a short incline, locally known as **Krishna's Butter Ball**.

⚠️ **How to use the dispute.** Name both readings and attribute the disagreement to historians. This is a small but genuine opportunity: the record itself carries an unresolved interpretation, so reporting it accurately costs one clause and demonstrates that the candidate distinguishes established description from contested reading. That is the same skill Lesson 14 required for the erotic register, and examiners reward its consistency.

✅ **Fact - the dynasty and capital.** The Pallava dynasty ruled over parts of southern India, particularly Tamil Nadu, during the **4th to 9th centuries AD**, with their capital at **Kanchipuram**. Under the Pallavas, Mahabalipuram flourished especially during the **7th-8th century AD**.

✅ **Fact - other Pallava sites.** The **Mandagapattu cave temple, Villupuram**, under Mahendravarman I (610-630 AD), is the earliest Pallava rock-cut work, its inscription stressing a temple made with **no timber, no metal and no brick**. The **Mamandur cave temples near Kanchipuram** are a series of four cave temples carrying Mahendravarman I's titles, including **Vichitrachitta** and **Satrumalla**.

⚠️ **The Mandagapattu inscription is worth memorising in substance.** A ruler who records that his temple used no timber, no metal and no brick is telling you what temples were normally made of, and announcing a change of medium. It is the clearest single piece of evidence in the topic for a deliberate technical transition, and it is an *inscription*, which is the strongest class of evidence available.

✅ **Fact - external notice and status.** **Marco Polo** referred to the port town as the **"Seven Pagodas"**, of which the Shore Temple survives; **Niccolao Manucci**, a Venetian traveller who never visited the site but saw the monuments from a distance and heard about them, conflated Chinese and Burmese Buddhist pagoda designs with the Hindu temples and assumed these temples were built by the Chinese. **In 1984, UNESCO gave the town World Heritage status under the name "Group of Monuments at Mahabalipuram".**

### 4. Kanchipuram, and the two things that belong to other topics

✅ **Fact.** In the **Avantisundari Katha**, the **7th-8th century Sanskrit scholar Dandin**, who lived in Tamil Nadu and was associated with the Pallava court, **praised artists for their repair of a Vishnu sculpture at Mamallapuram**.

⚠️ **Why this single citation is unusually efficient.** It evidences literary patronage at the Pallava court *and* contemporary art practice at Mamallapuram in one sentence - a text about sculpture, written near the court that commissioned it. Literature as such belongs to Topic 11, so use this as an architectural-practice citation and leave Dandin's literary standing to that topic.

✅ **Fact, routed.** The **fragments at the Kailasanatha Temple, Kanchipuram**, along with the remnants at the **Talagishwara (Shiva) temple at Panamalai**, are significant as **the only two surviving examples of Pallava mural paintings**. ⚠️ This is a statement about **survival**, not about how much was originally painted, and mural painting belongs to Topic 07. Cite it once, correctly hedged, as evidence that the Pallava programme included painting.

### 5. The limits to state when the directive is "estimate"

⚠️ Three limits keep an estimation honest, and the directive specifically rewards them.

- **Mural survival is minimal** - two surviving examples is a fact about preservation, and cannot support a claim about the scale of Pallava painting.
- **Longhurst's four-style scheme is a modern classification**, not a Pallava self-description. Attribute it - "A. H. Longhurst classified..." - rather than presenting it as how the Pallavas saw themselves.
- **The trajectory did not end with the Pallavas.** The later Cholas, **continuing the Pallavas' legacy of temple construction**, culminated it by constructing a large number of magnificent temples across the empire. The Pallava contribution is therefore best framed as **initiating a trajectory** rather than completing one.

### Comparative note: two ways of beginning

Set the Pallava beginning against the Chandella one and the difference in *kind* becomes clear. The Chandellas begin with a political moment and build, within a mature regional tradition, at the highest level that tradition had reached. The Pallavas begin with a **technique problem** and solve it by experiment over three reigns - the Pancha Rathas explicitly using "all known forms of plan and elevation" in one premises, which is itself read as the architect's experimental tendency. One is a culmination; the other is a laboratory. An answer that names that difference is doing comparative work rather than parallel description.

### Examination use

The verified 2024 demand asks you to *estimate* the Pallava contribution to the art and literature of South India. "Estimate" means weigh: name the innovations, show the sequence, and state the limits. The architecture half is this lesson - Mahendravarman's rock-cut beginning, the Mamalla monolithic rathas, the Rajasimha structural shift at the Shore Temple, with Mandagapattu's inscription as the technical evidence and the 1984 inscription as the closing status line. The literature half belongs jointly with Topic 11, and the Dandin citation is the bridge. Note the ownership position honestly: the routing ledger assigns that question to an Ancient History owner while this topic's own owner carries it as a direct architectural demand; both claims are recorded in the final PYQ section.

### Revision notes

1. Temple architecture in South India began under the Pallava ruler Mahendravarman I, 610-630 AD.
2. A. H. Longhurst classified Pallava architecture into four chronological styles: Mahendravarman (610-640), Mamalla (640-670, under Narasimhavarman I), Rajasimha (674-800), Nandivarman (800-900).
3. Mahendravarman style: rock-cut temples with pillared mandapas and a garbhagriha at the end.
4. Mamalla style: mandapas divided into separate monolithic rathas, notably the Pancha Rathas - five temples carved from a single rock, named after the Pandavas and Draupadi.
5. The five rathas: Draupadi, hut-like; Arjuna, dvitala vimana with mukhamandapa; Bhima, rectangular with a wagon-vaulted roof; Dharmaraja, tritala vimana; Nakula-Sahadeva, apsidal - showing the architect's experimental tendency.
6. Rajasimha style: the Shore Temple complex, 8th century, overlooking the Bay of Bengal; during his reign the rock-cut technique was replaced by structural temples; three shrines with a Shiva linga, a Somaskanda sculpture, and Vishnu resting on Ananta.
7. Mahabalipuram: Coromandel coast, about 60 km south of Chennai - the printed "south-west" is a disclosed direction slip; named after Narasimhavarman I, titled Mamalla, "a great hero"; most monuments are monolithic rock-cut temples.
8. Ganesh Ratha: late 7th century, built with a Shiva linga, now deified with Ganesha. Varaha and Mahisasurmardini mandapas are other rock-cut cave temples.
9. The gigantic open-air relief across two boulders is interpreted by historians either as the descent of the Ganga or as Arjuna's penance; Krishna's Butter Ball lies nearby.
10. Mandagapattu cave temple, Villupuram, Mahendravarman I: the earliest Pallava rock-cut work, its inscription stressing no timber, no metal, no brick.
11. Mamandur cave temples near Kanchipuram carry Mahendravarman I's titles, including Vichitrachitta and Satrumalla.
12. Pallava capital: Kanchipuram; the dynasty ruled parts of southern India in the 4th-9th centuries AD.
13. Dandin, 7th-8th century Sanskrit scholar associated with the Pallava court, praised artists in the Avantisundari Katha for repairing a Vishnu sculpture at Mamallapuram.
14. Kailasanatha, Kanchipuram, and Panamalai preserve the only two surviving examples of Pallava mural painting - a survival statement, and Topic 07's material.
15. Marco Polo's "Seven Pagodas", of which the Shore Temple survives; Manucci, who never visited, assumed Chinese builders; UNESCO 1984, "Group of Monuments at Mahabalipuram".
16. Limits for an "estimate": minimal mural survival; Longhurst's scheme is a modern classification; and the Cholas continued the Pallavas' legacy, so the contribution initiates rather than completes.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Shore Temple is a more analytically valuable monument than the Pancha Rathas in an answer that must *estimate* the Pallava contribution, and identify the inscriptional evidence that supports the same argument.

**Model answer:** The Pancha Rathas demonstrate range - five monolithic temples carved from a single rock, using all known forms of plan and elevation, from Draupadi's hut-like form to Dharmaraja's tritala vimana and Nakula-Sahadeva's apsidal plan - read as the architect's experimental tendency. But they remain subtractive: brilliant work within the rock-cut method. The Shore Temple, assigned to Rajasimha in the eighth century, marks a change of method, because during his reign the rock-cut technique of temple building was replaced by structural temples. That is a contribution in the strict sense - a capability that did not previously exist and that everything afterwards depends on, since structural building can be sited anywhere, extended and scaled. The supporting inscriptional evidence is at Mandagapattu, Villupuram, under Mahendravarman I, whose inscription stresses a temple made with no timber, no metal and no brick: a ruler announcing a deliberate change of medium, recorded in stone at the beginning of the sequence.

**Misconception to avoid:** Equating fame with analytical value. The Pancha Rathas are the more famous monuments and the better illustration of variety, but an estimation question rewards the monument that marks a change of capability, and that is the Shore Temple.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Explain why a documented change of building technique is stronger historical evidence than a change of decorative style, illustrating your answer from the Pallava sequence. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

Ornament changes with taste and can be renewed, imitated or misdated; technique changes only when a new capability is acquired, and it leaves consequences that cannot be reversed.

The Pallava sequence demonstrates this. At Mandagapattu, Villupuram, Mahendravarman I's inscription stresses a temple made with no timber, no metal and no brick - a ruler announcing a deliberate change of medium, recorded in stone. Under Narasimhavarman I the method was tested rather than decorated: the Pancha Rathas, cut from a single rock, deploy hut-like, dvitala, wagon-vaulted, tritala and apsidal forms in one premises. Under Rajasimha the rock-cut technique was replaced by structural temples, as at the Shore Temple.

The consequence is irreversible. Structural building can be sited anywhere, extended and scaled, which every later southern development presupposes.

Decoration would have told us about preference; technique tells us what became possible.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the capability-versus-preference distinction argued at the start 3 + the Mandagapattu inscription as a recorded announcement of a medium change 2 + the experimental middle stage with named ratha forms 2 + the structural shift at the Shore Temple 2 + the irreversibility point as close 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 16:** The Pallavas made structural building possible. The dynasty that inherited it built the largest granite temple in the world, endowed it, painted it, inscribed its administration on its walls, and left it functioning - which is why the examiner asked, in 2024, why they are still remembered with pride.

## Lesson 16 - Chola: phases, the granite climax, and the documented temple

### Visual: four phases, and three achievements that survive together

```text
  PHASE 1  mid-9th c.  NARTTAMALAI Shiva temple, built by VIJAYALAYA
           vimana joined by ARDHA-MANDAPA; main shrine surrounded by
           SIX subsidiary shrines = PARIVARALAYAS; DVARAPALAS flank

              |
  PHASE 2  late 9th / early 10th c.  ADITYA I and PARANTAKA I
           BRAHMAPURESHVARA · NAGESHVARASWAMI · KORANGANATHA
           ANTARALA and MUKHAMANDAPA added; pilasters divide the
           outer walls into DEVAKOSHTHA niches holding deity images
              |
  PHASE 3  10th c.  Queen SHEMBIYAN MAHADEVI, patron during the reign
           of her husband GANDARADITYA and her son UTTAMA I
           -> many BRICK temples REBUILT IN STONE
              |
  PHASE 4  early 11th c.  THE CLIMAX
           BRIHADISHVARA, THANJAVUR, by RAJARAJA I
           (also Rajarajeshvara; also called PERIYA KOVIL and DAKSHINA MERU)

           - pillared porch | mukhamandapa | ardhamandapa | antarala |
             garbhagriha
           - profuse outer-wall ornament; huge rectangular enclosure
             with large gopurams
           - big NANDI MANDAP with the country's SECOND-LARGEST
             monolithic Nandi (largest: Lepakshi Veerbhadra, AP)
           - "THE FIRST FULLY GRANITE TEMPLE IN THE WORLD"
              |
  THEN     RAJENDRA I: Brihadishvara at GANGAIKONDACHOLAPURAM
           - CANNOT be compared with Thanjavur: it was INCOMPLETE,
             has a LOWER vimana, shikhara CURVED INWARDS,
             walls MORE HEAVILY embellished with sculptures
              |
  LAST     12th-13th c.  GOPURA BECOMES MORE DOMINANT THAN VIMANA
  PHASE    CHIDAMBARAM, under KULOTTUNGA I
              |
  ALSO     12th c.  AIRAVATESVARA, DARASURAM, by RAJARAJA II,
           named after Indra's white elephant; displays Vaishnava and
           Shakta traditions with Shaiva, and the legends of the
```
