```text
           elegant stone chariots drawn by horses

  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
  | WHY THEY ARE "REMEMBERED WITH PRIDE" - three things survive   |
  | TOGETHER, and a fourth makes them unusual:                    |
  |   1. MONUMENTAL stone building (Thanjavur)                    |
  |   2. PORTABLE bronze icons (Nataraja; Topic 06)               |
  |   3. A DOCUMENTARY record (inscriptions on the buildings)     |
  |   4. and they are STILL IN USE - "GREAT LIVING CHOLA TEMPLES" |
  |      Thanjavur 1987; Gangaikondacholapuram and Darasuram      |
  |      added as extensions in 2004                              |
  +--------------------------------------------------------------+
```

*The fourth box is the answer to the 2024 wording. "Remembered with pride" is explained by survival in use, not merely by quality.*

### 1. The problem: why "magnificent" is not an answer

Chola achievement is usually met as a list of superlatives, and an examiner asking why this dynasty is *remembered* rather than merely admired will not accept them. A claim about memory has to be explained, and adjectives cannot do it. What can is the observation that three different kinds of achievement survive together, and that the buildings are still functioning.

### 2. The frame and the features

✅ **Fact.** In the **9th century AD** the Chola Empire emerged as the dominant power in the south, and their core area of control became **Cholamandalam** - the area around **Thanjavur** and up to the eastern coast, **from which the Portuguese derived the name Coromandel**. **Continuing the Pallavas' legacy of temple construction, these later Cholas culminated it by constructing a large number of magnificent temples across the empire.** While Pallava temples were mostly located in and around Kanchipuram, the Chola temples were concentrated further south, around **Tanjore or Thanjavur**.

The full Dravida feature list from Lesson 6 is conventionally presented as the "features of Chola or Dravidian style temple architecture" - high boundary walls, gopuram, panchayatana layout, the linearly rising stepped vimana, the octagonal crowning shikhara, one vimana only, the antarala, dvarapala-mithuna-yaksha at the sanctum entrance, the internal water tank, and **the frieze of lions along the base as a typical feature of Chola temples**. That last item is the one Chola-specific entry in the list and should be named as such.

### 3. The four phases, with what each adds

✅ **Phase one.** The earliest phase is represented by a **Shiva temple at Narttamalai, built by Chola king Vijayalaya in the mid-9th century**. It consisted of a **vimana - the pyramidical tower above the sanctum - joined by an ardha-mandapa**; the main shrine is **surrounded by six subsidiary shrines known as parivaralayas**, and **dvarapalas flank the entrance**.

✅ **Phase two.** Temples built by **Aditya I and Parantaka I during the late 9th and early 10th century** - **Brahmapureshvara, Nageshvaraswami and Koranganatha**. To the vimana and ardhamandapa were subsequently added the **antarala or vestibule and the mukhamandapa or porch**, and **pilasters divide the outer walls into niches known as devakoshthas, which contain images of various deities**.

✅ **Phase three.** Associated with **Chola queen Shembiyan Mahadevi**, a major patron of temple architecture during the reign of **her husband Gandaraditya and her son Uttama I**, under whom **a large number of brick temples were rebuilt in stone**.

⚠️ **Why phase three is worth more than its length.** It shows Chola patronage was not exclusively male or exclusively royal-reigning: a queen patronised across two reigns. And "brick temples rebuilt in stone" is a material-upgrade programme, which is a *policy* rather than a single commission - evidence of sustained institutional investment. She is elsewhere recorded as a tenth-century Chola queen, a patron of the arts and one of the most powerful queens of the empire, who built many temples including at **Kutralam, Virudhachalam and Aduthurai**; that sculptural-patronage detail belongs to Topic 06 and is cited here only to confirm the scale of her programme.

✅ **Phase four - the climax.** The finest example of Chola temple architecture is the **Brihadishvara temple of Thanjavur, constructed in the early 11th century** by **Rajaraja I**, also known as **Rajarajeshvara temple**, and also called **Periya Kovil** and **Dakshina Meru**. This Shiva temple has a **huge vimana and a towering pyramidical shikhara**, and was one of the largest and most grand structures of its age; the vimana stands at **approximately 66 m, about 216 ft**. The main shrine consists of a **pillared porch, pillared mukhamandapa and ardhamandapa, an antarala, and the garbhagriha**. The **ornamentation of the outer walls is much more profuse**. The temple stands with a **huge rectangular enclosure and large gopurams**. In front of the temple is a **big Nandi Mandap with a massive monolithic image of Nandi, the second largest in the country** - **the largest Nandi monolith is at the Lepakshi Veerbhadra Temple in Andhra Pradesh**. **The entire temple structure is made out of granite, which makes it the first fully granite temple in the world.**

⚠️ **A disclosed measurement discrepancy, and the figure to use.** The standard textbook account prints the vimana as "60 m". The figure carried by the protecting authority and by most published measurements is **approximately 66 m, about 216 ft**, and the lower number is best explained as rounding or an older measurement, or as a measurement taken to a different point on the structure. ✅ This session uses **approximately 66 m** throughout and discloses the textbook's about-60 m approximation once, here. ❌ Do not quote a decimal height, and ❌ do not quote a precise consecration year: "early 11th century" is what is safely supported.

### 4. The successor, the late shift, and Darasuram

✅ **Fact.** **Rajaraja's son Rajendra I built a similar Shiva temple with the same name, Brihadishvara, in his new capital Gangaikondacholapuram**, after his successful expedition to the Ganges. **It cannot be compared with its counterpart in Thanjavur as it was incomplete, has a lower vimana and a shikhara curved inwards, though the walls are more heavily embellished with sculptures.**

⚠️ **Use the incompleteness.** A candidate who names Gangaikondacholapuram as a second triumph has not read the evidence. A candidate who names it as an **incomplete** successor with a lower vimana and more heavily sculpted walls has both a fact and an argument: imperial ambition outran execution, and the sculptural compensation is visible.

✅ **Fact.** **The last phase of Chola temple architecture belonged to the 12th-13th centuries, where gopura became more dominant than vimana.** The **Chidambaram temple**, dedicated to Shiva and constructed during the reign of **Kulottunga I**, is a famous example of this phase.

⚠️ **This is the hinge of the whole southern trajectory** and connects directly to Lesson 18. The Chola formula was not perpetuated; it was superseded by an emphasis the Cholas themselves initiated in their last phase.

✅ **Fact.** The **Airavatesvara Temple at Darasuram**, constructed in the **12th century by Rajaraja II** and **named after the white elephant of Indra**, is dedicated to Shiva and **also reverentially displays Vaishnavism and Shaktism traditions, along with the legends associated with the sixty-three Nayanars, the Bhakti movement saints of Shaivism**. One striking feature is the **Rajagambhira Thirumandapam, a royal courtyard with intricately carved pillars, long steps made of granite stones and elegant chariots drawn by horses**.

### 5. The documentary achievement, which is the strongest Chola card

✅ **Fact.** **Various inscriptions have been found in the Brihadishvara temple premises.** ✅ At Airavatesvara there is a **Tamil inscription which describes the temple, the deities, the methods of daily worship, donations, the festivals to be celebrated, and the way the temple needs to be administered**; this kind of elaborate inscribed guideline for administering a temple is **"rather unprecedented in the history"**. ✅ **King Rajaraja Chola even appointed an astronomer called "Prenkani" for announcing the dates, based on planetary movements, for celebrating the temple festivals.**

⚠️ **Why this outranks the architecture in an argument about memory.** A building tells you what was made. An administrative inscription tells you how it was *run* - worship schedules, donations, festivals, staffing. That converts the Chola temple from a monument into a documented institution, and institutions leave a deeper historical trace than masonry. The astronomer appointment is the single most vivid detail available: a salaried specialist employed to compute the ritual calendar.

### 6. Bronzes and painting, routed correctly

✅ **Fact, routed to Topic 06.** Chola temples placed exceptional importance on sculptural decoration. The **Nataraja** in the *tandava* posture is the key piece, whose earliest known example is at the **Ravana Phadi cave, Aihole**, under early Chalukya rule, and which reached its peak under the Cholas. ✅ Many South Indian Shiva temples have a separate **natana-sabha** where the Nataraja image is placed, and the standard comparative set is the **Chidambaram** bronze Nataraja, the **Thanjavur Brihadeeswara** Nataraja, **Pallava cave stone reliefs** and **Hoysala wall panels**.

✅ **Fact, routed to Topic 07.** The Brihadishvara **fresco paintings and miniature sculptures of scenes from the Puranas and epics, predominantly related to Shaivism but also to Vaishnavism and Shaktism traditions, display the inclusive character of the Chola kingdom.**

⚠️ **The architectural use of both.** Name them as *components of the temple programme* - a hall built for an icon, walls painted with narrative - and let the iconography and the painting technique belong to Topics 06 and 07. That is how a candidate gets credit for breadth without straying off-topic.

### 7. The living close, and the honest counterpoints

✅ **Fact.** Three Chola temples have been declared a UNESCO World Heritage site under the title **"Great Living Chola Temples"**. The **Brihadisvara Temple at Thanjavur was initially inscribed in 1987**; the other two, the **Brihadisvara Temple at Gangaikondacholapuram** and the **Airavatesvara Temple at Darasuram**, were **added as extensions to the site in 2004**.

❌ **A recorded trap.** This is **one serial World Heritage property comprising three temple complexes**, not three separate properties.

⚠️ **The word "living" is the best closing device this material offers.** These are functioning temples, which is precisely why Chola achievement remains present rather than merely excavated.

⚠️ **Counterpoints a "comment" directive requires.** Gangaikondacholapuram was left incomplete. The late shift from vimana to gopura shows the Chola formula being superseded rather than perpetuated. Monumental granite building and temple endowment presuppose the extraction of surplus and organised labour, which the art-historical record celebrates but does not describe. And the inclusive Shaiva-Vaishnava-Shakta iconography at Brihadishvara and Darasuram should be read as the temple's own claim to comprehensiveness, not as proof of social equality. ❌ Political and revenue history belongs to Ancient and Medieval History; do not import a dynastic narrative.

### Comparative note: Chandella and Chola on the same four dimensions

| Dimension | Chandella / Khajuraho | Chola / Thanjavur |
|---|---|---|
| Enclosure | None; high platform terrace | Huge rectangular enclosure with large gopurams |
| Fabric | Fine-grained sandstone, buff, pink and pale yellow | Entirely granite - the first fully granite temple in the world |
| Superstructure | Curvilinear shikhara massed with clustered urushringas | A single stepped pyramidal vimana, approximately 66 m, with a towering pyramidical shikhara |
| Documentary record | External travellers' notice - Al-Biruni, Ibn Battuta - and general attribution to Dhanga's reign | Inscriptions in the temple premises and an unprecedented Tamil administrative inscription at Darasuram |
| Status now | UNESCO 1986; Matangeshvara alone in active worship | One serial UNESCO property, 1987 with 2004 extensions; all three are living temples |

⚠️ The decisive difference for an examiner is the fourth row. Khajuraho must be argued from form, because its documentation is thin and its attributions general. Thanjavur can be argued from record, because the temple documents itself.

### Examination use

The verified 2024 demand is a "comment" on a claim about memory, and the structure that answers it is: three achievements that survive together, a phased development showing they were not a single stroke of luck, a named technical climax, the documentary record, the limits, and the living-temple close. The word "pride" in the question is answered by the word "living" in the property's own title. The same material supplies the Chola segment of any 20-mark southern-trajectory answer.

### Revision notes

1. The Chola Empire emerged as the dominant southern power in the 9th century AD; the core area was Cholamandalam around Thanjavur, from which the Portuguese derived "Coromandel".
2. The Cholas continued the Pallavas' legacy and culminated it with a large number of temples; Pallava temples cluster near Kanchipuram, Chola temples further south around Thanjavur.
3. A frieze of lions along the base is the typical Chola entry in the Dravida feature list.
4. Phase one: Narttamalai Shiva temple, Vijayalaya, mid-9th century - vimana joined by ardha-mandapa, six parivaralayas, dvarapalas at the entrance.
5. Phase two: Aditya I and Parantaka I, late 9th and early 10th century - Brahmapureshvara, Nageshvaraswami, Koranganatha; antarala and mukhamandapa added; pilasters form devakoshtha niches with deity images.
6. Phase three: Queen Shembiyan Mahadevi, patron during the reigns of her husband Gandaraditya and her son Uttama I - many brick temples rebuilt in stone.
7. Phase four: Brihadishvara, Thanjavur, Rajaraja I, early 11th century; also Rajarajeshvara, Periya Kovil, Dakshina Meru; vimana approximately 66 m, about 216 ft, with a towering pyramidical shikhara - the textbook figure of 60 m is a disclosed approximation; pillared porch, mukhamandapa, ardhamandapa, antarala, garbhagriha; profuse outer-wall ornament; huge rectangular enclosure with large gopurams.
8. Nandi Mandap with the country's second-largest monolithic Nandi; the largest is at Lepakshi Veerbhadra Temple, Andhra Pradesh.
9. Brihadishvara is entirely granite - the first fully granite temple in the world.
10. Gangaikondacholapuram, Rajendra I: incomplete, lower vimana, shikhara curved inwards, walls more heavily embellished - it cannot be compared with Thanjavur.
11. Last phase, 12th-13th centuries: the gopura becomes more dominant than the vimana - Chidambaram under Kulottunga I.
12. Airavatesvara, Darasuram, Rajaraja II, 12th century, named after Indra's white elephant; displays Vaishnava and Shakta traditions with Shaiva, and the legends of the sixty-three Nayanars; Rajagambhira Thirumandapam royal courtyard with carved pillars, granite steps and horse-drawn stone chariots.
13. Inscriptions in the Brihadishvara premises; the Darasuram Tamil inscription records deities, daily worship, donations, festivals and administration - "rather unprecedented in the history"; Rajaraja appointed an astronomer, "Prenkani", to announce festival dates from planetary movements.
14. Nataraja in tandava: earliest known example at the Ravana Phadi cave, Aihole, under the early Chalukyas; peak under the Cholas; natana-sabha halls; comparative set at Chidambaram, Thanjavur, Pallava cave reliefs and Hoysala panels - iconography belongs to Topic 06.
15. Brihadishvara frescoes show Shaiva, Vaishnava and Shakta subjects - painting belongs to Topic 07.
16. "Great Living Chola Temples": one serial UNESCO property - Thanjavur inscribed 1987, Gangaikondacholapuram and Darasuram added as extensions in 2004.
17. Counterpoints: the incomplete successor, the formula superseded by the gopura, the unstated labour and surplus, and inclusive iconography as the temple's own claim rather than social proof.
18. Never quote a decimal vimana height or a precise consecration year.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why an account of Chola achievement built only on the scale of Brihadishvara would be incomplete, and identify the two further achievements and the one condition that together explain why the Chola record has stayed continuously visible.

**Model answer:** Scale alone explains admiration, not memory: many large monuments are admired and few are continuously remembered. The two further achievements are portable and documentary. Chola bronze sculpture, above all the Nataraja in tandava, reached its peak under the Cholas and travelled as icons independent of any single building; and the temples document themselves, with inscriptions in the Brihadishvara premises and a Tamil inscription at Airavatesvara recording the deities, the methods of daily worship, donations, the festivals to be celebrated and the way the temple was to be administered - guidelines described as rather unprecedented, with Rajaraja even appointing an astronomer, Prenkani, to announce festival dates from planetary movements. The condition is that these are the Great Living Chola Temples: Thanjavur inscribed in 1987 with Gangaikondacholapuram and Darasuram added as extensions in 2004, all still functioning places of worship. Monumental, portable and documented achievement, still in use, is what sustains a memory rather than a reputation.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the three inscribed temples as three separate World Heritage properties. It is one serial property comprising three temple complexes, with an initial 1987 inscription and a 2004 extension - a recorded distinction that objective questions exploit.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Assess whether Chola temple building is better described as a sustained dynastic programme or as a sequence of individual royal commissions. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model answer

The evidence favours a sustained programme, with two qualifications that keep the judgement honest.

Three features point to programme rather than commission. First, continuity of form across reigns: Vijayalaya's mid-ninth-century Narttamalai shrine, with its vimana joined by an ardha-mandapa and six encircling parivaralayas, is recognisably the same enterprise as the temples of Aditya I and Parantaka I, which add the antarala and mukhamandapa and divide outer walls into devakoshtha niches. Each stage inherits and extends rather than restarts.

Second, patronage that outlives a single ruler. Queen Shembiyan Mahadevi patronised across the reigns of her husband Gandaraditya and her son Uttama I, and under her many brick temples were rebuilt in stone - a conversion policy applied to a class of buildings, not a gift of one.

Third, standing arrangements for operation. The Airavatesvara inscription prescribes deities, daily worship, donations, festivals and administration, and Rajaraja appointed an astronomer, Prenkani, to fix festival dates: temples designed to be run, not merely raised.

Two qualifications. Rajendra I's Gangaikondacholapuram was left incomplete, which a fully institutionalised programme would be expected to finish; and the record celebrates results while saying nothing about quarrying, transport, labour or revenue, so continuity of form is not by itself proof of continuity of administration.

The balanced verdict is a programme in form, patronage and operation, evidenced unevenly - strongest where inscriptions survive and weakest where only fabric does.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the programme-versus-commission judgement stated with its qualifications built in 3 + continuity of form across three named phases 4 + cross-reign patronage evidenced by a named queen and a conversion policy 3 + standing operational arrangements 3 + the incompleteness and silent-labour qualifications 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 17:** Between the Nagara north and the Dravida south lies the region that refused to choose. Its dynasties produced a cave complex with a dated inscription, a temple carved rather than built, a star-shaped plan in soapstone, and a temple named after its architect - and all four are needed before Vijayanagara can be explained.

## Lesson 17 - The Deccan laboratory: Chalukya, Rashtrakuta, Hoysala, Kakatiya

Progress: 17/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: Aihole and Badami with their dated inscriptions, the Rashtrakuta monolith held at its boundary, the Hoysala stellate morphology and signed craftsmen, and the Kakatiya engineering cases

*Four dynasties, four different experiments, one region. This is the lesson that makes the word "Deccan" mean something specific.*

### 1. Why the Deccan gets one long lesson

Because it is where the two great families meet and neither wins, and because the examiner tests it in three different ways: through the Vesara label, through objective questions about individual monuments, and through the 2026 Vijayanagara demand, which cannot be answered without knowing what Vijayanagara had to draw on. Treating the Deccan as an appendix to the south is the commonest structural error in a southern-trajectory answer.

The material is dense, so it is organised here as four case studies, each with a single analytical job.

### 2. Early Chalukya of Badami - the laboratory, with dated evidence

**Aihole.** ✅ On the banks of the **Malprabha river in Karnataka**, Aihole is often referred to as the **"Cradle of Hindu Temple Architecture"**; it is home to numerous **Early Chalukyan** temples constructed mainly during the **6th-8th century AD**. Later, in the **14th century**, the Vijayanagara Empire took steps to protect and preserve these architectural marvels. Aihole served as the **first capital of the Early Chalukyas till 543 AD**, after which **King Pulakeshin I shifted the capital to Vatapi**, the present Badami. It got its name from a powerful merchant guild, **Ayyavole or Ayyavole 500**, among other important guilds such as **Valanjiyar and Manigraman** of South India; the 500 Lords of Ayyavole provided trade links between the trading communities of **Tamil Nadu, Karnataka and Andhra Pradesh**, engaged in **long-distance maritime trade**, and their inscriptions between the **9th and 14th centuries record endowments made to temples**; they were the protectors of the **Veera-Banaju-Dharma**, the law of the heroic or noble merchants. **Aihole boasts over 120 temples.**

✅ The **Durga Temple**, perhaps named after a nearby fort - *durg* - is **believed to be dedicated to the Sun God Aditya, and not to goddess Durga**; built around the **7th-8th century AD** during the reign of Chalukyan king **Vijayaditya**, it has an **apsidal shape believed to be inspired by Buddhist chaityas**, with high-relief sculptures including **Narasimha, Ardhanarishvara, Vishnu riding Garuda, the Varaha avatar, Mahisamardini and Harihara**.

⚠️ **Two analytical points.** The apsidal plan is the single clearest case in the topic of temple form crossing sectarian lines: a Buddhist congregational shape adopted for a Hindu temple. And ❌ the name must not be translated into a dedication - the record explicitly corrects the assumption that the Durga temple is dedicated to Durga.

✅ The **Lad Khan Temple**, built around the **7th-8th century AD** and perhaps named after a general named Lad Khan who lived there, has two mandapas - the **Mukhamandapa and the Sabhamandapa** - a **square ground plan akin to a village panchayat hall**, and a **rooftop shrine instead of a shikhara**; its **sloping two-tiered roof, fitted with stone logs, is definitely inspired by wooden structures** of earlier times. **An inscription referring to the Ayyavole merchant guild is found in the Lad Khan temple.**

⚠️ **Why Lad Khan is disproportionately valuable.** It joins architectural form to a **commercial** patron class rather than to royalty; it preserves a timber prototype in stone, which is a technology-transfer argument; and its plan is itself likened to a village assembly hall, which links monumental religious architecture to vernacular civic form. Three separate arguments from one small temple.

✅ The **Ravana Phadi** is one of the earliest rock-cut cave temples in Aihole, dating back to the **6th century AD**, with many relief sculptures, the most important being a **Nataraja surrounded by larger-than-life-size saptamatrikas**. ⚠️ Note that rock-cut and structural work proceed **side by side** here, so the Deccan does not offer a clean rock-cut-to-structural ladder of the Pallava kind.

✅ Other Aihole complexes include the **Mallikarjuna**, **Ramalingeshvara** and **Galagnath** temple groups and the **Kontigudi Group**. ✅ On **Meguti hill** there is a partially rock-cut two-storey **Buddhist monument** with a damaged headless Buddha statue in front, and a **Jain temple**.

✅ The **Aihole inscription or Aihole Prashasti**, on the walls of the Jain temple on Meguti hill, was **composed by the court poet Ravikirti in honour of the Chalukya king Pulakeshin II**, written in **Sanskrit language and in old Kannada Chalukyan script**; it elucidates Pulakeshin II's victories against the **Gangas of Mysore, the Alupas, Konkan, the Kadambas of Banavasi and the Kunala island**, and **boldly mentions the names of the famous poets Bharavi and Kalidasa**; it is often compared with the records of the Chinese pilgrim-traveller Hiuen Tsang.

⚠️ **Discipline.** The Prashasti is a **eulogy**; its claims are commemorative, not neutral. Its value here is that a monument is also a dated, authored text naming its patron. Its treatment as literature and as script history belongs to Topic 11.

✅ Aihole's role explains its inclusion in **UNESCO's Tentative List** under the title **"Evolution of Temple Architecture - Aihole-Badami-Pattadakal"**.

**Badami.** ✅ The Badami rock-cut cave temple complex is in **Bagalkote district, northern Karnataka**, near the **Malprabha** river, with a **man-made Agastya lake** alongside. The town, formerly **Vatapi**, was the **second capital** of the Early Chalukyas, the first being Aihole. The Early Chalukyas ruled the area comprising **southern Maharashtra and northern Karnataka from the 6th to the 8th century AD**. **Pulakeshin I**, the first ruler, believed to be a follower of the Vedic religion, **started the construction**, which was **continued by his son Kirtivarman I and later completed by his brother Mangalesh**. The complex is **carved out of soft red sandstone**, with **four rock-cut cave temples - three of Brahmanical origin with elements of both Shaivism and Vaishnavism, and one of Jain origin**.

✅ **Cave 1**, Brahmanical, has sculptures of **Shiva as Nataraja**, **Mahishasura Mardini with Chaturbhuj**, and **Ardhanarishvara**. ✅ **Cave 2**, similar to Cave 1, is dedicated primarily to **Vishnu**, with **Trivikrama** - the Vamana avatar - and the prominent **Harihara**, the fused half-Shiva half-Vishnu image; its ceiling and door-head carvings have reliefs of **Agni, Indra and Varuna**, the famous **Gajalakshmi** and **Swastik** symbols. ✅ **Cave 3** is the **largest**, depicting the **Varaha, Trivikrama and Narsingh** avatars of Vishnu in standing posture with Harihara also featured, and is **the earliest dated Hindu temple in the Deccan region**; it carries **an inscription by King Mangalesh of 578 AD**, a **four-line inscription written in the old Kannada language recording the grant from the income of a nearby village**, which becomes an important source of information about Badami and the Chalukyan dynasties. ✅ **Cave 4** is dedicated to the Jain **tirthankaras**, with sculptures of **Parsvanath, Mahavir, Bahubali** and the symbolic display of others. ✅ Around the Agastya lake, Badami has additional caves, **one of which may be a Buddhist cave**, and a **Bhutanatha Temple** alongside the lake.

✅ **Status.** Badami is **still not a UNESCO World Heritage Site**; it is on the **Tentative List** under "Evolution of Temple Architecture - Aihole-Badami-Pattadakal".

⚠️ **The value of Cave 3.** A dated inscription of 578 AD on the earliest dated Hindu temple in the Deccan is the strongest chronological anchor available for this material, and it is a *grant* record, which connects architecture to revenue and endowment. Use it whenever a question asks how temple chronology is established.

### 3. Rashtrakuta - named, bounded, and handed over

✅ **Fact.** The **Rashtrakuta dynasty (750-983 AD)** is named among the three makers of Vesara-style temples, for the **Kailashnatha Temple at the Ellora cave complex**. ✅ The **rock-cut Kailasha temple of the Ellora caves, constructed in the 8th century by Krishna I of the Rashtrakuta dynasty, was monolithic**, like most of the architecture at Mahabalipuram.

⚠️ **Its analytical job here, and only here.** It is the **limiting case** of Deccan experiment: an entire temple excavated from living rock, so that plan, elevation and sculpture are a single operation performed from the top down, with no possibility of correction. Set against the Pallava sequence - excavate, then carve free, then build up - the Kailasa shows the subtractive method pushed to a scale that additive building would normally require.

❌ **The boundary.** Its architectural detail, its inscriptional record and its place in the rock-cut sequence belong to **Topic 02**. Cross-link rather than duplicate: one paragraph in a Deccan answer, and no more.

### 4. Hoysala - the stellate plan, the soft stone and the signed work

✅ **Fact - period and seats.** The **Sacred Ensembles of the Hoysala** refer to Hoysala-era temples in Karnataka built during the **12th-13th centuries AD**, known for exquisite architecture and detailed iconography. The **Hoysala Kingdom ruled large parts of present Karnataka, parts of Tamil Nadu and south-western Telangana between the 11th and the 14th centuries AD**; the capital was initially at **Belur**, later moved to **Dwarasamudra**, modern **Halebidu**. **More than 100 temples survive.**

✅ **Fact - the three canonical temples.** **Chennakeshava Temple, Belur**, on the banks of the **Yagachi**; **Hoysaleswara Temple, Halebidu**, on the banks of the **Dwarasamudra lake**; and **Chennakeshava Temple, Somanathapura**, on the banks of the **Kaveri**. **The above three temples were inscribed as a UNESCO World Heritage Site in 2023.**

⚠️ **A recorded naming variant.** The official property is titled *Sacred Ensembles of the Hoysalas*, and its official component names are **Channakeshava Temple, Belur**; **Hoysalesvara Temple, Halebidu**; and **Keshava Temple, Somanathapura**, while widely used tables label the Somanathapura temple "Chennakeshava". Prefer the official naming for the inscribed property and do not treat the variation as an error to argue about.

✅ **Fact - patron and motive.** The most notable ruler was **Vishnuvardhana of the 12th century AD**, who **converted from Jainism to Vaishnavism under the influence of the philosopher Ramanujacharya**, and who **commissioned the Chennakeshava Temple at Belur to commemorate his victory over the Cholas**, and also the **Hoysaleswara Temple in Halebidu**. Other important rulers include **Nripa Kama I** and **Nripa Kama II**.

⚠️ This is the richest patronage row in the topic: a war, a religious conversion and a named philosopher in one commission. ❌ The conversion is recorded as influence, not as a dated event, so do not build a chronology on it, and the doctrine of Vishishtadvaita belongs to Topic 13.

✅ **Fact - material.** A notable feature is the use of **chloritic schist, or soapstone** - **soft and easy to carve when quarried, but it hardens over time**.

⚠️ This is the cleanest material-to-style explanation after the Bengal terracotta case. Soft stone at the quarry permits intricate carving with hand tools; hardening afterwards gives the finished work durability. The carving density is therefore explained, not merely admired.

✅ **Fact - plan.** Hoysala temples were normally built upon a **raised platform called jagati**, following a **stellate or star-shape design**, with the walls following a **zig-zag pattern**; they have **multiple shrines arranged around a central pillared hall, forming a complex star shape**; and the **star-shaped four-foot-high platform has an extended terrace surrounding the temple, acting as the pradakshinapath**.

⚠️ Note what this does to the axial chain: the Hoysala temple multiplies the sanctum end around one hall rather than extending a single axis, and it puts the ambulatory *outside* on an open terrace rather than enclosing it. The word *jagati* is shared with Khajuraho; the geometry is not.

✅ **Fact - surface and support.** Outer walls are **covered with sculptural friezes** depicting scenes from the **Ramayana and Mahabharata**, mythological scenes, animals and floral motifs; **lathe-turned pillars** were used inside the mandapas; bands of friezes include **elephants, lions, scrolls, horses, makaras, swans** and deities; and the **Nandi Pavilion** and a separate **kalyani**, or temple tank, are noted for ceremonial use. ⚠️ Frieze order varies between the three temples; do not fix a sequence.

✅ **Fact - the label, stated precisely.** Hoysala temples "are constructed in **Vesara** style of architecture. The temple has a **Dravidian morphology** along with substantial influences from the **Bhumija style** prevalent in Central India, the **Nagara traditions** of northern and western India."

✅ **Fact - named makers.** **Dasoja, Chavana and Mallithamma** were prominent architects and sculptors of the Hoysala dynasty. ⚠️ Do not assign a specific temple to a specific named sculptor.

⚠️ **Why named craftsmen matter.** Almost the entire history of Indian architecture is written in the names of patrons. A tradition in which the makers are named shifts the historical record from commissioning to execution, and it is the strongest available answer to any question about artistic agency.

✅ **Fact - plurality.** The Hoysala rulers **were followers of Jainism and Hinduism**, patronised **Kannada and Sanskrit** literature with contributions from **Jain and Brahmin poets**, and **Halebidu has a walled complex containing three Jain basadi of the Hoysala period**. ⚠️ Literary patronage detail belongs to Topic 11.

### 5. Kakatiya - foundation engineering, and a temple named after its architect

✅ **Fact - the Thousand Pillar Temple.** **Orugallu**, modern Warangal, was the capital of the **Kakatiya dynasty, which ruled from the 12th to the 14th centuries**, and is often referred to as the cultural capital of Telangana; **Hanumakonda** was the older capital before the shift to Warangal under **Ganapati Deva**. The **Thousand Pillar temple** is believed to have been constructed between the **12th and 14th centuries by order of the Kakatiya king Rudra Deva**, dedicated primarily to Shiva and known as **Sri Rudreshwara Swamy Temple**. It was built in the **later Chalukyan and the early Kakatiyan style**, with a **star-shaped platform** and the **sand-box technique** to strengthen its foundation. It has **one thousand richly carved pillars**, perforated screens, decorative icons, rock-cut elephants and a **monolithic Nandi**; it stands on a **double platform** and consists of a central mandapa with **three shrines - the Rudreswara, the Vasudeva and the Surya - known collectively as the Trikutalayam**. The main entrance is in the east with a mandapa of two pillars; adjacent is a **granite pillar containing the inscription of King Rudradeva recording the date of the construction**. The temple was **damaged by the Tughlaq dynasty in the 14th century during their invasion in the Deccan**; the **7th Nizam of Hyderabad, Mir Osman Ali Khan, donated a grant towards its reconstruction**.

✅ **Fact - Warangal Fort.** The ruins of the **Warangal Fort** were constructed by Kakatiya rulers in the **13th century**; the 13th-century Kakatiya queen **Rudrama Devi** is renowned for strengthening and fortifying it; the fort features **four large ornamental stone gateways, known as the Kakatiya Kala Thoranam, which have been adopted into the state emblem of Telangana**. ✅ The Thousand Pillar Temple, the Warangal Fort and the Kakatiya Kala Thoranam have been added to the **Tentative List** of UNESCO World Heritage Sites - ❌ not inscribed.

✅ **Fact - Ramappa.** The **Ramappa Temple**, near **Ramappa Lake** in **Mulugu district, Telangana**, was built in the **13th century AD** by the Kakatiya dynasty and is dedicated to Shiva; it has been **recognised as a UNESCO World Heritage Site in 2021**. Also known as **Rudreswara or Ramalingeswara**, it was **constructed in the interest of Recharla Rudra, a general of the Kakatiya king Ganapati Deva**, and took **around 40 long years** to complete. **It is perhaps the only temple in the country known by the name of its architect, Ramappa, rather than the king who commissioned it or its presiding deity.** The **sandbox technique** was used: **the foundation pit is filled with a mixture of sand-lime, jaggery and black myrobalan fruit**, which **acts as a cushion**. Another feature is the use of **floating stone**: the ceiling is supported by **light stones that float on water**. In front of the **Nandi Pavilion** stands the main temple, made on a **star-shaped platform**. The stone is a **sandstone of pinkish hue**, while **black basalt has been lavishly used** in the decoration of the central temple, with **almost life-size statues worked in highly polished black basalt**; inside is a display of sculptures depicting scenes from the early myths, the Ramayana, the Puranas and later Hindu texts, with **Krishna playing his flute represented in several places**. ⚠️ The Italian traveller **Marco Polo**, during his visit to the Kakatiya kingdom in the late 13th century, **supposedly** called the temple "the brightest star in the galaxy of temples" - reported as *supposed*, so cite it as a traveller's attribution, not as documentation.

⚠️ **Ramappa's analytical job.** It is the topic's single strongest "technology of temple building" case: a named foundation technique, a named lightweight ceiling material, a named architect, a recorded construction duration and a dated inscription-free status anchor. Any question touching engineering, craftsmanship or artistic agency should reach for it.

### Comparative note: what unites four very different Deccan experiments

| Dynasty | The experiment | The decisive evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Early Chalukya | Trying out plan types and both superstructure grammars at one group of sites | Aihole's 120-plus temples; Cave 3 at Badami dated 578 AD; the Virupaksha-Papanatha pair at Pattadakal |
| Rashtrakuta | Pushing the subtractive method to full temple scale | The monolithic Kailashnatha, Cave 16, Ellora, by Krishna I - detail owned by Topic 02 |
| Hoysala | Rethinking the plan itself as a star, and the material as a soft stone that hardens | Stellate jagati with the terrace as pradakshinapath; chloritic schist; signed work by Dasoja, Chavana and Mallithamma |
| Kakatiya | Solving the engineering of foundation and ceiling | Sandbox foundations of sand-lime, jaggery and black myrobalan; floating stone; Ramappa named for its architect; UNESCO 2021 |

⚠️ **The unifying claim to write.** The Deccan is where Indian temple architecture was *experimented on* rather than merely practised - in plan type, superstructure grammar, construction method, material and foundation engineering. That claim is supported by four independent dynasties over eight centuries, and it explains why a later Deccan empire could describe its own architecture as an amalgamation.

### Examination use

This lesson feeds three demands directly. It supplies the full evidence for any Vesara or Deccan-hybrid question. It supplies the inherited repertoire that the 2026 Vijayanagara demand asks you to trace. And it supplies several of the highest-frequency objective facts in the topic: the Durga temple's solar dedication, Badami Cave 3 as the earliest dated Hindu temple in the Deccan with its 578 AD inscription, the Jain Narayana temple's Rashtrakuta authorship at Pattadakal, the Hoysala 2023 inscription, Ramappa's 2021 inscription and its architect-name, and the Tentative-List status of Badami, Aihole, the Thousand Pillar Temple and the Warangal Fort.

### Revision notes

1. Aihole: Malprabha river, Karnataka; "Cradle of Hindu Temple Architecture"; over 120 temples; first Early Chalukya capital till 543 AD, when Pulakeshin I shifted to Vatapi (Badami); named after the Ayyavole 500 merchant guild, alongside Valanjiyar and Manigraman, protectors of the Veera-Banaju-Dharma.
2. Durga Temple, Aihole, c. 7th-8th century under Vijayaditya: apsidal, believed inspired by Buddhist chaityas; named after a nearby fort, *durg*; believed dedicated to the Sun God Aditya, not to Durga.
3. Lad Khan Temple, Aihole, c. 7th-8th century: square plan akin to a village panchayat hall; rooftop shrine instead of a shikhara; sloping two-tiered roof with stone logs, inspired by wooden structures; carries the Ayyavole guild inscription.
4. Ravana Phadi, Aihole, 6th century: earliest rock-cut here; Nataraja surrounded by larger-than-life saptamatrikas - rock-cut and structural work proceed side by side.
5. Aihole Prashasti, Meguti hill, on a Jain temple wall: by court poet Ravikirti for Pulakeshin II, in Sanskrit language and old Kannada script; victories over the Gangas of Mysore, Alupas, Konkan, Kadambas of Banavasi and Kunala; names Bharavi and Kalidasa; a eulogy, so commemorative rather than neutral.
6. Badami: Bagalkote district; Malprabha and the man-made Agastya lake; formerly Vatapi, the second capital; begun by Pulakeshin I, continued by Kirtivarman I, completed by Mangalesh; soft red sandstone; four caves - three Brahmanical, one Jain.
7. Badami Cave 1: Nataraja, Mahishasura Mardini, Ardhanarishvara. Cave 2: Vishnu, Trivikrama, Harihara, with Agni, Indra, Varuna, Gajalakshmi and Swastik on the ceiling. Cave 3: largest, the earliest dated Hindu temple in the Deccan, with Mangalesh's four-line old-Kannada inscription of 578 AD recording a village grant. Cave 4: Jain - Parsvanath, Mahavir, Bahubali.
8. Badami is not inscribed; it is on the Tentative List with Aihole and Pattadakal as "Evolution of Temple Architecture - Aihole-Badami-Pattadakal".
9. Rashtrakuta, 750-983 AD: the monolithic Kailashnatha at Ellora by Krishna I, 8th century - the limiting case of the subtractive method; detail belongs to Topic 02.
10. Hoysala: 11th-14th centuries; Karnataka, parts of Tamil Nadu, south-western Telangana; capital Belur, then Dwarasamudra (Halebidu); more than 100 temples survive.
11. The three inscribed temples, UNESCO 2023: Belur on the Yagachi, Halebidu on the Dwarasamudra lake, Somanathapura on the Kaveri; prefer the official component names for the inscribed property.
12. Vishnuvardhana, 12th century: converted from Jainism to Vaishnavism under Ramanujacharya's influence; commissioned Belur to commemorate his victory over the Cholas, and Halebidu.
13. Hoysala material: chloritic schist or soapstone - soft when quarried, hardens over time.
14. Hoysala plan: raised jagati, stellate design, zig-zag walls, multiple shrines around a central pillared hall, the four-foot-high star platform's extended terrace acting as pradakshinapath.
15. Hoysala surface: friezes of the epics, mythological scenes, animals and floral motifs; lathe-turned pillars; bands of elephants, lions, scrolls, horses, makaras, swans and deities; Nandi Pavilion; separate kalyani tank.
16. Hoysala style statement: Vesara, with Dravidian morphology plus substantial Bhumija and Nagara influences - three sources.
17. Named Hoysala makers: Dasoja, Chavana and Mallithamma; do not assign a temple to a named sculptor.
18. Hoysala plurality: rulers followed Jainism and Hinduism; Kannada and Sanskrit literature by Jain and Brahmin poets; three Jain basadi in a walled complex at Halebidu.
19. Thousand Pillar Temple, Warangal: Rudra Deva; later Chalukyan and early Kakatiyan style; star-shaped platform; sand-box technique; double platform; Trikutalayam of Rudreswara, Vasudeva and Surya; Rudradeva's inscription records the construction date; damaged by the Tughlaqs in the 14th century; restored with a grant from Mir Osman Ali Khan, the 7th Nizam.
20. Warangal Fort: 13th century, fortified by Rudrama Devi; Kakatiya Kala Thoranam, four ornamental stone gateways, in the Telangana state emblem; Tentative List, not inscribed.
21. Ramappa, Mulugu district, 13th century, UNESCO 2021: built in the interest of Recharla Rudra, a general of Ganapati Deva; about 40 years to build; perhaps the only temple known by its architect's name; sandbox technique of sand-lime, jaggery and black myrobalan; floating stone ceiling; star-shaped platform; pinkish sandstone with lavish black basalt for polished near-life-size figures; Marco Polo's phrase reported as *supposed*.

### Concept check

**Question:** Identify the three distinct kinds of experiment the Deccan dynasties conducted, name one monument for each, and explain why this record makes the later Vijayanagara description of its own architecture as an amalgamation historically plausible rather than merely flattering.

**Model answer:** The first experiment was in plan type and superstructure grammar: at Aihole the apsidal Durga temple borrows a Buddhist chaitya shape for a Hindu temple believed dedicated to the Sun God Aditya, while at Pattadakal the Virupaksha and Papanatha carry Dravidian and Nagara shikharas on similar plans. The second was in construction method: the Rashtrakuta Kailashnatha at Ellora, a monolithic temple of Krishna I, pushes excavation to full temple scale, while Ramappa in Mulugu district solves foundation and ceiling engineering with a sandbox of sand-lime, jaggery and black myrobalan and with floating stone. The third was in plan geometry and material: the Hoysala temples adopt a stellate jagati with zig-zag walls, multiple shrines around a central pillared hall and an extended terrace serving as the pradakshinapath, carved in chloritic schist that is soft when quarried and hardens over time. Because Deccan practice had for eight centuries consisted of selecting and combining rather than inheriting one grammar, a later Deccan empire describing its temple architecture as combining Chola, Hoysala, Pandya, Chalukya and even Indo-Islamic features is describing a regional habit, not claiming a courtesy.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Deccan as a transitional zone between north and south. It is not a corridor through which styles passed; it is where alternatives were tested, often side by side at one site, and where several devices - the stellate plan, sandbox foundations, signed sculpture - appear that neither the northern nor the southern family produced.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The Deccan was less a meeting ground of northern and southern styles than a laboratory of architectural experiment." Discuss with reference to named monuments. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model answer

The reformulation is the stronger one, because meeting implies passive convergence while the Deccan record shows deliberate testing.

Plan type was tested first. Aihole, called the cradle of Hindu temple architecture, carries over 120 temples: the apsidal Durga temple, believed dedicated to the Sun God Aditya rather than to Durga, takes its shape from Buddhist chaityas, while Lad Khan reproduces a timber prototype in stone with a rooftop shrine instead of a shikhara and a plan likened to a village panchayat hall, bearing an inscription of the Ayyavole merchant guild.

Superstructure grammar was tested next, at Pattadakal, where the Virupaksha commissioned by Queen Lokmahadevi carries a Dravidian shikhara and the Papanatha, similar in plan, a north Indian Nagara one.

Method was tested at two extremes. The Rashtrakuta Kailashnatha at Ellora, a monolithic temple of Krishna I, excavated a complete temple from living rock. The Kakatiya Ramappa temple, inscribed by UNESCO in 2021 and built in the interest of Recharla Rudra over some forty years, used a sandbox foundation of sand-lime, jaggery and black myrobalan and floating stone in its ceiling.

Geometry and material were tested by the Hoysalas, whose stellate jagati with zig-zag walls and terrace pradakshinapath was carved in chloritic schist, soft when quarried and hardening over time, by named craftsmen - Dasoja, Chavana and Mallithamma.

The qualification is that the region did inherit both grammars, and Badami Cave 3, dated 578 AD, anchors the chronology. But what it did with them was experimental.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the reformulation from meeting to laboratory argued rather than asserted 3 + plan-type experiment at Aihole with two contrasting temples 4 + the Pattadakal superstructure pair 2 + the two extremes of method, Ellora and Ramappa 4 + Hoysala geometry, material and named makers 1 + the inheritance qualification with the 578 AD anchor 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 18:** Everything the Deccan tested was available to the empire that rose on the Tungabhadra in 1336. What it did with that inheritance is the subject of the most recent question the examiner has set on this topic - and the answer is not simply that it copied.

*The diagram answers the 2026 demand directly: amalgamation is a list of named sources plus a native invention plus a division between sacred and secular registers.*

### 1. The demand, read exactly

The verified 2026 demand asks you to elucidate, with examples, a claim that Vijayanagara architecture combines inherited traditions with practices current in its own day. Notice that it has two halves, exactly like the 2025 Chandella demand.

**"Past traditions"** means the inherited southern and Deccan repertoire - and the inherited traditions are named explicitly, which is a gift. **"Contemporary practices"** means what was current in the fourteenth to sixteenth centuries: the Indo-Islamic architectural vocabulary of the neighbouring Deccan sultanates, and the ceremonial and urban requirements of a functioning imperial capital.

⚠️ **The thesis that makes the answer analytical.** Vijayanagara amalgamates by **register**, not by fusion: it keeps the Dravidian grammar for temples and adopts Islamicate forms for palace and court buildings, while inventing new devices - the Raya Gopuram, the chariot street - to serve an imperial capital's ceremonial needs. That is the precise, defensible version of "synthesis", and it avoids the error of claiming Indo-Islamic form inside the temples.

### 2. Setting, dynasties and the named borrowings

✅ **Fact.** From the **14th to 16th century AD** the Vijayanagara kingdom prospered on the banks of the **Tungabhadra in present central Karnataka**, surrounded by a landscape of granite boulders, and is also known as **Pampa Kshetra**, derived from the River Pampa, the erstwhile name of the Tungabhadra.

✅ **Fact.** The Vijayanagara empire, the **"city of victory"**, was established by two brothers **Harihar and Bukka in 1336 AD**; **Hampi** became its capital. It was ruled by four successive ruling houses: **Sangama**, founded by Harihara I; **Saluva**, founded by Saluva Narasimha; **Tuluva**, founded by Tuluva Narasa Nayaka, to which **Krishnadeva Raya** belonged; and **Aravidu**, founded by Tirumala. ⚠️ The conventional date ranges commonly printed for them - Sangama 1336-1485, Saluva 1485-1503, Tuluva 1491-1570 and Aravidu 1542-1646 - **overlap rather than succeed one another cleanly**, because each transfer of power was a contested usurpation in which a regent or commander ruled in the name of a displaced line before assuming the throne. Cite the houses in order and treat the years as conventional and approximate; ❌ do not present them as crisp non-overlapping reigns.

✅ **Fact - infrastructure.** The rulers **fortified the city, dammed the river, carried its waters through an aqueduct cut out of the solid rock, and transformed the barren landscape into a cultivable and splendid site**; the **Hiriya canal, built by Sangam rulers**, was one of the most prominent waterworks, drawing water from the dam across the Tungabhadra and irrigating the valley.

✅ **Fact - the amalgamation claim, in its recorded wording.** Temple architecture flourished in the Vijayanagara kingdom and **"combined the architectural features of the Cholas, the Hoysalas, the Pandyas, the Chalukyas, and even the Indo-Islamic style of Bijapur"**. ✅ **One of the unique features of its temple complexes was the wide chariot streets flanked by a row of pillared mandapas.**

✅ **Fact - the capital's scale and reputation.** **By 1500 AD, Hampi became the world's second-largest medieval-era city after Beijing**, and probably India's richest at that time, attracting traders from Persia and Portugal. Travellers' notices include **Abdur Razzaq**, who visited in the 15th century under **Devaraya II**; **Niccolo de' Conti**, around 1420 AD under Deva Raya I; **Afanasy Nikitin**, a Russian merchant of the 15th century who described the Bahmani Sultanate and Vijayanagara in *A Journey Beyond the Three Seas*; **Domingo Paes**, a Portuguese traveller who visited around 1520 AD under Krishnadevaraya and compared Hampi's wealth with Rome; **Ludovico di Varthema**, who called Hampi "a second paradise" in the early 16th century; **Fernao Nuniz**, who stayed from 1535-37 under Achyuta Deva Raya; and **Duarte Barbosa**, who noted the religious tolerance of the Vijayanagara kings, stating that the king allowed every man to come, go and live according to his own creed. ✅ **Krishnadevaraya**, under whom the empire reached the climax of its glory in the early 16th century, was a great patron of architecture and literature and composed the **Amuktamalyada**, a philosophical poetic work in **Telugu**.

⚠️ **Discipline on travellers.** These are eyewitness or hearsay reports, and several are explicitly about wealth and markets rather than architecture. Use one or two for the capital's scale and reputation; do not convert a traveller's simile into an architectural fact.

### 3. The sacred zone, monument by monument

✅ **Virupaksha Temple, Hampi.** Dedicated to **Shiva, the patron deity of Vijayanagara**; it consists of a **50-metre large gopuram**; its construction was **started in the Sangama period** and was **later expanded by King Krishnadevaraya of the Tuluva dynasty**. The **Raya Gopuram**, the monumental entrance tower present here, was **"introduced in temple architecture for the first time by Raja Krishnadevaraya"**.

⚠️ **This is the single most important row in the lesson.** It is the documented completion of the shift that began in the last Chola phase: the gateway is not merely large, it is a newly named type introduced by an emperor. ❌ And it is the row where the dynastic trap sits: do not confuse the **Hampi** Virupaksha with the **Pattadakal** Virupaksha of the Early Chalukyas.

✅ **Vitthala Temple Complex.** Dedicated to **Vishnu, worshipped there in the Vitthala form**, constructed by **Krishnadevaraya**. It has the iconic **stone chariot** - **"though not monolithic"** - so elegant that it features on the **₹50 currency note**. The **musical pillars in the Rang Mandapa** are greatly admired. The complex **"also has Kalyana Mandapa which refers to a wedding hall, and often used for various celebrations and events"**.

❌ **Two traps in one monument.** The stone chariot is frequently called monolithic; it is explicitly recorded as not monolithic. And the Kalyana Mandapa is frequently assigned to Indo-Islamic or Chalukyan architecture; it is recorded here as a Vijayanagara temple-complex feature.

✅ **Hazara Rama Temple.** Constructed by **Devaraya II**, dedicated to **Rama**, with **Ramayana reliefs** on the temple walls; **its pillars produce different melodious sounds on being hit**. ⚠️ Do not merge these with the Vitthala Rang Mandapa's musical pillars; they are two separate recorded cases.

✅ **The Yali.** A **mythical creature, part horse part lion**, is **a prominent sculpture engraved in the temple pillars of the Vijayanagara empire**, said to be a **guardian creature, protecting human beings both physically and spiritually**. ⚠️ In Tamil literature, the Yali has a **cat-like body, the head of a lion with the tusks of an elephant and the tail of a serpent**, and is described as more powerful than the lion, the tiger or the elephant. ⚠️ The two descriptions differ slightly; report the tradition rather than one canonical anatomy. ⚠️ The Yali later **becomes symbolic of the Nayaka architectural identity**, which makes it a single motif demonstrably carrying across the Vijayanagara-to-Nayaka transition - and a descendant of the Vyala family met at Khajuraho.

### 4. The royal and secular zone, and the precise version of "Indo-Islamic influence"

✅ **Mahanavami Dibba.** A **huge and high platform at Hampi used for ritual purposes**. The Portuguese traveller **Domingo Paes records that it was erected by Raja Krishnadevaraya to mark his victorious military campaign in Odisha in the early 16th century**. It is here that the king **celebrated the nine days of the Mahanavami festival**, which marks the victory of Durga over Mahishasura, and **this was the occasion when kings used to review the armies and check their preparedness for battle**. **Numerous sculptures depicting various scenes of daily life** are found on the walls of the **three-tiered stepped platform**.

❌ **Two recorded distinctions.** It is **royal ceremonial architecture, not a temple**. And the Virupaksha temple is **not** located on the Mahanavami Dibba - the two are separate structures. ⚠️ Note also that the builder and occasion rest on a **traveller's account**, which should be attributed rather than stated flatly.

✅ **Lotus Mahal.** It **has an arched roof and Indo-Islamic influence in its construction**; the palace is **built in the Zenana enclosure and was reserved for the royal women**. ✅ A **domed structure that housed royal elephants** is also significant, and there is a **Queen's Bath with a stepped design and Indo-Islamic arches**. ✅ The **monolithic rock-cut large sculpture of Lakshmi Narasimha on Shesha** is known as **one of the masterpieces of Indian sculpture**.

❌ **The precision that earns the mark.** Do **not** claim Indo-Islamic influence in the Hampi *temples*. The evidence locates arches and domes in the **secular and royal buildings** - Lotus Mahal, the elephant stable, the Queen's Bath. The defensible formulation is that the empire adopted Islamicate form for **palace and court architecture** while retaining Dravidian grammar for **temples**, which is a division by register, and that division is precisely what an amalgamation claim is describing.

### 5. The Kalyana Mandapa question, and its correct ownership

A **Civil Services 2019** objective item asked which kingdom made the building of Kalyana Mandapas a notable feature of its temple construction, offering four dynastic options. Its exact stem, options and published key are reproduced in the final linkage section; what matters here is the substantive finding.

⚠️ **Stated with its provenance:** on that published key, the answer is **Vijayanagara**, and the substantive evidence supports it independently - the Vitthala Temple Complex at Hampi has a Kalyana Mandapa, a wedding hall used for celebrations and events, and a second, independent case exists at the **Lepakshi Veerbhadra temple**, where the **Kalyana Mandapa is incomplete but carries an intricately sculpted depiction of the divine wedding of Shiva and Parvati**. Two Vijayanagara cases make it a hall *type*, not a one-off.

⚠️ **Why the distractors attract, and how to kill them.** Chalukya owns the Pattadakal Virupaksha and Papanatha pair; Chandela owns Khajuraho; Rashtrakuta owns the Ellora Kailasa. None of them is associated with the wedding-hall type anywhere on this record. ❌ And a Kalyana Mandapa is a Hindu temple hall, so it must never be answered from mosque, tomb or palace vocabulary - a correction this topic carries and that Topic 04 records on its side.

### 6. Fall, rediscovery and status

✅ **Fact.** In the late 16th century the Vijayanagara empire was **defeated by a coalition of sultanates including the Adil Shahi, the Nizam Shahi, the Qutub Shahi and the Barid Shahi**; the capital was **conquered, looted and destroyed in 1565 in the Battle of Talikota**, after which Hampi remained in ruins, the capital shifting to **Penukonda** and thereafter **Chandragiri**, but Hampi never regained its prosperity.

✅ The ruins **were brought to light in 1800 by an archaeological engineer named Colonel Colin Mackenzie, who was also the first Surveyor General of India**; **Alexander Rea** also contributed to the discovery. ✅ The archaeologist **Robert Sewell published his scholarly treatise *A Forgotten Empire* about Hampi in 1900**. ✅ **In 1986, UNESCO gave Hampi World Heritage status under the name "Group of Monuments at Hampi".**

⚠️ **The pattern worth naming.** Hampi and Khajuraho share a destruction-or-decline, disappearance, rediscovery and inscription arc, with 1800 and 1838 as the respective rediscovery dates and 1986 as a shared inscription year. Lesson 20 develops the pattern; here it supplies the closing line for a Vijayanagara answer.

### 7. Nayaka: the arc completed

✅ **Fact - origin.** The **Nayakas were feudatories under the Vijayanagara Empire**. After the **Battle of Talikota in 1565** and the fall of the Vijayanagara empire, the **Madurai Nayakas asserted greater autonomy**, established their own kingdoms and **ruled from the 16th to the 18th century**. They were **great patrons of art**, **revitalised temples previously ransacked by the Delhi Sultans**, and founded a new architectural style known as the **Nayaka style**. ⚠️ The "ransacked by the Delhi Sultans" phrase is the standard account's framing; do not extend it into a general destruction narrative.

✅ **Fact - position in the trajectory.** **"The Nayaka style is a continuation of the late Vijayanagara style and was influenced by the local Chola and Pandyan traditions. One can say that Pallavas and Cholas were the pioneers of early Dravidian architecture whereas Nayakas were the pioneers of the late Dravidian architecture."** ⚠️ This is a modern periodisation and should be attributed as such - but it is also the sentence that makes a full Pallava-to-Nayaka trajectory answer defensible end to end.

✅ **Fact - centres and chief patron.** **Madurai, Thanjavur, Tiruchirapalli, Gingee and Ikkeri** were the centres; the style **flourished principally under the rule of King Thirumalai Nayak in the 17th century**. ⚠️ The ruler's name appears both as **Thirumalai Nayak** and as **Tirumala Nayaka**; record the variant rather than silently normalising it.

✅ **Fact - the plan device.** The entire composition of the temple was **enclosed within rectangular premises having high walls**, entered through **gopurams on the east and west**. Later, **concentric rectangles were added, contained within higher enclosing walls, leaving another open space between the inner and outer perimeters**. **Four entrances adorned the four walls in the centre, each wall consisting of a gopuram larger than the previous one.** On the outer side, **two large important structures - a pillared hall and a square water tank** - were built.

✅ **Fact - material logic.** For the **core construction, granite was generally used**, whereas for **superstructures and gopurams, ornamentation made with stucco, that is, mortar over brick**, was used. ⚠️ This explains why gopuras could be enormous and brightly figured while the sanctum stayed austere - and it carries a dating caution: stucco imagery is repeatedly renewed, so the dates of visible figures are not construction dates.

✅ **Fact - devices.** Nayaka design shows much emphasis on **large gopurams, large temple tanks and wide corridors called prakarams**, facilitating processions and devotional circumambulation. A **prakaram** is an outer part around the temple sanctum, enclosed or open, typically enclosed for the innermost prakaram; **Shri Ramanathaswamy Temple in Rameswaram, with its outer set of large corridors, has the longest prakaram in the world** - ⚠️ a superlative that is a reported claim and should be cited as such. ✅ Nayakas used **narrative and composite columns**, and the gopurams were **heavily painted depicting warriors, deities and animals - especially the Yali**.

✅ **Fact - the exemplar.** The **Meenakshi temple is one of the finest examples of the Nayaka style**. Though the temple has **historic roots, most of the present campus structure was repaired, renovated and expanded in the 17th century by Tirumala Nayaka**. Located on the banks of the **Vaigai** river, it is dedicated to **Goddess Meenakshi, a form of Parvati**, and her consort **Sundareshwar, a form of Shiva**, and her brother **Alagar, a form of Vishnu**, so it **represents the confluence of the Shaivism, Shaktism and Vaishnavism denominations of Hinduism**. It is one of the **Paadal Petra Sthalam** - the **276 temples of Shiva revered in the verses of the Tamil Saiva Nayanars of the 6th-9th century AD**. There is a temple tank known as **Potramarai Kulam**, the Golden-Lotus Pond; the premises has **14 gopurams**; and **the design of the Tamil Nadu State Emblem is based on the gopuram of the Meenakshi temple**. ✅ **Madurai celebrates the Chithirai festival every year in April-May**, a month-long festival commemorating the wedding of Meenakshi and Sundareshwar and the arrival of Kallazhagar.

❌ **Do not date the whole temple to the Nayakas** - the evidence says the present *campus* is 17th-century Nayaka work on older roots.

✅ **Fact - further Nayaka contributions.** **Shri Ranganathaswamy temple**, dedicated to Ranganatha, a form of Vishnu, on the banks of the Kaveri at **Srirangam**, and **Shri Ramanathaswamy temple** dedicated to Shiva at **Rameswaram**, "had immense contribution from the Nayaka rulers". ⚠️ "Contribution to", not "built by".

### Comparative note: the whole southern arc on one mechanism

| Stage | What carries the emphasis | Named evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Pallava | Neither - the problem is technique, not emphasis | Mandagapattu; Pancha Rathas; Shore Temple |
| Early Chola | The vimana | Narttamalai; Brihadishvara's roughly 66 m vimana |
| Late Chola | The gopura begins to dominate | Chidambaram under Kulottunga I |
| Vijayanagara | The gopura becomes a named imperial type | Hampi Virupaksha's 50 m gopuram; the Raya Gopuram introduced for the first time by Krishnadevaraya |
| Nayaka | Graded gopurams over concentric enclosures | Meenakshi, Madurai - 14 gopurams, each outer wall's gopuram larger than the last |

⚠️ **One sentence carries a 20-mark answer:** because an enclosure can be added to and a sanctum tower cannot, the growth of southern temples had to be outward, and artistic emphasis migrated with the growth - from the vimana at Thanjavur to the gopura at Chidambaram, to the Raya Gopuram at Hampi, to fourteen graded gopurams at Madurai.

### Examination use

The verified 2026 demand is answered by the register argument: Dravidian grammar for temples, Islamicate form for palaces, named borrowings from Cholas, Hoysalas, Pandyas, Chalukyas and Bijapur, and native inventions in the Raya Gopuram and the chariot street. The objective material here is unusually dense: the stone chariot's non-monolithic status, the Mahanavami Dibba as a royal platform and not a temple, the Kalyana Mandapa's Vijayanagara ownership, the two Virupakshas, the 1565 Talikota destruction, Mackenzie in 1800, Sewell in 1900, Hampi's 1986 inscription, and Madurai's fourteen gopurams.

### Revision notes

1. Vijayanagara: 14th-16th centuries, on the Tungabhadra in central Karnataka; also Pampa Kshetra; founded by Harihar and Bukka in 1336 AD; capital Hampi.
2. Four successive ruling houses: Sangama, Saluva, Tuluva (Krishnadeva Raya) and Aravidu. Their conventional ranges - 1336-1485, 1485-1503, 1491-1570 and 1542-1646 - overlap because each transition was a contested usurpation; cite the order, treat the years as approximate.
3. Infrastructure: city fortified, river dammed, water carried through a rock-cut aqueduct; the Hiriya canal built by Sangam rulers irrigated the valley.
4. The amalgamation claim: temple architecture combined the features of the Cholas, the Hoysalas, the Pandyas, the Chalukyas, and even the Indo-Islamic style of Bijapur.
5. A unique feature: wide chariot streets flanked by a row of pillared mandapas.
6. By 1500 AD Hampi was the world's second-largest medieval-era city after Beijing; travellers include Abdur Razzaq, Niccolo de' Conti, Afanasy Nikitin, Domingo Paes, Ludovico di Varthema, Fernao Nuniz and Duarte Barbosa; Krishnadevaraya composed the Amuktamalyada in Telugu.
7. Virupaksha, Hampi: Shiva, the patron deity; 50-metre gopuram; begun in the Sangama period, expanded by Krishnadevaraya; the Raya Gopuram introduced in temple architecture for the first time by Krishnadevaraya.
8. Vitthala complex: Vishnu in the Vitthala form, built by Krishnadevaraya; the stone chariot, explicitly not monolithic, on the ₹50 note; musical pillars in the Rang Mandapa; a Kalyana Mandapa, that is, a wedding hall.
9. Hazara Rama temple: Devaraya II; Rama; Ramayana reliefs; pillars that produce different melodious sounds - distinct from the Vitthala pillars.
10. Yali: part horse part lion, a guardian creature on Vijayanagara temple pillars; in Tamil literature cat-like body, lion's head with elephant tusks and a serpent's tail; later symbolic of Nayaka identity.
11. Mahanavami Dibba: a huge high platform used for ritual purposes; per Domingo Paes erected by Krishnadevaraya to mark his Odisha campaign; site of the nine-day Mahanavami festival and army reviews; three-tiered stepped platform with daily-life scenes - a royal platform, not a temple, and the Virupaksha temple does not stand on it.
12. Indo-Islamic influence is located in the secular zone: Lotus Mahal's arched roof in the Zenana enclosure for royal women, the domed elephant stable, the Queen's Bath with stepped design and Indo-Islamic arches; the monolithic rock-cut Lakshmi Narasimha on Shesha is called one of the masterpieces of Indian sculpture.
13. Kalyana Mandapa: a Hindu temple wedding hall; the Vitthala complex has one and the Lepakshi Veerbhadra temple has an incomplete one carrying the divine wedding of Shiva and Parvati - a Vijayanagara feature, with the reproduced 2019 item's own key marking the same answer.
14. Fall and recovery: defeated by a coalition of the Adil Shahi, Nizam Shahi, Qutub Shahi and Barid Shahi; capital conquered, looted and destroyed in 1565 at Talikota; capital moved to Penukonda then Chandragiri; ruins brought to light in 1800 by Colonel Colin Mackenzie, the first Surveyor General of India, with Alexander Rea contributing; Robert Sewell published *A Forgotten Empire* in 1900; UNESCO 1986, "Group of Monuments at Hampi".
15. Nayakas: Vijayanagara feudatories; after Talikota in 1565 the Madurai Nayakas asserted autonomy and ruled from the 16th to the 18th century; revitalised temples previously ransacked by the Delhi Sultans.
16. Periodisation: the Nayaka style continues the late Vijayanagara style, influenced by local Chola and Pandyan traditions; Pallavas and Cholas pioneered early Dravidian architecture, Nayakas late Dravidian architecture.
17. Centres: Madurai, Thanjavur, Tiruchirapalli, Gingee and Ikkeri; chief patron Thirumalai Nayak, also spelled Tirumala Nayaka, 17th century.
18. Plan: rectangular high-walled premises entered by east and west gopurams; concentric rectangles added within higher walls; four entrances, each wall's gopuram larger than the previous; a pillared hall and a square water tank outside.
19. Material: granite core, stucco - mortar over brick - for superstructures and gopurams; so visible gopura figures are renewed and their dates are not construction dates.
20. Devices: large gopurams, large tanks, wide prakaram corridors for procession and pradakshina; Rameswaram's Ramanathaswamy temple is claimed to have the longest prakaram in the world; narrative and composite columns; heavily painted gopurams featuring the Yali.
21. Meenakshi temple, Madurai, on the Vaigai: most of the present campus repaired, renovated and expanded in the 17th century by Tirumala Nayaka; Meenakshi (Parvati), Sundareshwar (Shiva) and her brother Alagar (Vishnu), representing the confluence of Shaivism, Shaktism and Vaishnavism; one of the 276 Paadal Petra Sthalam revered by the Tamil Saiva Nayanars of the 6th-9th centuries; Potramarai Kulam tank; 14 gopurams; the Tamil Nadu State Emblem is based on its gopuram; the month-long Chithirai festival in April-May.
22. Srirangam's Ranganathaswamy and Rameswaram's Ramanathaswamy had immense contribution from the Nayaka rulers - contribution to, not built by.

### Concept check

**Question:** State the most defensible version of the claim that Vijayanagara architecture is an amalgamation, name the traditions it draws on, and identify the single distinction that prevents the claim from collapsing into vagueness.

**Model answer:** The defensible version is that Vijayanagara amalgamates by register rather than by fusion. Its temple architecture combined the architectural features of the Cholas, the Hoysalas, the Pandyas, the Chalukyas and even the Indo-Islamic style of Bijapur, and it added native devices - the Raya Gopuram, introduced in temple architecture for the first time by Krishnadevaraya, and the wide chariot streets flanked by rows of pillared mandapas. The distinction that saves the claim is between the sacred and the royal zone: Islamicate arches and domes appear in the secular and royal buildings - the Lotus Mahal's arched roof in the Zenana enclosure, the domed elephant stable, the Queen's Bath with Indo-Islamic arches - while the temples retain Dravidian grammar. Without that distinction, "amalgamation" becomes an unfalsifiable compliment; with it, the claim identifies exactly which tradition was used for which purpose, which is what elucidation with examples requires.

**Misconception to avoid:** Claiming Indo-Islamic influence in the Hampi temples. The evidence locates it in the palace and court buildings, and a candidate who places arches on the Virupaksha has converted a precise, creditable observation into an error that the same record refutes.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Explain why a Nayaka temple complex could go on growing while a Chola temple could not, and assess what that difference implies for dating what a visitor sees today. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model answer

The difference is structural, and it has a direct consequence for evidence.

A sanctum tower is fixed at construction. Brihadishvara's vimana of approximately sixty-six metres could be surpassed only by building another temple, so Chola growth had nowhere to go once the tower was complete; the late phase accordingly shifted emphasis outward, the gopura becoming more dominant than the vimana at Chidambaram under Kulottunga I.

The Nayaka plan institutionalises that escape. The temple sits within rectangular premises behind high walls entered by gopurams east and west; concentric rectangles are then added within higher enclosing walls, leaving open space between the inner and outer perimeters, with four entrances and each wall's gopuram larger than the previous one. Madurai's Meenakshi temple carries fourteen. Growth is therefore additive and indefinite, because nothing already built has to be touched.

Material completes the mechanism. Granite is used for the temple core, while superstructures and gopurams are built up in stucco - mortar over brick - which makes great height affordable and, crucially, renewable.

The dating consequence is the examinable point. Stucco imagery is repeatedly refreshed, so the warriors, deities and Yali figures a visitor photographs on a gopuram today date the last renewal, not the structure. At Madurai the temple has older roots while most of the present campus is seventeenth-century Nayaka work.

Read a southern complex, therefore, as a sequence of datable additions rather than as one building of one date.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the fixed-tower against additive-enclosure contrast stated as the mechanism 4 + the Nayaka concentric plan with graded gopurams and the Madurai count 4 + the granite-core and stucco-superstructure material logic 3 + the renewal-dating consequence drawn explicitly 3 + the closing rule for reading a complex 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 19:** Twelve lessons of monuments have accumulated a great deal of evidence about how these buildings were made, paid for, used and carved. The next lesson gathers all of it into the four arguments an examiner is most likely to ask for - including the one about what temple sculpture can, and cannot, tell us about society.
