*Four arguments, each of which can carry a paragraph or a whole answer. This is the lesson that converts accumulated monuments into usable positions.*

### 1. Engineering: what these buildings actually had to solve

Temples are usually discussed as art and occasionally as religion, and almost never as construction. Yet every monument in this topic represents a solved structural problem, and several of the solutions are named on the record.

⚠️ **Foundations.** The **sandbox technique** at Ramappa fills the foundation pit with a **mixture of sand-lime, jaggery and black myrobalan fruit**, which **acts as a cushion**; the same technique appears at the **Thousand Pillar Temple** in Warangal, used **to strengthen its foundation**. A deformable, energy-absorbing foundation layer under a heavy masonry temple is a genuine engineering idea, and it is named, described and attached to two monuments - which makes it the single best construction fact in the topic.

⚠️ **Roofing and weight.** Ramappa's ceiling is **supported by light stones that float on water**. Whatever the exact material, the principle is weight reduction at the top of the structure, which is the classic problem of corbelled masonry.

⚠️ **Mass and permanence.** Brihadishvara is **entirely granite**, recorded as **the first fully granite temple in the world**, carrying a vimana of roughly sixty-six metres. Granite is durable and almost unworkable at speed; a temple built wholly from it implies quarrying, transport, dressing and lifting on an extraordinary organisational scale.

⚠️ **Composite construction.** Nayaka practice separates the problem: **granite for the core**, **stucco - mortar over brick - for superstructures and gopurams**. That is a rational division. The sanctum needs permanence; the gateway needs height, figural richness and the possibility of renewal. It is also why gopura imagery is repeatedly refreshed, and therefore why the date of a visible figure is not the date of the structure.

⚠️ **The subtractive extreme.** The Ellora Kailasa, excavated as a complete temple from living rock, and the Pancha Rathas, five temples from a single rock using all known forms of plan and elevation, show the opposite method carried to its limit: no correction is possible, so the whole design must be resolved before the first cut.

### 2. Material drives form - the chain, stated once and reused

⚠️ **The claim.** Across this topic, material does not merely execute a design; it determines what designs are available. The chain runs: local geology, then workable material, then possible carving depth and span, then the surface character and the plan devices that follow.

The visual above sets out eight cases. Two are worth spelling out because they are the strongest.

**Khajuraho.** Fine-grained sandstone in buff, pink and pale-yellow shades takes deep undercutting without shearing, so figures can be released from the wall plane. That is what makes an unwalled, ratha-offset elevation worth covering with sculpture.

**Bengal.** With no building stone in the delta, burnt bricks and clay are the principal material, so relief is moulded and repeated rather than undercut - and the region's vernacular bamboo-hut roof is reproduced in masonry as the Bangla roof, later adopted by Mughal architects.

⚠️ **The qualification that keeps this honest.** Material explains surface, fabric and some plan devices; it does not explain the shared grammar. Bengal builds a tall curving shikhara crowned by a large amalaka **similar to the Odisha school** in an entirely different material, which shows that form can be inherited across a material change. State both halves.

### 3. Patronage: the full spectrum, not just kings

The single most common weakness in patronage answers is that they name only emperors. The record supports a far wider account.

**Sovereign rulers**, where independence or consolidation precedes building: Dhanga taking the title *maharajadhiraja*; Rajaraja I at Thanjavur; Krishnadevaraya at Hampi; Narasimhadeva I at Konark.

**Royal women**, and this is the strongest under-used material in the topic: **Queen Lokmahadevi** commissioned the Pattadakal Virupaksha to commemorate Vikramaditya II's conquest of Kanchipuram; **Queen Trilok Mahadevi** commissioned the neighbouring Mallikarjuna; **Queen Tribhuvana-Mahadevi** of the Bhauma Kara dynasty built the Vaitala temple at Bhubaneswar in the ninth century; and **Queen Shembiyan Mahadevi** was a major patron across the reigns of her husband Gandaraditya and her son Uttama I, under whom many brick temples were rebuilt in stone. Four named women, three dynasties, two regions, one of them commissioning a war memorial and another running a material-upgrade programme.

**A general rather than a king:** Ramappa was constructed **in the interest of Recharla Rudra, a general of the Kakatiya king Ganapati Deva**.

**A merchant guild:** the **Ayyavole 500**, whose inscription is found in the Lad Khan temple at Aihole and whose inscriptions between the ninth and fourteenth centuries **record endowments made to temples**, linking trading communities across Tamil Nadu, Karnataka and Andhra Pradesh.

**A later donor across a religious boundary:** the Thousand Pillar Temple, damaged by the Tughlaq invasion of the Deccan in the fourteenth century, was reconstructed with a **grant from Mir Osman Ali Khan, the 7th Nizam of Hyderabad**.

**Named craftsmen:** **Dasoja, Chavana and Mallithamma** among the Hoysalas, and **Ramappa**, after whom a temple is named rather than after the king who commissioned it or its presiding deity.

⚠️ **The argument to write.** Indian temple building was financed and executed by a wider society than the dynastic record suggests: sovereigns, royal women, military officers, merchant corporations and, in rare and valuable cases, the makers themselves. ❌ Do not assign a specific temple to a specific named sculptor, and do not convert a single recorded grant into a general claim about a donor's religious policy.

### 4. Ritual movement as a design driver

⚠️ **The claim.** Circulation is not a by-product of temple plans; it is one of the things plans are for. The evidence supports this at four levels.

- The **covered pradakshina path** around the sanctum appears as a distinct addition in the second Gupta stage, and is recorded as a Nagara feature.
- The **Hoysala** star-shaped four-foot-high platform carries an **extended terrace surrounding the temple** which **acts as the pradakshinapath** - the same rite solved in the open, on a star plan.
- The **Nayaka prakaram** is a wide corridor **facilitating processions and devotional circumambulation**, with Rameswaram claimed to have the longest in the world; the innermost prakaram is typically enclosed.
- **Vijayanagara chariot streets**, wide and flanked by rows of pillared mandapas, extend ritual movement outside the temple altogether, into the city.

⚠️ **The synthesis.** Over roughly a millennium, circumambulation moves outward: from a dark enclosed passage around the sanctum, to an open terrace around the building, to corridors around successive enclosures, to processional streets through the town. The same rite, expressed at four increasing scales. That is a genuinely original-sounding argument that is fully supported by the evidence, and it pairs exactly with the vimana-to-gopura migration from Lesson 18: both describe emphasis moving outward.

### 5. Temple sculpture as social evidence - the registers and the ceiling

This supports a demand family that asks how medieval Indian temple sculptures represent the social life of their day - a demand whose directive typically supplies the claim and asks you to explain it, which means the marks lie in **qualifying and evidencing** rather than in repeating it.

⚠️ **The thesis.** Temple sculpture is *selected* social evidence: it is representation commissioned by patrons, so it evidences what patrons chose to make visible. That is a real historical fact and a bounded one.

⚠️ **The registers, with their ceilings**, are set out in the visual above. Three are worth stating in prose because they carry an answer.

**Performance and instruction.** Dancers, musicians, teachers and disciples appear in the Khajuraho programme. This evidences that performance and the transmission of learning were socially valued and visually codified. It does not evidence the repertoire performed or the extent of literacy.

**Ritual and life-cycle.** The ninth-century **Kalyanasundara Murti** is remarkable for the manner in which **panigrahana, the ceremony of marriage, is represented by two separate statuettes**: **Shiva with his extended right hand accepts Parvati's right hand, who is depicted with a bashful expression and taking a step forward.** This evidences that a marriage rite was known, codified and worth depicting in detail. It does not evidence that the rite was performed identically outside elite contexts. ⚠️ The iconographic reading of the image belongs to Topic 06; what is used here is the social inference and its limit.

**Labour and craft.** Signed Hoysala work by Dasoja, Chavana and Mallithamma, and the Ayyavole guild's inscription at Aihole, evidence that named artisans and organised merchant bodies existed and could claim visibility. They do not evidence wage levels, workshop size or artisan social status.

⚠️ **The register that shows the limit, and which a good answer names explicitly.** The erotic register cannot be read as a record of social behaviour, and royal temples cannot be read as evidence of village life. Naming a limit case is what converts assertion into method.

⚠️ **The master rule to state in one sentence:** temple sculpture is representation commissioned by patrons, so it is reliable evidence of what patrons chose to make visible and unreliable evidence of anything they had no reason to depict.

### Criticism and reply: is the patronage-legitimacy argument circular?

**The objection.** If every great temple is explained as a statement of political legitimacy, the explanation becomes unfalsifiable. A newly independent dynasty builds to assert itself; an established empire builds to display its consolidation; a feudatory builds to claim autonomy. Any building fits.

**The reply.** The argument becomes falsifiable when it is tied to *timing and scale* rather than to motive. The claim is not that rulers built temples because they wanted legitimacy; it is that the most ambitious building follows political self-assertion, which is a testable ordering. The record supplies the ordering in several cases - Dhanga's independence before the Khajuraho programme, Vikramaditya II's conquest before the Pattadakal Virupaksha, Vishnuvardhana's victory before Belur, Krishnadevaraya's Odisha campaign before the Mahanavami Dibba - and it also supplies counter-cases that the motive-only version cannot accommodate: a general's commission at Ramappa, a merchant guild's endowments at Aihole, a queen's programme of rebuilding brick temples in stone, and a twentieth-century Nizam's restoration grant. A theory with named counter-cases is not circular; it is bounded.

### Examination use

This lesson supplies the analytical spine for four separate demand families: engineering and craftsmanship, material and regional style, patronage and state formation, and sculpture as social evidence. For the social-evidence demand in particular, the structure is: the selected-evidence thesis, three registers with named monuments, one register that shows the limit, the commissioned-representation rule, and a verdict. That structure also protects against the commonest failure, which is reading sculpture as a photograph of society.

### Revision notes

1. Sandbox technique: the foundation pit filled with sand-lime, jaggery and black myrobalan, acting as a cushion - used at Ramappa and at the Thousand Pillar Temple, Warangal.
2. Floating stone: light stones that float on water support Ramappa's ceiling - weight reduction at the top of the structure.
3. Brihadishvara is entirely granite, the first fully granite temple in the world, with a vimana of approximately 66 m - implying quarrying, transport and lifting at extraordinary scale.
4. Nayaka composite construction: granite core, stucco over brick for superstructures and gopurams - scale plus renewability, so visible gopura figures are not dated by the structure.
5. Subtractive extremes: the Ellora Kailasa excavated as a whole temple; the Pancha Rathas carved from one rock using all known forms of plan and elevation.
6. Material-to-form chain: fine sandstone permits deep undercutting (Khajuraho); soft red sandstone permits cave excavation (Badami); soapstone is soft when quarried and hardens (Hoysala); granite gives mass and austerity (Chola); pinkish sandstone with black basalt gives polished figure sculpture (Ramappa); terracotta brick gives moulded relief (Pala-Sena); stucco gives renewable scale (Nayaka).
7. Qualification: material explains surface, fabric and some plan devices, not the shared grammar - Bengal builds an Odisha-like amalaka-crowned shikhara in an entirely different material.
8. Patronage spectrum: sovereign rulers; royal women - Lokmahadevi, Trilok Mahadevi, Tribhuvana-Mahadevi, Shembiyan Mahadevi; a general - Recharla Rudra at Ramappa; a merchant guild - the Ayyavole 500 at Aihole; a later donor - Mir Osman Ali Khan for the Thousand Pillar Temple.
9. Named makers: Dasoja, Chavana and Mallithamma among the Hoysalas; Ramappa, after whom a temple is named rather than its king or deity.
10. Ritual movement at four scales: covered pradakshina around the sanctum; the Hoysala open terrace on a star platform; the Nayaka prakaram corridor; the Vijayanagara chariot street.
11. Circumambulation moves outward over a millennium, exactly as architectural emphasis migrates from vimana to gopura.
12. Sculpture-as-evidence thesis: temple sculpture is representation commissioned by patrons, so it evidences what patrons chose to make visible.
13. Registers with ceilings: performance evidences valuation not repertoire; instruction evidences a value not literacy; courtly scenes evidence depiction not description; the 63 Nayanars at Darasuram evidence bhakti entering royal art not inner belief; women patrons evidence elite agency not women's general condition; the Kalyanasundara Murti's panigrahana by two statuettes evidences a codified marriage rite not its uniform practice; signed work and guilds evidence existence and visibility not wages or status.
14. The limit case to name: the erotic register cannot be read as social behaviour, and royal temples cannot be read as village life.
15. The patronage-legitimacy argument is bounded, not circular, because it claims an ordering of timing and scale and admits named counter-cases.

### Concept check

**Question:** A question asks how medieval Indian temple sculptures represent the social life of their day. Construct the answer's skeleton in five moves, naming the evidence for each, and state the sentence that prevents the answer from over-claiming.

**Model answer:** Move one, the thesis: temple sculpture is selected social evidence, because it is representation commissioned by patrons. Move two, a performance-and-instruction register: dancers, musicians, teachers and disciples in the Khajuraho programme evidence that performance and the transmission of learning were valued and visually codified. Move three, a ritual and life-cycle register: the ninth-century Kalyanasundara Murti renders panigrahana through two separate statuettes, Shiva's extended right hand accepting Parvati's as she steps forward with a bashful expression, evidencing a known and codified marriage rite. Move four, a labour and patronage register: signed Hoysala work by Dasoja, Chavana and Mallithamma, the Ayyavole guild's inscription at Aihole recording temple endowments, and Shembiyan Mahadevi's programme of rebuilding brick temples in stone evidence named artisans, organised merchant bodies and elite women patrons. Move five, the limit: the erotic register cannot be read as social behaviour and royal temples cannot be read as village life. The controlling sentence is that temple sculpture evidences what patrons chose to make visible - reliable for the represented world, unreliable for whatever a patron had no reason to depict.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a limitation as a weakness in the answer. In this demand family the limitation *is* the analysis: anyone can list carved subjects, while stating precisely what a commissioned representation can and cannot prove is the discrimination the question exists to test.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Examine the evidence for technological and organisational sophistication in medieval Indian temple construction. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model answer

Medieval Indian temple building shows sophistication in four areas - foundation, superstructure, material selection and the organisation of labour and finance - and the evidence is specific enough to be examined rather than asserted.

Foundation engineering is the clearest case. The Ramappa temple in Mulugu district used the sandbox technique, filling the foundation pit with a mixture of sand-lime, jaggery and black myrobalan to act as a cushion; the same technique strengthened the Thousand Pillar Temple at Warangal. Ramappa's ceiling was additionally carried by light stones described as floating on water, reducing load at the top of the structure.

Superstructure and material show deliberate selection. Brihadishvara at Thanjavur is entirely granite, described as the first fully granite temple in the world, carrying a vimana of roughly sixty-six metres; Nayaka builders instead separated the problem, using granite for the temple core and stucco over brick for gopurams, achieving height and renewable ornament. Hoysala builders chose chloritic schist, soft when quarried and hardening over time, which permits intricate carving that then endures.

Organisation is evidenced by scale and by record. Ramappa took around forty years and is known by its architect's name rather than its patron's; Dasoja, Chavana and Mallithamma are named Hoysala architects and sculptors; the Ayyavole 500 guild's inscriptions record endowments to temples; and the Airavatesvara Tamil inscription prescribes worship, donations, festivals and administration.

The examined limit is that sources celebrate results without describing quarrying, transport, wages or workforce, so sophistication is demonstrable while its social cost remains undocumented.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = four-area framing stated and then followed 4 + foundation evidence with the exact sandbox composition and two monuments 5 + material-selection reasoning across granite, stucco and soapstone 4 + organisational evidence from duration, named makers, guild endowment and administrative inscription 4 + the examined limit on undocumented labour and cost 3.**

**Bridge to Lesson 20:** All of this was built, used, endowed - and then, in several cases, forgotten. The last substantive lesson follows the monuments out of use and back into visibility, and confronts the one question where an answer must be careful about what is true *today* rather than what was true then.

## Lesson 20 - Loss, rediscovery, protection and the discipline of a current claim

### Visual: the arc, and what may be claimed at each stage

        Thousand Pillar Temple + Warangal Fort + Kakatiya Kala Thoranam

*The diagram is also a rule: describe the past from the fabric and the record, and claim the present only from something dated.*

### 1. The problem: a topic where the present tense is dangerous

Everything in Lessons 2 to 19 is a claim about the past, and the past does not change. The moment an answer says "today", "currently", "at present" or "is protected", it has made a claim that can go out of date between the writing of a textbook and the sitting of an examination.

The discipline is simple and worth stating as a rule: **inscription years are stable facts and may be cited; counts, statuses, budgets, danger-listings and management measures are dynamic and must be routed to a dated official record.** A candidate who writes "Khajuraho was inscribed on the World Heritage List in 1986" is safe forever. A candidate who writes "India has N World Heritage Sites" has staked the sentence on a number that changes.

### 2. The Khajuraho arc, with its named actors

✅ **Fact - external notice while the site mattered.** The **11th-century Persian traveller Al-Biruni mentioned Khajuraho as the capital of Jajahuti**, and **Ibn Battuta, who visited India in the 14th century, referred to Khajuraho as "Kajarra"**.

✅ **Fact - the loss.** **The temple town sank from public consciousness soon after the Chandella dynasty expired in the 13th century.**

✅ **Fact - the recovery.** **In 1838, T. S. Burt, a British engineer, ventured into the forest in search of the "wonders of a place called Khajrao". Later, the temples were found under the leadership of Alexander Cunningham.**

✅ **Fact - the inscription.** **Because of their outstanding architecture, diversity of temple forms, and testimony to the Chandela civilisation, the monuments at Khajuraho were inscribed on the UNESCO World Heritage List in 1986.**

⚠️ **Two analytical points.** First, the loss is attributed to the *end of the dynasty*, not to an act of destruction - the town ceased to matter when its political centre did. That is a different mechanism from Hampi's, and contrasting the two makes a strong paragraph. Second, the inscription citation is unusually useful because it states the *grounds*: outstanding architecture, diversity of temple forms, and testimony to the Chandela civilisation. Those three grounds are effectively a summary of Lessons 11 to 13, and quoting them closes a Khajuraho answer on the site's own recognised significance rather than on a generic conservation sentence.

### 3. The Hampi arc, and what the contrast shows

✅ **Fact.** Vijayanagara was **defeated by a coalition of the Adil Shahi, Nizam Shahi, Qutub Shahi and Barid Shahi**; the capital was **conquered, looted and destroyed in 1565 in the Battle of Talikota**, after which Hampi remained in ruins, the capital shifting to **Penukonda** and then **Chandragiri**. The ruins were **brought to light in 1800 by Colonel Colin Mackenzie**, an archaeological engineer and **the first Surveyor General of India**, with **Alexander Rea** also contributing; **Robert Sewell published *A Forgotten Empire* in 1900**; and **UNESCO gave Hampi World Heritage status in 1986** as the **"Group of Monuments at Hampi"**.

⚠️ **The contrast, which is the examinable content.** Khajuraho was *forgotten*; Hampi was *destroyed*. Both were recovered by named individuals in the first half of the nineteenth century and inscribed in the same year. The pairing shows that heritage visibility is a function of modern survey and scholarship as much as of ancient importance - Mackenzie was Surveyor General, Cunningham led the archaeological effort, Sewell wrote the book that gave Hampi its modern name in English. That observation belongs to this topic because it concerns how the architectural record itself came to be known.

### 4. Status anchors, used correctly

✅ The inscription years listed in the visual above are all firmly recorded and may be cited directly: **Ellora 1983**, **Mahabalipuram 1984**, **Konark 1984**, **Khajuraho 1986**, **Hampi 1986**, **Pattadakal 1987**, the **Great Living Chola Temples 1987 with a 2004 extension**, **Ramappa 2021**, and the **Sacred Ensembles of the Hoysalas 2023**.

❌ **Three recorded cautions.** The Great Living Chola Temples is **one serial property comprising three temple complexes**, not three properties. **Badami and Aihole are not inscribed** - they sit on the Tentative List with Pattadakal under "Evolution of Temple Architecture - Aihole-Badami-Pattadakal". And the **Thousand Pillar Temple, Warangal Fort and Kakatiya Kala Thoranam** are on the Tentative List, not inscribed.

⚠️ **What must be routed.** Any count of inscribed properties, any danger-listing history, any statement about institutional structure, funding or policy, and any claim about current management belongs to Topic 14 and to the dated official record. This session cites years and grounds only.

### 5. The living monument, and the tension it creates

✅ **Fact.** At Khajuraho, **the Matangeshvara temple remains an active site of worship**. ✅ Three Chola temples are inscribed under the title **"Great Living Chola Temples"** precisely because they remain functioning temples. ✅ The Meenakshi temple at Madurai hosts the **month-long Chithirai festival** each April-May, and its gopurams are built with **stucco over brick**, a material that is renewed rather than preserved unchanged.

⚠️ **The tension, stated as an examiner's angle.** A monument in worship is not a museum object. Conservation at such a site must accommodate ritual use, footfall, lighting, offerings and periodic renewal of surfaces, and the renewal of stucco imagery on a gopuram is a *tradition* as much as a repair. Meanwhile at Khajuraho the temples stand on open, unwalled terraces, so visitor access and the display surface are literally the same surface - the tourism-versus-integrity trade-off is structural there, not incidental. And a World Heritage inscription, which brings attention and visitors, therefore imposes costs as well as conferring benefits. ⚠️ A complete answer notes this trade-off rather than treating inscription as an unqualified good.

### 6. The single current linkage in this topic

📰 **Dated current anchor.** The **Parliamentary Standing Committee on Transport, Tourism and Culture**, chaired by **Sanjay Kumar Jha**, presented its report on the **Demands for Grants (2026-27) of the Ministry of Culture** in Parliament on **25 March 2026**. It recommended that the Ministry, in coordination with the **Archaeological Survey of India**, undertake a **"comprehensive review of maintenance standards and visitor facilities at all World Heritage Sites"** and prepare a **time-bound upgradation plan** so that the condition, interpretation, infrastructure and management of these sites are aligned with their global recognition. It further recommended a **five-year UNESCO nomination strategy** with a pipeline of **at least ten potential nominations**, a structured engagement plan for multinational nominations, and dedicated staff and budgetary provision for preparing nomination dossiers, to be presented within one year. The report records that the **ASI maintains 3,685 centrally protected monuments through 38 circles** and that the **ASI was allocated ₹1,235.78 crore for 2026-27**, constituting **36.17 per cent** of the Ministry's total allocation.

⚠️ **How to use it, and how not to.** This item is used once, in this lesson, for one purpose: it shows that the conservation-versus-access tension described above is not a textbook abstraction but a live institutional problem, being addressed at the level of parliamentary oversight and budget. ❌ It is not evidence about Khajuraho's architecture, it asserts nothing about the condition of any named monument, and no figure from it should be carried into an answer about temple form. Budgetary and institutional detail belongs to Topic 14; a Mains answer on temple architecture needs at most one clause from it, and only where the question genuinely reaches conservation.

⚠️ **The honest boundary.** No other current item is used anywhere in these lessons. The other twenty record truthfully in their pre-teach checklists that no current linkage is required, because the material is architectural and historical and a forced current anchor would be decoration rather than evidence.

### Comparative note: three ways a temple survives

| Mode of survival | Example | What it permits an answer to say | What it forbids |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Ruin, recovered and conserved** | Hampi - destroyed 1565, recovered 1800, inscribed 1986 | Full architectural description of what stands; a destruction-and-recovery arc | Claims about original completeness or about what was lost |
| **Abandoned, rediscovered, conserved** | Khajuraho - lost after the 13th century, sought by Burt in 1838, inscribed 1986 | The disappearance mechanism, the named rediscoverers, the inscription grounds | A current count of standing temples, which belongs to the dated official record |
| **Continuously living** | Matangeshvara at Khajuraho; the Great Living Chola Temples; Meenakshi, Madurai | That ritual use is unbroken; that conservation must accommodate worship; that renewed stucco is tradition, not damage | Treating visible surfaces as original fabric, or dating figures on a renewed gopuram to the structure |

### Examination use

Conservation appears in three ways. As a **closing line**: every Khajuraho, Chola, Hampi or Hoysala answer should end with the inscription year, which is a stable fact and signals current awareness. As a **demand in its own right**: "conservation of temple heritage" wants rediscovery, protection, the living-monument problem, the tourism-integrity trade-off and the institutional route - not a generic appeal to preserve heritage. And as a **discipline**: the examiner is testing whether the candidate can distinguish a stable date from a dynamic status, which is exactly the skill this lesson trains.

### Revision notes

1. Rule: inscription years are stable and citable; counts, danger-listings, budgets and management measures are dynamic and must be routed to the dated official record and to Topic 14.
2. Al-Biruni, 11th century, records Khajuraho as the capital of Jajahuti; Ibn Battuta, 14th century, calls it "Kajarra".
3. Khajuraho sank from public consciousness soon after the Chandella dynasty expired in the 13th century - loss by political irrelevance, not by destruction.
4. T. S. Burt, a British engineer, ventured into the forest in 1838 in search of "a place called Khajrao"; the temples were later found under the leadership of Alexander Cunningham.
5. Khajuraho was inscribed on the UNESCO World Heritage List in 1986 for outstanding architecture, diversity of temple forms, and testimony to the Chandela civilisation - quote the grounds.
6. Hampi was conquered, looted and destroyed in 1565 at the Battle of Talikota; the capital moved to Penukonda then Chandragiri.
7. Hampi's ruins were brought to light in 1800 by Colonel Colin Mackenzie, the first Surveyor General of India, with Alexander Rea contributing; Robert Sewell published *A Forgotten Empire* in 1900; UNESCO inscribed the "Group of Monuments at Hampi" in 1986.
8. The contrast: Khajuraho was forgotten, Hampi destroyed - yet both were recovered by named surveyors in the early nineteenth century and inscribed in the same year.
9. Inscription years citable here: Ellora 1983; Mahabalipuram 1984; Konark 1984; Khajuraho 1986; Hampi 1986; Pattadakal 1987; Great Living Chola Temples 1987, extended 2004; Ramappa 2021; Sacred Ensembles of the Hoysalas 2023.
10. Not inscribed: Aihole and Badami sit on the Tentative List with Pattadakal under "Evolution of Temple Architecture - Aihole-Badami-Pattadakal"; so do the Thousand Pillar Temple, Warangal Fort and Kakatiya Kala Thoranam.
11. The Great Living Chola Temples is one serial property of three temple complexes, not three properties.
12. Living monuments: Matangeshvara at Khajuraho remains in worship; the Chola temples are inscribed as "living"; Madurai's month-long Chithirai festival continues; Nayaka gopura stucco is renewed rather than frozen.
13. The trade-off: at Khajuraho the unwalled terraces make visitor access and the display surface the same surface, so tourism and integrity are structurally in tension, and inscription brings cost as well as benefit.
14. The session's single current linkage: the Parliamentary Standing Committee on Transport, Tourism and Culture report on the Demands for Grants (2026-27) of the Ministry of Culture, presented on 25 March 2026, recommending a comprehensive review of maintenance standards and visitor facilities at all World Heritage Sites with the ASI and a time-bound upgradation plan, plus a five-year nomination strategy with at least ten potential nominations; it records that the ASI maintains 3,685 centrally protected monuments through 38 circles and was allocated ₹1,235.78 crore for 2026-27, 36.17 per cent of the Ministry's allocation.
15. That item evidences an institutional tension only; it asserts nothing about the condition of any named monument and must not be imported into an architectural description.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why "Khajuraho is a UNESCO World Heritage Site with 22 surviving temples under ASI protection" is a riskier sentence than "Khajuraho was inscribed on the World Heritage List in 1986 for its outstanding architecture, diversity of temple forms and testimony to the Chandela civilisation", and state the general rule the comparison illustrates.

**Model answer:** The first sentence mixes a stable fact with two volatile ones. The inscription is permanent, but a count of surviving temples is a site-inventory figure that depends on the protecting authority's own dated record and on what is being counted - standing, protected, or within the inscribed boundary - and a protection statement is an administrative status that can change. Neither can be verified from the architectural record, so both are unsupported claims dressed as facts. The second sentence cites only the inscription year and the recorded grounds for it, both of which are fixed and quotable, and the grounds additionally summarise the site's architectural significance, so the sentence does analytical work as well as being safe. The general rule is that inscription years and recorded grounds are stable facts and may be cited freely, while counts, protection statuses, danger-listings, budgets and management measures are dynamic and must be routed to a dated official record rather than asserted from memory.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that adding more current-sounding detail strengthens a conservation paragraph. Volatile numbers weaken it, because a single stale figure discredits the surrounding analysis; a dated year plus a stated trade-off is both safer and more analytical.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Discuss the challenges of conserving India's temple heritage, using the Khajuraho group as your principal example. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model answer

Temple conservation in India faces four distinct challenges, and Khajuraho exhibits three of them structurally.

The first is recovery. Khajuraho sank from public consciousness soon after the Chandella dynasty expired in the thirteenth century, and was sought out only in 1838 by the British engineer T. S. Burt, the temples being found subsequently under Alexander Cunningham. Conservation therefore begins after a five-century gap in which the site's own record was lost, so most of what is known must be read from fabric rather than documents - unlike the Chola temples, which carry inscriptions prescribing their administration.

The second is exposure. Khajuraho's temples are lofty structures on high platform terraces with no enclosure wall, which is precisely what makes their sculptural programme so ambitious - and precisely what leaves the display surface identical with the visitor surface. Access and integrity are the same problem here, not competing priorities.

The third is living use. Matangeshvara remains an active site of worship, so conservation must accommodate ritual rather than freeze fabric - the same requirement that defines the Great Living Chola Temples, inscribed in 1987 and extended in 2004.

The fourth is institutional. Khajuraho's 1986 inscription, granted for its outstanding architecture, diversity of temple forms and testimony to the Chandela civilisation, brings visitors and scrutiny together; in March 2026 a parliamentary committee recommended a comprehensive review of maintenance standards and visitor facilities at all World Heritage Sites with a time-bound upgradation plan.

Dated status, not assumed status, should close any such answer.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the four-challenge framing carried through the answer 3 + the recovery gap with named rediscoverers and dates 3 + exposure argued from the unwalled morphology rather than asserted 4 + the living-monument requirement with Matangeshvara and the Chola case 3 + the dated institutional anchor used once and correctly bounded 2.**

**Bridge to Lesson 21:** Everything needed is now on the table - vocabulary, families, regions, the Chandella case, the southern trajectory, engineering, patronage and conservation. The last lesson does only one thing: converts it into answers.

## Lesson 21 - Building the answer: demands, theses, structures and verdicts

### Visual: from demand to verdict, in six moves

```text
   READ THE VERB AND THE NOUN
   Estimate | Comment | Elucidate | Discuss | Examine | Compare | Trace
   + Chandella? Pallava? Chola? Vijayanagara? Nagara/Dravida? sculpture?
   conservation?
                          |
                          v
   PICK ONE THESIS - a SENTENCE, not a definition
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | Chandella : vigour and breadth are ONE achievement             |
   | Pallava   : a TRANSITION, not a style                          |
   | Chola     : three achievements SURVIVE TOGETHER, and LIVING    |
   | Vijayanagara: amalgamation BY REGISTER, not by fusion          |
   | Vesara    : a label for a PRACTICE OF SELECTION                |
   | Nagara/Dravida: CENTRIPETAL vs CENTRIFUGAL composition         |
   | Regional schools: MATERIAL AND ENVIRONMENT set the problem     |
   | Sculpture-as-evidence: SELECTED evidence, commissioned         |
   | Patronage : building FOLLOWS political self-assertion          |
   | Conservation: stable YEARS, dynamic STATUS                     |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                          |
                          v
   VOCABULARY FRAME - one short sentence using 3-4 exact terms
   (garbhagriha | antarala | mandapa | jagati | shikhara/vimana |
    amalaka/kalasha | gopuram | prakaram | ratha | urushringa)
                          |
                          v
   NAMED EVIDENCE - monument + patron/dynasty + approximate date
   (never an adjective where a name will fit)
                          |
                          v
   ONE COMPARISON + ONE LIMIT
   (a rival school, a counter-case, an incompleteness, a source silence)
                          |
                          v
   VERDICT IN THE QUESTION'S OWN WORDS + dated STATUS close
```

*Six moves. Every model answer in these lessons uses them, and so should yours.*

### 1. Decode the demand before writing a word

| If the question says | It is really testing | Do NOT write |
|---|---|---|
| A two-part quotation about a school's sculpture, with *elucidate* | Two separable claims - how the figures are carved, and what is carved - each needing its own evidence | A paragraph about erotic sculpture and nothing else |
| A dynasty's *contribution*, with *estimate* | Weighing: name the innovations, show the sequence, state what the contribution did not amount to | A monument list with no development logic |
| A claim about why a dynasty is *remembered*, with *comment* | Why the memory survives: scale, technique, portable icons, inscriptions and continued use | Praise adjectives without a named temple or technique |
| A claim that a style *amalgamates*, with *elucidate with examples* | Which inherited element came from where, plus what was contemporary and what was invented | "Eclectic synthesis" as an unexamined compliment |
| *Compare Nagara and Dravida temple architecture* | Morphology at named monuments, with the reason for each difference | A dynastic list masquerading as a comparison |
| *Regional schools of Nagara architecture* | Odisha, Khajuraho, Solanki - and Pala-Sena - as distinct solutions | Treating "Nagara" as one uniform style |
| *Trace the development of temple architecture in South India* | A trajectory with a mechanism: rock-cut, monolithic, structural, imperial vimana, gateway-dominated enclosure | A dynasty-by-dynasty paragraph list with nothing explained |
| *Vesara / the Deccan hybrid style* | Whether you can describe plan, tower and material at named monuments before using the label | "Vesara is a mixture of Nagara and Dravida" as the whole answer |
| *Temple architecture as evidence of state formation or patronage* | Patronage as legitimacy: newly independent or newly imperial dynasties build | "Kings were religious, so they built temples" |
| *Temple sculpture as a source of social history* | Secular registers read as evidence, with the commissioned-representation caution | Reading sculpture as a literal photograph of society |
| *Conservation of temple heritage* | Rediscovery, inscription, living-worship status, tourism pressure, institutional route | "Government should preserve our heritage" |

### 2. The qualified theses, written out

A thesis is a sentence that could be disagreed with. These are the ones the evidence in these lessons supports.

⚠️ **Chandella.** "Khajuraho's sculptors achieved vigour through carving method - deep relief in fine-grained sandstone, twisting and flexed bodies integrated into the wall mass - and breadth through programme, placing deities, Vyalas, dancers, musicians, worshippers, courtly life and maithuna reliefs within one continuous scheme; the erotic register is one part of that breadth, not its definition."

⚠️ **Pallava.** "The Pallava contribution is best estimated as a *transition* rather than a style: within roughly three centuries the same dynasty moved from excavated rock-cut mandapas to monolithic rathas to structural stone temples, while its capital Kanchipuram sustained Sanskrit letters."

⚠️ **Chola.** "Chola pride rests on three achievements that survive together - monumental granite temple building, portable bronze icons and an inscriptional-administrative record - and on the fact that these temples remain living places of worship rather than ruins."

⚠️ **Vijayanagara.** "Vijayanagara amalgamated by register rather than by fusion: Dravidian grammar for temples, Islamicate form for palace and court buildings, and new imperial devices - the Raya Gopuram and the chariot street - for a capital that had to stage ceremony as well as house worship."

⚠️ **Vesara.** "Vesara is best defended as a label for a practice rather than a third box: at Pattadakal the same builders raised a Dravida shikhara over the Virupaksha and a Nagara shikhara over the Papanatha, and the Hoysalas are described as Dravidian in morphology with Bhumija and Nagara influences - so Deccan patrons selected from both grammars, and the category came afterwards to describe what they had already done."

⚠️ **Comparative.** "Nagara and Dravida are best distinguished by where architectural emphasis falls: Nagara concentrates it on the sanctum spire in an open, wall-less terrace, while Dravida distributes it across a walled axial enclosure with a pyramidal vimana and an increasingly dominant gateway."

⚠️ **Regional schools.** "Regional temple schools are solutions, not preferences: the environment sets the problem, the available material sets the possible answers, and what we call a style is the solution that resulted - which is why Bengal builds in terracotta and Bundelkhand in deeply undercut sandstone while sharing one superstructure grammar."

⚠️ **Patronage.** "In Bundelkhand as in the Tamil country, the most ambitious temple building follows political self-assertion - Dhanga's independence and Rajaraja I's imperial consolidation - which makes the temple a statement of sovereignty as much as of devotion, while queens, generals and merchant guilds show that the patronage base was wider than the dynastic record suggests."

⚠️ **Sculpture as evidence.** "Medieval temple sculpture is selected social evidence: it is representation commissioned by patrons, so it reliably evidences what patrons chose to make visible and cannot be read as a description of society at large."

### 3. Mark-scaled structures

| Marks and demand | Structure |
|---|---|
| **10, Chandella, 150 words** | Thesis separating vigour from breadth → carving method and material → three or four named subject registers → one comparative line (Konark and Bhubaneswar parallel) → Kandariya Mahadeva as culmination → 1986 inscription close |
| **10, Pallava, 150 words** | Thesis (transition) → Mahendravarman I rock-cut beginning with the Mandagapattu inscription → Mamalla monolithic rathas → Rajasimha structural Shore Temple → 1984 inscription close with the three stated limits |
| **10, Nagara-Dravida comparison, 150 words** | Enclosure as the generative difference → tank and gateway consequences → the vimana-to-gopura migration as proof → the one-vimana rule → centripetal-against-centrifugal close |
| **15, Chola, 250 words** | Thesis (three surviving achievements) → phased development with named patrons → Brihadishvara as technical climax → bronzes and inscriptions → limits (incompleteness, formula superseded, undocumented labour) → living-temple close with 1987 and 2004 |
| **15, Vijayanagara amalgamation, 250 words** | Thesis (amalgamation by register) → the five named borrowed traditions → Islamicate form confined to the royal zone with three named buildings → native inventions: Raya Gopuram and chariot streets → cautions (Mahanavami Dibba not a temple; no Indo-Islamic form in the temples) → 1565 and 1986 close |
| **15, Vesara or Deccan hybridity** | Thesis (a label for a practice) → Aihole incubator, Badami refinement, Pattadakal culmination → the Virupaksha-Papanatha pair on one site as decisive evidence → the three-source Hoysala formulation → label caution → verdict |
| **15, regional schools of Nagara** | Family grammar → Odisha deul types and walled precinct → Khajuraho unwalled terrace and figured wall → Solanki bare walls, torans and surya kund with Modhera's 1026-27 date → Pala-Sena terracotta and the Bangla roof → material-and-environment as the organising mechanism |
| **20, trace one southern trajectory** | Vocabulary frame → Pallava Mandagapattu, Pancha Rathas, Shore Temple → Chola Narttamalai, Brihadishvara, the incomplete Gangaikondacholapuram → Deccan counter-current at Aihole, Badami, Pattadakal → Rashtrakuta monolithic Kailasa → Hoysala Belur, Halebidu, Somanathapura → Vijayanagara Hampi with the Raya Gopuram → Nayaka Meenakshi with graded gopurams → the vimana-to-gopura shift as the single organising mechanism → status years → graded verdict |
| **20, any broad temple demand** | Vocabulary frame → three or four regional schools with named monuments *and named patrons* → patronage mechanism → sculptural and iconographic programme → social-history register with its caution → comparison across schools → conservation and status → graded verdict answering the exact wording |
| **10, temple sculpture as social evidence** | Selected-evidence thesis → three registers with named monuments → the register that shows the limit → the commissioned-representation rule → verdict |

### 4. The complete factual-risk control list

These are the errors this topic actually punishes. Read this list before any practice attempt.

❌ **Do not state** a percentage or fraction of Khajuraho sculpture that is erotic; no record supplies one.
❌ **Do not assign** an individual Khajuraho temple to a named ruler beyond the general statement that several were built during Dhanga's reign.
❌ **Do not state** a current count of surviving Khajuraho temples as today's figure.
⚠️ **Record, do not resolve,** the Chausath Yogini plan conflict: the textbook calls the Khajuraho shrine circular, the heritage record rectangular and granite, and the textbook's own sidebar allows either for the type.
❌ **Do not merge** Khajuraho's Chausath-yogini with the Morena or Mitaoli Chausath Yogini, which is circular and of the Kachchhapaghata period.
⚠️ **Use approximately 66 m, about 216 ft, for the Brihadishvara vimana**, and disclose that the standard textbook account prints about 60 m; ❌ do not quote a decimal height or a precise consecration year - "early 11th century" is what is safely supported.
⚠️ **Spelling discipline:** both "Chandela" and "Chandella" are in use; the 2025 question uses "Chandella", which must be preserved when answering it.
⚠️ **Years are stable; counts are not.** Inscription years may be cited; totals, danger-listings, budgets and management statuses must be routed to the dated official record.
❌ **Do not attribute** the Jain Narayana Temple at Pattadakal to the Chalukyas; the evidence assigns it to the Rashtrakutas in the 9th century.
❌ **Do not call** the Hampi Vitthala stone chariot monolithic; the evidence says explicitly that it is not.
❌ **Do not claim** Indo-Islamic influence in the Hampi temples; it is located in the secular and royal buildings.
❌ **Do not treat** the Mahanavami Dibba as a temple, and do not place the Virupaksha temple on it.
❌ **Do not translate** the Aihole Durga temple's name into a dedication; it is believed dedicated to the Sun God Aditya.
❌ **Do not date** the whole Meenakshi temple to the Nayakas; most of the present campus is 17th-century work on older roots.
❌ **Do not state** a Hoysala surviving-temple count more precisely than "more than 100", or a Meenakshi gopura count other than 14.
⚠️ **Naming variants recorded, not silently resolved:** Lokmahadevi and Lokamahadevi; Thirumalai Nayak and Tirumala Nayaka; and the Somanathapura temple, called Chennakeshava in widely used tables and Keshava Temple in the official property's component naming.
⚠️ **Traveller-sourced attributions are flagged, not upgraded:** Domingo Paes on the Mahanavami Dibba's builder and occasion, and Marco Polo's reported phrase for Ramappa, which is itself reported only as *supposed*.
❌ **Do not present** Vyalas as unique to Khajuraho, and do not invent named postures for specific Khajuraho panels.
❌ **Do not assign** a specific temple to a specific named Hoysala sculptor.
⚠️ **Do not upgrade** Tentative-List sites: Aihole, Badami, the Thousand Pillar Temple, the Warangal Fort and the Kakatiya Kala Thoranam are not inscribed.
❌ **Do not treat** the Great Living Chola Temples as three separate properties; it is one serial property of three temple complexes.
⚠️ **Write the Gurjara-Pratihara association** for Chandella expansion under Harsha and Yashovarman, and treat the textbook's hedged Devapala or Pala-overlordship clause as a disclosed discrepancy rather than as settled chronology.

### 5. The verdict lines

A verdict is the last sentence, and it should answer the question's own wording rather than summarise the answer.

⚠️ **Chandella:** "The Chandella sculptors' achievement is that vigour and breadth are the same achievement: the carving method that gives figures their movement is what allows an entire social and sacred world to be placed on one wall."

⚠️ **Pallava:** "Estimated fairly, the Pallava contribution is foundational rather than final - they converted rock into architecture, architecture into structure, and a capital into a literary centre, and the Cholas inherited all three."

⚠️ **Chola:** "The Cholas are remembered because their achievement was simultaneously monumental, portable and documented - and because, uniquely, it is still in use."

⚠️ **Vijayanagara:** "Vijayanagara's amalgamation is legible precisely because it was disciplined: it borrowed where borrowing served, invented where inheritance would not, and kept the two registers apart."

⚠️ **Deccan and Vesara:** "The Deccan's contribution was not a style but a method - describing, selecting and testing - which is why the category invented to name its results has never quite fitted them."

⚠️ **Regional schools:** "What the schools finally demonstrate is that a shared religious form can be built in any material a region possesses, and that the differences we read as taste are usually solutions."

⚠️ **Sculpture as evidence:** "Temple sculpture is an honest witness to what a patron wished to be seen, and to nothing beyond it - which is a smaller claim than it first appears, and a far more useful one."

### Concept check

**Question:** A 15-mark demand asks you to assess a dynasty's architectural achievement. Explain what an opening sentence must do that a definition cannot, what a closing sentence must do that a summary cannot, and why a stated limit strengthens rather than weakens an assessment.

**Model answer:** An opening must commit. A definition tells the examiner what a word means and leaves the question unanswered; a thesis states a position that could be disagreed with and, if it is well built, also supplies the answer's structure - "the achievement is a transition rather than a style" announces a judgement and three stages in one sentence, whereas "Dravida architecture is the southern style of temple building" announces nothing. A closing must decide. A summary repeats material the examiner has just read; a verdict answers the question in its own words and allocates weight between the competing claims the body has set up, so the last sentence adds rather than recycles. A stated limit strengthens an assessment because assessing means weighing, and weighing requires knowing what is *not* being claimed: naming an incomplete successor, a modern classification scheme, or a silence in the record demonstrates that the candidate has tested the claim rather than agreed with it, which is precisely the discrimination an evaluative directive exists to measure.

**Misconception to avoid:** Saving limits for a final apologetic sentence. In an assessment the limits are part of the judgement and should be visible in the thesis as well as the verdict; an answer that praises for most of its length and hedges in the last line has not assessed anything.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Every major change in Indian temple architecture was a solution to a problem created by the form that preceded it." Critically examine with reference to named monuments from at least three regions. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model answer

The proposition explains a great deal of the sequence and fails at precisely the points where patronage or geology, not form, decided.

It holds through the Gupta stages. A flat-roofed square shrine on a low plinth cannot be circumambulated, so the platform rises and a covered ambulatory is enclosed at Nachna Kuthara; a sanctum so enclosed is invisible from outside, so a curvilinear spire rises over it at Deogarh; a single dedication looks thin beside so much built mass, so four subsidiary shrines flank the hall in the panchayatana plan.

It holds in the Pallava south, where the constraint is method. Excavation cannot be sited freely or extended, so building moved from Mandagapattu's rock-cut cell, whose inscription stresses a temple made with no timber, no metal and no brick, through the monolithic Pancha Rathas, to structural construction at the Shore Temple.

It holds in Odisha, where one tower could not serve two functions: the rekha deula crowns the sanctum, the pyramid-roofed pidha deula covers the ritual hall, and the barrel-roofed khakhara deula answers a third, often a goddess shrine.

It fails twice. Khajuraho's abandonment of the enclosure solves no prior architectural problem; it follows Dhanga's political self-assertion and a quarry of fine-grained sandstone. And the Pala-Sena terracotta tradition answers an absence of building stone, not an earlier building.

Examined, the proposition names the tradition's internal engine while understating the external ones - patronage and geology - that start and stop it.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the proposition tested with its failure conditions announced at the start 4 + the Gupta problem-solution chain with two named monuments 5 + the Pallava method constraint with the Mandagapattu inscription 4 + the Odisha one-tower-per-function solution with all three deul types 4 + two argued failures, patronage at Khajuraho and geology in Bengal 3.**

## Ownership audit, and two disclosed routing positions

The official local papers and the repository's routing ledgers were checked directly for this topic through 2026. The result is **four directly relevant General Studies Mains demands**, **one further Mains demand claimed jointly with another owner**, **one directly relevant official Prelims item reproduced from the official paper**, and **two further objective items that survive only as reproductions inside the core art source**. Two routing positions are disclosed rather than resolved silently.

**Disclosed position one - the 2024 Pallava demand.** The repository's Mains routing ledger for 2024-2025 routes the Pallava question to `Ancient-Indian-History/basic/23_Peninsular-India-Pallavas-Chalukyas`, on the ground that its subject is a dynasty. This topic's own canonical owner retains it as a direct architectural demand answerable from its own evidence. Both positions are defensible: the question's subject matter is dynastic, its demand is art-and-literature. The question is therefore treated here as **directly relevant and jointly claimed**, reproduced exactly, with the ledger's assignment left untouched.

**Disclosed position two - the 2022 temple-sculpture demand.** The repository's Mains routing ledger for 2018-2023 routes it to `Indian-Art-and-Culture/advanced/06_Sculpture-Pottery-and-Iconography`. The demand is therefore treated as **jointly owned**, and the ledger's assignment is again left untouched. ⚠️ **Honest verification limit:** the official 2022 Mains General Studies Paper I is **not among the locally held papers**, so the question's exact printed wording has **not** been verified against an official paper in this pass. It is recorded from the routing ledger and the canonical owner, and is flagged as unverified wording rather than reproduced as a verbatim quotation.

No model answer is supplied anywhere in this file for any official question. Every Mains question set for practice in this file - the twenty-one lesson-local questions and the six final questions - is **original**: none reproduces an official question, none paraphrases an official question's operation, and none is a re-wording of an official demand under a different verb. Where an official demand is discussed, it appears only in this section, with its exact printed wording, its directive analysis and a concise answer approach, and with no answer text, opening sentence, closing sentence or skeleton model attached to it anywhere in the file.

## Exact official Mains questions

### 2026 Civil Services (Main) Examination, General Studies Paper I, Question 11 - 15 marks

> "Vijayanagara architecture exemplifies the amalgamation of past traditions with contemporary practices. Elucidate with examples. (Answer in 250 words)"

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Paper and year | Civil Services (Main) Examination 2026, General Studies Paper I |
| Question number | 11 |
| Marks | 15 |
| Word limit | 250 words, printed with the question as "(Answer in 250 words)" |
| Directive | Elucidate, with examples |
| Booklet | KVMS-G-GSA/35, printed page 3 |
| Ownership | Recorded in the complete 2026 GS-I routing ledger as owned by this topic, with no support owner and readiness marked FULL |
| Lessons that teach what the question requires | Lessons 4, 6, 7, 17 and 18, with the answer construction in Lesson 21 |
| Official local source | `books\mains\2026\QP-CSM-26-010926-GENERAL STUDIES PAPER - I.pdf`, PDF page 3, read from the rendered page because the scan carries no usable text layer |

**Directive and demand analysis.** "Elucidate with examples" means make the claim clear by explanation *and* substantiate every step with a named case. The claim has two halves - past traditions, contemporary practices - and a candidate who treats it as a single idea about eclecticism will write half an answer twice. The examiner has effectively asked you to itemise the inheritance and to identify what was contemporary about the borrowing.

**Answer approach, without a model answer.** Open with a thesis that distinguishes amalgamation by register from amalgamation by fusion. Itemise the inheritance using the sources the evidence base itself names. Confine Islamicate form to the royal and secular buildings and name three of them. Add the native inventions, which are what prevent the answer from reducing the empire to a borrower. Close on the destruction date and the inscription year.

**Common failures this question punishes.** Calling the architecture "eclectic" without itemising; placing Indo-Islamic form on the temples; treating the Mahanavami Dibba or the Lotus Mahal as temples; confusing the Hampi Virupaksha with the Pattadakal Virupaksha; and omitting the contemporary half of the demand entirely by writing only about inherited Dravidian features.

### 2025 Civil Services (Main) Examination, General Studies Paper I, Question 3 - 10 marks

> "'The sculptors filled the Chandella artform with resilient vigor and breadth of life.' Elucidate. (Answer in 150 words)"

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Paper and year | Civil Services (Main) Examination 2025, General Studies Paper I |
| Question number | 3 |
| Marks | 10 |
| Word limit | 150 words, printed with the question as "(Answer in 150 words)" |
| Directive | Elucidate |
| Ownership | Routed to this topic by the 2024-2025 Mains ledger and retained by this topic's canonical owner |
| Lessons that teach what the question requires | Lessons 10, 11, 12, 13 and 14, with the answer construction in Lesson 21 |
| Official local source | `books\mains\UPSC Mains 2025 GS Paper 1.pdf`, PDF page 2; wording verified against the extracted English text of that page |

**Directive and demand analysis.** "Elucidate" means make clear by explanation, so the marks lie in causes rather than in praise. The quotation contains two separable claims - *resilient vigour* describes how the figures are carved, *breadth of life* describes what is carved - and the single highest-value decision in the answer is to split them. ⚠️ Note the spelling: the printed question uses "vigor" and "Chandella", and the answer should preserve the question's own spelling of the dynastic name.

**Answer approach, without a model answer.** State the split as the thesis. Explain vigour from material, exposure, wall offsets and figural treatment. Explain breadth from the named subject registers and from the site's plural dedications. Give one comparative line so that the erotic register is correctly proportioned. Name Kandariya Mahadeva as the culminating case, and close with the 1986 inscription.

**Common failures this question punishes.** Writing about erotic sculpture and nothing else; supplying an invented proportion; claiming the imagery is unique to Khajuraho; praising with adjectives instead of explaining with causes; and opening with the sculpture rather than with the architectural and material conditions that made it possible.

### 2024 Civil Services (Main) Examination, General Studies Paper I, Question 11 - 15 marks

> "'Though the great Cholas are no more yet their name is still remembered with great pride because of their highest achievements in the domain of art and architecture. Comment. (Answer in 250 words)"

| Field | Value |

**Directive and demand analysis.** "Comment" on an evaluative claim requires agreement or qualification with reasons, not a description of Chola temples. The operative words are *still remembered* and *with great pride*, which make the question about the persistence of memory, and that is what a candidate must explain.

**Answer approach, without a model answer.** Identify what survives, and note that three different kinds of achievement survive together. Show phased development so that the climax is not presented as a single stroke. Use the technical climax with correctly hedged figures. Bring in the documentary-administrative record, which is the strongest and least-used evidence. State the counterpoints a "comment" requires. Close on the word that the question's own phrasing invites.

**Common failures this question punishes.** Describing Brihadishvara at length and answering nothing about memory; quoting a decimal vimana height or an exact consecration year; presenting Gangaikondacholapuram as a second triumph rather than as incomplete; treating the three inscribed temples as three separate properties; and importing dynastic political history.

**Directive and demand analysis.** "Estimate" is the most specific directive in this set. It requires a weighed judgement, which means the answer must name what was achieved, show the sequence in which it was achieved, and state what the achievement did *not* amount to. An estimation without limits is not an estimation.

**Answer approach, without a model answer.** Thesis as transition. Three architectural stages with named monuments, anchored by the inscription that records a deliberate change of medium. One literary citation that simultaneously evidences court patronage and contemporary art practice. Then the three limits, stated as part of the estimate rather than as an apology. Close with the inscription year and a survival caveat.

**Common failures this question punishes.** Listing Mahabalipuram monuments with no development logic; treating Longhurst's scheme as a Pallava self-description; inferring the scale of Pallava painting from two surviving examples; and omitting literature entirely, which is half the printed demand.

### 2026 Civil Services (Preliminary) Examination, General Studies Paper I, Question 4

> Which of the following temples has/have a *Nagara*-style *shikhara* ?
>
> 1. Malegitti Shivalaya, Badami
>
> 2. Huchimalligudi Temple, Aihole
>
> 3. Dashavatara Temple, Deogarh
>
> 4. Virupaksha Temple, Pattadakal
>
> Select the answer using the code given below :
>
> (a) 1 and 2
>
> (b) 2 and 3
>
> (c) 3 only
>
> (d) 3 and 4

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Paper and year | Civil Services (Preliminary) Examination 2026, General Studies Paper I |
| Question number | 4 |
| Booklet | Set A, booklet code TDMN-A-ASO, printed page (3-A) |
| Official local source | `books\prelima_question_paper_answers\2026-GS1-Set A.pdf`, PDF page 3, rendered and read directly because the scan's text layer garbles the statement numbering and the option block |
| Routing | The 2026 Prelims routing ledger assigns the item to this topic |
| Lessons that teach the content | Lessons 2, 4, 5, 7 and 17 |
| Key control | The local 2026 key is **provisional**; **no answer letter is recorded or inferred here** |

**What makes this item a test of method rather than of memory.** All four named monuments are Deccan or central Indian, and two of the four sit on sites already established in this session as places where both superstructure grammars occur. The item is therefore not asking where a temple is; it is asking whether the candidate treats "Nagara" as a morphological description of a superstructure or as a geographical label. Lesson 7's Pattadakal pair is the exact discrimination being tested, and Lesson 2 supplies the Deogarh row.

**Approach note, kept answer-neutral.** Each named monument must be assessed independently on the form of its sanctum superstructure - whether it curves inward to a point in the northern manner or steps up linearly as a tiered southern vimana - rather than on the region in which it stands. No combination is endorsed here.

⚠️ **Disclosed evidence limit on two of the four names.** The core art source describes the Dashavatara temple at Deogarh as a stage-three Gupta temple with an emergent curvilinear shikhara, and states explicitly that the shikhara of the Virupaksha temple at Pattadakal is built in Dravidian style. It contains **no description of the Malegitti Shivalaya at Badami or of the Huchimalligudi temple at Aihole**. Those two names were resolved in this session's single bounded material-fact pass, recorded in the source ledger, which establishes that the Malegitti Shivalaya carries a tiered Dravida vimana and that the Huchchimalli Gudi carries an early curvilinear northern superstructure. Those attributions are carried as reference-level material-fact findings, not as evidence-base facts, and no answer letter is derived from them.

## Objective items surviving only as reproductions inside the core source

Neither of the following is an officially held paper. Both are reproduced inside the core art source among its own previous-years' multiple-choice questions, and that source supplies its own answer key. Each is recorded here with that exact provenance. **No official UPSC key is held locally for either year.**

### Civil Services 2021 - Chausath Yogini Temple near Morena

The core art source reproduces, as its item 35, a Civil Services 2021 question beginning "With reference to Chausath Yogini Temple situated near Morena, consider the following statements", with four statements: that it is a circular temple built during the reign of the Kachchhapaghata dynasty; that it is the only circular temple built in India; that it was meant to promote the Vaishnava sect in the region; and that its design has given rise to a popular belief that it was the inspiration behind the Indian Parliament building. **That source's own answer key marks item 35 as (c)**, which on the printed options corresponds to "1 and 4".

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Paper and year | Civil Services (Preliminary) Examination 2021, per the reproducing source |
| Routing | The 2018-2023 Prelims routing ledger assigns the item to this topic and records that the official key is unavailable locally |
| Lessons that teach the content | Lesson 12 |
| Provenance and key control | Question text and key are **the reproducing source's own**, at its previous-years' question set and its answer key page. The official 2021 paper and the official key are **not held locally**. The key is therefore attributed to the book, not to the examining authority |

**What it establishes, stated with that provenance.** On the reproducing source's own key, the Morena or Mitaoli monument is circular and of the Kachchhapaghata period, and the Parliament-inspiration story is a popular belief - a reading corroborated inside the same book's note that the old Parliament building of 1927, designed by Herbert Baker and Edwin Lutyens, was influenced by the Chausath Yogini Temple at Mitaoli. The claims that it is the only circular temple in India and that it was meant to promote the Vaishnava sect are not supported. ❌ The monument must not be conflated with Khajuraho's Chausath-yogini.

### Civil Services 2019 - Kalyana Mandapas

The core art source reproduces, as its item 41, a Civil Services 2019 question asking in which kingdom the building of "Kalyana Mandapas" was a notable feature in temple construction, with the options Chalukya, Chandela, Rashtrakuta and Vijayanagara. **That source's own answer key marks item 41 as (d)**.

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Paper and year | Civil Services (Preliminary) Examination 2019, per the reproducing source |
| Routing | The 2018-2023 Prelims routing ledger assigns the item to the Indo-Islamic and regional architecture topic; this topic's canonical owner records a substantive-ownership correction, because a Kalyana Mandapa is a Hindu temple hall type |
| Lessons that teach the content | Lesson 18 |
| Provenance and key control | Question text and key are **the reproducing source's own**. The official 2019 paper and key are **not held locally** |

**What it establishes, stated with that provenance.** The substantive evidence is independent of the key: the Vitthala Temple Complex at Hampi has a Kalyana Mandapa, a wedding hall used for celebrations and events, and the Lepakshi Veerbhadra temple has a second, incomplete one carrying the divine wedding of Shiva and Parvati. Two Vijayanagara cases establish the hall as a type. ❌ The demand must not be answered from Indo-Islamic mosque, tomb or palace vocabulary.
