# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These checks run across lesson blocks rather than inside a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks.

## Block A - Vocabulary, classification and the limits of style labels, Lessons 1-7

### Cumulative check A1 - the vocabulary in working order

**Question:** Without using the words "north Indian" or "south Indian", distinguish a Nagara temple from a Dravida temple on five dimensions, using the exact terms for each, and then state which single dimension generates the other four.

**Model answer:** Superstructure: a curvilinear shikhara, of the Latina, Phamsana or Valabhi types, whose vertical end carries a fluted amalaka surmounted by a spherical kalash, against a stepped pyramidal vimana rising linearly and crowned by an octagonal element. Subsidiary shrines: they may carry their own superstructures in the first tradition, and carry no vimanas at all in the second, where only the main temple has one. Threshold sculpture: Ganga and Yamuna images placed outside the garbhagriha, against dvarapala, mithuna and yaksha at the sanctum entrance. Water: generally no tank within the premises, against a water tank inside the enclosure. Enclosure: generally no elaborate boundary wall or gateway, against high boundary walls entered through a gopuram. The last dimension generates the rest, because an unwalled temple is approached and seen from every side, making the outer wall the display surface and the composition centripetal, while a walled precinct can only be entered at controlled points and can be extended by adding further walls - which migrates emphasis outward to the gateway and drives the documented shift from vimana to gopura.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reaching for geography as the discriminator. The 2026 objective item on Nagara shikharas exists precisely because Deccan sites carry both superstructure grammars, so a candidate who classifies by location rather than by morphology has no way to answer it.

### Cumulative check A2 - the five Gupta stages as a chain of problems

**Question:** Convert the five-stage Gupta sequence into a chain of problems and solutions, and identify the stage at which the temple acquires an exterior identity.

**Model answer:** Stage one solves containment: a square, flat-roofed shrine on a low platform with a portico on shallow pillars, as at Temple 17, Sanchi. Stage two solves circulation and elevation: the platform is raised, some instances are two-storeyed, and a covered ambulatory is enclosed around the sanctum for circumambulation, as at the Parvati temple, Nachna Kuthara. Stage three solves legibility and composite dedication: a low, almost square curvilinear shikhara replaces the flat roof and the panchayatana plan places four subsidiary shrines opposite one another beside an elongated mandapa, producing a cruciform ground plan, as at Deogarh and at the Durga temple, Aihole. Stage four solves the growing hall by making the main shrine more rectangular, as at Ter. Stage five tests whether rectilinearity is necessary at all, introducing circular temples with shallow rectangular projections, as at Maniyar Math, Rajgir. The temple acquires an exterior identity at stage three, when the superstructure rises over the sanctum and the building becomes recognisable at a distance.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the five stages as a strict national chronology. They describe a general progression of solutions; Deccan evidence in particular shows rock-cut and structural work proceeding side by side rather than in a clean ladder.

### Cumulative check A3 - testing the third box

**Question:** A candidate defines Vesara as a mixture of Nagara and Dravida and moves on. Give the two pieces of evidence from this session that make that definition untenable, and state the formulation that should replace it.

**Model answer:** The first is the Pattadakal pair: on one site, in the same Early Chalukyan period, the Virupaksha commissioned by Queen Lokmahadevi carries a Dravidian shikhara while the Papanatha, described as similar in plan, carries a north Indian Nagara one. A mixture would have produced one intermediate superstructure; selection produced two distinct ones. The second is the Hoysala formulation, which describes those temples as having a Dravidian morphology with substantial influences from the Bhumija style of Central India and the Nagara traditions of northern and western India - three named sources, which no two-way average can express. The replacement formulation is that Vesara names a practice of selection rather than a third object: Deccan patrons chose from both northern and southern grammars, and the category was devised afterwards to describe what they had already done. The corresponding method rule is to describe plan, tower and material at a named monument first and apply the label only afterwards.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming the label must be abandoned because its formula fails. The practice it names is real and sustained across the Aihole-Badami-Pattadakal sequence and beyond; what fails is the two-way-mixture definition, not the category's usefulness as a closing description.

## Block B - The Nagara sub-schools and the Chandella case, Lessons 8-14

### Cumulative check B1 - four solutions, one grammar

**Question:** Name the four northern regional schools treated in this session, give each one distinguishing decision that the others did not make, and state the single argument that explains the pattern.

**Model answer:** Odisha, or Kalinga, develops a named vocabulary of three tower types for three functions - rekha deula over the sanctum, pidha deula over the ritual hall, khakhara deula for often-goddess shrines - fronted by the jagamohan and usually enclosed by boundary walls. Khajuraho, under the Chandellas, abandons the enclosure entirely, raising lofty temples on a high platform terrace so the outer wall becomes the display surface. Solanki, or Maru-Gurjara, leaves temple walls devoid of carvings and concentrates ornament in arched torans, while adding a stepped surya kund whose steps carry small temples, and orienting most temples east so equinox sunlight reaches the central shrine. Pala-Sena in Bengal builds in burnt bricks and clay - terracotta - and reproduces the curved bamboo-hut roof in masonry as the Bangla roof, later adopted by Mughal architects. The explanatory argument is that environment sets the problem, available material sets the possible answers, and style is the resulting solution; the shared curvilinear-shikhara grammar, with its amalaka and kalasha, is inherited convention on top of those local solutions.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating Khajuraho as the definition of the northern family. It is the least typical member, since it abandons the enclosure entirely and pushes surface sculpture to an extreme that Solanki practice explicitly rejects.

### Cumulative check B2 - the Chandella case, opened correctly

**Question:** Construct the first three sentences of a Khajuraho answer in the correct order, explaining what each sentence buys you, and then identify the fact that must not appear in any of them.

**Model answer:** First: the tract known in medieval times as Jejabhukti was ruled by the Chandelas of central India between the ninth and thirteenth centuries from their capital Kharjju-ravahaka, and on the decline of the Pratiharas and Palas, Dhanga, the first independent Chandela king, took the title maharajadhiraja, during whose reign several of the Khajuraho temples were built - this buys the patronage mechanism, establishing that the building programme follows political self-assertion. Second: all the temples were built in the Nagara style, which reached its climax here, as lofty structures on a high platform terrace with no enclosure wall, in fine-grained sandstone of buff, pink and pale-yellow shades - this buys the morphology and the material that the sculpture depends on. Third: the site's surviving temples are dedicated six to Shiva, eight to Vishnu, one to Ganesha, one to the Sun and four to Jain Tirthankaras, in a single architectural idiom - this buys the plural-patronage argument before any individual sculpture is discussed. The fact that must not appear is any attribution of a specific named temple to a specific named ruler, since the evidence supports only the general statement about Dhanga's reign.

**Misconception to avoid:** Opening with the erotic sculpture because it is the site's most famous feature. Opening with political autonomy and morphology signals analytical control and correctly proportions the register that follows.

### Cumulative check B3 - three questions about one register

**Question:** State the three separate questions that must be asked about Khajuraho's erotic sculpture, give the evidentiary standard appropriate to each, and name the answer that is available for each.

**Model answer:** The first is proportion, whose standard is direct source statement: the evidence records that both the Hindu and Jain temples feature a variety of artwork including erotic element depicted through maithuna reliefs, which establishes that the register is one item within a set, and no proportion exists in the evidence base, so none may be stated. The second is distinctiveness, whose standard is comparison: the same evidence states that, like the Khajuraho temples, the erotic element is also prominent at Konark and Bhubaneswar, records erotic mithuna scenes on Konark's chariot walls, places mithuna figures at the sanctum entrance in Dravida practice, and lists a dozen Indian temples famous for erotic sculpture across many states - so the register is a regional and pan-regional parallel rather than a Chandella invention. The third is meaning, whose standard is scholarly consensus, which does not exist: Tantric, ritual, symbolic, didactic and worldly-life readings compete and the evidence base endorses none, so the answer is to report the disagreement and adjudicate none.

**Misconception to avoid:** Fusing the three questions into one confident sentence. A single claim that asserts a proportion, an exclusivity and a meaning is wrong in three independent ways, and it is the error the topic's evidence base is explicitly organised to prevent.

## Block C - The southern and Deccan trajectory, Lessons 15-18

### Cumulative check C1 - one mechanism, five stages

**Question:** Name the single mechanism that organises southern temple development from the Pallavas to the Nayakas, give one named monument for each of five stages, and state the structural reason the mechanism operates.

**Model answer:** The mechanism is the migration of architectural emphasis from the sanctum tower to the gateway. Stage one is technical rather than emphatic: under the Pallavas, the Mandagapattu cave temple records a temple made with no timber, no metal and no brick, the Pancha Rathas carve five temples from one rock, and the Shore Temple marks the replacement of rock-cut technique by structural building. Stage two concentrates emphasis on the vimana, at Brihadishvara, Thanjavur, whose vimana rises roughly sixty-six metres over an entirely granite structure. Stage three begins the shift, in the last Chola phase of the twelfth and thirteenth centuries, when the gopura becomes more dominant than the vimana, as at Chidambaram under Kulottunga I. Stage four names the new type, at the Hampi Virupaksha, whose fifty-metre gopuram accompanies the Raya Gopuram introduced in temple architecture for the first time by Krishnadevaraya. Stage five systematises it, at the Meenakshi temple, Madurai, where concentric enclosures carry fourteen gopurams, each outer wall's larger than the last. The structural reason is that a sanctum tower is fixed once built while an enclosure can always be extended by adding another wall, so growth - and with it artistic emphasis - had to move outward.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the shift as decline. It is a change in where a tradition invested, driven by what could still be added to a completed temple, and the Nayaka enclosures are among the largest and most elaborate religious complexes in the subcontinent.

### Cumulative check C2 - the Deccan as laboratory

**Question:** Give three experiments the Deccan conducted that neither the northern nor the southern family produced on its own, name the monument for each, and state what each experiment contributed.

**Model answer:** First, plan-type borrowing across sectarian lines: the Durga temple at Aihole is apsidal, believed inspired by Buddhist chaityas, and is believed dedicated to the Sun God Aditya rather than to the goddess Durga - contributing the demonstration that temple form could cross religious traditions. Second, geometry: Hoysala temples are raised on a stellate jagati with zig-zag walls and multiple shrines around a central pillared hall, the four-foot-high star platform's extended terrace serving as the pradakshinapath, carved in chloritic schist that is soft when quarried and hardens over time - contributing a plan and a material that neither family had used. Third, foundation and ceiling engineering: the Ramappa temple used the sandbox technique of a foundation pit filled with sand-lime, jaggery and black myrobalan acting as a cushion, and light floating stone in its ceiling, over about forty years - contributing named construction technology and, uniquely, a temple known by its architect's name. A supporting fourth is the preservation of a timber prototype in stone at Lad Khan, Aihole, with its rooftop shrine and stone-log roof and its Ayyavole merchant-guild inscription.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Deccan as a transitional corridor. Its monuments show alternatives being tested side by side at single sites, and several of its devices - the stellate plan, sandbox foundations, signed sculpture, a temple named for its architect - have no northern or southern precedent in this evidence base.

### Cumulative check C3 - amalgamation, made precise

**Question:** Explain how an examiner can tell the difference between a candidate who understands Vijayanagara amalgamation and one who has memorised the word "synthesis", using three specific discriminations.

**Model answer:** The first discrimination is itemisation: the informed candidate names the sources the evidence itself names - Cholas, Hoysalas, Pandyas, Chalukyas and even the Indo-Islamic style of Bijapur - while the memorising candidate writes "eclectic" and moves on. The second is register: the informed candidate confines Islamicate arches and domes to the royal and secular zone, naming the Lotus Mahal's arched roof in the Zenana enclosure, the domed elephant stable and the Queen's Bath with its Indo-Islamic arches, while keeping the temples Dravidian; the memorising candidate places arches on the Virupaksha, which the evidence refutes. The third is invention: the informed candidate names what was new - the Raya Gopuram, introduced in temple architecture for the first time by Krishnadevaraya, and the wide chariot streets flanked by pillared mandapas - so that the empire is not reduced to a borrower, while also distinguishing the Mahanavami Dibba as royal ceremonial architecture rather than a temple.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that naming many influences is the same as explaining amalgamation. Without the register distinction, a list of influences describes an inventory; with it, the list becomes an argument about how a state used architecture for different purposes.

## Block D - Synthesis, evidence and answer construction, Lessons 19-21

### Cumulative check D1 - the patronage record, in full

**Question:** A question asks who built India's temples. Give six categories of patron or maker from this session with a named instance for each, and state the one qualification that must accompany the answer.

**Model answer:** Sovereign rulers asserting or consolidating power: Dhanga, the first independent Chandela king, during whose reign several Khajuraho temples were built. Royal women: Queen Lokmahadevi commissioning the Pattadakal Virupaksha to commemorate Vikramaditya II's conquest of Kanchipuram, with Queen Trilok Mahadevi's neighbouring Mallikarjuna, Queen Tribhuvana-Mahadevi's ninth-century Vaitala temple, and Queen Shembiyan Mahadevi's programme of rebuilding brick temples in stone. Military officers: Ramappa, built in the interest of Recharla Rudra, a general of Ganapati Deva. Mercantile corporations: the Ayyavole 500, whose inscription appears in the Lad Khan temple at Aihole and whose inscriptions between the ninth and fourteenth centuries record endowments to temples. Later donors across religious boundaries: Mir Osman Ali Khan, the seventh Nizam, granting funds for the Thousand Pillar Temple's reconstruction. Named makers: Dasoja, Chavana and Mallithamma among the Hoysalas, and Ramappa, after whom a temple is named rather than its king or deity. The qualification is that a specific temple must not be assigned to a specific named sculptor, and a single recorded grant must not be converted into a general claim about a donor's religious policy.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming the dynastic record is the patronage record. Naming only emperors omits queens, officers, guilds and craftsmen, all of which the evidence base documents by name.

### Cumulative check D2 - the evidence ceiling

**Question:** State the master rule for reading temple sculpture as social evidence, apply it to three registers, and identify the register that demonstrates the rule's limit.

**Model answer:** The master rule is that temple sculpture is representation commissioned by patrons, so it evidences what patrons chose to make visible and nothing beyond it. Applied to performance, the dancers and musicians of the Khajuraho programme evidence that performance was socially valued and visually codified, but not the repertoire performed. Applied to ritual, the ninth-century Kalyanasundara Murti, which renders panigrahana through two separate statuettes with Shiva's extended right hand accepting Parvati's as she steps forward, evidences that a marriage rite was known, codified and worth depicting, but not that it was performed identically outside elite contexts. Applied to labour, signed Hoysala work and the Ayyavole guild's temple endowments evidence that named artisans and organised merchant bodies existed and could claim visibility, but not wage levels, workshop size or artisan social status. The limit is demonstrated by the erotic register, which cannot be read as a record of social behaviour at all - and more generally by the fact that royal temples cannot be read as evidence of village life.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading an absence as evidence of absence. What a patron had no reason to commission simply does not appear, so silence in a sculptural programme tells you about commissioning priorities rather than about the society outside the temple wall.

### Cumulative check D3 - the whole topic in one answer plan

**Question:** You are given a 20-mark demand to trace the development of Indian temple architecture and assess what drives its regional diversity. Produce the answer plan in eight moves, naming the evidence at each.

**Model answer:** One, a vocabulary frame in one sentence using garbhagriha, antarala, mandapa, jagati, shikhara or vimana, amalaka and gopuram. Two, origins in the Gupta five-stage sequence from Temple 17 at Sanchi through Nachna's ambulatory to Deogarh's shikhara and panchayatana, then Ter and Maniyar Math. Three, the northern family with its shared grammar and its four solutions - Odisha's deul types and walled precincts, Khajuraho's unwalled terrace and figured wall, Solanki's bare walls, torans and surya kund at Modhera in 1026-27 under Bhima I, and Pala-Sena's terracotta and Bangla roof. Four, the southern family from Mahendravarman I's rock-cut beginnings and Rajasimha's Shore Temple through Rajaraja I's wholly granite Brihadishvara to the gopura-dominant Chidambaram. Five, the Deccan as laboratory at Aihole, Badami's 578 AD Cave 3 inscription, Pattadakal's Virupaksha-Papanatha pair, Hoysala soapstone and stellate plans, and Kakatiya sandbox foundations at Ramappa. Six, the late synthesis at Vijayanagara, amalgamating five named traditions with Islamicate form confined to the royal zone, and the Nayaka completion with fourteen graded gopurams at Madurai. Seven, the drivers of diversity: material and environment set the possible answers, patronage decides the scale, ritual movement decides the circulation, and style labels describe the outcome. Eight, a verdict plus the dated inscription years.

**Misconception to avoid:** Writing the chronology without the driver. A 20-mark demand that asks what drives diversity will not reward a well-ordered list of dynasties; the marks are in the explanatory claim, which the named monuments then support.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

These are original questions. None reproduces or paraphrases an official paper's question or its operation, and no official question is given a model answer anywhere in this file. Attempt each under time before reading the model. Every model follows the same discipline: a claim, named evidence, analysis, and a qualification - and every model is written inside its stated word ceiling.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

**Explain the significance of the panchayatana plan in the development of Indian temple architecture.**

### Model answer

The panchayatana plan places a principal shrine with four subsidiary shrines, the main temple square with an elongated mandapa in front and the subsidiaries set opposite one another beside that hall, so the ground plan reads as a cross. It appears at the third Gupta stage, alongside the shikhara, at Deogarh's Dashavatara temple and Aihole's Durga temple.

Its significance is threefold. It converts a single dedication into a programme, subordinating four deities to one without excluding them. It fixes the axis, since flanking shrines make sense only against a governed centre. And it presupposes a patron able to endow five shrines, evidencing planning and resources at site level rather than one improvement to one roof.

It then travels, recorded both as a Nagara habit and as the Dravida enclosure layout, which is what makes plan-level comparison between the two families possible at all.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = an exact definition including the cruciform result 3 + correct placement in the Gupta sequence with two named examples 2 + the three significances argued rather than listed 4 + the point that it recurs in both families 1.**

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

**"The absence of an enclosure wall is the most consequential decision in Khajuraho's architecture." Comment.**

### Model answer

The claim is sound, because the decision governs the site's sculptural economy.

Khajuraho's temples are lofty structures on a high platform terrace without any enclosure wall, and three consequences follow. There is no precinct and no controlled gate, so the temple is seen whole from every side in daylight and the outer wall becomes the only public surface. The raised terrace sets that wall above the eye, so bands of figures stay legible up the whole elevation. And circulation is external, making circumambulation the viewing sequence, which is what makes a continuous programme worth commissioning.

Comparison confirms it: Odisha temples usually have boundary walls and generally bare interiors, while Dravida complexes migrate emphasis to the gopuram.

The qualification is that fabric matters too, since fine-grained sandstone permits the deep undercutting that exposure rewards.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = agreement stated as a governing decision 2 + the three consequences derived rather than asserted 4 + one comparative counter-case 2 + the fabric qualification that prevents single-cause explanation 2.**

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

**Discuss the different ways in which water was incorporated into Indian temple complexes, and explain what those differences reveal.**

### Model answer

Water appears in four distinct architectural relations, and the differences track enclosure, climate and the scale at which a patron was operating.

The northern family generally excludes it. Nagara premises are recorded as generally having no water tanks or reservoirs, so ritual water remained a matter of vessels and nearby rivers rather than of built form.

The southern family encloses it. A water tank inside the temple enclosure is one of the defining Dravida features, and the Nayakas enlarged it into a major device, pairing large temple tanks with prakaram corridors; Madurai's Potramarai Kulam, the Golden-Lotus Pond, sits within the walls beside fourteen gopurams.

Western India monumentalises it outside the temple. The Solanki signature is the stepped surya kund near the shrine, its steps themselves carrying small temples with wooden carvings - as at the Modhera Sun Temple of 1026-27 AD under Bhima I, whose orientation also admits equinox sunlight to the central shrine. Water storage here becomes a second sacred structure.

The Deccan works at landscape scale. Hoysala temples add a separate kalyani for ceremonial use and stand beside the Dwarasamudra lake; Badami's caves overlook the man-made Agastya lake; Ramappa stands by Ramappa Lake; and at Vijayanagara the rulers dammed the Tungabhadra, cut an aqueduct from solid rock and built the Hiriya canal.

The pattern is legible: an unwalled tradition keeps water outside, a walled one brings it in, an arid region builds it, and an imperial capital engineers it.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the four-relations framing stated before evidence 3 + the Nagara exclusion and the Dravida internal tank as a paired contrast 4 + the Solanki surya kund with its dated exemplar and its small temples 4 + Deccan landscape-scale water with at least three named cases 3 + the closing pattern that links each relation to enclosure, climate or scale 1.**

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

**Examine the proposition that the temple in early medieval India was as much an institution as a monument.**

### Model answer

The proposition holds strongly where inscriptions survive and weakens where only fabric does - which is itself the significant finding.

The institutional case is strongest among the Cholas. Inscriptions occur in the Brihadishvara premises, and the Tamil inscription at Airavatesvara, Darasuram, describes the deities, the methods of daily worship, donations, the festivals to be celebrated and the way the temple was to be administered - guidelines described as rather unprecedented. Rajaraja appointed an astronomer, Prenkani, to announce festival dates from planetary movements: a salaried specialist running a ritual calendar. Prescribed worship, endowments, festivals, staff and administration make an institution by any working definition.

Endowment evidence widens the case. The Ayyavole 500 guild's inscriptions between the ninth and fourteenth centuries record endowments to temples across Tamil Nadu, Karnataka and Andhra Pradesh, and Queen Shembiyan Mahadevi's programme rebuilt many brick temples in stone across two reigns. Both indicate continuing income and management rather than single acts of building.

Architecture corroborates. Nayaka complexes added concentric enclosures with graded gopurams, prakaram corridors and tanks - forms that presuppose processions, crowds and recurring festivals.

The limit is evidential. Khajuraho's institutional life is almost undocumented: travellers noticed the site, attribution is general, and no administrative record survives, so its institutional character must be inferred from form alone.

The examined verdict is that the temple was an institution wherever the record allows us to see it, and that the variation lies in documentation rather than in function.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the proposition tested against documentation rather than endorsed 3 + the Chola inscriptional-administrative evidence including the astronomer 5 + endowment evidence from a guild and a queen 3 + architectural corroboration from Nayaka processional form 2 + the Khajuraho evidential limit and the verdict that variation lies in documentation 2.**

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

**"Indian temple architecture is best understood as a series of solutions to the problem of making the sacred visible." Critically examine.**

### Model answer

The proposition is illuminating and partial, and testing it across the tradition shows where it holds.

It holds for the superstructure. The sanctum is a small, generally cubicle womb house, invisible from outside; the shikhara, defined as a mountain-like spire, marks that point at a distance. Northern development is a sustained effort at visibility - Latina, Phamsana and Valabhi types, ratha-divided walls, clustered urushringas massing a mountain-range silhouette at Khajuraho - while the southern vimana solves the same problem by stepped linear ascent, rising roughly sixty-six metres at Brihadishvara.

It holds for enclosure. An unwalled Khajuraho temple on a high terrace makes the whole exterior visible and therefore worth carving; a walled Dravida complex makes the gateway visible instead, which is why the gopura becomes more dominant than the vimana in the last Chola phase, acquires an imperial form as the Raya Gopuram under Krishnadevaraya, and is graded into fourteen gopurams at Madurai.

It holds for ritual visibility. Circumambulation moves outward over a millennium: the covered pradakshina of the second Gupta stage, the Hoysala terrace on a stellate platform, the Nayaka prakaram, Vijayanagara's chariot streets.

Three limits qualify it. Material constrains what visibility can attempt, since terracotta Bengal cannot undercut like Bundelkhand sandstone. Some solutions are invisible by design, notably Ramappa's sandbox foundation of sand-lime, jaggery and black myrobalan. And Solanki walls are devoid of carvings, so visibility was not the only value.

Visibility organises much of the tradition; material, engineering and restraint are independent drivers.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the proposition genuinely tested with a graded verdict 4 + superstructure evidence across both families with exact type names and figures 5 + the enclosure-to-gateway argument carried through four named stages 5 + the outward migration of circumambulation at four scales 3 + three independent limits including an invisible engineering solution 3.**

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

**Evaluate the view that style labels have done more to obscure than to illuminate the study of Indian temple architecture.**

### Model answer

The view is half right; the useful formulation distinguishes where labels describe tightly from where they describe loosely.

The obscuring case is real. "Vesara" is defined as combining Nagara and Dravidian features into a hybridised style, yet at Pattadakal the Virupaksha, commissioned by Queen Lokmahadevi to commemorate Vikramaditya II's conquest of Kanchipuram, carries a Dravidian shikhara while the Papanatha, similar in plan, carries a north Indian Nagara one - selection, not mixture. The category is also stretched across a rock-cut cave complex at Badami, a monolithic temple at Ellora and a soapstone stellate plan at Belur, and the Hoysala description names three sources - Dravidian morphology with Bhumija and Nagara influences - which no two-way label can express.

"Nagara" obscures differently. Its members share upraised platforms, a curvilinear spire under an amalaka and kalasha, and ratha-divided walls, but disagree on enclosure, surface and material - Odisha's walled precincts and bare interiors, Khajuraho's unwalled figured elevations, Solanki's uncarved walls.

The illuminating case is equally real. "Dravida" tracks a genuine institutional continuity - high walls, a gopuram, one vimana, an internal tank, an antarala - persisting from Chola to Nayaka, and the standard periodisation makes Pallavas and Cholas pioneers of early Dravidian architecture and Nayakas of the late.

The evaluation is therefore graded: labels are loosest in the Deccan, moderate in the north, tightest in the south. They obscure when they open an answer and illuminate when they close one - a complaint about terminology turned into a rule of method.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = an evaluation that grades rather than agrees or rejects 4 + the Vesara case with the Pattadakal pair, the stretched extension and the three-source formulation 6 + the internal disagreement within the Nagara family 4 + the southern counter-case where the label tracks real continuity 4 + the method rule as verdict 2.**

# REMEDIATION

Work down this table before an answer-writing session rather than after it. Each row names a claim that looks plausible in a hurry, the reason it fails, and the short cue that should replace it. The lessons explain these errors in full; the table keeps to cues so that it can be used for rapid pre-exam retrieval.

## Error map

| Misconception | Why it fails | Repair cue |
|---|---|---|
| Nagara means north and Dravida means south | They are morphological families; Deccan sites carry both superstructure grammars | Morphology, not map |
| A temple's shikhara is the same object in both traditions | North: the whole sanctum superstructure. South: the octagonal crowning element on the vimana | Tower or finial, ask which |
| Dravida subsidiary shrines have small vimanas | Only the main temple has a vimana; subsidiary shrines have none, unlike Nagara | One vimana only |
| A gopuram is a kind of sanctum tower | It is a gateway in the boundary wall | Gate, not spire |
| Panchayatana simply means five shrines | It is four subsidiaries flanking an elongated mandapa, giving a cruciform plan | Arrangement, not count |
| Panchayatana and parivaralaya are the same | Parivaralaya is the Chola term; Narttamalai has six | If a number is given, it decides |
| All Nagara temples are richly carved outside | Solanki walls are devoid of carvings, with ornament in torans | Ask which sub-school |
| No Nagara temple has a boundary wall | Odisha temples usually have boundary walls; Khajuraho has none | Khajuraho is the exception, not the rule |
| Nagara temples have tanks like Dravida ones | Nagara premises generally have no tank; the Solanki surya kund sits outside the temple | Inside enclosure or beside it |
| Vesara is a fifty-fifty mixture | Pattadakal shows selection between grammars; Hoysala is described from three sources | Selection, not average |
| There is one Virupaksha temple | Pattadakal's is Early Chalukyan with a Dravidian shikhara; Hampi's is Vijayanagara with a 50 m gopuram | Two Virupakshas, two dynasties |
| The Aihole Durga temple is dedicated to Durga | It is named after a nearby fort and believed dedicated to the Sun God Aditya | Durg, not Durga |
| The Jain Narayana temple at Pattadakal is Chalukyan | It was built by the Rashtrakutas in the 9th century | Rashtrakuta on a Chalukya site |
| Badami and Aihole are World Heritage Sites | They are on the Tentative List with Pattadakal; only Pattadakal is inscribed, in 1987 | Pattadakal inscribed, the other two listed |
| Khajuraho's temples are entirely sandstone | The evidence is internally inconsistent; granite is identified in the earliest and some smaller structures | Predominantly sandstone, granite exceptions |
| All Khajuraho temples face east | All except the Chaturbhuja face the sunrise | One exception, always carry it |
| Dhanga built the Kandariya Mahadeva | Only the general statement that several temples were built in his reign is supported | Several, not specified |
| A proportion of Khajuraho sculpture is erotic | No source supplies any figure | No number exists |
| The erotic register is unique to Khajuraho | It is prominent at Konark and Bhubaneswar, and a dozen temples are listed nationally | Regional parallel |
| Vyalas are a Khajuraho speciality | They are found in other Indian temples too | Specific but shared |
| The erotic sculpture is definitively Tantric | Several readings compete and none is established | Report the disagreement |
| Khajuraho's Chausath-yogini is settled as circular | The textbook says circular, the heritage record rectangular and granite, and the sidebar allows both | Record the conflict |
| Khajuraho's and Morena's Chausath Yogini are the same | Different sites, dynasties, materials and plans | Morena is Kachchhapaghata |
| Mitaoli definitely inspired the Parliament building | The reproduced item's own key supports only a popular belief | Popular belief, debated resemblance |
| Brihadishvara's vimana is about 60 m | That is a textbook rounding; the measured figure is approximately 66 m, about 216 ft | Approximately 66 m, early 11th century |
| Gangaikondacholapuram matched Thanjavur | It was incomplete, with a lower vimana and an inward-curving shikhara | Incomplete successor |
| The Great Living Chola Temples are three properties | One serial property: Thanjavur 1987, two extensions 2004 | One property, two dates |
| Chola bronzes are architecture | Iconography belongs to the sculpture topic; name the natana-sabha instead | Hall here, icon there |
| The Hampi stone chariot is monolithic | The evidence says explicitly that it is not | Not monolithic |
| Hampi's temples show Indo-Islamic influence | It is located in the royal zone: Lotus Mahal, elephant stable, Queen's Bath | Palace, not temple |
| The Mahanavami Dibba is a temple | It is a royal ceremonial platform used for ritual purposes, and the Virupaksha is not on it | Platform, not shrine |
| The Kalyana Mandapa is an Indo-Islamic form | It is a Hindu temple wedding hall, evidenced twice in Vijayanagara contexts | Wedding hall, Vijayanagara |
| Meenakshi temple is a Nayaka foundation | The temple has older roots; the present campus is 17th-century Nayaka work | Campus, not foundation |
| Nayaka gopura figures date the structure | Stucco over brick is renewed; the core is granite | Renewed surface, older core |
| Ramappa's fame rests on its patron | It is perhaps the only temple known by its architect's name | Architect, not king |
| Marco Polo documented Ramappa's praise | The phrase is reported as supposed | Supposed, not documented |
| Hoysala temple counts can be given precisely | The evidence says more than 100 survive | More than 100 |
| A named Hoysala sculptor can be tied to a temple | Three makers are named; no attribution is given | Named, not assigned |
| Kerala and Ahom temple architecture can be described here | This evidence base carries a Chera temple statement but no developed Kerala or Ahom architectural vocabulary | State the boundary, do not invent |
| Pala-Sena is simply a Nagara sub-school | It is treated as a distinct regional school sharing the Nagara superstructure grammar | Shares the tower, differs in fabric |
| Ellora's Kailasa can be described in full here | Its detail belongs to the rock-cut topic | Name once, route away |
| The temple count of a World Heritage site can be stated | Counts and statuses are dynamic | Years stable, counts not |
| Conservation means "government should preserve heritage" | The demand wants rediscovery, protection, living use, tourism tension and an institutional route | Five components, not a slogan |
| Style labels can open an answer | They describe outcomes; describe first, then classify | Describe, then label |

## Targeted drills

| Drill | Task | Checks |
|---|---|---|
| **Vocabulary walk** | Describe any temple aloud from jagati to garbhagriha in under thirty seconds, glossing each term in one clause. | Lessons 3, 4 |
| **Spire sort** | Given six monuments from anywhere in the subcontinent, assign each a superstructure grammar using morphology only, never location. | Lessons 4, 5, 7, 17 |
| **Label audit** | Take any written answer and circle every use of Nagara, Dravida or Vesara. Each must follow a description, never precede one. | Lessons 1, 5, 6, 7 |
| **Name-per-claim sweep** | Read a practice answer and strike out every claim not attached to a named monument with a patron, dynasty or approximate date. | Lessons 8-18 |
| **Number hunt** | Search a written answer for any digit. Each must be an inscription year, a stated century, or a recorded figure such as "approximately 66 m", "more than 100", "14 gopurams" or "578 AD" - or nothing. | Lessons 16, 17, 20, 21 |
| **Proportion refusal** | Write a sentence about Khajuraho's erotic sculpture that is fully defensible and contains no quantity. | Lesson 14 |
| **Three-question split** | Take any contested feature and answer separately: how much, how distinctive, what does it mean. | Lessons 14, 19 |
| **Boundary test** | Strike out every sentence belonging to Topic 02, 04, 06, 07, 13 or 14, or to dynastic political history. | Lessons 1, 16, 17, 20 |
| **Register ladder** | Given a sculptural subject, state what it can evidence and what it cannot, in one sentence each. | Lesson 19 |
| **Trajectory sprint** | Recite the vimana-to-gopura migration in five named stages in under a minute. | Lessons 6, 16, 18 |
| **Conflict drill** | State the Chausath Yogini plan dispute in two sentences without choosing a side. | Lessons 12, 21 |
| **Status close** | End any practice answer with a dated inscription year appropriate to its subject. | Lesson 20 |

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Master causal chain: from geology to sculptural programme

```text
   LOCAL GEOLOGY
        |
        v
   AVAILABLE MATERIAL  ----------------------------------+
   sandstone | granite | soapstone | brick clay | basalt |
        |                                                |
        v                                                |
   WORKABILITY: how deep can it be cut? how far can it   |
   span? how fast can it be dressed?                     |
        |                                                |
        v                                                v

   deep undercutting (Khajuraho sandstone)      excavate (Badami's soft
   intricate then hardening (Hoysala soapstone) red sandstone)
   moulded, repeated relief (Bengal terracotta) monolithic carving
   austere mass (Chola granite)                 (Pancha Rathas, Ellora)
   polished figures (Ramappa black basalt)      structural assembly
   renewable ornament (Nayaka stucco)           (Shore Temple onward)
        |                                                |
        +----------------------+-------------------------+
                               |
                               v
                     PLAN AND ELEVATION DECISIONS
     enclosure or none | ratha offsets | stellate or axial plan |
     spire type | number of superstructures | gateway scale
                               |
        +----------------------+-------------------------+
        |                                                |
        v                                                v
   VIEWING CONDITIONS                            RITUAL CIRCULATION
   unwalled + raised terrace -> the whole         covered pradakshina ->
   exterior is the display surface, read from     open stellate terrace ->
   below in raking light                          prakaram corridor ->
   walled + gated -> the gateway is the public    chariot street
   surface, and can be enlarged by addition
        |                                                |
        +----------------------+-------------------------+
                               |
                               v
                    SCULPTURAL PROGRAMME AND ITS SCALE
     what is carved | where it is placed | how deeply | how much
                               |
                               v
                PATRONAGE DECIDES WHETHER IT IS ATTEMPTED
     sovereign assertion (Dhanga, Rajaraja I) | royal women
     (Lokmahadevi, Shembiyan Mahadevi) | officers (Recharla Rudra) |
     guilds (Ayyavole 500) | and the makers who sometimes signed
                               |
                               v
                     WHAT SURVIVES, AND HOW IT IS SEEN
     abandonment or destruction -> rediscovery -> protection ->
     inscription -> visitation -> the conservation-access tension
```

## Master comparison 1: the families, on eight dimensions

| Dimension | Nagara | Dravida | Where the Deccan sits |
|---|---|---|---|
| Enclosure | Generally no elaborate boundary walls or gateways | High boundary walls with a gopuram in the front wall | Varies by monument; Pattadakal's Virupaksha has an enclosed circumambulation passage within a pillared-hall plan |
| Water | Generally no tank in the premises | A water tank inside the enclosure | Hoysala temples have a separate kalyani for ceremonial use |
| Sanctum superstructure | Curvilinear shikhara - Latina, Phamsana, Valabhi | Vimana: stepped pyramid rising linearly | Both grammars occur, selected monument by monument |
| Finial | Fluted amalaka, then a spherical kalash | Octagonal crowning element, not spherical | Follows whichever grammar is selected |
| Subsidiary shrines | May carry superstructures | Carry no vimanas | Hoysala plans multiply shrines around one hall |
| Threshold sculpture | Ganga and Yamuna outside the garbhagriha | Dvarapala, mithuna and yaksha at the entrance | Dvarapalas recorded at the early Chola Narttamalai entrance |
| Wall organisation | Vertical ratha planes as narrative panels | Devakoshtha niches and a base lion frieze | Hoysala horizontal friezes of elephants, lions, scrolls, horses, makaras, swans |
| Growth mechanism | Elaboration of tower and wall offsets | Addition of enclosures and ever-larger gateways | Experiment rather than a single growth rule |

## Master comparison 2: the four northern schools

| | Odisha / Kalinga | Khajuraho / Chandella | Solanki / Maru-Gurjara | Pala-Sena / Bengal |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Period or patronage | Shailodbhava to Eastern Ganga; zenith 8th-13th c. | Chandelas, 9th-13th c.; climax under and after Dhanga | Solanki rulers; Modhera 1026-27 under Bhima I | Pala and Sena dynasties, 8th-12th c. |
| Superstructure | Rekha, pidha and khakhara deul types | Curvilinear shikhara with clustered urushringas | Shikhara, with the tank as signature complement | Tall curving shikhara under a large amalaka |
| Hall | Jagamohan | Connected axial halls on a high jagati | Garbhagriha connected to mandapa internally and externally | Not recorded in this evidence base |
| Enclosure | Boundary walls usual | None | Temple plus stepped surya kund nearby | Not recorded |
| Wall surface | Exteriors lavish, interiors bare | Densely figured across the whole elevation | Walls devoid of carvings; torans at porticos | Moulded terracotta |
| Material | Dressed stone, incl. reddish-gold Rajarani sandstone | Fine-grained buff, pink, pale-yellow sandstone, granite exceptions | Sandstone, black basalt, soft marble | Burnt bricks and clay - terracotta |
| Solar design | Not recorded as a school rule | All except Chaturbhuja face sunrise | Most east-facing; equinox light reaches the central shrine | Not recorded |
| Erotic register | Prominent at Konark and Bhubaneswar | Present, one register among many | Not the school's emphasis, though Modhera appears on the national list | Not recorded |
| Status anchor | Konark, UNESCO 1984 | Khajuraho, UNESCO 1986 | Not an inscribed property in this evidence base | Bishnupur is not inscribed |

## Master comparison 3: the southern and Deccan dynasties

| Dynasty | Period | Signature device | Named monument, patron | Status anchor |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Pallava | 4th-9th c.; capital Kanchipuram | Rock-cut to monolithic to structural | Shore Temple, Rajasimha; Pancha Rathas, Narasimhavarman I | Mahabalipuram, 1984 |
| Early Chalukya | 6th-8th c.; Aihole then Vatapi | Selecting between spire grammars | Virupaksha, Queen Lokmahadevi; Papanatha | Pattadakal, 1987; Aihole and Badami on the Tentative List |
| Rashtrakuta | 750-983 AD | Monolithic excavation at temple scale | Kailashnatha, Cave 16, Krishna I | Ellora, 1983 - detail owned by Topic 02 |
| Chola | 9th-13th c.; around Thanjavur | Granite mass, then gopura dominance | Brihadishvara, Rajaraja I; Airavatesvara, Rajaraja II | Great Living Chola Temples, 1987 and 2004 |
| Hoysala | 11th-14th c.; Belur then Halebidu | Stellate jagati in soapstone | Chennakeshava, Vishnuvardhana; Hoysaleswara | Sacred Ensembles of the Hoysalas, 2023 |
| Kakatiya | 12th-14th c.; Warangal | Sandbox foundation, floating stone | Ramappa, for Recharla Rudra; Thousand Pillar, Rudra Deva | Ramappa 2021; Thousand Pillar and Warangal Fort on the Tentative List |
| Vijayanagara | 14th-16th c.; Hampi; four houses with overlapping conventional dates | Raya Gopuram, chariot streets, sacred-secular split | Virupaksha and Vitthala, Krishnadevaraya; Hazara Rama, Devaraya II | Hampi, 1986 |
| Nayaka | 16th-18th c.; Madurai and others | Concentric enclosures with graded gopurams | Meenakshi, Tirumala Nayaka | Not inscribed in this evidence base |

## Master comparison 4: the four patronage types

| Type | Named instance | What the commission was for | What it evidences |
|---|---|---|---|
| Newly sovereign ruler | Dhanga, first independent Chandela king, *maharajadhiraja* | Several Khajuraho temples during his reign | Building follows political self-assertion |
| Commemorating ruler or queen | Queen Lokmahadevi; Vishnuvardhana; Krishnadevaraya | Pattadakal Virupaksha for the conquest of Kanchipuram; Belur Chennakeshava for victory over the Cholas; the Mahanavami Dibba for the Odisha campaign | Victory converted into permanent architecture, sometimes non-religious |
| Institutional patron | Queen Shembiyan Mahadevi; the Ayyavole 500 | Brick temples rebuilt in stone; endowments recorded across the 9th-14th centuries | Sustained programmes rather than single acts |
| Officer, donor or maker | Recharla Rudra; Mir Osman Ali Khan; Dasoja, Chavana, Mallithamma; Ramappa | Ramappa built in a general's interest; a reconstruction grant; signed Hoysala work; a temple named for its architect | Patronage and craft extend far beyond the dynastic record |

## Argument map: what temple architecture proves

```text
   WHAT THE FABRIC PROVES DIRECTLY
   plan, elevation, material, carving depth, spire type, enclosure,
   placement of images, evidence of incompleteness or later repair
            |  strong: observable, repeatable, checkable
            v
   WHAT AN INSCRIPTION ADDS
   patron, occasion, date, endowment, worship schedule, administration,
   sometimes the maker's name
            |  strong where it survives; ABSENT at Khajuraho
            v
   WHAT A TRAVELLER'S ACCOUNT ADDS
   external notice, reputation, occasion - Al-Biruni, Ibn Battuta,
   Marco Polo, Domingo Paes, Abdur Razzaq
            |  attribute it; a report, not a measurement
            v
   WHAT MAY BE INFERRED ABOUT SOCIETY
   that surplus existed | that authority wished to be seen | that
   certain subjects were worth depicting | that named artisans and
   guilds existed
            |  bounded by the commissioned-representation rule
            v
   WHAT MAY NOT BE INFERRED
   wages, workshop size, artisan status | repertoire, literacy, belief |
   village life from royal temples | social behaviour from the erotic
   register | a specific motive where the source is silent
```
