# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. What this topic owns, and the four reading questions

- A temple is a sacred object, a ritual route and a load-bearing structure at once; a fourth layer, the image programme, sits on the building.
- Four reading questions, always in order: plan, elevation, fabric, image programme.
- Plan, elevation and fabric are observational; the meaning of the image programme is interpretive.
- Owned: built temple form, fabric, superstructure, architectural placement of sculpture, patronage where it explains building, rediscovery and inscription years.
- Not owned: rock-cut detail and the Ellora Kailasa (Topic 02); Indo-Islamic form (Topic 04); the Parliament resemblance (Topic 05); freestanding sculpture and iconographic doctrine (Topic 06); painting (Topic 07); dance posture (Topic 09); inscriptions as literature and script (Topic 11); bhakti and Vedanta doctrine (Topic 13); heritage counts, policy and institutions (Topic 14); dynastic politics (Ancient and Medieval History).
- Method rule: describe first, classify second. Style labels are modern conveniences and must close a description, never open one.
- Unlike the Harappan topic, this one has named patrons, dated commissions and, rarely, signed makers - so generic sentences are indefensible.

## 2. The Gupta five-stage transition

- Structural temple architecture, with a square sanctum and pillared portico, emerged in the Gupta period; progression runs from flat-roofed shrines to the sculptured shikhara.
- Stage 1: flat roof, square, portico on shallow pillars, low platform - Temple 17, Sanchi (MP).
- Stage 2: raised platform, some two-storeyed instances, covered ambulatory around the garbhagriha used as a pradakshina path - Parvati temple, Nachna Kuthara (MP).
- Stage 3: shikhara replaces the flat roof, still low and almost square, that is, curvilinear; panchayatana introduced - Dashavatara, Deogarh (UP); Durga temple, Aihole (Karnataka).
- Stage 4: main shrine more rectangular - Ter, Maharashtra. Stage 5: circular temples with shallow rectangular projections - Maniyar Math, Rajgir (Bihar).
- Panchayatana: principal shrine plus four subsidiaries, main temple square with an elongated mandap in front, subsidiaries opposite each other beside it, giving a crucified ground plan.
- Stage 3 is the hinge: exterior legibility and composite dedication arrive together.
- The Gupta age saw a decline in stupa development; the Dhamek Stupa at Sarnath is a renovation of an earlier stupa at the site of the first sermon.
- Deogarh and Nachna are also central Indian Gupta centres, with Bhumara and Khoh - so Khajuraho is a culmination, not a sudden appearance.

## 3. Plan vocabulary, in walking order

- Jagati: the raised platform terrace. Ardhamandapa: the entrance porch or half-hall. Mandapa: the entrance hall for a large number of worshippers. Antarala: the vestibule connecting hall to sanctum. Garbhagriha: sanctum, "womb house", small, generally cubicle, holding the principal deity.
- Vahana: the deity's mount, placed just before the sanctum.
- Pradakshinapatha: the ambulatory around the sanctum, covered in the Nagara school.
- Regional hall names: jagamohan (Odisha frontal mandapa); mukhamandapa and sabhamandapa (Lad Khan); natana-sabha (a separate Nataraja hall in many South Indian Shiva temples); kalyana mandapa (a wedding hall used for celebrations and events).
- Subsidiary shrine terms: panchayatana (four subsidiaries, cruciform plan); parivaralaya (Chola subsidiary shrines - six at Narttamalai).
- Enclosure devices: gopuram, the boundary-wall gateway tower; prakaram, the wide corridor around the sanctum, enclosed or open; toran, the Solanki arched portico gateway.
- Niche and band terms: devakoshtha, Chola pilaster-formed wall niches holding deity images.
- Compression principle: wide and public at the front, narrow and private at the back; the sanctum stays small however large the temple.

## 4. Elevation vocabulary, and the two meanings of one word

- North: shikhara is the whole superstructure over the sanctum. South: vimana is the whole superstructure; shikhara is the octagonal crowning element.
- Nagara shikhara types: Latina or Rekha-prasad (square base, walls curve inward to a point); Phamsana (broader base, shorter, straight upward slope); Valabhi (rectangular base, roof rising into vaulted chambers, wagon-vaulted).
- The vertical end of the spire carries the amalaka, a horizontal fluted disc; a spherical kalash sits above it.
- Urushringas: subsidiary miniature spires clustered against the main shikhara, producing the mountain-range silhouette.
- Rathas: vertical wall planes - triratha, pancharatha, saptaratha, navaratha - used as panels for narrative sculpture; more offsets means more framed surfaces and more shadow.
- Dravida vimana: stepped pyramid rising linearly, not curved; only one vimana, over the main temple; subsidiary shrines carry none, unlike Nagara.
- Gopuram: a gateway tower in the boundary wall, never a sanctum tower.
- Silhouette argument: Nagara is centripetal, massing over the sanctum; mature Dravida is centrifugal, massing on the outer walls.

## 5. The Nagara family and its four regional solutions

- Nagara: northern India from the 5th century AD, with sub-schools in western, central and eastern parts.
- Shared grammar: panchayatana tendency; mandaps before the principal shrine; Ganga and Yamuna outside the garbhagriha; generally no water tanks in the premises; upraised platforms; pillared porticos; three spire types; amalaka and kalash; ratha divisions; covered pradakshina; generally no elaborate boundary walls or gateways.
- Three named sub-schools: Odisha (Kalinga), Khajuraho (Chandella), Solanki (Maru-Gurjara).
- Pala-Sena, Bengal, 8th-12th century AD: treated separately, but with a tall curving shikhara crowned by a large amalaka, "similar to the Odisha School".
- The label has authority over superstructure and plan logic and none over enclosure, wall surface or material.
- Khajuraho is the family's least typical member, not its definition.

## 6. Odisha, or Kalinga

- Deul = sanctum plus curvilinear spire, vertical almost to the top then curving sharply inward. Jagamohan = the frontal mandapa where devotees gather for rituals.
- Exteriors lavishly decorated, interiors generally bare; boundary walls usual.
- Three deul types: rekha (tower-like sanctum, curvilinear spire); pidha (square hall, pyramid-like roof, for rituals); khakhara (barrel-shaped, often goddess worship) - three jobs in one complex.
- Parsurameswar, Shailodbhava, 7th c.: earliest, simpler, modest rekha deula with jagamohana.
- Vaitala, Queen Tribhuvana-Mahadevi, Bhauma Kara, 9th c.: khakhara deula; Chamunda; tantric imagery and daily-life scenes.
- Mukteswar, Somavamshi, 10th c.: "Gem of Odishan Architecture"; sculpted Mangaltorana gateway.
- Rajarani, Somavamshi, 11th c.: renowned for beauty; lacks a presiding deity; named from its reddish-gold Rajarani sandstone.
- Lingaraja, three generations of Somavamshi, 11th c.: largest in Bhubaneswar; fully developed Kalinga style; rekha deula order.
- Jagannath, Puri, Anantavarman Chodaganga, Eastern Ganga, 12th c.: Purushottama worship raised to an imperial cult; monumental pidha deula; temple records credit an initial building to Indradyumna of Avanti.
- Konark, Narasimhadeva I, Eastern Ganga, 13th c., UNESCO 1984: around 35 km north-east of Puri near Chandrabhaga beach; colossal chariot of the Sun God; twelve wheels for twelve months; seven horses; erotic mithuna scenes; "Black Pagoda"; Gajasinghas - lions crushing elephants; ring-stirrups in the horse sculptures, also found at Khajuraho; Tagore's line; on the ₹10 note.
- Zenith of the school: 8th to 13th centuries AD.
- The erotic element is prominent at Konark and Bhubaneswar - a regional parallel to Khajuraho.

## 7. Solanki and Pala-Sena: material and water as determinants

- Solanki, or Maru-Gurjara: north-western India including Gujarat and Rajasthan, under Solanki patronage.
- Walls devoid of carvings; garbhagriha connected to the mandapa both internally and externally; porticos with decorative arched torans.
- Unique feature: the step tank, surya kund, near the temple, its steps full of small temples with wooden carvings.
- Materials: sandstone, black basalt and soft marble.
- Most temples east-facing; at the equinoxes sun rays fall directly into the central shrine.
- Modhera Sun Temple, Gujarat, built 1026-27 AD by Bhima I of the Chaulukya or Solanki dynasty.
- Pala-Sena: Bengal, 8th-12th century AD. Palas primarily Buddhist of the Mahayana tradition, tolerant, patronising both religions, building many viharas, chaityas and stupas; Senas Hindus who built Hindu temples and also sustained Buddhist architecture.
- Pala monuments: the universities of Nalanda, Jagaddala, Odantapuri and Vikramshila; Somapura Mahavihara in Bangladesh.
- Sena form: curved or sloping roof as in bamboo huts - the "Bangla roof", later adopted by Mughal architects.
- Principal material: burnt bricks and clay, that is, terracotta bricks. Tower: tall curving shikhara crowned by a large amalaka, similar to the Odisha school. Sculpture in stone and metal, stone the major component, figures unique in their highly lustrous finish.
- Named examples: Siddhesvara Mahadeva Temple, Barakar; temples around Bishnupur, West Bengal - not an inscribed property.
- Organising argument: environment sets the problem, material sets the possible answers, style is the solution.

## 8. Chandella political setting and patronage mechanism

- The tract: Vats in ancient times, Jejabhukti in medieval times, Bundelkhand since the 14th century; Jejabhukti named after Jayashakti, also Jejjaka or Jeja.
- 4th-6th c. AD Gupta resurgence of the arts in the region, with leading centres at Bhumara, Khoh, Nachna and Deogarh.
- The Chandela dynasty ruled central India between the 9th and 13th centuries AD; one of the most stable kingdoms of central India; contemporaries of the Rashtrakutas, Pratiharas and Palas; inscriptions connect its early kings with Kharjju-ravahaka, their capital.
- Jayashakti and Vijayashakti prominent early; expansion under Harsha and Yashovarman within the Gurjara-Pratihara political sphere on the mainstream account. The textbook account consulted here instead records, hedged, an acknowledgement of Pala overlordship under Devapala - disclosed as a discrepancy and not adopted.
- With the decline of the Pratiharas and Palas, the Chandelas asserted independence; Dhanga, the first independent Chandela king, took the title maharajadhiraja; several Khajuraho temples were built during his reign.
- Do not assign an individual temple to a named ruler.
- Mechanism: monumental temple building follows political self-assertion, which supplies both surplus and the need for a durable public statement.
- Parallel commemorative commissions: Lokmahadevi at Pattadakal; Vishnuvardhana at Belur; Krishnadevaraya's Mahanavami Dibba per Domingo Paes; Rajaraja I's Brihadishvara after imperial consolidation.
- Non-royal patronage exists: the Ayyavole 500 guild is named in a Lad Khan inscription and its inscriptions record temple endowments.
- External notice: Al-Biruni, 11th c., Khajuraho as the capital of Jajahuti; Ibn Battuta, 14th c., "Kajarra".

## 9. Khajuraho as built form

- All Khajuraho temples were built in the Nagara style, which reached its climax in this period.
- Lofty temples without any enclosure wall, erected on a high platform terrace.
- Consequences: the exterior is the public face; the raised terrace makes upper wall registers legible from below; circulation and viewing happen in the open, on the circuit.
- Fabric: fine-grained sandstone in buff, pink and pale-yellow shades; the fine even grain permits deep undercutting.
- Fabric is not uniform: the evidence base is internally inconsistent about the groups, and Chausath Yogini, Brahma and Mahadeva are identified as granite - state "predominantly sandstone, with granite exceptions".
- Axial chain: jagati, ardhamandapa, mandapa, antarala, garbhagriha, crowned by the shikhara, with connected halls forming one rising mass.
- Spire: the main shikhara is massed with clustered urushringas, producing the mountain-range silhouette.
- Orientation: all temples except the Chaturbhuja face the sunrise - always carry the exception.
- Dedications among surviving temples: six Shiva, eight Vishnu, one Ganesha, one Sun, four Jain Tirthankaras; Shaiva, Vaishnava and Jain sects in one idiom.
- Matangeshvara remains an active site of worship.
- Do not state a current count of standing temples.

## 10. The Khajuraho monuments, by the job each does

- Earlier, less splendid group: Chausath-yogini, Brahma, Varaha, Lalgu.
- Second group: Lakshmana and Duladeo temples and the Mahadeva temples.
- Other named temples: Parsvanath, Visvanath, Jagadambi, Chitragupta.
- Chausath-yogini: the earliest building at Khajuraho; open-air Yogini shrine; granite; Tantric and Shaiva traditions. Plan disputed - circular in the textbook account, rectangular in the heritage record, and the textbook's own sidebar allows either for the type.
- Chausath Yogini temples as a type: open-to-sky shrines to Yogini figures of the Shakti and Tantric traditions; typically circular or sometimes rectangular enclosures; mostly 9th-11th centuries AD; about 13 survive in India, most in Madhya Pradesh, then Uttar Pradesh, Odisha, Tamil Nadu.
- Morena or Mitaoli Chausath Yogini: circular and Kachchhapaghata on the reproduced item's own key; the Parliament-inspiration story is a popular belief; it is not the only circular temple in India and was not meant to promote the Vaishnava sect. The old Parliament building of 1927 was designed by Herbert Baker and Edwin Lutyens.
- Kandariya-Mahadeva: Shiva; largest and loftiest monument; the peak of the architectural and sculptural efflorescence - the named climax for any answer.
- Chitragupta: the Surya, or Sun, temple. Parsvanath: the principal Jain temple, built in the same Nagara idiom. Matangeshvara: the one living temple. Chaturbhuja: the one orientation exception.
- Bhand Deva Temple, Rajasthan, 10th century: built in the style of the Khajuraho monuments, often called "Little Khajuraho" - the idiom travelled.

## 11. The Chandella sculptural programme

- The 2025 wording contains two claims: vigour is how it is carved, breadth of life is what is carved.
- Four causes of vigour: fine-grained sandstone permitting deep undercutting; an unwalled elevation on a high terrace; ratha offsets creating light and shadow; flexed, twisting, interacting figures rather than frontal icons.
- "Resilient" also carries a structural sense: substantial bodies standing almost free while remaining part of a load-bearing wall.
- Breadth, strongest evidence first: the site's plural dedications; then the social registers - worshippers, dancers, musicians, teachers and disciples, courtly life; then the imaginative register of Vyalas, hybrid imaginary animals with lions' bodies on Vishnu-dedicated shrines, found in other Indian temples too; then maithuna reliefs in both the Hindu and Jain temples.
- Never state a proportion; never claim Vyalas are unique to Khajuraho; never invent named postures for specific panels.
- Placement rules across schools: Ganga and Yamuna outside the garbhagriha (Nagara); dvarapala, mithuna and yaksha at the garbhagriha entrance (Dravida); vahana just before the sanctum; ratha planes as narrative panels; devakoshtha niches and a base lion frieze (Chola); horizontal friezes of elephants, lions, scrolls, horses, makaras, swans and deities (Hoysala); a separate natana-sabha for the Nataraja image.
- Two organising logics: northern programmes read vertically by wall plane; Chola and Hoysala programmes read horizontally by band and niche.

## 12. The erotic register, disciplined

- Proportion: the evidence records "a variety of artwork, including erotic element depicted through maithuna reliefs" - one item within a set, with no figure anywhere.
- Parallel: "like Khajuraho temples, the erotic element is also prominent in the temples of Konark and Bhubaneswar"; Konark's chariot walls carry erotic mithuna scenes; mithuna figures stand at Dravida sanctum doors; and a list of twelve Indian temples famous for erotic sculptures spans Madhya Pradesh, Odisha, Karnataka, Gujarat, Tamil Nadu, Rajasthan, Maharashtra and Uttarakhand.
- Interpretation: Tantric, ritual, symbolic, didactic and worldly-life readings compete; none is established; report the disagreement and invent no textual authority.
- Defensible architectural observation: the register's placement alongside worship, teaching, performance and courtly life treats it as part of a depicted world.
- Doctrinal and normative readings belong to Topic 13 and to Society.
- The Chausath-yogini establishes early Shakta and Tantric presence at the site but does not explain the later maithuna reliefs; co-presence is not causation.

## 13. Pallava: the conversion of rock into architecture

- Temple architecture in South India began under Mahendravarman I, 610-630 AD.
- A. H. Longhurst classified Pallava architecture into four chronological styles: Mahendravarman 610-640; Mamalla 640-670, under Narasimhavarman I; Rajasimha 674-800; Nandivarman 800-900.
- Mahendravarman: rock-cut temples with pillared mandapas and a garbhagriha at the end. Mandagapattu, Villupuram, the earliest Pallava rock-cut work, with an inscription stressing no timber, no metal, no brick. Mamandur cave temples near Kanchipuram carry his titles, including Vichitrachitta and Satrumalla.
- Mamalla: mandapas divided into separate monolithic rathas - the Pancha Rathas, five temples carved from a single rock, named after the Pandavas and Draupadi, using all known forms of plan and elevation: Draupadi hut-like; Arjuna a dvitala vimana with mukhamandapa; Bhima rectangular with a wagon-vaulted roof; Dharmaraja a tritala vimana; Nakula-Sahadeva apsidal - the architect's experimental tendency.
- Rajasimha: the Shore Temple complex, 8th century, on the Bay of Bengal; during his reign the rock-cut technique was replaced by structural temples; three shrines with a Shiva linga, a Somaskanda sculpture and Vishnu resting on Ananta.
- Mahabalipuram: Coromandel coast, about 60 km south of Chennai, the printed "south-west" being a disclosed direction slip; named after Narasimhavarman I, titled Mamalla, "a great hero"; most monuments are monolithic rock-cut temples. Ganesh Ratha, late 7th century, built with a Shiva linga, now deified with Ganesha. Varaha and Mahisasurmardini mandapas.
- The gigantic open-air relief across two boulders is interpreted by historians either as the descent of the Ganga or as Arjuna's penance; Krishna's Butter Ball lies nearby.
- Capital Kanchipuram; the dynasty ruled parts of southern India in the 4th-9th centuries AD.
- Dandin, 7th-8th century Sanskrit scholar of the Pallava court, praised artists in the Avantisundari Katha for repairing a Vishnu sculpture at Mamallapuram.
- Kailasanatha, Kanchipuram, and Panamalai preserve the only two surviving examples of Pallava mural painting - a survival statement; painting belongs to Topic 07.
- Marco Polo's "Seven Pagodas", of which the Shore Temple survives; Manucci, who never visited, assumed Chinese builders; UNESCO 1984.
- Limits for an "estimate": minimal mural survival; Longhurst's scheme is a modern classification; the Cholas continued the legacy.

## 14. Chola: granite, bronzes and a documented institution

- Chola power emerges in the 9th century AD; the core area is Cholamandalam around Thanjavur, whence the Portuguese derived "Coromandel"; the Cholas continued the Pallavas' legacy.
- The lion frieze along the base is the typical Chola entry in the Dravida feature list.
- Phase 1: Narttamalai, Vijayalaya, mid-9th c. - vimana joined by ardha-mandapa, six parivaralayas, dvarapalas at the entrance.
- Phase 2: Aditya I and Parantaka I, late 9th-early 10th c. - Brahmapureshvara, Nageshvaraswami, Koranganatha; antarala and mukhamandapa added; devakoshtha niches with deity images.
- Phase 3: Queen Shembiyan Mahadevi, patron during the reigns of her husband Gandaraditya and her son Uttama I - many brick temples rebuilt in stone; recorded elsewhere as building at Kutralam, Virudhachalam and Aduthurai.
- Phase 4: Brihadishvara, Thanjavur, Rajaraja I, early 11th c.; also Rajarajeshvara, Periya Kovil, Dakshina Meru; vimana approximately 66 m, about 216 ft, with a towering pyramidical shikhara, the textbook 60 m being a disclosed approximation; pillared porch, mukhamandapa, ardhamandapa, antarala, garbhagriha; profuse outer-wall ornament; huge rectangular enclosure with large gopurams; Nandi Mandap with the country's second-largest monolithic Nandi, the largest being at Lepakshi Veerbhadra, Andhra Pradesh; entirely granite - the first fully granite temple in the world.
- Gangaikondacholapuram, Rajendra I: incomplete, lower vimana, shikhara curved inwards, walls more heavily embellished - not comparable with Thanjavur.
- Last phase, 12th-13th c.: the gopura becomes more dominant than the vimana - Chidambaram under Kulottunga I.
- Airavatesvara, Darasuram, Rajaraja II, 12th c., named after Indra's white elephant; Vaishnava and Shakta traditions alongside Shaiva, with the legends of the sixty-three Nayanars; Rajagambhira Thirumandapam with carved pillars, granite steps and horse-drawn stone chariots.
- Documentary achievement: inscriptions in the Brihadishvara premises; the Darasuram Tamil inscription on deities, daily worship, donations, festivals and administration, "rather unprecedented in the history"; Rajaraja's astronomer "Prenkani" for festival dates from planetary movements.
- Sculpture and painting, routed: Nataraja in tandava, earliest known at Ravana Phadi, Aihole, peak under the Cholas, with natana-sabha halls and a comparative set at Chidambaram, Thanjavur, Pallava cave reliefs and Hoysala panels (Topic 06); Brihadishvara frescoes with Shaiva, Vaishnava and Shakta subjects (Topic 07).
- "Great Living Chola Temples": one serial UNESCO property - Thanjavur 1987, with Gangaikondacholapuram and Darasuram added as extensions in 2004.
- Counterpoints: the incomplete successor; the formula superseded by the gopura; undocumented labour and surplus; inclusive iconography as the temple's own claim.
- Chera entry, recorded with its corrected chronology: Early Cheras of the Sangam age, broadly c. 3rd century BCE to c. 3rd century CE, seated at Vanji with the seaports Tondi and Musiri; later Chera Perumals of Mahodayapuram, modern Kodungalloor, broadly c. 9th to 12th century CE, to which the named temples belong; the single printed span from the 4th century BCE to the 12th century CE compresses two separated phases and must not be reproduced. Emblem the bow and arrow; kings also called Keralaputas; Uthiyan Cheralathan the earliest known ruler; named temples Thirunelli, Vadakkunnathan, Kodungallur Bhagavathy and Kandiyur Siva; temples built in Dravidian style, octagonal or rectangular, in granite or sandstone, with a prominent gopuram and intricately carved wooden pillars and ceilings along with mural paintings. Do not extend this into a Kerala plan vocabulary that nothing here supplies.

## 15. The Deccan laboratory

- Vesara, the Karnataka school, is defined as combining Nagara and Dravidian features; its three named makers are the Early Chalukyas of Badami, the Rashtrakutas at Ellora and the Hoysalas at Belur, Halebidu and Somanathapura.
- Aihole: Malprabha river; "Cradle of Hindu Temple Architecture"; over 120 temples; first Early Chalukya capital till 543 AD, when Pulakeshin I shifted to Vatapi; named after the Ayyavole 500 guild, with Valanjiyar and Manigraman, protectors of the Veera-Banaju-Dharma.
- Durga Temple, Aihole, c. 7th-8th c. under Vijayaditya: apsidal, believed inspired by Buddhist chaityas; named after a nearby fort, *durg*; believed dedicated to the Sun God Aditya.
- Lad Khan Temple, Aihole: square plan akin to a village panchayat hall; rooftop shrine instead of a shikhara; sloping two-tiered roof with stone logs, inspired by wooden structures; mukhamandapa and sabhamandapa; carries the Ayyavole guild inscription.
- Ravana Phadi, 6th c.: earliest rock-cut here; Nataraja with larger-than-life saptamatrikas - rock-cut and structural work proceed side by side.
- Aihole Prashasti, Meguti hill, on a Jain temple wall: by Ravikirti for Pulakeshin II, in Sanskrit language and old Kannada script; victories over the Gangas of Mysore, Alupas, Konkan, Kadambas of Banavasi and Kunala; names Bharavi and Kalidasa; a eulogy.
- Badami: Bagalkote district; Malprabha and the man-made Agastya lake; formerly Vatapi, the second capital; begun by Pulakeshin I, continued by Kirtivarman I, completed by Mangalesh; soft red sandstone; four caves, three Brahmanical and one Jain. Cave 1 Nataraja, Mahishasura Mardini, Ardhanarishvara; Cave 2 Vishnu, Trivikrama, Harihara, with Agni, Indra, Varuna, Gajalakshmi and Swastik; Cave 3 the largest and the earliest dated Hindu temple in the Deccan, with Mangalesh's four-line old-Kannada inscription of 578 AD recording a village grant; Cave 4 Jain - Parsvanath, Mahavir, Bahubali. A Bhutanatha Temple stands by the lake, and one further cave may be Buddhist.
- Pattadakal: UNESCO 1987; site of Chalukyan coronations, Pattabhisheka; largely 7th-8th c.; nine Hindu and one Jain temple; blends the north Indian Naagar and South Indian Dravidian styles.
- Virupaksha, Pattadakal: largest and most profusely sculpted; commissioned by Queen Lokmahadevi to commemorate Vikramaditya II's conquest of Kanchipuram; pillared hall, antechamber, sanctum with an enclosed circumambulation passage; Dravidian shikhara; deep outer-niche carvings mostly of Shiva. Mallikarjuna: similar but smaller, by Queen Trilok Mahadevi, with Samudra Manthan and Mahisasuramardini. Papanatha: similar in plan, north Indian Nagara shikhara, Ramayana panels. Jain Narayana Temple: built by the Rashtrakutas in the 9th century.
- The three-stage spine: Aihole the incubator, Badami the refinement in the 6th-7th centuries, Pattadakal the matured culmination.
- Rashtrakuta, 750-983 AD: the monolithic Kailashnatha, Cave 16, Ellora, by Krishna I, 8th century - the limiting case of the subtractive method; detail owned by Topic 02; Ellora inscribed 1983.
- Hoysala: 11th-14th centuries; Karnataka, parts of Tamil Nadu, south-western Telangana; capital Belur then Dwarasamudra (Halebidu); more than 100 temples survive; the three temples at Belur on the Yagachi, Halebidu on the Dwarasamudra lake and Somanathapura on the Kaveri were inscribed by UNESCO in 2023, with official component naming preferred.
- Vishnuvardhana, 12th c.: converted from Jainism to Vaishnavism under Ramanujacharya's influence; commissioned Belur to commemorate his victory over the Cholas, and Halebidu. Other rulers include Nripa Kama I and II.
- Hoysala material and plan: chloritic schist or soapstone, soft when quarried and hardening over time; raised jagati on a stellate design with zig-zag walls; multiple shrines around a central pillared hall; the four-foot-high star platform's extended terrace acting as the pradakshinapath.
- Hoysala surface: friezes of the epics, mythological scenes, animals and floral motifs; lathe-turned pillars; frieze bands of elephants, lions, scrolls, horses, makaras and swans; Nandi Pavilion; separate kalyani tank.
- Hoysala style statement: Vesara, with Dravidian morphology plus substantial Bhumija and Nagara influences. Named makers: Dasoja, Chavana, Mallithamma. Plurality: rulers followed Jainism and Hinduism; Kannada and Sanskrit literature by Jain and Brahmin poets; three Jain basadi in a walled complex at Halebidu.
- Kakatiya: Orugallu, modern Warangal, capital from the 12th-14th centuries, with Hanumakonda the older capital before Ganapati Deva's shift. Thousand Pillar (Sri Rudreshwara Swamy) Temple, ordered by Rudra Deva, in later Chalukyan and early Kakatiyan style, star-shaped platform, sand-box technique, double platform, Trikutalayam of Rudreswara, Vasudeva and Surya, a granite pillar carrying Rudradeva's construction-date inscription; damaged by the Tughlaqs in the 14th century and restored with a grant from Mir Osman Ali Khan, the 7th Nizam.
- Warangal Fort: 13th century, fortified by Rudrama Devi; Kakatiya Kala Thoranam, four ornamental stone gateways, in the Telangana state emblem; on the Tentative List with the Thousand Pillar Temple.
- Ramappa, Mulugu district, 13th century, UNESCO 2021: built in the interest of Recharla Rudra, general of Ganapati Deva; about 40 years; perhaps the only temple known by its architect's name; sandbox technique of sand-lime, jaggery and black myrobalan acting as a cushion; floating stone ceiling; star-shaped platform; pinkish sandstone with lavish black basalt for polished near-life-size figures; Marco Polo's phrase reported as supposed.

## 16. Vijayanagara

- 14th-16th centuries, on the Tungabhadra in central Karnataka; also Pampa Kshetra; founded by Harihar and Bukka in 1336 AD; capital Hampi; four successive ruling houses - Sangama, Saluva, Tuluva, Aravidu - whose conventional ranges (1336-1485, 1485-1503, 1491-1570, 1542-1646) overlap because each transition was a contested usurpation.
- Infrastructure: the city fortified, the river dammed, water carried through a rock-cut aqueduct; the Hiriya canal built by Sangam rulers irrigated the valley.
- Temple architecture combined the features of the Cholas, the Hoysalas, the Pandyas, the Chalukyas and even the Indo-Islamic style of Bijapur; a unique feature was wide chariot streets flanked by a row of pillared mandapas.
- By 1500 AD Hampi was the world's second-largest medieval-era city after Beijing; travellers include Abdur Razzaq, Niccolo de' Conti, Afanasy Nikitin, Domingo Paes, Ludovico di Varthema, Fernao Nuniz and Duarte Barbosa; Krishnadevaraya composed the Amuktamalyada in Telugu.
- Virupaksha, Hampi: Shiva, the patron deity; 50-metre gopuram; begun in the Sangama period, expanded by Krishnadevaraya; the Raya Gopuram introduced in temple architecture for the first time by Krishnadevaraya.
- Vitthala complex: Vishnu in the Vitthala form, by Krishnadevaraya; the stone chariot, explicitly not monolithic, on the ₹50 note; musical pillars in the Rang Mandapa; a Kalyana Mandapa, a wedding hall.
- Hazara Rama temple: Devaraya II; Rama; Ramayana reliefs; pillars producing different melodious sounds - distinct from the Vitthala pillars.
- Yali: part horse part lion, a guardian creature on temple pillars; in Tamil literature cat-like body, lion's head with elephant tusks, serpent's tail; later symbolic of Nayaka identity.
- Mahanavami Dibba: a huge high platform used for ritual purposes; per Domingo Paes erected by Krishnadevaraya to mark his Odisha campaign; site of the nine-day Mahanavami festival and army reviews; three-tiered stepped platform with daily-life scenes. Not a temple, and the Virupaksha temple is not on it.
- Indo-Islamic form is in the secular zone: Lotus Mahal's arched roof in the Zenana enclosure for royal women, the domed elephant stable, the Queen's Bath with stepped design and Indo-Islamic arches; the monolithic rock-cut Lakshmi Narasimha on Shesha is called one of the masterpieces of Indian sculpture.
- Kalyana Mandapa: a Hindu temple wedding hall; Vitthala has one, and the Lepakshi Veerbhadra temple has an incomplete one with the divine wedding of Shiva and Parvati - a Vijayanagara feature.
- Fall and recovery: defeated by a coalition of the Adil Shahi, Nizam Shahi, Qutub Shahi and Barid Shahi; capital destroyed in 1565 at Talikota; capital moved to Penukonda then Chandragiri; ruins brought to light in 1800 by Colonel Colin Mackenzie, the first Surveyor General of India, with Alexander Rea; Robert Sewell's *A Forgotten Empire*, 1900; UNESCO 1986.

## 17. Nayaka

- Vijayanagara feudatories; after Talikota in 1565 the Madurai Nayakas asserted autonomy, ruling from the 16th to the 18th century; revitalised temples previously ransacked by the Delhi Sultans.
- Periodisation: the Nayaka style continues the late Vijayanagara style, influenced by local Chola and Pandyan traditions; Pallavas and Cholas pioneered early Dravidian architecture, Nayakas late Dravidian architecture.
- Centres: Madurai, Thanjavur, Tiruchirapalli, Gingee, Ikkeri; chief patron Thirumalai Nayak, also spelled Tirumala Nayaka, 17th century.
- Plan: rectangular high-walled premises entered by east and west gopurams; concentric rectangles added within higher walls; four entrances, each wall's gopuram larger than the previous; a pillared hall and a square water tank outside.
- Material: granite core, stucco - mortar over brick - for superstructures and gopurams; visible gopura figures are renewed, so their dates are not construction dates.
- Devices: large gopurams, large tanks, wide prakaram corridors for processions and pradakshina; Rameswaram's Ramanathaswamy temple claimed to have the longest prakaram in the world; narrative and composite columns; heavily painted gopurams featuring the Yali.
- Meenakshi temple, Madurai, on the Vaigai: most of the present campus repaired, renovated and expanded in the 17th century by Tirumala Nayaka; Meenakshi (Parvati), Sundareshwar (Shiva) and her brother Alagar (Vishnu), representing the confluence of Shaivism, Shaktism and Vaishnavism; one of the 276 Paadal Petra Sthalam revered by the Tamil Saiva Nayanars of the 6th-9th centuries; Potramarai Kulam tank; 14 gopurams; the Tamil Nadu State Emblem is based on its gopuram; the month-long Chithirai festival in April-May.
- Srirangam's Ranganathaswamy and Rameswaram's Ramanathaswamy had immense contribution from the Nayaka rulers - contribution to, not built by.

## 18. Engineering, material and patronage as one argument

- Foundations: the sandbox technique - a pit filled with sand-lime, jaggery and black myrobalan, acting as a cushion - at Ramappa and the Thousand Pillar Temple.
- Ceilings: Ramappa's floating stone, light stones supporting the ceiling.
- Mass: Brihadishvara entirely granite, the first fully granite temple in the world, with a vimana of approximately 66 m.
- Composite construction: Nayaka granite cores with stucco over brick for gopurams - scale plus renewability.
- Subtractive extremes: the Ellora Kailasa excavated whole; the Pancha Rathas from one rock using all known forms of plan and elevation.
- Material-to-form chain: fine sandstone permits deep undercutting; soft red sandstone permits cave excavation; soapstone is soft then hardens; granite gives mass and austerity; pinkish sandstone with black basalt gives polished figures; terracotta brick gives moulded relief; stucco gives renewable scale.
- Qualification: material explains surface, fabric and some plan devices, not the inherited grammar - Bengal builds an Odisha-like amalaka-crowned shikhara in terracotta.
- Patronage spectrum: sovereign rulers; royal women - Lokmahadevi, Trilok Mahadevi, Tribhuvana-Mahadevi, Shembiyan Mahadevi; a general, Recharla Rudra; a merchant guild, the Ayyavole 500; a later donor, Mir Osman Ali Khan; and named makers - Dasoja, Chavana, Mallithamma, and Ramappa.
- Ritual movement at four scales, moving outward over a millennium: the covered pradakshina; the Hoysala open terrace; the Nayaka prakaram; the Vijayanagara chariot street - mirroring the vimana-to-gopura migration.

## 19. Temple sculpture as social evidence

- Master rule: temple sculpture is representation commissioned by patrons, so it evidences what patrons chose to make visible.
- Performance - dancers and musicians: evidences valuation and codification, not repertoire.
- Instruction - teachers and disciples: evidences a depictable social value, not literacy.
- Court and everyday life - secular figures at Khajuraho, daily-life scenes on the Mahanavami Dibba and the Rajagambhira courtyard at Darasuram: evidences that temples depicted the social world, not that depiction equals description.
- Devotional movement - the sixty-three Nayanars at Darasuram: evidences bhakti hagiography entering royal temple art, not inner belief.
- Gender and patronage - Shembiyan Mahadevi's rebuilding programme: evidences elite women as major patrons, not the condition of women generally.
- Ritual and life-cycle - the 9th-century Kalyanasundara Murti, rendering panigrahana through two separate statuettes with Shiva's extended right hand accepting Parvati's as she steps forward with a bashful expression: evidences a known, codified marriage rite, not its uniform practice.
- Labour and craft - signed Hoysala work and the Ayyavole guild's endowments: evidences named artisans and organised merchant bodies, not wages, workshop size or status.
- The limit case: the erotic register cannot be read as social behaviour, and royal temples cannot be read as village life.

## 20. Loss, rediscovery, protection and current-claim discipline

- Rule: inscription years are stable and citable; counts, protection statuses, danger-listings, budgets and management measures are dynamic and must be routed to a dated official record and to Topic 14.
- Khajuraho: sank from public consciousness soon after the Chandella dynasty expired in the 13th century; T. S. Burt, a British engineer, ventured into the forest in 1838 in search of "a place called Khajrao"; the temples were later found under Alexander Cunningham's leadership; inscribed on the UNESCO World Heritage List in 1986 for outstanding architecture, diversity of temple forms and testimony to the Chandela civilisation.
- Hampi: destroyed in 1565 at Talikota; brought to light in 1800 by Colin Mackenzie; Sewell's book, 1900; inscribed 1986.
- The contrast: Khajuraho forgotten, Hampi destroyed - both recovered by named surveyors in the early nineteenth century and inscribed in the same year.
- Citable inscription years: Ellora 1983; Mahabalipuram 1984; Konark 1984; Khajuraho 1986; Hampi 1986; Pattadakal 1987; Great Living Chola Temples 1987 with a 2004 extension; Ramappa 2021; Sacred Ensembles of the Hoysalas 2023.
- Not inscribed: Aihole and Badami, on the Tentative List with Pattadakal under "Evolution of Temple Architecture - Aihole-Badami-Pattadakal"; the Thousand Pillar Temple, Warangal Fort and Kakatiya Kala Thoranam; Bishnupur.
- The Great Living Chola Temples is one serial property of three temple complexes.
- Living monuments: Matangeshvara at Khajuraho; the three Chola temples; Madurai's Chithirai festival; renewed Nayaka stucco.
- The trade-off: at Khajuraho the unwalled terraces make visitor access and the display surface the same surface, so tourism and integrity are structurally in tension, and inscription brings cost as well as benefit.
- The session's single current linkage: the Parliamentary Standing Committee on Transport, Tourism and Culture report on the Demands for Grants (2026-27) of the Ministry of Culture, presented 25 March 2026, recommending a comprehensive review of maintenance standards and visitor facilities at all World Heritage Sites with the ASI and a time-bound upgradation plan, plus a five-year nomination strategy with at least ten potential nominations; it records that the ASI maintains 3,685 centrally protected monuments through 38 circles and was allocated ₹1,235.78 crore for 2026-27, 36.17 per cent of the Ministry's allocation. Used once, as institutional evidence only.

## 21. Answer construction and factual-risk controls

- Six moves: read the directive and the noun; pick one thesis; lay a vocabulary frame; attach every claim to a named monument with patron or date; give one comparison and one limit; close with a verdict in the question's own words plus a dated status line.
- Nine theses are written out in Lesson 21; seven verdict lines close them.
- Mark-scaled structures exist for ten demand families, from the 10-mark Chandella and Pallava routes to the 20-mark southern trajectory.
- The complete factual-risk control list is in Lesson 21 and must be run before every practice attempt; its highest-frequency items are: no erotic proportion; no ruler-to-temple attribution at Khajuraho; no current temple count; the Chausath Yogini plan conflict recorded not resolved; Khajuraho and Morena kept apart; approximately 66 m, with the textbook about-60 m approximation disclosed, and early 11th century, for Brihadishvara; the Jain Narayana temple as Rashtrakuta; the Hampi stone chariot as not monolithic; Indo-Islamic form in the royal zone only; the Mahanavami Dibba as a platform; the Aihole Durga temple's solar dedication; Meenakshi's campus not its foundation; more than 100 Hoysala temples and 14 Madurai gopurams; naming variants recorded; traveller attributions flagged; Tentative-List sites not upgraded; the Chola serial property counted once; and the Gurjara-Pratihara association written with the hedged Devapala claim disclosed rather than adopted.

9. **Honest nil and verification records.** No official 2022 Mains paper is held locally, so the 2022 temple-sculpture demand's exact wording is recorded from the ledger and the canonical owner and is explicitly not reproduced as a verbatim quotation. No official 2019 or 2021 Prelims paper or key is held locally; the two objective items for those years survive here only as reproductions inside the core art source, with that source's own answer key, and are labelled with that provenance throughout. Three further reproduced objective items - a Combined Defence Services II 2025 Deogarh-location item, a Combined Defence Services I 2018 Vijayanagara ritual-platform item and a Central Armed Police Forces statement pair on the Hampi Virupaksha and the Mahanavami Dibba - are recorded as corroboration only, never as examinable precedents, because none is a Civil Services paper and none of the original papers is held locally.

## Bounded material-fact pass

10. **One finite pass, pre-declared and then closed.** The bounded-research rule permits a single material-fact pass for genuinely disputed points or for a short pre-declared list of standard connecting names whose omission would create a real syllabus gap. The pass covered exactly two items and was then closed.
    - **Item one, a disputed plan and fabric.** The core art source describes Khajuraho's Chausath-yogini as having "circular and open-air structures", while this topic's own canonical owners follow heritage documentation describing this particular shrine as **rectangular** and built of **granite**. A single reference check confirmed the heritage-side description: a rectangular or quadrangular courtyard ringed with small shrine cells, built of coarse granite blocks rather than the sandstone used for the later and more ornate Khajuraho temples, making it the site's one major granite structure. This session follows the heritage description, states the conflict openly in Lesson 12, and notes that the same core source's own sidebar concedes that Chausath Yogini temples are "typically with circular or sometimes rectangular enclosures".
    - **Item two, two monument attributions created by an official 2026 Prelims item.** The verified 2026 Prelims Question 4 names four temples, of which the core art source describes only two - Deogarh's Dashavatara temple as a stage-three Gupta temple with an emergent curvilinear shikhara, and Pattadakal's Virupaksha as carrying a Dravidian shikhara. It contains no description of the **Malegitti Shivalaya at Badami** or of the **Huchimalligudi temple at Aihole**. A single reference check established that the Malegitti Shivalaya carries a tiered Dravida vimana with an octagonal crowning element and that the Huchchimalli Gudi carries an early curvilinear northern superstructure of the rekha type. These two attributions are carried as reference-level findings, are labelled as such in the PYQ section, and are not used to derive or imply an answer letter for the provisionally keyed item.
11. No other external research was conducted. No optional source, exhaustive corroboration or decorative current affairs was pursued.

## Current-affairs source used

12. **Lesson 20, the single current linkage.** Search run: "Khajuraho ASI conservation and UNESCO World Heritage Site maintenance review, India, 2026". Item used: the **Parliamentary Standing Committee on Transport, Tourism and Culture**, chaired by **Sanjay Kumar Jha**, report on the **Demands for Grants (2026-27) of the Ministry of Culture**, **presented in Parliament on 25 March 2026**. Recorded content used: the recommendation that the Ministry, in coordination with the Archaeological Survey of India, undertake a comprehensive review of maintenance standards and visitor facilities at all World Heritage Sites and prepare a time-bound upgradation plan; the recommendation of a five-year UNESCO nomination strategy with a pipeline of at least ten potential nominations, a structured engagement plan for multinational nominations and dedicated staff and budgetary provision, to be presented within one year; the record that the ASI maintains 3,685 centrally protected monuments through 38 circles; and the record that the ASI was allocated ₹1,235.78 crore for 2026-27, constituting 36.17 per cent of the Ministry's total allocation. The report was read directly from a national newspaper's detailed report of the tabled document; the primary committee report itself was not opened in this pass, and the item is therefore attributed to that dated secondary account of a tabled parliamentary report rather than to a directly retrieved primary document.
13. No other current item is used anywhere in this session. Twenty of the twenty-one lessons record truthfully in their pre-teach checklists that no current linkage is required.

## Evidence, discrepancy and prohibition controls

- **Original-practice control, verified after repair.** No official or near-official question is set as practice anywhere in this file, and no official question carries a model answer, an opening sentence, a closing sentence or an answer skeleton outside its answer-neutral entry in the verified PYQ section. All twenty-one lesson-local Mains questions and all six final Mains questions are original in wording **and in operation**: each tests a demand type that no official paper in this topic's range has set. Four lesson-local practices that previously restated official demands - the 2025 Chandella, 2024 Pallava, 2024 Chola and 2026 Vijayanagara operations - were replaced outright, and a Lesson 21 concept check that supplied an opening and closing sentence for the 2024 Pallava demand was replaced with a directive-analysis question carrying no official answer text.
- **Practice-duplication control.** Lesson 21's final practice and final Question 6 were separated so that they test different reasoning on different evidence, and only Question 6 now carries the graded Deccan-north-south verdict on style labels. The material-and-regional-schools overlap was reduced from three questions to one: Lesson 9 retains the material-and-environment demand, Lesson 5 now tests the scope of a family label, and final Question 3 now tests the architectural incorporation of water.
- **Word-ceiling control.** Every one of the twenty-seven model answers was recounted independently after repair and is inside its stated ceiling.
- **Learner-facing provenance control.** Source roles, owner files, evidence-base labels and generation machinery are excluded from all twenty-one lessons, which use ordinary evidence-aware teaching language instead. Exact provenance is recorded only in this ledger and in the verified PYQ section, and the two published objective keys used in Lessons 12 and 18 are attributed there as published keys rather than as official UPSC keys.
- **Disclosed measurement discrepancy - Brihadishvara's vimana.** The standard textbook account prints "60 m". The figure carried by the protecting authority and by most published measurements is approximately 66 m, about 216 ft. This session uses approximately 66 m at every occurrence, discloses the textbook approximation once in Lesson 16 and again in the factual-risk list, and records the divergence here rather than choosing silently.
- **Disclosed chronological correction - the Cheras.** The standard account prints a single span, "the Sangam period, around the 4th century BCE to the 12th century CE", which conflates the Early Cheras of the Sangam age, conventionally c. 3rd century BCE to c. 3rd century CE with their seat at Vanji and the ports Tondi and Musiri, with the later Chera Perumals of Mahodayapuram, modern Kodungalloor, conventionally c. 9th to 12th century CE, to which the named temples belong. Lesson 6 section 5 separates the two phases, records the gap between them and states that the compressed range must not be reproduced.
- **Disclosed geographical correction - Mamallapuram.** The standard account prints "around 60 km south-west of Chennai". A Coromandel-coast town cannot lie inland of the city it is measured from; the session writes about 60 km south of Chennai and discloses the printed direction as a slip in Lesson 15 and in the register notes.
- **Disclosed attribution correction - Chandella overlordship.** Mainstream accounts place Chandella expansion under Harsha and Yashovarman within the Gurjara-Pratihara political sphere, the dynasty rising as Pratihara feudatories in Jejabhukti before asserting independence. The textbook account consulted here instead records, in hedged form, an acknowledgement of Pala overlordship under Devapala. The session writes the Gurjara-Pratihara association, discloses the Devapala claim as a disagreement, and does not adopt it.
- **Disclosed dating convention - the Vijayanagara ruling houses.** The conventional ranges commonly printed for the four houses - Sangama 1336-1485, Saluva 1485-1503, Tuluva 1491-1570 and Aravidu 1542-1646 - overlap rather than succeed one another, because each transfer of power was a contested usurpation in which a regent or commander ruled in the name of a displaced line before assuming the throne. The session cites the houses in order, marks the years as conventional and approximate, and refuses to present them as crisp non-overlapping reigns.

- **Disclosed PYQ routing position one.** The 2024 GS-I Question 2 on the Pallavas is assigned by the Mains routing ledger to an Ancient History owner and retained by this topic's canonical owner as a direct architectural demand. Both positions are stated; the question is treated as jointly claimed; the ledger's assignment is not overwritten.
- **Disclosed PYQ routing position two.** The 2022 GS-I Question 1 on medieval temple sculpture is assigned by the ledger to the sculpture topic's advanced owner, while this topic's canonical owner records a Core routing supplying the named-evidence half. Both positions are stated, the demand is treated as jointly owned, and its exact wording is flagged as unverified because no official 2022 paper is held locally.
- **Disclosed PYQ routing position three.** The Civil Services 2019 Kalyana Mandapas item is routed by the ledger to the Indo-Islamic and regional architecture topic, while this topic's canonical owner records a substantive-ownership correction on the ground that a Kalyana Mandapa is a Hindu temple hall type. Both are stated, and the ledger is not overwritten.
- **Disclosed source discrepancy one - the Chausath-yogini plan and fabric.** Recorded in Lessons 12 and 21 and in the material-fact pass above. The conflict is stated, not silently resolved.
- **Disclosed source discrepancy two - the Khajuraho temple groups' fabric.** The core art source describes the second group as "composed entirely of sandstone structures" and, in the immediately following sentence, as "made partly of granite and partly of sandstone". The session uses the groups as a relative chronology and states fabric separately with its qualification, rather than reproducing a self-contradicting sentence. Recorded in Lessons 11 and 12.
- **Disclosed source discrepancy three - Kerala and Chera architecture.** This topic's canonical Basic owner records that no Kerala architectural style is described in its sources and bounds the region to murals, performance and named palaces. The core art source, however, carries a Chera architecture passage at PDF p. 94 stating that Chera temples were built in Dravidian style, characterised by octagonal or rectangular temples of granite or sandstone with a prominent gopuram and incorporating intricately carved wooden pillars and ceilings along with mural paintings, and naming Thirunelli, Vadakkunnathan, Kodungallur Bhagavathy and Kandiyur Siva temples. The discrepancy is recorded rather than resolved: the passage is reproduced in full in Lesson 6 section 5 with its own boundary note, this session does not build a Kerala architectural school on a single short entry, it states the owner's boundary in the error map and the register notes, and it does not assert any Kerala plan vocabulary - *srikovil*, *nalambalam* or *koothambalam* - that neither source supplies.
- **Disclosed naming variants, recorded and not normalised.** Chandela and Chandella, with the 2025 question's "Chandella" preserved when answering it; Lokmahadevi and Lokamahadevi; Thirumalai Nayak and Tirumala Nayaka; Visvanath and Vishvanath; Huchimalligudi, as printed in the official 2026 Prelims item, and Huchchimalli Gudi, as used in the reference check; and the Somanathapura temple, called Chennakeshava in the core source's table and Keshava Temple in the inscribed property's official component naming.
- **Numerical prohibitions observed absolutely.** No percentage or fraction of Khajuraho sculpture is stated anywhere. No decimal vimana height and no precise consecration year appear for Brihadishvara; the figures used are "approximately 66 m, about 216 ft" and "early 11th century", with the textbook's about-60 m approximation disclosed once in Lesson 16 and again in the factual-risk list. No current count of standing Khajuraho temples is asserted. Hoysala survival is stated only as "more than 100" and Madurai's gopurams only as 14. No World Heritage totals are stated.
- **Attribution prohibitions observed absolutely.** No individual Khajuraho temple is assigned to a named Chandella ruler. No Hoysala temple is assigned to a named sculptor. The Jain Narayana Temple at Pattadakal is attributed to the Rashtrakutas. The Hampi stone chariot is not called monolithic. Indo-Islamic form is confined to the Vijayanagara royal and secular zone. The Mahanavami Dibba is treated as a royal ceremonial platform and never as a temple.
- **Hedges preserved.** The Chandella acknowledgement of Pala overlordship is reproduced with the source's own "seem to have"; Marco Polo's phrase for Ramappa is reproduced as "supposed"; Domingo Paes's attribution for the Mahanavami Dibba is marked as a traveller's account; the Rameswaram prakaram superlative and the Hampi second-largest-city claim are attributed to the source; the Mitaoli Parliament influence is stated as a popular belief and a debated resemblance.
- **Interpretive openness preserved.** Competing readings of the erotic register, and the two historians' readings of the Mahabalipuram open-air relief, are reported as unresolved. No textual authority is invented for any of them.
- **Answer-neutral PYQ display.** The reproduced 2026 Prelims item appears with its exact printed stem, statements and options and with no answer letter, truth-value marking, option elimination or corrective mapping. The two items reproduced from inside the core source carry that source's own key with explicit attribution to the book rather than to the examining authority.
- **Status-claim discipline.** Only inscription years and recorded inscription grounds are asserted. Tentative-List sites are never upgraded. The Great Living Chola Temples is treated as one serial property. All counts, danger-listings, policy and institutional structure are routed to the heritage topic.
- **Boundary discipline.** Rock-cut detail and the Ellora Kailasa are named once and routed; Indo-Islamic building form is routed; the Parliament resemblance is routed; freestanding sculpture and iconographic doctrine are routed; mural painting is named only as a component of a temple programme and routed; dance posture is routed; inscriptions as literature and script history are routed; bhakti and Vedanta doctrine are routed; and dynastic political, military and revenue history is routed throughout.
- **Bounded-research discipline.** The substantive base is the complete canonical Basic owner, the complete canonical Advanced owner and one core OCR art source. External work was limited to exact PYQ verification and routing, one pre-declared two-item material-fact pass, and one brief dated current linkage, after which the pass was closed.
